12.4 Equations with prescribed singularities

Definitions, results, and proofs on this subject.

14 Equations with prescribed singularities

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Throughout this chapter we fix a Hermitian metric $\omega $ on $X$ and a smooth positive probability volume form $\mathrm{d}V$. Unless another measure is named, $L^ p$ norms, volumes and the phrase “almost everywhere” refer to $\mathrm{d}V$, while capacity and convergence in capacity refer to $\operatorname {Cap}_\omega $ from section 1.8. The background $\theta $ is a smooth real $(1,1)$-form whose positivity is specified in each section. For a smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\alpha $ and $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\alpha )$ we write $\alpha _ u=\alpha +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$, and we call $\{ u=-\infty \} $ the pole set of $u$; it is pluripolar and has zero volume. We write $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)$ for the extended non-pluripolar Monge–Ampère measure $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)$ of theorem 6.1.2. It equals $(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$ when $u$ is bounded, charges no pluripolar set, and may have infinite total mass; we keep the superscript where this possibility matters. Finally, $s_+=\max (s,0)$ for real $s$.

We use the following notation from earlier chapters. As in definition 1.12.1, $u\preceq v$ means that $u\leq v+C$ for some constant $C$, and $u\sim v$ means that $u\preceq v$ and $v\preceq u$; the equivalence classes of $\sim $ are the ordinary singularity types. For an obstacle $f$, $P_\theta (f)$ is the largest $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function lying below $f$ quasi-everywhere, $P_\theta (u,v)=P_\theta (\min (u,v))$ is the rooftop envelope, and $V_\theta =P_\theta (0)$; a potential has minimal singularities if it is equivalent to $V_\theta $ (section 1.19). The ordinary envelope $P_\theta [u]$ and the envelope fixed points, those $u$ with $P_\theta [u]=u$, are defined in definition 1.19.4. Recall that $\theta $ is big if some $\rho \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfies $\theta _\rho \geq \delta \omega $ with $\delta >0$. We call such a $\rho $ a strict barrier; by theorem 1.15.3 it can be chosen with analytic singularities.

We seek potentials $u$ in the ordinary singularity type of a given reference $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ solving

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}\mu \quad (\lambda >0) \qquad \text{or}\qquad \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=c\mu , \]

where $\mu $ is a given measure, typically $f\, \mathrm{d}V$ with $f\in L^ p$ and $p>1$, and the constant $c>0$ is part of the unknown. When $\varphi $ is unbounded, the comparison principles for bounded potentials no longer apply, and a limit of approximate solutions may leave the prescribed type. For a semipositive big background, both difficulties are overcome by a smaller background. If $P_{\delta \theta }(\varphi )\not\equiv -\infty $ for some $0<\delta <1$, then the part $\delta \theta $ of the background can carry the singularity of $\varphi $, while bounded envelopes in the remaining big background $(1-\delta )\theta $ have Monge–Ampère measures of positive mass. This gives a domination principle for singular potentials (theorem 14.1.2), and hence uniqueness. Existence starts from bounded equations with $L^ p$ densities, solved with a subsolution built from a strict barrier. A compactness criterion with a common lower barrier identifies limits of Monge–Ampère measures, and an addition theorem for weighted measures in a fixed type removes a possible deficit of mass in a limit. Together with a uniform estimate relative to the reference (theorem 14.11.3), these results solve both equations in the type of an envelope fixed point that admits such a smaller-background envelope (theorem 14.11.1). At exponent zero, comparison determines the constant $c$ but, in general, not the potential.

If $\theta $ is big but not semipositive, $V_\theta $ may be unbounded and the smaller-background construction is not available. A strict analytic barrier $\rho $ takes over its role. For two potentials bounded below by $\rho $ minus a constant, a small perturbation by $\rho $ forces a negative infimum of their difference to be attained away from the poles of $\rho $, where a local comparison applies. Resolving the singularities of analytic approximants of $V_\theta $ reduces the equations to semipositive big backgrounds. Stability above the barrier then produces solutions with minimal singularities which are locally Hölder continuous off the poles of $\rho $ (theorem 14.15.2, theorem 14.15.4, theorem 14.16.1). They are smooth off these poles when the density is smooth and strictly positive on the whole complement (theorem 14.17.1).

The same barrier controls smooth equations as a semipositive background degenerates (theorem 14.18.3). For small resolutions of threefold nodes with a closed background, the potentials converge smoothly away from the exceptional curves, and comparing the limiting and approximating metrics reduces to controlling the diameters of neighborhoods of these curves. In the projective example of theorem 11.6.1, where the normalized potential is not unique, symmetry selects the zero-exponent limit, while local examples show that a positive density on a small open set does not by itself give regularity. Resolved logarithmic poles and minimal types on blowups give explicit equations whose data are determined by the residual form and the Jacobian of the modification.

The last part of the chapter concerns a Hermitian background $h$ and general right-hand sides. Solvability of $\mathrm{MA}_ h(u)=c\mu $ for every nonzero finite non-pluripolar measure $\mu $ is equivalent to a positive lower bound for the masses of bounded $h$-plurisubharmonic functions. For singular data supported on shrinking coordinate spheres, a bounded-density test against a fixed probability supplies a strict Hölder reference, a normalized Hölder solution of the homogeneous equation and bounded exponential solutions (proposition 14.25.1, corollary 14.25.2, theorem 14.25.3), even when the lower mass vanishes. For an individual nonnegative density $f\in L^1$, bounded truncations and Gauduchon tests determine the largest coefficient $a$ for which $\mathrm{MA}_ h(u)\geq af\, \mathrm{d}V$ has a solution. Passing from this threshold to an exact equation at exponent zero requires control of the potentials as well as of their measures. At a positive exponent, exponential decay controls the mass on deep sublevels (theorem 14.33.1); this control is lost as the exponent tends to zero.

14.1 Domination with a smaller background

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Throughout this section $\theta $ is semipositive and big. Let $0<\delta <1$. When a singular potential lies above a $\delta \theta $-plurisubharmonic function, the gap between them can be measured by an envelope in the remaining background $(1-\delta )\theta $. This background is again semipositive and big, so its bounded potentials have Monge–Ampère measures of positive mass by proposition 13.2.1, and bounded domination applies to them. It bounds the envelope from below. A family of stretched envelopes then converts a strict measure inequality into a pointwise inequality, as in the bounded case of theorem 13.2.2.

We say that $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ admits a smaller-background envelope if $P_{\delta \theta }(u)\not\equiv -\infty $ for some $0<\delta <1$. This property depends only on the ordinary singularity type of $u$: if $u'\geq u-C$, then $P_{\delta \theta }(u)-C$ is a competitor for $P_{\delta \theta }(u')$.

Lemma 14.1.1. Suppose $u,v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, $v\preceq u$, and $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)(\{ u<v\} )=0$. If $0<\delta <1$ and $v_\delta =P_{\delta \theta }(v)\not\equiv -\infty $, then $u\geq v_\delta $.

Proof. Choose $C$ with $v-C\leq u$. Since $v_\delta \leq v$, the obstacle $u-v_\delta $ is bounded below by $-C$ off the pole sets, and it is finite almost everywhere. Hence the envelope

\[ p=P_{(1-\delta )\theta }(u-v_\delta ) \]

is bounded by lemma 1.19.2, and by contact support (theorem 13.1.4) its measure is carried by $D=\{ p+v_\delta =u\} $. Moreover $p+v_\delta \leq u$ everywhere, and

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(p+v_\delta ) =((1-\delta )\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p)+(\delta \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\delta ) \]

is a sum of two positive currents. Positivity of mixed products and ordered contact therefore give

\[ \mathbf1_ D\mathrm{MA}_{(1-\delta )\theta }(p) \leq \mathbf1_ D\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u); \]

this inequality is first proved on common finite truncation levels and then extended by exhaustion. Off the pole sets, every point of $D\cap \{ p<0\} $ satisfies $u=p+v_\delta <v_\delta \leq v$. The hypothesis therefore gives $\mathrm{MA}_{(1-\delta )\theta }(p)(\{ p<0\} )=0$. Since $(1-\delta )\theta $ is semipositive and big, bounded domination (theorem 13.2.2, with proposition 13.2.1), applied to $p$ and the constant $0$ with coefficient zero, yields $p\geq 0$. Hence $u\geq p+v_\delta \geq v_\delta $. $\square$

Theorem 14.1.2. Let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfy $P_{\delta \theta }(u)\not\equiv -\infty $ for some $0<\delta <1$. Suppose $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, $v\preceq u$, and $0\leq c<1$. If

\[ \mathbf1_{\{ u<v\} }\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u) \leq c\, \mathbf1_{\{ u<v\} }\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (v), \]

then $u\geq v$.

Proof. Since $\max (u,v)=v$ on the plurifine open set $\{ u<v\} $, plurifine locality shows that replacing $v$ by $\max (u,v)$ preserves the hypothesis; it does not change the conclusion. We may therefore assume $u\leq v$, and since $v\preceq u$ there is $C\geq 0$ with $u\leq v\leq u+C$. For $b>1$ consider the stretched envelopes

\[ p_ b=P_\theta (bu-(b-1)v),\qquad D_ b=\{ p_ b=bu-(b-1)v\} . \]

The function $u-(b-1)C$ is a competitor, so $p_ b\not\equiv -\infty $; and since $bu-(b-1)v\leq u$, we have $u-(b-1)C\leq p_ b\leq u$. By contact support, the measure of $p_ b$ is concentrated on $D_ b$. The potential $b^{-1}p_ b+(1-b^{-1})v$ lies below $u$ and agrees with it on $D_ b$, so ordered contact and positivity of mixed products give

\[ \mathbf1_{D_ b}\bigl [b^{-n}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (p_ b) +(1-b^{-1})^ n\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (v)\bigr ] \leq \mathbf1_{D_ b}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u). \]

Choose $b$ so large that $(1-b^{-1})^ n>c$. On $D_ b\cap \{ u<v\} $ the hypothesis then forces $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (p_ b)$ to vanish. Since the masses may be infinite, we justify this subtraction as follows. On each set where $u,v,p_ b>-k$, every measure involved is finite, being bounded by the mass of the corresponding bounded truncation. We subtract the finite terms on each such set and then take the union over $k$, so no infinite masses are ever subtracted.

It follows that the measure of $p_ b$ is carried by $D_ b\cap \{ u=v\} \subset \{ p_ b=u\} $. We apply lemma 14.1.1 to the pair $(p_ b,u)$: their singularities are equivalent, $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (p_ b)(\{ p_ b<u\} )=0$, and $P_{\delta \theta }(u)\not\equiv -\infty $. We obtain

\[ p_ b\geq P_{\delta \theta }(u) \]

for all sufficiently large $b$. Suppose now that $u\geq v$ fails. By lemma 1.3.3, the set $\{ u<v\} $ has positive volume. Since both potentials are finite almost everywhere, some set $E=\{ u<v-a\} $ with $a>0$ has positive volume and both functions are finite on $E$. On $E$ the obstacle inequality gives

\[ P_{\delta \theta }(u)\leq p_ b\leq v-ba \]

quasi-everywhere. Letting $b$ tend to infinity through the integers shows that the quasi-plurisubharmonic function $P_{\delta \theta }(u)\not\equiv -\infty $ equals $-\infty $ on a set of positive volume, a contradiction. Thus $u\geq v$ off the pole sets, and lemma 1.3.3 gives the inequality everywhere. $\square$

Corollary 14.1.3. Let $u,v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfy $v\preceq u$, and suppose that $u$ admits a smaller-background envelope. If $\lambda >0$ and

\[ \mathrm{e}^{-\lambda u}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u) \leq \mathrm{e}^{-\lambda v}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (v) \]

off the pole sets, then $u\geq v$. In particular, for every $\lambda >0$ and every positive measure $\mu $, the equation $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}\mu $ has at most one solution in an ordinary singularity type admitting a smaller-background envelope.

Proof. Fix $t>0$. On $\{ u<v-t\} $ the assumed inequality gives $\mathrm{MA}_\theta ^{\mathrm{ext}}(u)\leq \mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}\mathrm{MA}_\theta ^{\mathrm{ext}}(v-t)$. The theorem, applied to $u$ and $v-t$ with $c=\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}$, gives $u\geq v-t$; now let $t\downarrow 0$. For two solutions of the same equation in the stated type, apply this result in both directions. The weighted measures are compared off the common pole sets, which these measures do not charge, so no value of an infinite weight is needed. $\square$

14.2 Bounded equations in a semipositive big background

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

In this section $\theta $ is again semipositive and big, and we solve the exponential and the normalized equations with $L^ p$ densities by bounded potentials. The basic tool is a bounded subsolution. A strict barrier, corrected by the solution of a Hermitian equation, is composed with a bounded convex function; the result is a bounded $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function whose Monge–Ampère measure dominates a prescribed density. The derivative of the convex function decays polynomially near the poles of the barrier, and we compensate for this decay by multiplying the auxiliary density by the reciprocal weight.

Fix a strict barrier $\rho \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and $0<a\leq 1$ such that

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq a\omega ,\qquad \rho \leq -1. \]

No property of $\rho $ other than these two inequalities will be used.

Lemma 14.2.1. Fix $p>1$ and $L>0$. There is $b>0$ such that, for every $f\geq 0$ with $\| f\| _{L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)}\leq L$, one can find $s\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfying

\[ -1\leq s\leq 0,\qquad \mathrm{MA}_\theta (s)\geq b f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

The constant $b$ depends only on the fixed geometry and on $\rho ,a,p,L$.

Proof. Choose $1<q<p$. Since a fixed quasi-plurisubharmonic function belongs to every finite $L^ r$ space, Hölder's inequality gives a uniform $L^ q$ bound for

\[ g=(-\rho )^{2n}f. \]

By theorem 10.1.1 there are $c>0$ and a continuous $\omega $-plurisubharmonic $v$, normalized by $\sup _ Xv=-1$, such that

\[ \omega _ v^ n=c(1+g)\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

The construction in that theorem gives a uniform upper bound for $c$, because in its Gauduchon trace estimate the root integral of $1+g$ is bounded below by the root integral of $1$. The lower-density estimate of theorem 10.3.3 gives $c\| 1+g\| _ q\geq c_ q>0$, so $c$ is also bounded below. The potential estimate of the same theorem then gives $-C\leq v\leq -1$ with a uniform $C$.

Put $h=\rho +av\leq -1$. Then $\theta _ h\geq a\omega _ v$. On $(-\infty ,-1]$ define

\[ \chi (t)=-t^{-1}-1,\qquad \chi '(t)=t^{-2}\in (0,1],\qquad \chi ''(t)=-2t^{-3}>0, \]

and set $s=\chi (h)$, with value $-1$ at the poles of $h$. To justify both the positivity of $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}s$ and the computation of its Monge–Ampère measure, we first work with the bounded functions $h_ k=\max (h,-k)$, $k\geq 1$. The chain rule for bounded potentials and $\theta \geq 0$ give

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi (h_ k) =(1-\chi '(h_ k))\theta +\chi '(h_ k)\theta _{h_ k} +\chi ''(h_ k)\, \mathrm{d}h_ k\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}h_ k\geq 0. \]

These bounded potentials decrease to $s$, so $s\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and $-1\leq s\leq 0$. On $\{ h>-k\} $, locality and positivity of mixed products give

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (s) \geq a^ n|h|^{-2n}\omega _ v^ n \geq a^ nc|h|^{-2n}(-\rho )^{2n}f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Letting $k\to \infty $, we obtain this inequality on the set where $h$ is finite; its complement is pluripolar and has zero volume. Since $|h|\leq (-\rho )+aC\leq (1+aC)(-\rho )$, the assertion follows with any common lower bound for $a^ nc(1+aC)^{-2n}$. $\square$

The lemma gives a subsolution for each density in an $L^ p$-bounded family. It does not give a lower bound for the masses of all bounded $\theta $-plurisubharmonic functions.

14.2.1 The exponential equation

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We approximate $\theta $ from above by the Hermitian forms $\theta +\varepsilon \omega $. The bounded subsolution provides a common lower barrier for the corresponding solutions, and exponential comparison shows that they decrease as $\varepsilon $ decreases. Their limit solves the equation in the original background.

Theorem 14.2.2. Let $0\leq f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$, $p>1$, be nonzero, and let $\lambda >0$. There is a unique bounded $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ such that

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (v)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v}f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

For fixed $\lambda $, these solutions are uniformly bounded when $\| f\| _ p$ is bounded above and $\int _ X f^{1/n}\, \mathrm{d}V$ is bounded away from zero.

Proof. For $0<\varepsilon \leq 1$, set $\theta _\varepsilon =\theta +\varepsilon \omega $. By the Hermitian existence theorem theorem 10.1.2, there is a continuous solution $v_\varepsilon $ of

\[ \mathrm{MA}_{\theta _\varepsilon }(v_\varepsilon ) =\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\varepsilon }f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Take $s,b$ from lemma 14.2.1, and put $w=s+\lambda ^{-1}\log b$. Positivity of bounded products gives

\[ \mathrm{MA}_{\theta _\varepsilon }(w) \geq \mathrm{MA}_\theta (s)\geq bf\, \mathrm{d}V \geq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda w}f\, \mathrm{d}V, \]

and bounded exponential comparison (corollary 10.2.4) yields $w\leq v_\varepsilon $. If $0<\varepsilon '<\varepsilon $, then $v_{\varepsilon '}$ is also $\theta _\varepsilon $-plurisubharmonic and

\[ \mathrm{MA}_{\theta _\varepsilon }(v_{\varepsilon '}) \geq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_{\varepsilon '}}f\, \mathrm{d}V, \]

so the same comparison gives $v_{\varepsilon '}\leq v_\varepsilon $. Hence $v_\varepsilon $ decreases to a bounded $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function $v$ with $w\leq v\leq v_1$.

To pass to the limit in the equation, we use the fixed Hermitian background $\Omega =\theta +\omega $ and the expansion

\[ (\theta _\varepsilon +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\varepsilon )^ n =\sum _{k=0}^ n\binom nk (\Omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\varepsilon )^ k \wedge \bigl (-(1-\varepsilon )\omega \bigr )^{n-k}. \]

Continuity of bounded products along monotone sequences applies to every term, and the smooth coefficients converge. Dominated convergence applies to the right-hand sides. This proves the equation. Uniqueness follows from bounded domination in the semipositive big background (theorem 13.2.2, proposition 13.2.1) by the usual exponential comparison argument.

For the uniform assertion, note first that the lower barrier $w$ is uniform by lemma 14.2.1. The upper barrier $v_1$ solves an equation in the fixed Hermitian background $\Omega $. The proof of lemma 10.4.1 bounds its supremum using only an upper bound for the density norm and a positive lower bound for the root integral: these two bounds control, respectively, the Hölder term and the denominator in (10.16), and they survive the smooth approximation used there. Thus $v_1$, and hence $v$, has a uniform upper bound. $\square$

Local continuity of this bounded solution will follow from theorem 14.5.1.

14.2.2 The normalized equation

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

For the normalized equation the approximating potentials need not be monotone. Upper envelopes and lower envelopes of their tails provide monotone sequences on either side of an $L^1$ cluster limit. Comparison identifies the two limits, and hence the equation, while the constant is controlled by the Gauduchon trace and the bounded subsolution.

Theorem 14.2.3. For every nonzero $0\leq f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$, $p>1$, there are a bounded $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and a uniquely determined $c>0$ such that

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (\varphi )=cf\, \mathrm{d}V,\qquad \sup _ X\varphi =0. \]

The construction gives uniform bounds for $\| \varphi \| _\infty $, $c$ and $c^{-1}$ when $\| f\| _ p$ is bounded above and $\int _ Xf^{1/n}\, \mathrm{d}V$ is bounded away from zero.

Only the constant $c$ is asserted to be unique; the normalized potential need not be unique, as theorem 11.6.1 shows already for a Hermitian background.

Proof. Step 1: approximating equations and their constants. For integers $j\geq 1$, put $\omega _ j=\theta +2^{-j}\omega $, and choose continuous normalized solutions

\[ \mathrm{MA}_{\omega _ j}(\varphi _ j)=c_ jf\, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad \sup _ X\varphi _ j=0. \]

We first bound $c_ j$. Fix a Gauduchon metric $\gamma _0$; when $n=1$ any Hermitian form will do, and the calculation below reduces to Stokes' theorem. The smooth approximation in the proof of theorem 10.1.1, together with the Gauduchon trace, gives a common upper bound: the numerator $\int _ X\omega _ j\wedge \gamma _0^{n-1}$ is bounded above by $\int _ X(\theta +\omega )\wedge \gamma _0^{n-1}$, and the root integral of $f$ is positive and fixed. Here the estimate is applied to the smooth approximating equations before passing to their continuous limits.

For a lower bound, take $s,b$ from lemma 14.2.1. If $c_ j<b$, then

\[ \mathrm{MA}_{\omega _ j}(\varphi _ j) \leq (c_ j/b)\mathrm{MA}_{\omega _ j}(s+A) \]

for every constant $A$, and strict domination in the Hermitian background $\omega _ j$ (theorem 10.2.2) would give $\varphi _ j\geq s+A$ for every $A$, which is absurd. Thus $c_ j\geq b$. In fact the constants decrease with $j$: if $k>j$, positivity gives $\mathrm{MA}_{\omega _ j}(\varphi _ k)\geq c_ kf\, \mathrm{d}V$, and the same argument excludes $c_ j<c_ k$. Hence $c_ j\downarrow c>0$.

Step 2: a uniform bound for the potentials. For the Hermitian form $\Omega =\theta +\omega $, all $\varphi _ j$ are normalized $\Omega $-plurisubharmonic functions. Using uniform exponential integrability (lemma 1.5.1) and Hölder's inequality, choose $\gamma >0$ small and $1<q<p$ such that

\[ g_ j=\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma \varphi _ j}f \]

is bounded in $L^ q$. Since $\varphi _ j\leq 0$, we have $g_ j\geq f$. Theorem 14.2.2, applied with integrability exponent $q$ and exponential exponent $\gamma $, therefore gives uniformly bounded $w_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (w_ j)=\mathrm{e}^{\gamma w_ j}g_ j\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Choose $C_0\geq \max \{ 1,\sup _ jc_ j\} $ and set $z_ j=w_ j-\gamma ^{-1}\log C_0$. Then

\[ \mathrm{MA}_{\omega _ j}(z_ j) \geq \mathrm{e}^{\gamma z_ j}c_ jg_ j\, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad \mathrm{MA}_{\omega _ j}(\varphi _ j) =\mathrm{e}^{\gamma \varphi _ j}c_ jg_ j\, \mathrm{d}V, \]

and exponential comparison gives $z_ j\leq \varphi _ j\leq 0$. Thus all $\varphi _ j$ take values in a common bounded interval.

Step 3: the upper envelopes. Extract a subsequence converging in $L^1$ and almost everywhere to the canonical $\theta $-plurisubharmonic representative $\varphi $. We keep the indices $j$; the coefficients of $\omega $ in $\omega _ j$ still decrease to zero. Define

\[ \Phi _ j^+=\left(\sup _{\ell \geq j}\varphi _\ell \right)^*, \qquad h_ j=\inf _{\ell \geq j}\varphi _\ell , \qquad p_ j=P_\theta (h_ j). \]

All these functions take values in a common bounded interval. The upper envelopes are $\omega _ j$-plurisubharmonic and decrease to $\varphi $, since the regularized upper envelope agrees almost everywhere with the $L^1$ limit; this also follows from the local Hartogs lemma applied to the bounded tails.

For $\ell \geq j$, positivity gives $\mathrm{MA}_{\omega _ j}(\varphi _\ell )\geq c_\ell f\, \mathrm{d}V\geq cf\, \mathrm{d}V$. By ordered contact (lemma 1.7.2), finite maxima of these functions satisfy the same lower bound. Continuity of bounded products along increasing sequences, followed by the decreasing limit in $j$ and the expansion in the smooth background, gives

14.1
\begin{equation} \mathrm{MA}_{\omega _ j}(\Phi _ j^+)\geq cf\, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad \mathrm{MA}_\theta (\varphi )\geq cf\, \mathrm{d}V. \end{equation}

Step 4: the lower envelopes. For the reverse inequality we use lower envelopes, which carry a useful exponential weight. For $N\geq j$ put

\[ p_{j,N}=P_\theta \left(\min _{j\leq \ell \leq N}\varphi _\ell \right). \]

The obstacle is continuous and bounded, so the measure of $p_{j,N}$ is carried by its contact set. Partition this set into disjoint Borel pieces according to the first index $\ell $ at which $p_{j,N}=\varphi _\ell $. On such a piece, positivity and ordered contact in the background $\omega _\ell $ give

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (p_{j,N}) \leq \mathrm{MA}_{\omega _\ell }(p_{j,N}) \leq c_\ell f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Moreover $p_{j,N}=\varphi _\ell \geq h_ j$ there, and $c_\ell \leq c_ j$. Summing over the pieces therefore yields the global inequality

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (p_{j,N}) \leq \mathrm{e}^{p_{j,N}-h_ j}c_ jf\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

As $N\to \infty $, the envelopes decrease to $p_ j$. Indeed, their limit lies below each tail obstacle quasi-everywhere and is thus a competitor for $P_\theta (h_ j)$, while the reverse inequality is immediate. Continuity of bounded products along monotone sequences and dominated convergence give

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (p_ j)\leq \mathrm{e}^{p_ j-h_ j}c_ jf\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

The functions $p_ j$ increase. Let $\varphi '=(\lim _ jp_ j)^*$. Since $h_ j\to \varphi $ almost everywhere, we have $\varphi '\leq \varphi $ for the canonical representatives. Passing to the increasing limit and using (14.1), we obtain

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (\varphi ') \leq \mathrm{e}^{\varphi '-\varphi }cf\, \mathrm{d}V \leq \mathrm{e}^{\varphi '-\varphi }\mathrm{MA}_\theta (\varphi ). \]

Bounded exponential comparison forces $\varphi '\geq \varphi $. Thus $\varphi '=\varphi $, and the last inequality, together with (14.1), proves the equation. If necessary, subtracting $\sup _ X\varphi $ gives the required normalization.

Step 5: uniformity and uniqueness of $c$. All bounds used above depend only on the two stated density bounds and the fixed data, which proves the uniform assertion for the constructed solution. Finally, two different positive constants for the same $f$ would contradict strict domination after an arbitrary translation of one of the bounded solutions, exactly as in the lower bound for $c_ j$. This proves the uniqueness of $c$. $\square$

For $\lambda >0$, let $v_\lambda $ be the bounded solution of $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v_\lambda )=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda }f\, \mathrm{d}V$, and let $(\varphi ,c)$ be a normalized solution given by theorem 14.2.3. The two exponential barriers used in proposition 10.6.1 give

\[ \| \lambda v_\lambda -\log c\| _\infty \leq \lambda \operatorname {osc}_ X\varphi \]

also in this semipositive background.

14.3 A quantitative stability estimate

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

If a $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function exceeds a bounded solution only by a small amount in $L^1$, the excess is also uniformly small. To prove this, we restrict the density of the solution to a strict superlevel set of the difference and construct a subsolution for the restricted density. Domination bounds the height of the superlevel set in terms of the norm of the restricted density, and Hölder's inequality converts its volume into a power of the $L^1$ error.

Throughout this section $\theta $ is semipositive and big, and $p>1$ is fixed.

Theorem 14.3.1. Fix $L,C_0>0$. Let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfy

\[ -C_0\leq u\leq 0,\qquad \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=f\, \mathrm{d}V,\qquad 0\leq f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V),\quad \| f\| _ p\leq L. \]

For every

\[ 0<\eta <\frac{p-1}{np+p-1} \]

there is a constant $C$, depending only on the fixed data and $\eta $, such that every $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $v\leq 0$ satisfies

\[ \sup _ X(v-u)_+ \leq C\left(\int _ X(v-u)_+\, \mathrm{d}V\right)^\eta . \]

The comparison potential $v$ need not be bounded.

Proof. Replacing $v$ by $\max (v,u)$ leaves both sides unchanged, so we may assume $-C_0\leq u\leq v\leq 0$. Put $I=\int _ X(v-u)\, \mathrm{d}V$. If $I=0$, the two canonical potentials agree everywhere and there is nothing to prove, so we assume $I>0$.

Choose $q\in (1,p)$, to be specified at the end. For $\delta >0$ put

\[ E_\delta =\{ u<v-\delta \} ,\qquad g_\delta =\mathbf1_{E_\delta }f,\qquad m_\delta =\| g_\delta \| _ q. \]

If $m_\delta >0$, apply lemma 14.2.1 to $g_\delta /m_\delta $. It gives $h\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and $b>0$ such that

\[ -1\leq h\leq 0,\qquad \mathrm{MA}_\theta (h)\geq b\, g_\delta /m_\delta \, \mathrm{d}V. \]

The constant $b$ does not depend on $\delta ,f,u,v$, since the auxiliary density has $L^ q$ norm one.

First suppose $0<m_\delta <b/2$, and take

\[ t=(2m_\delta /b)^{1/n}\in (0,1),\qquad w=(1-t)v+th-\delta -C_0t. \]

Because $h-v\leq C_0$, we have $w\leq v-\delta $, and hence $\{ u<w\} \subset E_\delta $. Positivity of bounded mixed products gives

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (w)\geq t^ n\mathrm{MA}_\theta (h) \geq 2g_\delta \, \mathrm{d}V. \]

On $\{ u<w\} $ the last measure equals $2\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)$. Bounded strict domination in the semipositive big background (theorem 13.2.2) therefore implies $u\geq w$. Consequently

\[ v-u\leq v-w =\delta +C_0t+t(v-h)\leq \delta +(C_0+1)t. \]

If $m_\delta =0$, the measure of $u$ vanishes on $E_\delta $, and the same domination theorem with coefficient zero, applied to $u$ and $v-\delta $, gives $v-u\leq \delta $. If $m_\delta \geq b/2$, the bound $v-u\leq C_0$ gives the following inequality after an increase of its constant. Thus in every case

14.2
\begin{equation} \sup _ X(v-u)\leq \delta +C_1m_\delta ^{1/n}. \end{equation}

Hölder's inequality and Chebyshev's inequality give

\[ m_\delta \leq \| f\| _ p\, \mathrm{d}V(E_\delta )^{1/q-1/p} \leq L(I/\delta )^{(p-q)/(pq)}. \]

Set $a=(p-q)/(npq)>0$. For $0<I\leq 1$, choose $\delta =I^{a/(1+a)}$. Substituting in (14.2) yields

\[ \sup _ X(v-u)\leq C_2 I^{\eta _ q}, \qquad \eta _ q=\frac{p-q}{npq+p-q}. \]

As $q\downarrow 1$, the exponent $\eta _ q$ tends to $(p-1)/(np+p-1)$. For the prescribed $\eta $ we choose $q>1$ so close to $1$ that $\eta _ q>\eta $; then $I^{\eta _ q}\leq I^\eta $ on $(0,1]$. When $I>1$, the bound $v-u\leq C_0$ gives the same conclusion after increasing $C$. This proves the theorem. $\square$

Corollary 14.3.2. Fix $C_0>0$. Let $u_ j,u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfy $-C_0\leq u_ j,u\leq 0$ and

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u_ j)=f_ j\, \mathrm{d}V,\qquad \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=f\, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad \sup _ j\| f_ j\| _ p+\| f\| _ p<+\infty . \]

If $u_ j\to u$ in $L^1$, then $u_ j\to u$ uniformly. More precisely, for every exponent $\eta $ as in the theorem,

\[ \| u_ j-u\| _\infty \leq C\| u_ j-u\| _{L^1(\mathrm{d}V)}^\eta . \]

Proof. Apply the theorem first with solution $u$ and comparison potential $u_ j$, and then with solution $u_ j$ and comparison potential $u$. The constants are uniform because of the common range and the common density bound. The larger of the two suprema is the uniform norm, and each integral of a positive part is at most the full $L^1$ difference. $\square$

The corollary assumes that the limit $u$ already solves an equation with an $L^ p$ density. In an existence proof, this must be established before the corollary can be applied.

14.4 Hölder continuity from bounded stability

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Bounded stability also gives a Hölder modulus. We apply it to Demailly's Chern-connection regularization of a solution: the $L^1$ error of the regularization controls its pointwise error, and a gradient estimate for the smooth averages then gives the modulus. We use the averaging operators $R_ t$ and the transforms $\Phi _{c,\delta }$ of lemma 1.21.1, with the constants $A,K$ given there, together with the estimates of lemma 1.21.2. Interpolating between the smoothing error and the gradient bound yields the exponent below.

Theorem 14.4.1. Let $p>1$ and let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )\cap L^\infty (X)$ solve

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\omega (u)=f\, \mathrm{d}V,\qquad 0\leq f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V). \]

Then $u$ is Hölder continuous with every exponent

\[ 0<\beta <\frac{2(p-1)}{np+3(p-1)}. \]

The Hölder constants are uniform for a fixed bound $-C_0\leq u\leq 0$ and a fixed bound for $\| f\| _ p$. In particular, the continuous solutions constructed in theorem 10.1.1 have this regularity.

Proof. After adding a constant we may assume $-C_0\leq u\leq 0$. Choose

\[ 0<\eta <\frac{p-1}{np+p-1},\qquad \alpha =2\eta <1. \]

For small $\delta >0$ set

\[ c=\delta ^\alpha ,\qquad e=Ac+2K\delta ,\qquad r=-C_0-1,\qquad v_\delta =\frac{\Phi _{c,\delta }+er}{1+e}. \]

The curvature estimate of lemma 1.21.1 gives $v_\delta \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$. Evaluating the infimum defining $\Phi _{c,\delta }$ at $t=\delta $ gives $\Phi _{c,\delta }\leq R_\delta u\leq 0$. On the other hand,

\[ \Phi _{c,\delta }\geq u-K(\delta ^2+\delta ), \]

since $R_ tu+Kt^2\geq u$, $Kt\geq 0$ and $-c\log (t/\delta )\geq 0$ for $0<t\leq \delta $. For small $\delta $ this lower bound is at least $r$, and therefore

\[ v_\delta \leq \Phi _{c,\delta }\leq R_\delta u\leq 0. \]

The Hermitian form $\omega $ is semipositive and big, so theorem 14.3.1 applies in the background $\omega $. Together with the $L^1$ estimate of lemma 1.21.2, it gives

\[ \sup _ X(v_\delta -u)_+ \leq C\left(\int _ X(v_\delta -u)_+\, \mathrm{d}V\right)^\eta \leq C\| R_\delta u-u\| _1^\eta \leq C\delta ^\alpha . \]

Since $e\leq C\delta ^\alpha $ and $u-r\leq C_0+1$, it follows that

14.3
\begin{equation} \Phi _{c,\delta }-u =(1+e)(v_\delta -u)+e(u-r)\leq C\delta ^\alpha . \end{equation}

We now bound the minimizing radius from below. Fix $z$ and a radius $t=t(z)\in (0,\delta ]$ at which the infimum is attained. The lower estimate at this radius and (14.3) give

\[ -c\log (t/\delta ) \leq \Phi _{c,\delta }(z)-u(z)+K(\delta ^2+\delta ) \leq C\delta ^\alpha =Cc. \]

Thus $t(z)\geq a\delta $ with the constant $a=\mathrm{e}^{-C}>0$, which is independent of $z$ and $\delta $. Since $R_ su+Ks^2$ is increasing in $s$,

\[ \begin{split} R_{a\delta }u+K(a\delta )^2 & \leq R_ tu+Kt^2\\ & =\Phi _{c,\delta }+K\delta ^2+K\delta -Kt+c\log (t/\delta )\\ & \leq \Phi _{c,\delta }+K\delta ^2+K\delta . \end{split} \]

Combining this with (14.3) and the lower bound $R_ su-u\geq -Ks^2$, and setting $s=a\delta $, we obtain

\[ \| R_ su-u\| _\infty \leq Cs^\alpha \]

for every sufficiently small $s>0$.

Finally, let $x,y$ be points at a small distance $d$. The gradient estimate $\| \nabla R_ su\| _\infty \leq C/s$ gives

\[ |u(x)-u(y)|\leq 2Cs^\alpha +C d/s. \]

With $s=d^{1/(1+\alpha )}$, both terms are bounded by a constant times $d^{\alpha /(1+\alpha )}$, and larger distances are controlled by the bound on the oscillation. This proves Hölder continuity with exponent $\alpha /(1+\alpha )$. Letting $\eta $ approach its upper endpoint gives every $\beta $ in the theorem; smaller exponents follow on compact $X$ by changing the constant. $\square$

14.4.1 Regularity for a measure with a Hölder reference

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The same smoothing argument applies to a singular solution measure dominated by the product of a Hölder potential. Its square-capacity bound supplies stability, and the weighted Chern estimate supplies the smoothing error.

Theorem 14.4.2. Let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )\cap C^{0,\alpha }(X)$, $0<\alpha \leq 1$, and suppose $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )\cap C(X)$ satisfies

\[ \omega _\varphi ^ n=\mu \leq A\omega _ u^ n, \qquad A>0. \]

Then $\varphi $ is Hölder continuous with exponent

\[ \beta =\frac{\alpha }{(\alpha +2n)(6n+1)+\alpha }>0. \]

Its Hölder constant is uniform for fixed bounds on $A$, $\| u\| _{C^{0,\alpha }}$ and the oscillation of $\varphi $.

Proof. Normalize $\sup _ X\varphi =0$ and write $M=\| \varphi \| _\infty $. By proposition 1.8.7, the measure $\mu $ has a square-capacity bound with a fixed constant. Put

\[ \eta =\frac{2\alpha }{\alpha +2n},\qquad \gamma =\frac1{6n+1},\qquad q=\eta \gamma /2<1. \]

The weighted estimate of lemma 1.21.6 gives $\| R_\delta \varphi -\varphi \| _{L^1(\mu )}\leq C\delta ^\eta $.

Use the constants $A_0,K$ of lemma 1.21.1, taking $A_0\geq 1$, and define the unshifted transform

\[ F=\inf _{0<t\leq \delta } \{ R_ t\varphi +Kt^2+Kt-b\log (t/\delta )\} . \]

It differs from $\Phi _{b,\delta }$ by the constant $K(\delta ^2+\delta )$, so

\[ \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F\geq -(A_0b+2K\delta )\omega , \qquad \varphi \leq F\leq R_\delta \varphi +2K\delta \]

when $\delta \leq 1$. Choose $\varepsilon =\delta ^ q$ and $b=(\varepsilon -2K\delta )/A_0$. For small $\delta $, $\varepsilon \leq 1/2$ and $\varepsilon /(2A_0)\leq b\leq \varepsilon /A_0$.

Set $T=(1-\varepsilon )F$. Then $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}T\geq \varepsilon ^2\omega $. The transform is continuous: its positive logarithmic penalty excludes radii near zero uniformly in the base point for each fixed $\delta ,b$, and the minimum of its continuous defining function on the resulting compact interval depends continuously on the base point. Take $B\geq M+2K+1$ and set $\psi =T-B\varepsilon $. Since $R_\delta \varphi \leq 0$, we have $-M-B\leq \psi \leq 0$. Moreover,

\[ \psi -\varphi \leq (1-\varepsilon )(R_\delta \varphi -\varphi +2K\delta ) +\varepsilon (M-B) \leq (1-\varepsilon )(R_\delta \varphi -\varphi +2K\delta ). \]

The weighted smoothing estimate and the fixed bound for $\mu (X)$ give $\int (\psi -\varphi )_+\, \mathrm d\mu \leq C\delta ^\eta $. Thus proposition 10.3.5, with a fixed competitor bound $H\geq M+B+1$, gives

\[ \sup _ X(T-\varphi )\leq C\delta ^{\eta \gamma }+B\varepsilon \leq C_1\varepsilon . \]

Since $\varphi \leq 0$,

\[ 0\leq F-\varphi =\frac{T-\varphi +\varepsilon \varphi }{1-\varepsilon } \leq 2C_1\varepsilon . \]

At a minimizing radius $t_0\in (0,\delta ]$, monotonicity of $R_ t\varphi +Kt^2$ implies

\[ b\log (\delta /t_0)\leq F-\varphi \leq 2C_1\varepsilon , \qquad t_0\geq \kappa \delta , \quad \kappa =\exp (-4A_0C_1)>0. \]

The constant $\kappa $ is independent of $\delta $ and the point. Applying monotonicity again gives $R_{\kappa \delta }\varphi -\varphi \leq 2C_1\delta ^ q$. With the lower bound $R_ r\varphi -\varphi \geq -Kr^2$ and $r=\kappa \delta $, this proves $\| R_ r\varphi -\varphi \| _\infty \leq Cr^ q$.

Finally lemma 1.21.2 gives $\| \nabla R_ r\varphi \| _\infty \leq C/r$. For points a distance $s$ apart,

\[ |\varphi (x)-\varphi (y)|\leq 2Cr^ q+Cs/r. \]

Take $r=s^{1/(1+q)}$ for small $s$; boundedness handles larger distances. The exponent is $q/(1+q)=\beta $. $\square$

14.5 Local continuity in a semipositive big background

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We return to a semipositive big background $\theta $. Where $\theta $ degenerates, regularization loses some positivity, and this curvature error can be repaired by adding a small multiple of a strict barrier. Bounded stability then shows that a bounded solution is continuous wherever the barrier is locally bounded.

Fix $\rho \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \kappa \omega $ for some $\kappa >0$. For bounded $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ we use the averages $R_ tu$, the transforms $\Phi _{c,\delta }$ and the curvature modulus $\epsilon (\delta )$, with the constants $A,K$, from 1.21.1, lemma 1.21.3.

Theorem 14.5.1. Let $p>1$ and let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\cap L^\infty (X)$ satisfy

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=f\, \mathrm{d}V,\qquad 0\leq f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V). \]

Then $u$ is continuous on every open set $U\subseteq X$ on which $\rho $ is locally bounded.

Proof. After adding constants we may assume $-C_0\leq u\leq 0$ and $\rho \leq -C_0-1$. Choose

\[ 0<\eta <\frac{p-1}{np+p-1},\qquad \alpha =2\eta <1, \]

and set, for small $\delta >0$,

\[ c=\delta ^\alpha +\epsilon (\delta ),\qquad e=Ac+\epsilon (\delta ),\qquad s=e/\kappa ,\qquad v_\delta =\frac{\Phi _{c,\delta }+s\rho }{1+s}. \]

Since $s\kappa =e$, the curvature bounds for $\Phi _{c,\delta }$ and $\rho $ give $v_\delta \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. Moreover

\[ \rho \leq -C_0-1\leq \Phi _{c,\delta } \leq R_\delta u\leq 0 \]

for small $\delta $: the lower bound follows from $\Phi _{c,\delta }\geq u-K(\delta ^2+\delta )$, exactly as in the proof of theorem 14.4.1. Hence $v_\delta \leq \Phi _{c,\delta }\leq R_\delta u\leq 0$.

Theorem 14.3.1 allows an unbounded comparison potential, so it applies to $v_\delta $. Together with the $L^1$ smoothing estimate, it gives

\[ \sup _ X(v_\delta -u)_+ \leq C\left(\int _ X(v_\delta -u)_+\, \mathrm{d}V\right)^\eta \leq C\delta ^\alpha . \]

Fix $M$ and consider the points where $\rho \geq -M$. Since $s\leq Cc$ and $c\to 0$, the identity

\[ \Phi _{c,\delta }-u=(1+s)(v_\delta -u)+s(u-\rho ) \]

gives $\Phi _{c,\delta }-u\leq C_ Mc$ there.

At a radius $t$ where the infimum defining $\Phi _{c,\delta }$ is attained, the same lower estimate yields

\[ -c\log (t/\delta ) \leq \Phi _{c,\delta }-u+K(\delta ^2+\delta ) \leq C_ Mc. \]

Thus $t\geq a_ M\delta $ with $a_ M>0$ independent of the point and of $\delta $. Since $R_ tu+Kt^2$ is increasing in $t$, we obtain

\[ R_{a_ M\delta }u+K(a_ M\delta )^2 \leq R_ tu+Kt^2 \leq \Phi _{c,\delta }+K\delta ^2+K\delta . \]

This upper bound and the lower bound $R_ su\geq u-Ks^2$ imply

\[ \sup _{\{ \rho \geq -M\} } |R_{a_ M\delta }u-u| \leq C_ M\bigl (\delta ^\alpha +\epsilon (\delta )\bigr ) \longrightarrow 0. \]

On every compact subset of $U$, the function $\rho $ is bounded below. The smooth functions $R_{a_ M\delta }u$ therefore converge uniformly to $u$ on each such compact set, which proves that $u$ is continuous on $U$. $\square$

The proof also gives a modulus of continuity on each compact subset of $U$, obtained by combining the uniform smoothing error with the gradient bound $C/t$. A Hölder exponent would require a power bound for the modulus $\epsilon (\delta )$. For potentials that are locally bounded off the poles of a strict analytic barrier, lemma 1.21.7 refines the classical expansion and gives a curvature error linear in $\delta $. Combined with stability above a strict barrier, this yields local Hölder continuity even for solutions with minimal singularities (theorem 14.16.1). Neither argument gives continuity across the pole set of the barrier.

14.6 Homogeneous replacement on a coordinate ball

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

To show that the Monge–Ampère measure of an envelope vanishes off the contact set, we shall replace the envelope on small balls by a solution of the homogeneous equation with the same boundary values. We first recall the smooth Dirichlet problem with a smooth positive density; the replacement is obtained by letting a constant density tend to zero, and continuous boundary values are then handled by uniform approximation.

In this section we work on the ball $B=B_ R=\{ |z|<R\} \subset \mathbb C^ n$, put $\beta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2$, and let $\theta $ be any smooth real $(1,1)$-form defined near $\overline B$. Distances and the Laplacian $\Delta $ are Euclidean.

Lemma 14.6.1. Let $\varphi \in C^\infty (\partial B)$, and let $F$ be a smooth strictly positive function on $\overline B$. There is a unique smooth function $v$ on $\overline B$ such that

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v>0,\qquad (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n=F\beta ^ n, \qquad v|_{\partial B}=\varphi . \]

Proof. Extend $\varphi $ to a smooth function $\widetilde\varphi $ on $\overline B$. For sufficiently large $A$, the function $\underline v=\widetilde\varphi +A(|z|^2-R^2)$ is a smooth strict subsolution with the prescribed boundary values: $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\underline v$ is positive and its determinant is at least $(\max _{\overline B}F)\beta ^ n$. For $n\geq 2$, the classical smooth Dirichlet theorem of Guan–Li therefore applies on the ball, with the arbitrary smooth background $\chi =\theta $ and the smooth positive right-hand side $F$. Their operator is $(\mathrm{i}/2)\partial \bar\partial $, so our potential is $\pi $ times theirs. No closedness condition is needed here; such conditions enter only in their separate theorems on compact manifolds. For $n=1$ the lemma is the Dirichlet problem for the Poisson equation. For uniqueness, integrate the derivative of $\log \det $ along the segment between the two positive matrices: the difference of the logarithms of the two determinants becomes a uniformly elliptic linear operator with smooth coefficients applied to the difference of the solutions, and the maximum principle applies. $\square$

The same argument gives smooth comparison on subdomains $D$ of the ball. Let $a,b$ be smooth functions on $\overline D$ with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a>0$ and $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b>0$. If $(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a)^ n\geq (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n$ in $D$ and $a\leq b$ on $\partial D$, then $a\leq b$ in $D$. Indeed, integrating the derivative of the logarithm of the determinant along the segment between the two positive matrices gives a linear elliptic inequality for $a-b$, and the maximum principle proves the assertion. The boundary of $D$ need not be smooth for this argument.

Theorem 14.6.2. For every $\varphi \in C(\partial B)$ there exists a continuous $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function $H_\varphi $ on $\overline B$ such that

\[ (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_\varphi )^ n=0\quad \hbox{on }B, \qquad H_\varphi |_{\partial B}=\varphi . \]

It dominates every continuous $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function $p$ on $\overline B$ with $p|_{\partial B}\leq \varphi $. For $\varphi ,\psi \in C(\partial B)$, the construction satisfies

\[ \| H_\varphi -H_\psi \| _{C(\overline B)} \leq \| \varphi -\psi \| _{C(\partial B)}. \]

For smooth boundary data the constructed function is Lipschitz on $\overline B$.

Proof. Step 1: boundary barriers. We first take $\varphi $ smooth and $0<\varepsilon \leq 1$, and let $v_\varepsilon $ be the solution of lemma 14.6.1 with the constant density $F\equiv \varepsilon $. The strict subsolution $\underline v$ constructed there for $F\equiv 1$ lies below every $v_\varepsilon $, by smooth comparison. Choose a smooth function $q$ with $q=\varphi $ on the boundary and $\Delta q=-C$, where $C$ is so large that $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\varepsilon \geq 0$ implies $\Delta v_\varepsilon \geq -C$. The maximum principle for the Laplacian gives $v_\varepsilon \leq q$. Both barriers are smooth and equal $\widetilde\varphi $ on the boundary. Consequently

14.7
\begin{equation} |v_\varepsilon (z)-\widetilde\varphi (z)| \leq C_1\operatorname {dist}(z,\partial B), \end{equation}

with $C_1$ independent of $\varepsilon $.

Step 2: a Lipschitz bound independent of $\varepsilon $. Let $h\in \mathbb C^ n$ be small, and set $D_ h=\{ z\in B:z+h\in B\} $. Since $\theta $ is smooth, there is $C_2>0$, independent of the translation $h$, such that

\[ \theta (z)-\theta (z+h)+C_2|h|\beta \geq 0. \]

For a constant $C_3>0$ to be fixed, define on $D_ h$

\[ w(z)=v_\varepsilon (z+h) +C_2|h|(|z|^2-R^2)-C_3|h|. \]

Then $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w\geq \theta (z+h)+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\varepsilon (z+h)>0$, and the determinant of $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w$ is at least $\varepsilon \beta ^ n$, because the Euclidean volume form is translation invariant. On $\partial D_ h$, one of the points $z$ and $z+h$ lies on $\partial B$, and the other has distance at most $|h|$ from $\partial B$. Estimate (14.7) and the Lipschitz bound for $\widetilde\varphi $ therefore give $w\leq v_\varepsilon $ on $\partial D_ h$ if $C_3$ is chosen sufficiently large, and smooth comparison extends this inequality to all of $D_ h$. Together with the same argument for $-h$, this gives

\[ |v_\varepsilon (z+h)-v_\varepsilon (z)|\leq C_4|h|, \]

with constants independent of $\varepsilon $. The same bound for arbitrary pairs of points follows by subdividing the segment joining them inside the convex ball, and it extends to $\overline B$ because each $v_\varepsilon $ is continuous there.

Step 3: the limit $\varepsilon \to 0$. Smooth comparison also shows that $v_\varepsilon $ increases as $\varepsilon $ decreases. By the barriers and the common Lipschitz bound, the limit $H_\varphi $ is Lipschitz and the convergence is uniform on $\overline B$. The inequality $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\varepsilon >0$ of currents passes to the limit, and continuity of local bounded products under uniform convergence gives $(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_\varphi )^ n=0$. For smooth boundary values $\varphi ,\psi $, denote the solutions of lemma 14.6.1 with $F\equiv \varepsilon $ by $v_{\varepsilon ,\varphi }$ and $v_{\varepsilon ,\psi }$. Smooth comparison gives $\| v_{\varepsilon ,\varphi }-v_{\varepsilon ,\psi }\| _\infty \leq \| \varphi -\psi \| _\infty $, and passing to the limit proves the contraction for smooth data.

Step 4: continuous boundary values. For continuous $\varphi $, choose smooth $\varphi _ j\to \varphi $ uniformly on $\partial B$. By the contraction, $H_{\varphi _ j}$ is uniformly Cauchy. Its limit $H_\varphi $ is continuous and $\theta $-plurisubharmonic, has the prescribed boundary values, and satisfies the homogeneous equation, again by continuity of bounded products under uniform convergence. Passing to the limit also proves the contraction for all continuous boundary data.

Step 5: domination. It remains to prove the domination property, which does not follow from a comparison of total masses. First take $\varphi $ smooth and $p$ as in the statement. For $\delta >0$ put $p_\delta =p+\delta (|z|^2-R^2)$, so that $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p_\delta \geq \delta \beta $. By lemma 1.3.9, every $C^2$ function $a$ touching $p_\delta $ from above at an interior point $z_0$ satisfies

\[ \theta (z_0)+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a(z_0)\geq \delta \beta . \]

Suppose that $p_\delta -v_\varepsilon $ had a positive maximum. Since this difference is nonpositive on the boundary, the maximum would be attained at an interior point, where $v_\varepsilon $ plus the maximum touches $p_\delta $ from above. The determinant of $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\varepsilon $ at that point would then be at least $\delta ^ n\beta ^ n$, contradicting the equation for $v_\varepsilon $ when $\varepsilon <\delta ^ n$. Hence $p_\delta \leq v_\varepsilon $ for small $\varepsilon $. Letting first $\varepsilon $ and then $\delta $ decrease to zero, we obtain $p\leq H_\varphi $.

Finally, for continuous $\varphi $, take the smooth approximants $\varphi _ j$ above and set $a_ j=\| \varphi _ j-\varphi \| _\infty $. The boundary values of $p-a_ j$ are at most $\varphi _ j$, so the result for smooth data gives $p-a_ j\leq H_{\varphi _ j}$. Uniform convergence proves $p\leq H_\varphi $. $\square$

By the domination property, $H_\varphi $ is the largest continuous $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function on $\overline B$ whose boundary values are at most $\varphi $. This is the property needed for replacing an envelope locally.

14.7 Contact support in a big background

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Throughout this section, $\theta $ is an arbitrary smooth big background, not necessarily semipositive. For a continuous obstacle, regularization shows that the envelope is continuous off the poles of a strict analytic barrier. On a small ball where the envelope stays below the obstacle, a homogeneous replacement then shows that the envelope already solves the homogeneous equation, so its Monge–Ampère measure vanishes away from the contact set.

By theorem 1.15.3, applied to a strict barrier with a sufficiently small curvature error, we may fix $\rho \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with analytic singularities and a smooth remainder after resolution such that

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \kappa \omega ,\qquad \kappa >0. \]

Its pole set $Z=\{ \rho =-\infty \} $ is an analytic, hence pluripolar, subset of $X$.

Theorem 14.7.1. Let $h\in C(X)$. Then $p=P_\theta (h)$ is a $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function, continuous on $X\setminus Z$, whose Monge–Ampère measure satisfies

\[ \mathbf1_{\{ p<h\} }\mathrm{MA}_\theta (p)=0. \]

Here $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (p)$ is allowed to have infinite total mass.

Proof. Subtract a constant from $\rho $ so that $\rho +2\leq h$. Then $\rho +2$ is a competitor, and since the obstacle is continuous, the envelope lies below it everywhere; thus

\[ \rho +2\leq p\leq h. \]

In particular, $p$ is locally bounded off $Z$. We first prove that $p$ is continuous there; this part does not use the contact assertion.

Apply lemma 1.21.7 to $p-\sup _ Xp$ and add back the constant. The transforms $\Phi _{c,\delta }$ of that lemma are defined on $X$ and, with fixed constants $A,K,C>0$ and $\epsilon (\delta )=C\delta $, satisfy

14.8
\begin{equation} \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Phi _{c,\delta }\geq -(Ac+\epsilon (\delta ))\omega . \end{equation}

Off $Z$ the infimum defining $\Phi _{c,\delta }$ is attained at a positive radius, and

\[ p-K(\delta ^2+\delta )\leq \Phi _{c,\delta } \leq R_\delta p\leq R_\delta h. \]

For small $\delta $ the first inequality gives $\Phi _{c,\delta }\geq \rho +1$, hence $\rho \leq \Phi _{c,\delta }$ on $X$.

Set

\[ m(\delta )=\sup _{0<t\leq \delta }\| R_ th-h\| _\infty , \qquad c=m(\delta )+\epsilon (\delta )+\delta , \qquad s=\frac{Ac+\epsilon (\delta )}{\kappa }. \]

The averaging kernels are positive, have integral one and are supported in balls of radius comparable to $t$, so uniform continuity of $h$ gives $m(\delta )\to 0$. Define

\[ v_\delta =\frac{\Phi _{c,\delta }+s\rho }{1+s}. \]

By the choice of $s$, the strict barrier and (14.8) show that $v_\delta $ is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic. Since $\rho \leq \Phi _{c,\delta }$,

\[ v_\delta \leq \Phi _{c,\delta }\leq R_\delta h\leq h+m(\delta ). \]

Thus $v_\delta -m(\delta )$ is a competitor for the envelope, and $v_\delta \leq p+m(\delta )$.

Let $L\Subset X\setminus Z$ be compact. On $L$ the difference $p-\rho $ is bounded above. Since $s\leq Cc$, the identity

\[ \Phi _{c,\delta }-p=(1+s)(v_\delta -p)+s(p-\rho ) \]

therefore gives $\Phi _{c,\delta }-p\leq C_ Lc$ on $L$. At a radius $t$ where the infimum is attained,

\[ -c\log (t/\delta ) \leq \Phi _{c,\delta }-p+K(\delta ^2+\delta )\leq C_ Lc. \]

Since $c\geq \delta $, this yields $t\geq a_ L\delta $ with a fixed $a_ L>0$. Because $R_ tp+Kt^2$ is increasing in $t$, it follows that

\[ p-K(a_ L\delta )^2\leq R_{a_ L\delta }p \leq \Phi _{c,\delta }+K\delta ^2+K\delta . \]

Consequently $R_{a_ L\delta }p\to p$ uniformly on $L$. These averages are smooth for positive radius, even though $p$ is unbounded on $X$. Applying this to compact neighborhoods of the points of $X\setminus Z$ proves that $p$ is continuous on $X\setminus Z$.

We turn to the contact assertion. By the continuity just proved, the set $\{ p<h\} \setminus Z$ is open. Let $x$ be one of its points, and choose a small coordinate ball $B_ r$ centered at $x$ whose closure lies in this set. Let $H$ be the homogeneous replacement of theorem 14.6.2 with boundary values $p|_{\partial B_ r}$. Its domination property gives $H\geq p$ on the ball.

We claim that $H\leq h$ on $B_ r$ if $r$ is small enough. Indeed, in the chosen Euclidean coordinates $z$, with Laplacian $\Delta $, the inequality $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H\geq 0$ implies a fixed bound $\Delta H\geq -C$. Comparison with the function $\sup _{\partial B_ r}p+C'(r^2-|z-x|^2)$, with $C'$ fixed large enough, gives

\[ H\leq \sup _{\partial B_ r}p+C'r^2. \]

As $r\to 0$, the right-hand side tends to $p(x)$, whereas $\inf _{B_ r}h\to h(x)>p(x)$. This proves the claim for sufficiently small $r$.

Now replace $p$ by $H$ on the ball. The resulting function is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic. To see this, fix $a>0$ and take $\max \{ p,H-a\} $ inside the ball and $p$ outside. Since $H$ and $p$ are continuous with equal boundary values, $H-a<p$ on a collar of $\partial B_ r$, so this is an ordinary local maximum construction. Letting $a\downarrow 0$ gives the function equal to $H$ on $B_ r$ and to $p$ outside, with locally uniform convergence near the boundary of the ball. This function is bounded above by $h$ on all of $X$, so the definition of the envelope forces it to be at most $p$. Thus $p=H$ on $B_ r$, and $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (p)$ vanishes there.

These balls cover $\{ p<h\} \setminus Z$. There the extended measure agrees with the local bounded product, and it does not charge the pluripolar set $Z$. This proves the contact assertion on $X$; no bound on the total mass of $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (p)$ was needed. $\square$

14.7.1 Quasi-continuous obstacles

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now extend the contact property to discontinuous obstacles. Continuous approximations of an obstacle need not preserve every competitor of its envelope pointwise. For lower semicontinuous obstacles, the approximation of lemma 1.19.5 repairs this, and quasi-continuity then allows us to pass to limits of weighted measures. An unbounded obstacle is truncated at finite levels; in an arbitrary smooth background this produces signed products, whose limits are controlled by lemma 1.8.10 before we restrict to the region where the product is positive. The strict analytic barrier $\rho $ and its pole set $Z$ are those fixed above.

Proposition 14.7.2. If $f$ is bounded and lower semicontinuous, then $p=P_\theta (f)$ is locally bounded on $X\setminus Z$, and

\[ \mathbf1_{\{ p<f\} }\mathrm{MA}_\theta (p)=0. \]

Proof. Using the infimum construction in the proof of proposition 13.1.2, take continuous $f_ j\uparrow f$ with common bounds $m\leq f_ j\leq M$, and shift $\rho $ below $m$. The envelopes $p_ j=P_\theta (f_ j)$ increase and satisfy

\[ \rho \leq p_ j\leq M. \]

Their regularized limit $q=(\lim _ jp_ j)^*$ is therefore $\theta $-plurisubharmonic. Outside a pluripolar set it equals the pointwise limit, which is at most $f$; hence $q\leq p=P_\theta (f)$.

We prove the reverse inequality without using a domination theorem. Let $u$ be any competitor for $P_\theta (f)$, and let $u_ t\leq f$ be the potentials given by lemma 1.19.5, which satisfy this inequality everywhere. For fixed $t,\eta >0$, the sets

\[ \{ u_ t<f_ j+\eta \} ,\qquad j\geq 1, \]

are open, increase with $j$, and cover $X$. By compactness, $u_ t\leq f_ j+\eta $ everywhere for all large $j$. Thus $u_ t-\eta $ is a competitor for $p_ j$, and $q\geq u_ t-\eta $. Letting first $\eta \downarrow 0$ and then $t\downarrow 0$, we obtain $q\geq u$ almost everywhere, hence everywhere by lemma 1.3.3. Taking the supremum over all competitors $u$ gives $q\geq p$, so $q=p$.

For fixed $k$, the set $U_ k=\{ p<f_ k\} $ is open. If $j\geq k$, then $p_ j\leq p<f_ k\leq f_ j$ on $U_ k$, so theorem 14.7.1 gives $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (p_ j)=0$ there. On every relatively compact open subset of $X\setminus Z$ the functions $p_ j$ have common bounds. After a common smooth correction and a finite expansion, the local Bedford–Taylor continuity along increasing sequences shows that their measures converge to $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (p)$ there. Since the measure of an open set is at most the lower limit of the measures of the approximating sequence, $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (p)$ vanishes on $U_ k\setminus Z$. The union of these sets is $\{ p<f\} \setminus Z$, and the extended measure does not charge the pluripolar set $Z$. $\square$

Proposition 14.7.3. The conclusion of proposition 14.7.2 holds for every bounded quasi-continuous obstacle $f$.

Proof. By lemma 1.8.8, there are bounded lower semicontinuous $f_ j$ with common bounds and $f_ j\downarrow f$ quasi-everywhere. The envelopes $p_ j=P_\theta (f_ j)$ decrease to $p=P_\theta (f)$. Indeed, the common lower barrier shows that the limit is not identically $-\infty $; the limit lies below $f$ quasi-everywhere and is thus a competitor for $P_\theta (f)$, while monotonicity gives the other inequality. All these functions have common local bounds off $Z$.

For fixed $k$, put $H_ k=\min \{ 1,(f-p_ k)_+\} $ on $X\setminus Z$, a bounded quasi-continuous function there. If $j\geq k$, then on $\{ H_ k>0\} $ we have $p_ j\leq p_ k<f\leq f_ j$ outside a pluripolar set, so proposition 14.7.2 gives $H_ k\mathrm{MA}_\theta (p_ j)=0$ off $Z$.

Let $g$ be continuous with compact support in $X\setminus Z$. The continuity of bounded products against quasi-continuous weights from section 1.6 applies to the fixed quasi-continuous coefficient $gH_ k$. Consequently $H_ k\mathrm{MA}_\theta (p)=0$ off $Z$. As $k\to \infty $, the sets $\{ H_ k>0\} $ exhaust $\{ p<f\} \setminus Z$, and $Z$ is not charged because it is pluripolar. $\square$

For an unbounded obstacle, we truncate and use the convergence of signed products in lemma 1.8.10.

Theorem 14.7.4. Let $\theta $ be smooth and big. Let $f$ be a quasi-continuous obstacle, finite almost everywhere, such that $p=P_\theta (f)\not\equiv -\infty $. Then $p\leq f$ quasi-everywhere and

\[ \mathbf1_{\{ p<f\} }\mathrm{MA}_\theta (p)=0. \]

As before, the measure $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (p)$ may have infinite total mass.

Proof. As in the proof of theorem 13.1.4, the local envelope theorem and a countable Choquet family give $p\leq f$ quasi-everywhere. Set $f_ j=\max (f,-j)$ and $p_ j=P_\theta (f_ j)$. Then $p$ is a competitor for every $f_ j$. Since $f_ j\leq f_1$ and $f_1$ is finite almost everywhere, the finite-sublevel argument in section 1.19 gives a common upper bound for all competitors. Thus the envelopes $p_ j$ are $\theta $-plurisubharmonic and uniformly bounded above. Choose $M$ strictly larger than this bound.

The obstacle $g_ j=\min (f_ j,M)$ is bounded and quasi-continuous. Since $p_ j<M$ and $p_ j\leq f_ j$ quasi-everywhere, $p_ j$ is a competitor for $P_\theta (g_ j)$; the reverse inequality follows from $g_ j\leq f_ j$. Thus $p_ j=P_\theta (g_ j)$, and proposition 14.7.3 gives contact support for $p_ j$ with respect to $g_ j$. Because $p_ j<M$, no contact occurs at the artificial upper cutoff, so

14.9
\begin{equation} \mathbf1_{\{ p_ j<f_ j\} }\mathrm{MA}_\theta (p_ j)=0. \end{equation}

Each $p_ j$ is locally bounded off $Z$, with a lower barrier of the form $\rho -C_ j$. The sequence decreases to $p$: its limit is not identically $-\infty $ because it dominates $p$, and it lies below $f$ quasi-everywhere, so it is itself a competitor for $P_\theta (f)$.

Fix a Hermitian form $\Omega \geq \theta $. For integers $C,k\geq 1$, consider the bounded $\Omega $-plurisubharmonic functions

\[ v_{j,C}=\max (p_ j,-C),\qquad v_ C=\max (p,-C), \]

and set

\[ H_{k,C} =\min \{ 1,(f-p_ k)_+\} \min \{ 1,(p+C)_+\} \quad \hbox{on }X\setminus Z, \]

extended by zero on $Z$. This function is bounded and quasi-continuous. Indeed, off $Z$ the function $p_ k$ is a finite quasi-plurisubharmonic function, truncation by bounded continuous functions preserves quasi-continuity, and the pluripolar set $Z$ can be removed by an open set of arbitrarily small capacity. At a pole of $p$ the second factor vanishes.

For $j\geq k$, on $\{ H_{k,C}>0\} $ we have, outside pluripolar sets,

\[ p_ j\geq p>-C,\qquad p_ j\leq p_ k<f\leq f_ j. \]

By plurifine locality, the signed measure $\nu _{j,C}=(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_{j,C})^ n$ therefore agrees there with $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (p_ j)$, and (14.9) gives

\[ H_{k,C}\nu _{j,C}=0. \]

For fixed $C$, the potentials $v_{j,C}$ have common global bounds and decrease to $v_ C$, so lemma 1.8.10 yields

\[ \int _ X H_{k,C}(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ C)^ n=0. \]

On the set where $H_{k,C}$ is positive we have $p>-C$, so the restricted product in theorem 6.1.2 identifies $H_{k,C}(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ C)^ n$ with the positive measure $H_{k,C}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (p)$; on the complement the coefficient is zero. Hence $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (p)$ vanishes on $\{ H_{k,C}>0\} $.

Finally, if $p(x)$ is finite and $p(x)<f(x)$, then $p_ k(x)<f(x)$ for large $k$ and $p(x)>-C$ for large $C$. The countable union of the sets $\{ H_{k,C}>0\} $ therefore covers $\{ p<f\} $ outside $Z\cup \{ p=-\infty \} $. Both excluded sets are pluripolar, and the theorem follows. $\square$

14.8 Compactness with a common lower barrier

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We return to a semipositive big background $\theta $. Convergence of potentials in $L^1$ does not by itself imply convergence of their Monge–Ampère measures. We give a criterion for convergence in capacity when the potentials stay within a bounded distance of a reference admitting a smaller-background envelope. This common lower barrier ensures that all finite and infinite rooftop envelopes of the sequence are $\theta $-plurisubharmonic. A small error integral of each potential against its own Monge–Ampère measure bounds the corresponding error of the rooftops by a summable tail. Weighted lower semicontinuity passes this bound to the limit, and singular domination identifies the limit of the lower envelopes with the $L^1$ limit. Squeezing the sequence between lower and upper envelopes then gives convergence in capacity, and a domination of the measures also controls their mass on deep sublevels.

Theorem 14.8.1. Let $\theta \geq 0$ be big. Suppose $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfies $P_{\delta \theta }(\varphi )\not\equiv -\infty $ for some $0<\delta <1$. Let $u_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfy, for a fixed $C$,

\[ \varphi -C\leq u_ j\leq \varphi +C,\qquad u_ j\longrightarrow u\text{ in }L^1(X). \]

If

14.10
\begin{equation} I_ j:=\int _ X|\mathrm{e}^{u_ j}-\mathrm{e}^ u|\, \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u_ j)\longrightarrow 0, \end{equation}

then $u_ j\to u$ in capacity.

Proof. The canonical representative of the limit also lies between $\varphi -C$ and $\varphi +C$. It suffices to show that every subsequence has a further subsequence converging in capacity. We may therefore assume that $I_ j\leq 2^{-j}$ and that $u_ j\to u$ almost everywhere. For $N\geq j$ define

\[ p_{j,N}=P_\theta (\min \{ u_ j,\ldots ,u_ N\} ),\qquad p_ j=P_\theta \left(\inf _{\ell \geq j}u_\ell \right). \]

All these envelopes lie between $\varphi -C$ and $\varphi +C$. The contact theorem and ordered contact give an inequality for the finite rooftops. Partition the contact set of $p_{j,N}$ according to the first index $\ell $ for which $p_{j,N}=u_\ell $. On the corresponding piece, the measure of $p_{j,N}$ is bounded by that of $u_\ell $, and $\mathrm{e}^{p_{j,N}}=\mathrm{e}^{u_\ell }$. Therefore

\[ \int _ X|\mathrm{e}^{p_{j,N}}-\mathrm{e}^ u|\, \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (p_{j,N}) \leq \sum _{\ell =j}^ N I_\ell \leq 2^{1-j}. \]

Only positive measures enter this argument, so it applies also when their total masses are infinite.

As $N\to \infty $, $p_{j,N}$ decreases to $p_ j$; the limit is the envelope of the infimum because every competitor lies below every finite rooftop. The sequence $p_ j$ increases, and its canonical limit $p$ is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic. By lemma 1.8.12, both monotone limits hold in capacity. Applying lemma 6.3.1 to the bounded nonnegative weights $|\mathrm{e}^{p_{j,N}}-\mathrm{e}^ u|$, first in $N$ and then in $j$, we obtain

\[ \int _ X|\mathrm{e}^ p-\mathrm{e}^ u|\, \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (p)=0. \]

The inequalities $p_ j\leq u_\ell $ for all $\ell \geq j$ imply $p\leq u$, first almost everywhere and then for the canonical representatives. Also $p\sim u\sim \varphi $. Off the pole sets, $\mathrm{e}^ p<\mathrm{e}^ u$ exactly on $\{ p<u\} $, so $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (p)(\{ p<u\} )=0$. Since $P_{\delta \theta }(\varphi )-C$ is a competitor for $P_{\delta \theta }(p)$, the latter is not identically $-\infty $. Theorem 14.1.2, applied with coefficient zero, gives $p\geq u$, and hence $p=u$.

Finally, by Hartogs' lemma and the almost-everywhere convergence, $U_ j=(\sup _{\ell \geq j}u_\ell )^*$ decreases to $u$. The inequalities $p_ j\leq u_ j\leq U_ j$, together with the convergence in capacity of both monotone sequences, prove the claim. $\square$

Proposition 14.8.2. In theorem 14.8.1, the two bounds on $u_ j$ may be replaced by

\[ \varphi -C\leq u_ j\leq C, \]

provided that the $L^1$ limit satisfies $u\preceq \varphi $. In particular, the approximants may be bounded while $\varphi $ is unbounded.

Proof. The $L^1$ limit satisfies $u\geq \varphi -C$, so the additional hypothesis gives $u\sim \varphi $. We repeat the construction of the finite rooftops in the theorem. Every $p_{j,N}$ is still $\theta $-plurisubharmonic and lies between $\varphi -C$ and $C$, which is enough for contact support and for the boundedness of the exponential weights. The resulting inequality is again

\[ \int _ X|\mathrm{e}^{p_{j,N}}-\mathrm{e}^ u|\, \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (p_{j,N})\leq 2^{1-j}. \]

The limits $p_ j$ exist because of the common lower bound, and $p_ j\leq u$ follows from $p_ j\leq u_\ell $ for every $\ell \geq j$ and almost-everywhere convergence. Thus $\varphi -C\leq p_ j\leq u$, so all $p_ j$ and their increasing limit $p$ have the same ordinary singularity type as $\varphi $. Monotone convergence in capacity and weighted lower semicontinuity give $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (p)(\{ p<u\} )=0$, and singular domination applies to $(p,u)$ exactly as before, so $p=u$. The upper envelopes of the tails decrease to $u$, and the same squeezing argument gives convergence in capacity. Neither the finite rooftops nor the individual approximants needed to have the singularity type of $\varphi $. $\square$

Corollary 14.8.3. In the setting of theorem 14.8.1, with the bounds $\varphi -C\leq u_ j\leq \varphi +C$, suppose either

  1. $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u_ j)\leq \nu $ for a fixed finite non-pluripolar measure $\nu $; or

  2. $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u_ j)\leq A\omega _{w_ j}^ n$, where $A$ is fixed and $w_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ are uniformly bounded and converge in capacity to a bounded $w$.

Then $u_ j\to u$ in capacity, all the measures $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u_ j)$ and $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)$ have finite mass, and

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u_ j) \rightharpoonup \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u). \]

Proof. In the first case, lemma 1.23.1, applied to the exponentials $\mathrm{e}^{u_ j}$, proves (14.10). In the second case we use lemma 1.23.4 instead, since these exponentials are bounded and have a common lower bound for their curvature. In both cases the criterion gives convergence in capacity.

The masses are uniformly bounded by those of the dominating measures. For the deep sublevels we use $u_ j\geq \varphi -C$, which gives $\{ u_ j<-T\} \subset \{ \varphi <C-T\} $. In the first case the masses of these sublevels are at most $\nu (\{ \varphi <C-T\} )$, which tends to zero as $T\to \infty $ because $\nu $ does not charge the pole set of $\varphi $. In the second case lemma 1.8.9 bounds them by $C'\operatorname {Cap}_\omega (\{ \varphi <C-T\} )$, which also tends to zero. The tail criterion theorem 7.4.1, in the general-background form of theorem 7.6.1, now gives finiteness of the limiting measure and weak convergence. $\square$

The reference $\varphi $ in these results need not be an envelope fixed point; the smaller-background condition suffices. Envelope fixed points enter when the two barriers must be derived from an a priori estimate, as in theorem 14.11.3. When no common lower barrier is available, one has to be constructed in a relative envelope class; this problem is treated in section 31.6.

14.9 A bounded subsolution theorem

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The bounded exponential equation remains solvable for right-hand sides that are singular with respect to smooth volume, provided they have a bounded density with respect to the Monge–Ampère measure of a bounded potential. We regularize this potential to obtain equations with bounded densities with respect to smooth volume; their solutions are controlled by a moving dominating measure. When the density is allowed to vanish, the normalizations are bounded by the mixed density inequality of lemma 1.7.6 and the trace estimate for a fixed reference in lemma 1.8.13.

Theorem 14.9.1. Let $\theta $ be semipositive and big. Suppose

\[ \mu =f\, \mathrm{MA}_\theta (\psi ),\qquad \psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\cap L^\infty (X),\qquad 0\leq f\in L^\infty (\mathrm{MA}_\theta (\psi )), \]

and $\mu (X)>0$. For every $\lambda >0$ there is a unique bounded $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfying

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}\mu . \]

Proof. Write $\nu =\mathrm{MA}_\theta (\psi )$, a finite non-pluripolar measure.

Step 1: smooth strictly positive density. First suppose $f$ is smooth and strictly positive. Choose a Hermitian form $\Omega \geq \theta $ and, by theorem 1.11.1, smooth $\Omega $-plurisubharmonic functions $\psi _ j\downarrow \psi $. The envelopes

\[ q_ j=P_\theta (\psi _ j) \]

lie between $\psi $ and $\psi _ j$, so they are uniformly bounded and decrease to $\psi $. Contact support and ordered contact in the background $\Omega $ give

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (q_ j) \leq \mathbf1_{\{ q_ j=\psi _ j\} }\Omega _{\psi _ j}^ n. \]

In particular, each $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (q_ j)$ has a bounded density with respect to smooth volume. Moreover $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (q_ j)(X)>0$ by proposition 13.2.1. Theorem 14.2.2 therefore gives bounded $u_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u_ j)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_ j}f\, \mathrm{MA}_\theta (q_ j). \]

Since $f$ is bounded above and away from zero, we can choose $C$ such that $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda (q_ j-C)}f\leq 1\leq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda (q_ j+C)}f$ for all $j$. Then $q_ j-C$ and $q_ j+C$ are a subsolution and a supersolution, and bounded exponential comparison gives $q_ j-C\leq u_ j\leq q_ j+C$.

Extract an $L^1$ limit $u$, which is again bounded. The moving domination

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u_ j)\leq C'\Omega _{\psi _ j}^ n \]

and corollary 14.8.3, applied with the reference zero, give convergence in capacity. Continuity of bounded products under convergence in capacity, with the quasi-continuous weights $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_ j}f$, then gives

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}f\nu . \]

Step 2: bounded measurable density. Choose smooth positive functions $f_ j$ with a common upper bound $L>0$ such that $f_ j\to f$ in $L^1(\nu )$. Such an approximation exists: by regularity of the finite measure $\nu $, $f$ can be approximated by continuous functions on large compact sets, and continuous functions by smooth ones uniformly; truncating to a fixed bounded interval and adding a positive constant tending to zero preserves the bounds and the convergence. Step 1 gives bounded $u_ j$ with

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u_ j)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_ j}f_ j\nu . \]

For a fixed $C$ we have $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda (\psi -C)}f_ j\leq 1$ for all $j$, so $\psi -C$ is a subsolution and $u_ j\geq \psi -C$. The upper bound requires a separate argument, because $\inf _ Xf_ j$ may tend to zero.

The construction in Step 1 also gives the mixed inequality

14.11
\begin{equation} \theta _{u_ j}\wedge \theta _\psi ^{n-1} \geq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_ j/n}f_ j^{1/n}\nu . \end{equation}

To prove it without a mixed inequality for arbitrary singular measures, we use the approximants $u_{j,k}$ and $q_ k$ of Step 1, for which

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u_{j,k}) =\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_{j,k}}f_ j\mathrm{MA}_\theta (q_ k), \]

and both Monge–Ampère measures have densities with respect to smooth volume. Lemma 1.7.6 applies to the pair $(u_{j,k},q_ k)$. For fixed $j$, both sequences are uniformly bounded and converge in capacity to $(u_ j,\psi )$, so continuity of bounded products against quasi-continuous weights passes the mixed inequality to the limit and yields (14.11).

Put $M_ j=\sup _ Xu_ j$, $v_ j=u_ j-M_ j$ and $c_ j=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda M_ j}$. Suppose the $M_ j$ were unbounded above. After extraction, $M_ j\to +\infty $ and $v_ j\to v$ in $L^1(X)$, where $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ is normalized. By lemma 1.23.1, $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_ j/n}\to \mathrm{e}^{\lambda v/n}$ in $L^1(\nu )$. Together with the inequalities

\[ |f_ j^{1/n}-f^{1/n}|^ n\leq |f_ j-f|, \qquad 0\leq f_ j\leq L, \]

this gives

\[ \int _ X \mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_ j/n}f_ j^{1/n}\, \mathrm{d}\nu \longrightarrow \int _ X\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v/n}f^{1/n}\, \mathrm{d}\nu >0; \]

the last integral is positive because $\mu (X)>0$ and $\nu $ does not charge the pole set of $v$. Integrating (14.11) and applying lemma 1.8.13, we find some $a>0$ such that, along the extraction,

\[ a c_ j^{1/n}\leq \int _ X\theta _{v_ j}\wedge \theta _\psi ^{n-1}\leq C_\psi , \]

contradicting $M_ j\to +\infty $. Thus the $u_ j$ are uniformly bounded above as well as below.

Extract an $L^1$ limit $u$. Now $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u_ j)\leq C''\nu $, so corollary 14.8.3 gives convergence in capacity and weak convergence of the Monge–Ampère measures. The right-hand sides converge in total variation, by the convergence of exponentials against the fixed measure $\nu $ and the estimate

\[ \int _ X|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_ j}f_ j-\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}f|\, \mathrm{d}\nu \leq C\| f_ j-f\| _{L^1(\nu )} +\| f\| _\infty \int _ X|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_ j}-\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}|\, \mathrm{d}\nu . \]

Consequently $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}\mu $. Uniqueness follows from bounded exponential comparison. $\square$

14.10 Adding weighted Monge–Ampère measures

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Let $u$ and $v$ be potentials of the same ordinary singularity type. We show that the sum of their weighted measures $\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda u}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)+\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda v}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v)$ is again of the form $\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda a}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (a)$ for a potential $a$ of that type. Truncating $u$ and $v$ gives bounded equations, solved by the bounded subsolution theorem, whose solutions have a common lower barrier. Their exponential error tends to zero even though the approximating masses may grow, so the lower-barrier criterion identifies the limit. This addition step will remove a deficit of mass in the existence proof of section 14.11.

Theorem 14.10.1. Let $\theta \geq 0$ be big, and suppose $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfies $P_{\delta \theta }(\varphi )\not\equiv -\infty $ for some $0<\delta <1$. Let $u,v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfy $u,v\sim \varphi $, and let $\lambda >0$. If

\[ \mu =\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda u}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u) +\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda v}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (v) \]

is a finite measure, then there exists $a\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, with $a\sim \varphi $, such that

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (a)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda a}\mu . \]

Here the weights are evaluated off the pole sets. The solution $a$ is unique in the ordinary singularity type of $\varphi $.

Proof. Step 1: truncated equations and barriers. Choose $S\geq \max (\sup _ Xu,\sup _ Xv)$. A common translation reduces the theorem to the case $u,v\leq 0$: translating $u$ and $v$ by $-S$ multiplies $\mu $ by $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda S}$, and the resulting solution is then translated back by $S$.

For integers $j\geq 1$, set $u_ j=\max (u,-j)$, $v_ j=\max (v,-j)$ and

\[ \mu _ j=\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda u_ j}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u_ j) +\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda v_ j}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v_ j). \]

For $s_ j=(u_ j+v_ j)/2$, positivity of mixed products gives

\[ \mu _ j\leq 2^ n \mathrm{e}^{\lambda j}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (s_ j). \]

Thus $\mu _ j$ has a bounded density with respect to the Monge–Ampère measure of the bounded potential $s_ j$, and $\mu _ j\neq 0$ by proposition 13.2.1.
