The bounded subsolution theorem theorem 14.9.1 therefore gives bounded $a_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (a_ j)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda a_ j}\mu _ j$, and bounded exponential comparison yields

\[ a_ j\leq \min (u_ j,v_ j)\leq 0. \]

Choose $C$ with $|u-v|\leq C$ off the common pole set; then $|u_ j-v_ j|\leq C$. Put

\[ D=C/2+n\lambda ^{-1}\log 2,\qquad b_ j=s_ j-D. \]

Because $|u_ j-v_ j|\leq C$, positivity of mixed products gives

\[ \mathrm{e}^{-\lambda b_ j}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (b_ j) \geq 2^{-n}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda D-\lambda s_ j} \bigl (\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u_ j)+\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v_ j)\bigr ) \geq \mu _ j. \]

Hence $a_ j\geq b_ j\geq w$, where

\[ w=(u+v)/2-D\sim \varphi \]

is a fixed $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function. Extract a subsequence with $a_ j\to a$ in $L^1(X)$. The upper barriers decrease to $\min (u,v)$, so $w\leq a\leq \min (u,v)$ and $a\sim \varphi $.

Step 2: convergence in capacity. Write $E_ j=\{ \min (u,v)>-j\} $. By locality, $\mu _ j=\mu $ on $E_ j$; since $a_ j\leq 0$, it follows that

\[ \int _{E_ j}|\mathrm{e}^{a_ j}-\mathrm{e}^ a|\, \mathrm{MA}_\theta (a_ j) \leq \int _ X|\mathrm{e}^{a_ j}-\mathrm{e}^ a|\, \mathrm{d}\mu \longrightarrow 0 \]

by lemma 1.23.1. On $X\setminus E_ j$ we have $a_ j\leq -j$ and $a\leq -j$, whence $|\mathrm{e}^{a_ j}-\mathrm{e}^ a|\leq \mathrm{e}^{-j}$. Moreover

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (a_ j) =\mathrm{e}^{\lambda (a_ j-u_ j)}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u_ j) +\mathrm{e}^{\lambda (a_ j-v_ j)}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v_ j) \leq \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u_ j)+\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v_ j). \]

Choose a Hermitian form $\Omega \geq \theta $. Since $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u_ j)\leq \mathrm{MA}_\Omega (u_ j)$ and $-j\leq u_ j\leq 0$, and similarly for $v_ j$, the range estimate of proposition 7.1.1, with $r_ n=\max (n-2,0)$, gives

\[ \int _{X\setminus E_ j}|\mathrm{e}^{a_ j}-\mathrm{e}^ a|\, \mathrm{MA}_\theta (a_ j) \leq C'\mathrm{e}^{-j}(1+j)^{r_ n}\longrightarrow 0. \]

The lower-barrier criterion proposition 14.8.2 now gives $a_ j\to a$ in capacity. Note that only a polynomial bound for the masses of the approximating measures was needed.

Step 3: the limiting equation. We identify the equation on sets where $w$ is bounded below, without first proving weak convergence of the measures $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (a_ j)$. For $T>0$, let $g_ T$ be the continuous cutoff from the proof of lemma 6.3.1, and put $\zeta _ T=g_ T(w)$. Where $\zeta _ T>0$, we have $w>-2T$, hence every $a_ j$ and $a$ is above $-2T$. If $j>2T$ and $x\notin E_ j$, the inequalities $w(x)\leq a_ j(x)\leq -j$ force $\zeta _ T(x)=0$. Consequently, for all $j>2T$,

\[ \zeta _ T\mathrm{MA}_\theta (a_ j) =\zeta _ T \mathrm{e}^{\lambda a_ j}\mu . \]

We test this identity against a continuous function. On the left we may replace $a_ j$ by $\max (a_ j,-2T)$ and use continuity of bounded products under convergence in capacity, with the fixed bounded quasi-continuous weight $\zeta _ T$. On the right, the exponentials converge against the fixed measure $\mu $, which gives convergence in total variation. Therefore

\[ \zeta _ T\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (a) =\zeta _ T\mathrm{e}^{\lambda a}\mu . \]

As $T\to \infty $, the cutoffs increase to one off the pole set of $w$, which no measure involved charges. This proves the equation and, in particular, shows that $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (a)$ has finite mass. Uniqueness follows from corollary 14.1.3. $\square$

14.11 Equations in a prescribed ordinary type

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We return to a semipositive big background $\theta $ and now require the solution to have the ordinary singularity type of a fixed reference $\varphi $. Throughout this section, $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ is an envelope fixed point admitting a smaller-background envelope; that is, (13.26) holds:

\[ P_\theta [\varphi ]=\varphi ,\qquad P_{\varepsilon \theta }(\varphi )\not\equiv -\infty \quad \text{for some }0<\varepsilon <1. \]

We fix such an $\varepsilon $. Note that $\varphi \leq 0$, since every competitor for $P_\theta [\varphi ]$ is nonpositive. For a normalized solution $u$, the fixed-point property gives the upper bound $u\leq \varphi $, while the smaller-background envelope $P_{\varepsilon \theta }(\varphi )$ yields a uniform lower bound $u\geq \varphi -C$. A supersolution and the addition theorem then prove existence in this type.

Theorem 14.11.1. Let $\theta $ and $\varphi $ be as above, and let $\mu =f\, \mathrm{d}V$, where $0\leq f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$, $p>1$, and $\mu (X)>0$. Then:

  1. For every $\lambda >0$ there is a unique $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $u\sim \varphi $ such that

    \[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}\mu . \]

  2. There are $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $u\sim \varphi $ and a uniquely determined constant $c>0$ such that

    \[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=c\mu . \]

The potential in part (2) need not be unique up to an additive constant, even for a Hermitian background and the reference $\varphi =0$; see the counterexample in theorem 11.6.1.

The proof uses two estimates in the prescribed type. The first is a lower bound for the norm of a Monge–Ampère density.

Lemma 14.11.2. Fix $q>1$. There is $b_ q>0$ such that every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $u\sim \varphi $ and

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)=F\, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad 0\leq F\in L^ q(\mathrm{d}V), \]

satisfies $\| F\| _ q\geq b_ q$. For fixed $\theta $ and $\varepsilon $, the constant $b_ q$ is independent of $\varphi $.

Proof. Since $u\sim \varphi $, the smaller-background envelope $P_{\varepsilon \theta }(u)$ is not identically $-\infty $. If $F=0$, then theorem 14.1.2, applied to $u$ and $u+1$ with coefficient zero, would give $u\geq u+1$; hence $N=\| F\| _ q>0$.

Apply lemma 14.2.1 in the semipositive big background $(1-\varepsilon )\theta $ to $F/N$, whose $L^ q$ norm is one. It gives a bounded $s$ with $-1\leq s\leq 0$ and a constant $b_ q>0$, depending only on this fixed background and on $q$, such that

\[ \mathrm{MA}_{(1-\varepsilon )\theta }(s)\geq (b_ q/N)F\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Put $h=P_{\varepsilon \theta }(\varphi )+s$. This is a $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function with $h\preceq u$. Since $\theta _ h$ is the sum of the positive currents $\varepsilon \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}P_{\varepsilon \theta }(\varphi )$ and $(1-\varepsilon )\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}s$, the same density bound holds for $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (h)$. To justify this, we truncate $P_{\varepsilon \theta }(\varphi )$ at finite levels, use positivity of bounded mixed products, and exhaust the set where $P_{\varepsilon \theta }(\varphi )$ is finite; its pole set has zero volume.

If $N<b_ q$, we would have

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u) \leq (N/b_ q)\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (h+t) \quad \text{for every }t\in \mathbb R. \]

Since $N/b_ q<1$, theorem 14.1.2 would then imply $u\geq h+t$ for every $t$, which is impossible at a point where both potentials are finite. Thus $N\geq b_ q$. The construction of $s$ used no property of $\varphi $, which proves the last assertion. $\square$

The lemma bounds the $L^ q$ norm of the density from below; it gives no positive lower bound for the total mass $\int _ XF\, \mathrm{d}V$.

The second estimate bounds a normalized solution from below by $\varphi $ minus a uniform constant. It is what keeps limits of solutions in the ordinary singularity type of $\varphi $.

Theorem 14.11.3. Let $\theta $ and $\varphi $ be as above. Suppose $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, $u\sim \varphi $, $\sup _ Xu=0$, and

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)\leq f\, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad 0\leq f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V),\quad p>1. \]

There is a constant $C$, depending only on the fixed geometry, $\varphi $, $p$, and an upper bound for $\| f\| _{L^ p}$, such that

\[ \varphi -C\leq u\leq \varphi . \]

Proof. The upper bound follows directly from the fixed-point property: since $u\leq 0$ and $u\preceq \varphi $, the function $u$ is a competitor for $P_\theta [\varphi ]=\varphi $.

For the lower bound we construct a subsolution $\psi $ of the type of $\varphi $ whose measure dominates a multiple of $\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma u}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)$, and then apply singular domination. Put $\varphi _\varepsilon =P_{\varepsilon \theta }(\varphi )$, so that $\varphi _\varepsilon \leq \varphi \leq 0$. Uniform exponential integrability (lemma 1.5.1) for the normalized potentials $u$ and exponential integrability for the fixed function $\varphi _\varepsilon $ allow us to choose $\gamma >0$ and $1<q<p$ such that

\[ g=\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma (\varphi _\varepsilon +u)}f \]

has a uniform $L^ q$ bound. Explicitly, we first choose $q<p$ and apply Hölder's inequality with exponents $p/q$ and $p/(p-q)$; then we choose $\gamma $ so small that the exponential factor is controlled by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and the two exponential integrability estimates.

Theorem 14.2.3, applied in the semipositive big background $(1-\varepsilon )\theta $ to the density $1+g$, gives a bounded $v$ with $\sup _ Xv=0$ and a constant $c>0$ such that

\[ ((1-\varepsilon )\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n=c(1+g)\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Both $\| v\| _\infty $ and $c^{-1}$ are uniformly bounded. Adding the constant one to $g$ makes the root integral of the density at least one, so no lower bound for the integral of $f$ is needed.

Put $h=\varphi _\varepsilon +v\leq \varphi $, which is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic, and define

\[ \chi (t)=\frac1{1-t}\quad (t\leq 0),\qquad \psi =\varphi +\chi (h-\varphi ), \]

where the formula for $\psi $ is evaluated off the pole sets. Then $0\leq \chi \leq 1$, $0<\chi '\leq 1$, and $\chi ''>0$. For bounded potentials with $h\leq \varphi $, lemma 1.7.9, applied with $G(x,y)=x+\chi (y-x)$, gives

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}G(\varphi ,h) \geq (1-\chi '(h-\varphi ))\theta _\varphi +\chi '(h-\varphi )\theta _ h\geq 0. \]

For unbounded potentials we apply this bounded assertion to $\varphi _ k=\max (\varphi ,-k)$ and $h_ k=\max (h,-k)\leq \varphi _ k$. Since $G$ is nondecreasing in both variables, $G(\varphi _ k,h_ k)$ decreases as $k$ increases. Its limit is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic and lies between $\varphi $ and $\varphi +1$; hence it is not identically $-\infty $, and it equals $\psi $. Locality on common finite levels and positivity of bounded products give

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (\psi ) \geq (\varphi -h+1)^{-2n}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (h) \geq c(\varphi -h+1)^{-2n}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma \varphi _\varepsilon } \mathrm{e}^{-\gamma u}f\, \mathrm{d}V, \]

where the second inequality uses $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (h)\geq ((1-\varepsilon )\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\geq cg\, \mathrm{d}V$. These inequalities are first obtained off the pole sets; since pole sets have zero volume, they extend by exhaustion to all Borel sets.

Write $T=-\varphi _\varepsilon \geq 0$. If $C_1\geq 1+\| v\| _\infty $, then $\varphi -h+1\leq T+C_1$. Since

\[ A:=\inf _{T\geq 0}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\gamma T}}{(T+C_1)^{2n}}>0, \]

we obtain, with a uniform $d>0$,

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (\psi ) \geq d \mathrm{e}^{-\gamma u}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u). \]

Choose $K=2+\gamma ^{-1}|\log d|$. On $\{ u<\psi -K\} $ we have $u<1-K$, because $\psi \leq \varphi +1\leq 1$; hence

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u) \leq d^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{\gamma (1-K)} \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (\psi -K) \leq \mathrm{e}^{-\gamma }\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (\psi -K). \]

The potentials $u$ and $\psi -K$ have the same ordinary singularity type. Moreover, $u\geq \varphi -C_ u$ for some constant $C_ u$, so $P_{\varepsilon \theta }(u)\geq \varphi _\varepsilon -C_ u$ is not identically $-\infty $. Theorem 14.1.2 therefore yields $u\geq \psi -K\geq \varphi -K$. All constants used to choose $K$ are uniform over the family in the theorem; the possibly nonuniform constant $C_ u$ serves only to show that the smaller-background envelope of $u$ is not identically $-\infty $, and it enters no estimate. $\square$

The existence proof starts from a supersolution in the prescribed type. It is obtained from a bounded solution by a rooftop envelope with $\varphi $.

Lemma 14.11.4. For nonzero $0\leq f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$, $p>1$, and $\lambda >0$, there exists $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $w\sim \varphi $ such that

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (w)\leq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda w}f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Proof. Let $v$ be the bounded solution of $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v}f\, \mathrm{d}V$ given by theorem 14.2.2, and write $M=\sup _ Xv$ and $a=v-M\leq 0$. The rooftop envelope $p=P_\theta (a,\varphi )$ satisfies $\varphi -\| a\| _\infty \leq p\leq \varphi $, so $p\sim \varphi $. By the rooftop inequality of proposition 13.1.5,

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (p) \leq \mathbf1_{\{ p=a\leq \varphi \} }\mathrm{MA}_\theta (a) +\mathbf1_{\{ p=\varphi <a\} }\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (\varphi ). \]

The second term vanishes: by proposition 13.1.6, the measure of $\varphi $ is carried by $\{ \varphi =0\} $, whereas $\varphi <a\leq 0$ on the set in question. On the first contact set, $a=p$ and $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (a)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda (a+M)}f\, \mathrm{d}V$. Thus $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (p)\leq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda (p+M)}f\, \mathrm{d}V$, and $w=p+M$ has the required properties. $\square$

14.11.1 Existence for a positive exponent

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We prove part (1) of theorem 14.11.1 by maximizing a weighted mass. Among the potentials $z\sim \varphi $ whose weighted measure $\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda z}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (z)$ lies below $\mu =f\, \mathrm{d}V$, we find one of maximal weighted mass; theorem 14.11.3 provides the compactness needed for this. If its weighted measure were strictly smaller than $\mu $, the addition theorem would produce a potential of larger weighted mass.

of theorem 14.11.1, part (1). Step 1: a maximizing sequence. Fix $\lambda >0$. By lemma 14.11.4, there is $w\sim \varphi $ such that

\[ \nu _0:=\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda w}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (w) \leq \mu =f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Consider all potentials $z\sim \varphi $ for which

\[ \nu _0\leq \nu _ z:=\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda z} \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (z)\leq \mu , \]

and let $B$ be the supremum of their weighted masses $\nu _ z(X)$. This family is nonempty, since it contains $w$, and $B\leq \mu (X)<+\infty $. Choose $u_ j$ in the family with $\nu _{u_ j}(X)\to B$. Since $\nu _0\leq \nu _{u_ j}$, corollary 14.1.3 gives $u_ j\leq w$.

Step 2: uniform bounds. We need a lower bound for the suprema of the $u_ j$. Put $\varphi _\varepsilon =P_{\varepsilon \theta }(\varphi )\leq 0$, and let $\psi $ be the bounded solution of

\[ ((1-\varepsilon )\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )^ n=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda \psi }f\, \mathrm{d}V \]

given by theorem 14.2.2. For $h=\psi +\varphi _\varepsilon $, positivity of the splitting $\theta _ h=(\varepsilon \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _\varepsilon )+((1-\varepsilon )\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )$ gives

\[ \mathrm{e}^{-\lambda h}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (h) \geq \mathrm{e}^{-\lambda \varphi _\varepsilon }f\, \mathrm{d}V \geq \mu \geq \nu _{u_ j}. \]

Here $h\preceq u_ j$, because $h\preceq \varphi $ and $u_ j\sim \varphi $, and each $u_ j$ admits a smaller-background envelope because $u_ j\sim \varphi $. Corollary 14.1.3 therefore gives $u_ j\geq h$. Consequently

\[ \sup _ Xh\leq M_ j:=\sup _ Xu_ j\leq \sup _ Xw, \]

and the lower bound is finite.

We now apply theorem 14.11.3 to $v_ j=u_ j-M_ j$. Their measures satisfy

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (v_ j) =\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u_ j) \leq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda \sup _ Xw}f\, \mathrm{d}V, \]

so $\varphi -C\leq v_ j\leq \varphi $. Together with the bounds for $M_ j$, this shows that all $u_ j$ lie within a fixed bounded distance of $\varphi $.

Step 3: the limit. Extract an $L^1$ limit $u$. By corollary 14.8.3, applied with the dominating measure $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda \sup _ Xw}\mu $, the convergence holds in capacity, $u\sim \varphi $, and

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u_ j) \rightharpoonup \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u). \]

After a further extraction we may also assume $\nu _{u_ j}\rightharpoonup \nu $. Since $\nu _0\leq \nu _{u_ j}\leq \mu $, the limit satisfies the same inequalities. We claim that

\[ \mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_ j}\nu _{u_ j}\rightharpoonup \mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}\nu . \]

Indeed, $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_ j}\to \mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}$ in $L^1(\mu )$ by lemma 1.23.1, and all the measures $\nu _{u_ j}$ are at most $\mu $; this controls the error caused by changing the weight. The fixed bounded weight $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}$ can be approximated in $L^1(\mu )$ by continuous functions, and since both $\nu _{u_ j}$ and $\nu $ are dominated by $\mu $, this approximation is uniform in the integrals. The weak convergence $\nu _{u_ j}\rightharpoonup \nu $ then gives the claim, and the same argument applies after multiplication by any continuous test function.

Since $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_ j}\nu _{u_ j}=\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u_ j)$, it follows that

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}\nu , \qquad \mathrm{e}^{-\lambda u}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)=\nu , \qquad \nu (X)=B. \]

The second identity holds off the pole set of $u$, which has zero $\mu $-measure. Hence $u$ belongs to the family and attains the supremum $B$. Write $\nu =g\, \mathrm{d}V$, with $0\leq g\leq f$.

Step 4: no deficit. Suppose that $g\neq f$ in $L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$. Applying lemma 14.11.4 to $f-g$, we find $v\sim \varphi $ with

\[ \nu _ v:=\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda v}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (v) \leq (f-g)\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

This measure is nonzero. In fact, no potential $v$ admitting a smaller-background envelope has zero Monge–Ampère measure, since theorem 14.1.2, applied with coefficient zero to $v$ and $v+1$, would give $v\geq v+1$. Thus $\nu _ v(X)>0$.

The sum $\nu +\nu _ v$ is finite, so theorem 14.10.1 gives $a\sim \varphi $ with

\[ \mathrm{e}^{-\lambda a}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (a)=\nu +\nu _ v. \]

This measure still lies between $\nu _0$ and $\mu $, but its mass is $B+\nu _ v(X)>B$, a contradiction. Therefore $g=f$, and $u$ solves the required equation. Uniqueness in the prescribed ordinary type follows from corollary 14.1.3. $\square$

14.11.2 The normalized equation

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

For the normalized equation we let the positive exponent tend to zero. Bounded comparison controls the normalizing constants from above, while the lower density estimate of lemma 14.11.2 keeps them away from zero. The common barriers then give convergence of the Monge–Ampère measures and keep the limit in the prescribed type.

of theorem 14.11.1, part (2). For each $j\geq 1$, part (1) gives $u_ j\sim \varphi $ with

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u_ j)=\mathrm{e}^{u_ j/j}f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Write $M_ j=\sup _ Xu_ j$, $v_ j=u_ j-M_ j$ and $c_ j=\mathrm{e}^{M_ j/j}$. Let $h$ be a bounded normalized solution of $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (h)=\alpha f\, \mathrm{d}V$, with $\alpha >0$, given by theorem 14.2.3.

For $H_ j=h+\| h\| _\infty +j\log \alpha $ we have

\[ \mathrm{e}^{-H_ j/j}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (H_ j) =\mathrm{e}^{-(h+\| h\| _\infty )/j}f\, \mathrm{d}V \leq f\, \mathrm{d}V =\mathrm{e}^{-u_ j/j}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u_ j). \]

The bounded function $H_ j$ admits a smaller-background envelope, and $u_ j\preceq H_ j$, so corollary 14.1.3 gives $u_ j\leq H_ j$. In particular,

\[ 0<c_ j\leq \alpha \exp (2\| h\| _\infty /j). \]

The lower density bound of lemma 14.11.2, applied to $v_ j$ with $q=p$, gives

\[ b_ p\leq \| c_ j\mathrm{e}^{v_ j/j}f\| _ p\leq c_ j\| f\| _ p, \]

so the $c_ j$ are also bounded away from zero.

Since $\sup _ Xv_ j=0$ and $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (v_ j)=c_ j\mathrm{e}^{v_ j/j}f\, \mathrm{d}V\leq C f\, \mathrm{d}V$, theorem 14.11.3 gives $\varphi -C'\leq v_ j\leq \varphi $. Extract a subsequence with $v_ j\to v$ in $L^1(X)$ and $c_ j\to c>0$. By corollary 14.8.3, the convergence holds in capacity, $v\sim \varphi $, and the Monge–Ampère measures converge weakly. Meanwhile, lemma 1.23.3 gives

\[ \int _ X|\mathrm{e}^{v_ j/j}-1|f\, \mathrm{d}V\longrightarrow 0. \]

Thus the right-hand sides converge in total variation to $cf\, \mathrm{d}V$, and $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (v)=cf\, \mathrm{d}V$.

For the uniqueness of the constant, suppose that $a,b\sim \varphi $ solve the normalized equation with constants $c_ a<c_ b$. For every real $t$,

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (a) =\frac{c_ a}{c_ b}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (b+t), \]

so theorem 14.1.2 gives $a\geq b+t$. This is impossible as $t\to +\infty $, at any point where both potentials are finite. Interchanging the roles of the two constants proves that $c$ is unique. $\square$

14.12 Comparison above a strict singular barrier

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

From now on, $\theta $ is an arbitrary smooth big real $(1,1)$-form. Then $V_\theta $ may be unbounded, and the smaller-background construction used above is no longer available. Its role is taken by a strict analytic barrier. When two potentials bounded below by the barrier are compared, adding a small multiple of the barrier forces a negative infimum of their difference to be attained away from its pole set, and there a local comparison principle rules it out.

Since $\theta $ is big, theorem 1.15.3 provides $\rho \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with analytic singularities and a smooth remainder after resolution such that $\theta _\rho \geq \kappa \omega $ for some $\kappa >0$. Replacing $\omega $ by a small positive multiple and subtracting a constant from $\rho $, we fix such a $\rho $ with $\rho \leq 0$ and $\theta _\rho \geq \omega $. Put $Z=\{ \rho =-\infty \} $ and $\Omega =X\setminus Z$. From now on until the end of section 14.17, these data remain fixed, and $\Omega $ always denotes this open set rather than an auxiliary Hermitian form. A potential bounded below by $\rho -C$ is locally bounded on $\Omega $, and there its extended Monge–Ampère measure agrees with the local bounded product.

Lemma 14.12.1. Let $B\Subset \Omega $ be a coordinate ball, and let $u,v$ be bounded $\theta $-plurisubharmonic functions on $B$. Assume

\[ \liminf _{x\to \partial B}(u-v)(x)\geq 0, \qquad \mathbf1_{\{ u<v\} }\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)\leq \mathbf1_{\{ u<v\} }\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v). \]

Then $u\geq v$ on $B$.

Proof. Fix $\varepsilon >0$ and set $v_\varepsilon =\max (u,v-\varepsilon )$, which equals $u$ near the boundary. We claim that

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)\leq \mathrm{MA}_\theta (v_\varepsilon ) \]

throughout $B$. On $\{ u>v-\varepsilon \} $ this follows from locality, since $v_\varepsilon =u$ there. On $\{ u\leq v-\varepsilon \} $ it follows from ordered contact and from the assumed inequality, which applies because this set is contained in $\{ u<v\} $.

Let $q$ be the plurisubharmonic envelope on $B$ of the obstacle $u-v_\varepsilon $, in the sense of (1.9). This obstacle is bounded, quasi-continuous and zero near the boundary. Hence it lies between $Ah$ and zero for a sufficiently large $A$, where $h<0$ is a smooth strictly plurisubharmonic defining function for the ball. Thus $q$ tends to zero at the boundary, and (1.9) shows that $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q)^ n$ is carried by $D=\{ q=u-v_\varepsilon \} $.

Since $q\leq u-v_\varepsilon $ quasi-everywhere, the canonical representatives satisfy $q+v_\varepsilon \leq u$. Ordered contact, followed by the measure inequality above, yields

\[ \mathbf1_ D\mathrm{MA}_\theta (q+v_\varepsilon ) \leq \mathbf1_ D\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u) \leq \mathbf1_ D\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v_\varepsilon ). \]

The positive expansion of $(\theta _{v_\varepsilon }+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q)^ n$ contains both $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v_\varepsilon )$ and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q)^ n$. Thus $\mathbf1_ D(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q)^ n=0$, and since $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q)^ n$ is carried by $D$, we get $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q)^ n=0$. The classical Bedford–Taylor comparison principle for bounded plurisubharmonic functions with zero boundary values implies $q=0$. Consequently $u\geq v_\varepsilon $, first quasi-everywhere and then everywhere. Finally let $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$. $\square$

Theorem 14.12.2. Suppose $u,v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfy, for constants $C_0,C_1\geq 0$,

\[ \min (u,v)\geq \rho -C_0,\qquad v\leq u+C_1. \]

If, for some $0\leq c<1$,

\[ \mathbf1_{\{ u<v\} }\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)\leq c\, \mathbf1_{\{ u<v\} }\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v), \]

then $u\geq v$.

The measures in the theorem need not have finite total mass.

Proof. We first modify the barrier. Let $r=a\rho -A$, where $a>1$ is sufficiently close to one and $A$ is sufficiently large. Since

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(a\rho )=a\theta _\rho +(1-a)\theta , \]

we can arrange $\theta _ r\geq \omega /2$ and $r-v\leq -1$, and moreover $u-r\to +\infty $ at $Z$, because $u-r\geq (a-1)(-\rho )+A-C_0$. When $Z$ is empty, the last property is not needed.

Choose $0<\delta <1$ with $(1-\delta )^ n>c$, and put $v_\delta =(1-\delta )v+\delta r$. Then

\[ u-v_\delta \geq \delta (u-r)-(1-\delta )C_1. \]

Suppose that the infimum $m$ of $u-v_\delta $ on $\Omega $ were negative. Since $u-v_\delta \to +\infty $ at $Z$, a sequence $x_ j$ along which $u-v_\delta $ tends to $m$ has a subsequence converging to a point $x_0\in \Omega $. Choose a coordinate ball $B\Subset \Omega $ centered at $x_0$, identify it with $\{ |z|<1\} $, and take

\[ \eta (z)=b(1-|z|^2),\qquad 0<b<1, \qquad \omega /2+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\eta \geq 0. \]

The function $w=v_\delta +\delta \eta +m$ is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic on $B$, and $\liminf _{\partial B}(u-w)\geq 0$ because $\eta $ vanishes on $\partial B$. Also

\[ w-v=\delta (r-v+\eta )+m<0. \]

Thus $\{ u<w\} \subset \{ u<v\} $, and positivity of mixed products gives on this set

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)\leq c\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v)\leq (1-\delta )^ n\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v)\leq \mathrm{MA}_\theta (w). \]

By lemma 14.12.1, $u\geq w$ on $B$. Evaluating at $x_ j$ gives $u(x_ j)-v_\delta (x_ j)\geq m+\delta \eta (x_ j)$, which contradicts $\eta (x_0)>0$ in the limit. Hence $u\geq v_\delta $ on $\Omega $. Letting $\delta \downarrow 0$ along values with $(1-\delta )^ n>c$, we obtain $u\geq v$ on $\Omega $, and lemma 1.3.3 extends this inequality to $X$. $\square$

Corollary 14.12.3. Let $u,v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfy $u,v\geq \rho -C$ for some $C\geq 0$ and $u\sim v$.

  1. If $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)\leq c\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v)$ for some $c\geq 0$, then $c\geq 1$.

  2. If $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)\leq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda (u-v)}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v)$ for some $\lambda >0$, then $u\geq v$.

In particular, let $\mu $ be a positive measure. Among potentials bounded below by $\rho $ minus a constant, each ordinary singularity type contains at most one solution of $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}\mu $, and the constant $c>0$ in $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=c\mu $ is uniquely determined within each type.

Proof. If $c<1$, apply the theorem to $u$ and $v+A$ for every constant $A>0$; the constants in its hypotheses may depend on $A$. The resulting inequality $u\geq v+A$ for all $A$ is impossible. For the second assertion, fix $a>0$ and restrict the measure inequality to $\{ u<v-a\} $. There the factor is at most $\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda a}<1$, so the theorem gives $u\geq v-a$; now let $a\downarrow 0$. For two solutions of the exponential equation, apply the second assertion in both orders; for two solutions of the normalized equation, apply the first. $\square$

14.13 Subsolutions and the Gauduchon trace

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The strict barrier also yields subsolutions within a bounded distance of any reference between $\rho $ and zero. The curvature estimate comes from composing a convex function with the difference between the reference and a barrier-based subsolution, which produces a convex combination of positive currents. Pairing the equation with a Gauduchon metric then bounds the normalizing constants through a linear trace.

Lemma 14.13.1. Fix $p>1$ and $L>0$. There is $m>0$ such that, whenever

\[ \rho \leq \eta \leq 0,\quad \eta \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ), \qquad g\geq 0,\quad \| g\| _{L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)}\leq L, \]

there exists $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with

\[ \eta \leq v\leq \eta +1,\qquad \mathrm{MA}_\theta (v)\geq m g\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

The constant $m$ depends only on the fixed geometry and on $\rho ,p,L$.

Proof. Choose $1<q<p$. By Hölder's inequality and the finiteness of all polynomial moments of the fixed quasi-plurisubharmonic function $\rho $, the functions $h=g(2-\rho )^{2n}$ have uniformly bounded $L^ q$ norms. Lemma 14.2.1, applied in the Hermitian background $\omega $, gives $s\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ with $-1\leq s\leq 0$ and

\[ \omega _ s^ n\geq m h\, \mathrm{d}V \]

for a fixed $m>0$. Put $u_1=\rho +s$. Then $\theta _{u_1}\geq \omega _ s$ and $u_1\leq \rho \leq \eta $. On $\Omega $ define

\[ t=1+\eta -u_1,\qquad v=\eta +t^{-1}, \]

so that $1\leq t\leq 2-\rho $. Lemma 1.7.9, applied on relatively compact subsets of $\Omega $ with $a=\eta $, $h=u_1$ and $\chi (x)=(1-x)^{-1}$, gives

\[ \theta _ v=(1-t^{-2})\theta _\eta +t^{-2}\theta _{u_1} +2t^{-3}\, \mathrm{d}(\eta -u_1)\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}(\eta -u_1) \geq t^{-2}\theta _{u_1}. \]

Since $\eta \leq v\leq \eta +1$, lemma 1.3.7 extends $v$ across $Z$, and the bounds extend by lemma 1.3.3. On $\Omega $, positivity of the local products gives

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (v)\geq t^{-2n}\theta _{u_1}^ n \geq (2-\rho )^{-2n}\omega _ s^ n \geq m g\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Neither side charges $Z$, so the inequality holds on $X$. $\square$

Proposition 14.13.2. Let $\gamma $ be a Gauduchon metric on $X$, write $\gamma ^ n=G\, \mathrm{d}V$, and put

\[ T_\gamma =\int _ X\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}. \]

Then $T_\gamma >0$. If $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfies $u\geq \rho -C$ and $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)\geq F\, \mathrm{d}V$ with $F\geq 0$, then

14.12
\begin{equation} \int _ X F^{1/n}G^{1-1/n}\, \mathrm{d}V\leq T_\gamma . \end{equation}

Consequently, let $0\leq f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$, $p>1$, be nonzero, and let $\lambda >0$. Every solution of $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}f\, \mathrm{d}V$ that is bounded below by $\rho $ minus a constant satisfies $\sup _ Xu\leq C'$, where $C'$ depends only on $\theta $, $\gamma $, $f$ and $\lambda $. Moreover, if $v_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, $j\geq 1$, and constants $C_ j\geq 0$ and $c_ j>0$ satisfy

\[ \sup _ Xv_ j=0,\qquad \mathrm{MA}_\theta (v_ j)=c_ j \mathrm{e}^{v_ j/j}f\, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad v_ j\geq \rho -C_ j, \]

then the constants $c_ j$ are uniformly bounded above.

Proof. The current $\theta _ u\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}$ is linear in $\theta _ u$, so it is a globally defined positive current, even at $Z$. Since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\gamma ^{n-1}=0$, Stokes' theorem in the sense of currents gives

\[ \int _ X\theta _ u\wedge \gamma ^{n-1} =\int _ X\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}. \]

Applied to $\rho $, this identity proves $T_\gamma \geq \int _ X\omega \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}>0$.

On $\Omega $, lemma 1.7.6, applied to $\theta _ u$ and $\gamma $, gives

\[ \theta _ u\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}\geq F^{1/n}G^{1-1/n}\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Exhausting $\Omega $ and using positivity of the global trace measure, we obtain (14.12), since the complement $Z$ has zero volume.

For the exponential equation, write $a=\sup _ Xu$ and $w=u-a$. The estimate becomes

\[ \mathrm{e}^{\lambda a/n}\int _ X\mathrm{e}^{\lambda w/n}f^{1/n}G^{1-1/n}\, \mathrm{d}V \leq T_\gamma . \]

The integral has a positive lower bound over all normalized $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. Otherwise, normalized compactness would give a sequence of such $w$ with integrals tending to zero and converging almost everywhere to a $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function; since $w\leq 0$ and $f^{1/n}G^{1-1/n}$ is integrable, dominated convergence would give a strictly positive limit integral, a contradiction. This bounds $a$ from above.

For the last assertion, the same estimate contains the factors $c_ j^{1/n}$ and $\mathrm{e}^{v_ j/(jn)}$. If the integrals $\int _ X\mathrm{e}^{v_ j/(jn)}f^{1/n}G^{1-1/n}\, \mathrm{d}V$ tended to zero along a subsequence with $j\to \infty $, normalized compactness and almost-everywhere convergence would give $v_ j/j\to 0$ almost everywhere along a further subsequence. Dominated convergence would then give the positive limit $\int _ Xf^{1/n}G^{1-1/n}\, \mathrm{d}V$. This contradiction bounds $c_ j$ for all large $j$, and each of the finitely many remaining constants is finite. Neither argument uses the total Monge–Ampère mass. $\square$

14.14 Stability above a strict barrier

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The subsolutions of lemma 14.13.1 stay within a bounded distance of a given reference. With them, the proof of theorem 14.3.1 extends to unbounded potentials lying above $\rho $, and the resulting estimate is uniform over all references between $\rho $ and zero. For a fixed reference, it upgrades $L^1$ compactness of solutions to uniform convergence of their differences on $\Omega $.

Theorem 14.14.1. Fix $C_0,L>0$, $p>1$, and

\[ 0<\gamma <\frac{p-1}{np+p-1}. \]

Let $\eta \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfy $\rho \leq \eta \leq 0$, and suppose that $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfies

\[ \eta -C_0\leq u\leq \eta ,\qquad \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=f\, \mathrm{d}V,\qquad 0\leq f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V), \quad \| f\| _ p\leq L. \]

There is a constant $C$, depending only on the fixed geometry and on $\rho ,C_0,L,p,\gamma $, such that every $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $v\leq \eta $ satisfies

14.13
\begin{equation} \sup _\Omega (v-u)_+ \leq C\left(\int _\Omega (v-u)_+\, \mathrm{d}V\right)^\gamma . \end{equation}

In particular, the constant does not depend on the reference $\eta $, and the comparison potential $v$ need not have the ordinary singularity type of $\eta $. The difference $v-u$ is evaluated on $\Omega $, where $u$ and $\eta $ are locally bounded; since $Z$ has zero volume, the integral is the same as over $X$. Since $Z$ is pluripolar, the resulting inequality $v\leq u+C\bigl (\int _\Omega (v-u)_+\, \mathrm{d}V\bigr )^\gamma $ on $\Omega $ extends to the canonical representatives on all of $X$.

Proof. Replacing $v$ by $\max (v,u)$, we may assume $\eta -C_0\leq u\leq v\leq \eta $, so that $0\leq v-u\leq C_0$ on $\Omega $. Fix $q\in (1,p)$ and $\delta >0$, and put

\[ E_\delta =\{ u<v-\delta \} \cap \Omega ,\qquad g_\delta =\mathbf1_{E_\delta }f,\qquad m_\delta =\| g_\delta \| _ q. \]

When $m_\delta >0$, lemma 14.13.1, followed by subtracting one from its potential, gives $h\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and a fixed $b>0$ such that

\[ \eta -1\leq h\leq \eta ,\qquad \mathrm{MA}_\theta (h)\geq b\, g_\delta /m_\delta \, \mathrm{d}V. \]

The constant $b$ is uniform in $\eta $, because the auxiliary density has $L^ q$ norm one and the strict barrier is fixed.

If $0<m_\delta <b/2$, set

\[ t=(2m_\delta /b)^{1/n},\qquad w=(1-t)v+th-\delta -C_0t. \]

Since $h-v\leq C_0$, we have $w\leq v-\delta $, so the set $\{ u<w\} \cap \Omega $ is contained in $E_\delta $. On this set the local expansion into positive mixed products gives $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (w)\geq t^ n\mathrm{MA}_\theta (h)\geq 2\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)$. Both potentials are bounded below by $\rho $ minus a constant, and $w\leq u+C_0$, so theorem 14.12.2 applies and gives $u\geq w$. It follows that

\[ v-u\leq \delta +C_0t+t(v-h)\leq \delta +(C_0+1)t. \]

If $m_\delta =0$, we apply the same domination theorem with coefficient zero to $u$ and $v-\delta $. If $m_\delta \geq b/2$, we use $v-u\leq C_0$. In all cases,

\[ \sup _\Omega (v-u)\leq \delta +C_1m_\delta ^{1/n}. \]

Write $I=\int _\Omega (v-u)\, \mathrm{d}V$. If $I=0$, the canonical potentials agree. Otherwise Hölder's and Chebyshev's inequalities give

\[ m_\delta \leq L(I/\delta )^{(p-q)/(pq)}. \]

For $0<I\leq 1$, the choice $\delta =I^{(p-q)/(npq+p-q)}$ yields the exponent $\gamma _ q=(p-q)/(npq+p-q)$. We choose $q>1$ so close to one that $\gamma _ q>\gamma $. The case $I>1$ follows from the bound $v-u\leq C_0$. This proves (14.13). $\square$

Corollary 14.14.2. Fix $\eta \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $\rho \leq \eta \leq 0$, constants $C_0,L>0$, and $p>1$. Consider the family of potentials $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfying

\[ \eta -C_0\leq u\leq \eta ,\qquad \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=f_ u\, \mathrm{d}V,\qquad 0\leq f_ u\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V), \quad \| f_ u\| _ p\leq L. \]

This family is compact for uniform convergence of differences on $\Omega $. If $u_ j$ belong to the family and $u_ j\to u$ in $L^1$, then $u$ belongs to the family and

\[ \sup _\Omega |u_ j-u|\leq C\| u_ j-u\| _{L^1(\mathrm{d}V)}^\gamma \longrightarrow 0 \]

for every exponent $\gamma $ as in theorem 14.14.1. Moreover, $f_{u_ j}$ converges weakly to $f_ u$ in $L^ p$.

Proof. Applying the theorem in both orders to $u_ j$ and $u_ k$, with the common reference $\eta $, we see that an $L^1$-Cauchy sequence in the family is uniformly Cauchy in differences on $\Omega $. Its uniform limit on $\Omega $ is locally $\theta $-plurisubharmonic and agrees almost everywhere, hence everywhere for the canonical representatives, with the $L^1$ limit $u$. The bounds $\eta -C_0\leq u\leq \eta $ hold on all of $X$. Letting $k\to \infty $ in the pairwise estimate proves the quantitative bound, before any equation for $u$ is used.

The densities form a bounded set in the reflexive space $L^ p$. Let $f$ be the weak limit of a subsequence $f_{u_{j_ k}}$. Since the potentials are locally bounded and converge locally uniformly on $\Omega $, Bedford–Taylor continuity, which holds also for the smooth nonclosed background, gives $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=f\, \mathrm{d}V$ on $\Omega $. Neither measure charges the analytic set $Z$, so this equality holds on $X$. Positivity and weak lower semicontinuity of the norm give $f\geq 0$ and $\| f\| _ p\leq L$. Thus every weak cluster point equals the density $f_ u$ of $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)$, which proves weak convergence of the whole sequence. Finally, the common bounds by $\eta $ and $\eta -C_0$ give $L^1$ compactness of the family, by the compactness of quasi-plurisubharmonic functions, and the preceding argument upgrades each convergent subsequence to uniform convergence of differences on $\Omega $. $\square$

For solutions with minimal singularities, normalized by $\sup _ Xu=0$, we take $\eta =V_\theta $. Then $\rho \leq V_\theta \leq 0$, and every normalized comparison potential $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfies $v\leq V_\theta $, as the theorem requires.

14.15 Minimal singularities in an arbitrary big background

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now solve the equations in the ordinary singularity type of $V_\theta $. Demailly's regularization approximates $V_\theta $ by strict potentials with analytic singularities. On a resolution of these singularities, the corresponding equations become equations in a semipositive big background, which we have already solved. The solutions satisfy bounds relative to the approximating singularities that are uniform in the approximation, and upper and lower envelopes then produce a solution with minimal singularities. For the normalized equation, the normalized exponential solutions lie in a common interval below $V_\theta $, so the compactness theorem of the preceding section applies directly.

Since $\rho \leq 0$ is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic, it is a competitor for $V_\theta $, and hence $\rho \leq V_\theta \leq 0$. We use the analytic regularization of theorem 1.15.3 in its resolved form.

Lemma 14.15.1. Let $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfy $\theta _\psi \geq a\omega $ for some $a>0$, and suppose that $\psi $ has a smooth remainder after resolution, as in theorem 1.15.3. For every nonzero $0\leq f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$, $p>1$, and every $\lambda >0$, there exists $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $u\sim \psi $ such that

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Proof. Let $\pi :Y\to X$ be a modification resolving the singularities of $\psi $. The pulled-back current has the form

\[ \pi ^*\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\pi ^*\psi =\beta +[D], \]

where $D$ is an effective real divisor, $[D]$ is its current of integration, and $\beta $ is a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $Y$. Off $D$ we have $\beta \geq a\pi ^*\omega $, and by continuity this inequality holds everywhere. In particular, $\beta $ is semipositive.

We check that $\beta $ is big. For a blowup with smooth center and exceptional divisor $E$, choose a metric on $\mathcal O(-E)$ whose curvature is positive in the directions tangent to the projective fibers. Along $E$, the pullback of a Hermitian metric is positive in the complementary directions. Consequently, its sum with a sufficiently small positive multiple of this curvature form is a Hermitian metric: positivity holds first along $E$, then on a neighborhood of $E$, and finally on the compact complement, where the pullback is already positive. Iterating over the blowups, we obtain an effective real exceptional divisor $F$, with logarithmic potential $\zeta $, and a smooth Hermitian form $\kappa $ such that

\[ \pi ^*\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\zeta =\kappa +[F]. \]

At each step of the iteration, we pull back the smooth positive form obtained so far, apply the construction for one blowup, and add the pullback of the previous divisor to the new positive multiple of $E$. Hence $\beta +a\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\zeta \geq a\kappa $, so $\beta $ is big. This argument only involves Hermitian metrics and never requires a closed positive form on $Y$.

Fix a smooth positive volume form $\mathrm{d}V_ Y$ on $Y$, and write $\pi ^*\mathrm{d}V=J\, \mathrm{d}V_ Y$. The function $J$ is smooth, nonnegative and bounded, and it is positive off the exceptional locus. Define

\[ H=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda \pi ^*\psi }(f\circ \pi )J. \]

This function is nonzero and belongs to $L^ p(\mathrm{d}V_ Y)$. Indeed, $\psi $ is bounded above, and the change of variables formula gives

\[ \int _ Y|(f\circ \pi )J|^ p\, \mathrm{d}V_ Y \leq \| J\| _\infty ^{p-1}\int _ X|f|^ p\, \mathrm{d}V; \]

the analytic exceptional sets do not affect these integrals. Theorem 14.2.2, applied on $Y$ in the semipositive big background $\beta $, gives a bounded $s\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\beta )$ with

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\beta (s)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda s}H\, \mathrm{d}V_ Y. \]

The function $\pi ^*\psi +s$ is $\pi ^*\theta $-plurisubharmonic, because the corresponding current is $\beta _ s+[D]\geq 0$, where $\beta _ s=\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}s$. By theorem A.2.1 it descends to some $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, and $u\sim \psi $ because $s$ is bounded. Off the exceptional set and the pole set of $\psi $, the change of variables formula gives the required equation. Neither the extended measure on the left nor the measure $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}f\, \mathrm{d}V$ on the right charges these analytic sets, so the equation holds on all of $X$. $\square$

Theorem 14.15.2. Let $\theta $ be any smooth big real $(1,1)$-form and let $0\leq f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$, $p>1$, be nonzero. For every $\lambda >0$ there is a unique $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ such that

\[ u\sim V_\theta ,\qquad \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Proof. Step 1: approximating singularity types. Apply theorem 1.15.3 to $V_\theta $. Set $\delta _ j=2^{-j}$ and pass to a subsequence whose curvature errors are at most $\delta _ j$. Denote its potentials by $\widetilde\psi _ j$; thus $\widetilde\psi _ j\downarrow V_\theta $ and $\theta _{\widetilde\psi _ j}\geq -\delta _ j\omega $. Set

\[ a_ j=(1-\delta _ j)\sup _ X\widetilde\psi _ j\geq 0, \qquad \psi _ j=(1-\delta _ j)\widetilde\psi _ j+\delta _ j\rho -a_ j. \]

By Hartogs' lemma, $\sup _ X\widetilde\psi _ j\to \sup _ XV_\theta =0$, so $a_ j\to 0$. A common resolution of the singularities of $\widetilde\psi _ j$ and $\rho $ shows that $\psi _ j$ has a smooth remainder after resolution. Moreover,

14.14
\begin{equation} \theta _{\psi _ j}\geq \delta _ j^2\omega , \qquad (1-\delta _ j)V_\theta +\delta _ j\rho -a_ j \leq \psi _ j\leq V_\theta . \end{equation}

The upper inequality holds because $\psi _ j$ is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic and nonpositive. Subtracting $a_ j$ explicitly keeps the normalization separate from the fixed barrier $\rho $.

Step 2: uniform bounds. By lemma 14.15.1 there are $u_ j\sim \psi _ j$ satisfying

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u_ j)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_ j}f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Each $u_ j$ is bounded below by $\rho $ minus a constant depending on $j$, so proposition 14.13.2 gives a uniform upper bound for $\sup _ Xu_ j$.

There is also a uniform lower bound. We apply lemma 14.13.1 with the barrier $\rho $ replaced by $\rho -1-\sup _ ja_ j$ and with the reference $\psi _ j-1$, which lies between this barrier and zero. The lemma gives $v_ j$ with $\psi _ j-1\leq v_ j\leq \psi _ j\leq 0$ and $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v_ j)\geq mf\, \mathrm{d}V$, where $m>0$ is independent of $j$. Put $w_ j=v_ j+\lambda ^{-1}\log m$. Then

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (w_ j)\geq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda w_ j}f\, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad u_ j\sim w_ j\sim \psi _ j, \]

and the exponential order in corollary 14.12.3 gives $u_ j\geq w_ j$. Thus, for a uniform constant $C$,

14.15
\begin{equation} \psi _ j-C\leq u_ j\leq V_\theta +C. \end{equation}

After extraction, $u_ j$ converges in $L^1$ and almost everywhere to a $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function $u$, and the preceding bounds give $V_\theta -C\leq u\leq V_\theta +C$.

Step 3: the equation for the limit. We identify the equation using envelopes. Put $A_ j=\sup _{k\geq j}a_ k$ and, for $N\geq j$, define

\[ p_{j,N}=P_\theta (\min \{ u_ j,\ldots ,u_ N\} ),\qquad t_{j,N}=\max _{j\leq k\leq N}u_ k. \]

Both functions lie above the $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function

\[ b_ j=(1-\delta _ j)V_\theta +\delta _ j\rho -C-A_ j, \]

which is a competitor for every $p_{j,N}$; in particular, no envelope is identically $-\infty $. All these functions are locally bounded on $\Omega $, uniformly in $j$ and $N$. Contact support (theorem 14.7.4), together with a partition of the contact set according to the first index of contact, gives

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (p_{j,N})\leq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda p_{j,N}}f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

No factor $N$ appears here: on each piece the envelope equals a single $u_ k$, and ordered contact bounds its measure by the measure of that $u_ k$. Similarly, locality and ordered contact for a finite maximum give

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (t_{j,N})\geq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda t_{j,N}}f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Now let $N\to \infty $, and set $p_ j=\lim _ Np_{j,N}$ and $t_ j=(\lim _ Nt_{j,N})^*$. Continuity of local bounded products along monotone sequences passes both inequalities to these limits. Next, $p_ j$ increases to a regularized limit $p$, while $t_ j$ decreases to $u$ by Hartogs' lemma. The bound by $b_ j$ gives $p\sim V_\theta $, and $p\leq u$. A second application of the monotone continuity gives

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (p)\leq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda p}f\, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)\geq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

These inequalities hold on $\Omega $, and they extend across $Z$ because no measure involved charges $Z$. Thus $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (p)\leq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda (p-u)}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)$, and the exponential order in corollary 14.12.3 gives $p\geq u$. Hence $p=u$, and the equation follows. Applying the same order in both directions proves uniqueness. Note that the Monge–Ampère operator was only ever applied along monotone sequences that are locally bounded on $\Omega $. $\square$

Lemma 14.15.3. Under the hypotheses of theorem 14.15.2, let $u_\lambda $ be the solution given there, and put

\[ v_\lambda =u_\lambda -\sup _ Xu_\lambda , \qquad c_\lambda =\mathrm{e}^{\lambda \sup _ Xu_\lambda }. \]

There are constants $B,C,m>0$, independent of $0<\lambda \leq 1$, such that

14.16
\begin{equation} V_\theta -B\leq v_\lambda \leq V_\theta , \qquad m\leq c_\lambda \leq C. \end{equation}

Proof. The normalized potential satisfies

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (v_\lambda )=c_\lambda \mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda } f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Fix a Gauduchon metric $\gamma $ and write $\gamma ^ n=G\, \mathrm{d}V$. Since $v_\lambda \leq 0$ and $0<\lambda \leq 1$, (14.12) gives

\[ c_\lambda ^{1/n} \int _ X\mathrm{e}^{v_\lambda /n}f^{1/n}G^{1-1/n}\, \mathrm{d}V \leq \int _ X\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}. \]

The integrals on the left have a positive lower bound independent of $\lambda $. Otherwise, the compactness of normalized quasi-plurisubharmonic functions would give an almost everywhere convergent sequence of the normalized $v_\lambda $ whose integrals tend to zero, with a $\theta $-plurisubharmonic limit; since $f$ is nonzero, dominated convergence would give a positive limit integral. Thus $c_\lambda \leq C$.

To control the singularity type, we use uniform exponential integrability (lemma 1.5.1) and Hölder's inequality to choose $\varepsilon >0$ and $1<q<p$ such that $\| \mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon v_\lambda }f\| _ q$ is uniformly bounded. Lemma 14.13.1, applied with the reference $V_\theta -1$ and the barrier $\rho -1$, gives $\eta _\lambda \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with

\[ V_\theta -1\leq \eta _\lambda \leq V_\theta , \qquad \mathrm{MA}_\theta (\eta _\lambda )\geq m\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon v_\lambda }f\, \mathrm{d}V \geq mf\, \mathrm{d}V \]

for a fixed $m>0$. The scalar order in corollary 14.12.3, together with $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v_\lambda )\leq c_\lambda f\, \mathrm{d}V$, gives $c_\lambda \geq m$. Set $D=\varepsilon ^{-1}\max \{ 0,\log (C/m)\} $ and $w_\lambda =\eta _\lambda -D$. Then

\[ \mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon (w_\lambda -v_\lambda )}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v_\lambda ) \leq m\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon v_\lambda }f\, \mathrm{d}V \leq \mathrm{MA}_\theta (w_\lambda ), \]

since $\lambda v_\lambda \leq 0$ and $\eta _\lambda \leq 0$. The exponential order in corollary 14.12.3 gives $v_\lambda \geq w_\lambda $, which proves the uniform bounds with $B=1+D$. $\square$

Theorem 14.15.4. Under the assumptions of theorem 14.15.2, there are $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, a constant $C>0$ and a uniquely determined $c>0$ such that

\[ \sup _ Xv=0, \qquad V_\theta -C\leq v\leq V_\theta , \qquad \mathrm{MA}_\theta (v)=cf\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

As in the semipositive case, only the constant $c$ is asserted to be unique, not the normalized potential.

Proof. Apply lemma 14.15.3 with $\lambda =1/j$, and write $v_ j=v_{1/j}$ and $c_ j=c_{1/j}$. Extract a subsequence with $v_ j\to v$ in $L^1$ and almost everywhere, and with $c_ j\to c\in [m,C]$. The limit is normalized, $\theta $-plurisubharmonic, and has minimal singularities. The densities $c_ j\mathrm{e}^{v_ j/j}f$ have a common $L^ p$ bound. Hence corollary 14.14.2, applied with the reference $V_\theta $, upgrades the convergence to uniform convergence of differences on $\Omega $ and identifies the weak $L^ p$ limit of these densities with the density of $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v)$.

At almost every point, $v_ j$ converges to a finite value, so $v_ j/j\to 0$. Since $v_ j\leq 0$ and $c_ j\leq C$, dominated convergence gives $c_ j\mathrm{e}^{v_ j/j}f\to cf$ in $L^ p$. Therefore $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v)=cf\, \mathrm{d}V$. The uniqueness of the constant follows from corollary 14.12.3. $\square$

14.16 Local Hölder continuity in a big background

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now combine the stability estimate above $\rho $ with the regularization of lemma 1.21.7, whose curvature error is linear in the regularization parameter. As in the proof of theorem 14.4.1, a small multiple of the strict barrier absorbs the curvature error of the transforms, and a lower bound for the minimizing radius converts the estimate for the transforms into an estimate for the smoothing on compact subsets of $\Omega $. This gives a local Hölder modulus for solutions with minimal singularities.

Theorem 14.16.1. Fix $C_0>0$. Let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfy

\[ \sup _ Xu=0,\qquad V_\theta -C_0\leq u\leq V_\theta , \qquad \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=f\, \mathrm{d}V, \]

where $0\leq f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$ and $p>1$. Then $u$ is locally Hölder continuous on $\Omega $, with every exponent

\[ 0<\beta <\frac{2(p-1)}{np+3(p-1)}. \]

For fixed bounds on $C_0$ and $\| f\| _ p$, the Hölder constants are uniform on each fixed compact subset of $\Omega $.

Proof. Since $u\geq V_\theta -C_0\geq \rho -C_0$, we may subtract a constant from the strict barrier so that $\rho \leq u-1$; it still satisfies $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \kappa \omega $ for a fixed $\kappa >0$. Choose $0<\gamma <(p-1)/(np+p-1)$ and set $\alpha =2\gamma <1$. Apply lemma 1.21.7 with $a=u$, and let $A,K,C$ and $\Phi _{c,\delta }$ be the constants and transforms given there. For small $\delta >0$ set

\[ c=\delta ^\alpha ,\qquad e=Ac+C\delta ,\qquad s=e/\kappa , \qquad v_\delta =\frac{\Phi _{c,\delta }+s\rho }{1+s}. \]

By the curvature bound of lemma 1.21.7, $v_\delta \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. The lower bound for $\Phi _{c,\delta }$ in that lemma implies $\rho \leq \Phi _{c,\delta }$ when $\delta $ is small. Thus

\[ v_\delta \leq \Phi _{c,\delta }\leq R_\delta u\leq 0. \]

In particular $v_\delta \leq V_\theta $, so theorem 14.14.1 applies with the reference $V_\theta $ and the comparison potential $v_\delta $. Together with the $L^1$ smoothing estimate, it gives

\[ \sup _\Omega (v_\delta -u)_+ \leq C\| (R_\delta u-u)_+\| _1^\gamma \leq C\delta ^\alpha . \]

Fix a compact subset of $\Omega $ on which $\rho \geq -M$. Since $s\leq C\delta ^\alpha $ and $u\leq 0$, the identity

\[ \Phi _{c,\delta }-u=(1+s)(v_\delta -u)+s(u-\rho ) \]

gives $\Phi _{c,\delta }-u\leq C_ M\delta ^\alpha $ there.

At a radius $t$ where the infimum defining $\Phi _{c,\delta }$ is attained, the monotone lower bound yields

\[ -c\log (t/\delta )\leq \Phi _{c,\delta }-u+K(\delta ^2+\delta ) \leq C_ Mc. \]

Hence $t\geq a_ M\delta $ for a fixed $a_ M>0$. As in the proof of theorem 14.4.1, monotonicity of the smoothing now gives

\[ R_{a_ M\delta }u-u\leq C_ M\delta ^\alpha , \qquad R_{a_ M\delta }u-u\geq -K(a_ M\delta )^2 \]

on the chosen compact set. Since every small radius has the form $a_ M\delta $, we obtain $|R_ tu-u|\leq C_ Mt^\alpha $ there for all small $t>0$, with constants uniform on that compact set. We apply this to a slightly larger compact neighborhood, on which also $\| \nabla R_ tu\| \leq C_ M/t$. For nearby points $x,y$ at distance $d>0$, the smooth function $R_ tu$ and a short path in that neighborhood give

\[ |u(x)-u(y)|\leq C_ M(t^\alpha +d/t). \]

The choice $t=d^{1/(1+\alpha )}$ gives the exponent $\alpha /(1+\alpha )$, and letting $\gamma $ approach its upper endpoint gives the asserted range. Pairs of points farther apart are handled by the boundedness of $u$ on the compact neighborhood. $\square$

It suffices that the equation hold on $\Omega $. Suppose that $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=cf\, \mathrm{d}V$ on $\Omega $, where $c>0$ and $0\leq f\in L^ p(X,\mathrm{d}V)$, and that $u$ satisfies the same bounds as in the theorem. The lower bound $u\geq V_\theta -C_0\geq \rho -C_0$ shows that $u$ is locally bounded on $\Omega $, so there $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)$ is the local bounded product. Since $Z$ has zero volume and is not charged by $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)$, the equation holds on $X$, and the theorem applies with $cf$ in place of $f$. The uniform estimates then depend on a bound for $\| cf\| _ p$, which includes the constant $c$.

14.16.1 Limits of normalized exponential solutions

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

For the normalized exponential family $(v_\lambda ,c_\lambda )$ of lemma 14.15.3, the reference interval $[V_\theta -B,V_\theta ]$ is uniform in $0<\lambda \leq 1$. Stability therefore controls differences on all of $\Omega $, and the local Hölder estimates strengthen the convergence on compact subsets. We fix the nonzero density $0\leq f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$, $p>1$, and use the constants $B,C,m$ of that lemma.

Proposition 14.16.2. For the family $(v_\lambda ,c_\lambda )$ of lemma 14.15.3, the normalized potentials are relatively compact for uniform convergence of differences on $\Omega $. As $\lambda \downarrow 0$, every cluster limit $v$ is normalized, has minimal singularities, and satisfies

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (v)=cf\, \mathrm{d}V, \]

where $c>0$ is the unique constant of theorem 14.15.4. Moreover,

\[ c_\lambda \longrightarrow c, \qquad c_\lambda \mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda }f \longrightarrow cf\quad \text{in }L^ p(\mathrm{d}V). \]

Every sequence of parameters in $(0,1]$ has a subsequence for which the potentials converge in $C^\beta $ on each compact subset of $\Omega $, for all

\[ 0<\beta <\frac{2(p-1)}{np+3(p-1)}. \]

Proof. By lemma 14.15.3, all $v_\lambda $ lie in a common reference interval, and their densities are bounded in $L^ p$ by $C\| f\| _ p$. We may therefore apply corollary 14.14.2 with $\eta =V_\theta $. Given any sequence $\lambda _ j\downarrow 0$, we pass to a subsequence with $v_{\lambda _ j}\to v$ uniformly in differences on $\Omega $ and in $L^1(X)$, and with $c_{\lambda _ j}\to c_*\in [m,C]$. The limit satisfies the same reference bounds and is normalized. Off the pole set,

\[ 0\leq 1-\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda } \leq 1-\mathrm{e}^{\lambda (V_\theta -B)}\longrightarrow 0 \]

as $\lambda \downarrow 0$. Multiplying by $f$ and using dominated convergence, we obtain $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda }f\to f$ in $L^ p$ for the whole family. The compactness corollary therefore identifies the limit equation as $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v)=c_*f\, \mathrm{d}V$, and the uniqueness of the constant gives $c_*=c$. Since every subsequential limit of $c_\lambda $ as $\lambda \downarrow 0$ equals $c$, we conclude that $c_\lambda \to c$, and the stated convergence of the densities follows.

Finally, theorem 14.16.1 gives uniform local $C^\alpha $ bounds for every $\alpha $ below its endpoint, and the uniform limit inherits these bounds by pointwise convergence. For $0<\beta <\alpha $, uniform convergence and the elementary interpolation inequality on smaller coordinate balls give

\[ [v_{\lambda _ j}-v]_{C^\beta } \leq C\| v_{\lambda _ j}-v\| _\infty ^{1-\beta /\alpha } [v_{\lambda _ j}-v]_{C^\alpha }^{\beta /\alpha } \longrightarrow 0, \]

where the Hölder bounds are taken on slightly larger compact neighborhoods. The uniformly convergent subsequence given by compactness thus converges in $C^\beta $ on every compact subset of $\Omega $ and for every exponent below the endpoint; no further extraction depending on the exponent is needed. $\square$

Corollary 14.16.3. Suppose a group $G$ of biholomorphisms of $X$ preserves $\theta $ and the measure $f\, \mathrm{d}V$. Then every $v_\lambda $ and every cluster limit as $\lambda \downarrow 0$ is $G$-invariant. If the normalized zero-exponent equation has only one $G$-invariant solution with minimal singularities, then the whole family $v_\lambda $ converges to it as $\lambda \downarrow 0$, uniformly in differences on $\Omega $ and in each local $C^\beta $ topology of the proposition.

Proof. For $g\in G$, pullback by $g$ preserves $\theta $-plurisubharmonicity, and $V_\theta \circ g=V_\theta $ by the definition of the envelope. Pullback also preserves the positive-exponent equation, so its uniqueness gives $u_\lambda \circ g=u_\lambda $, and the normalization preserves this equality. Pullback by a fixed smooth biholomorphism is continuous on $L^1(X)$, since its Jacobian relative to the fixed smooth volume is bounded. Hence every cluster limit is invariant almost everywhere, and therefore everywhere for the canonical representatives. This argument does not require $g$ to preserve the barrier $\rho $ or the set $\Omega $.

If the invariant solution is unique, every cluster limit is the same potential. Relative compactness then implies convergence of the whole family for uniform convergence of differences on $\Omega $: a sequence staying at a fixed positive distance from that potential would have a convergent subsequence, a contradiction. The interpolation argument above upgrades this to the asserted local $C^\beta $ convergence. $\square$

The proposition gives compactness and identifies the equation of every cluster limit, and the symmetry criterion is one way to identify the limit itself. In general, theorem 15.12.1 shows that the whole family converges, and it describes the limit through the greatest subsolution of the zero-exponent equation that satisfies a family of integral normalizations determined by how the constant $c$ depends on the density. This holds even when the zero-exponent equation has several normalized solutions.

14.17 Interior smoothness on the regular locus

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Throughout this section, $\theta $ is a smooth big real $(1,1)$-form, and $\rho $, $Z=\{ \rho =-\infty \} $ and $\Omega =X\setminus Z$ are the strict analytic barrier and its regular locus fixed in section 14.12. Thus $\rho \leq 0$ has analytic singularities with smooth remainder, and $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \omega $. If $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ has minimal singularities, then $\varphi \geq V_\theta -C\geq \rho -C$ for a constant $C$. Hence $\varphi $ is locally bounded on $\Omega $, and its Monge–Ampère measure there is the bounded-potential product.

For such a potential, theorem 14.16.1 gives local Hölder continuity on $\Omega $ when the density belongs to $L^ p$ for some $p>1$. We show that a smooth strictly positive density on the whole of $\Omega $ then makes the potential smooth on $\Omega $. Local information alone cannot give this: example 14.20.5 below is a locally Lipschitz plurisubharmonic function with constant Monge–Ampère density that is not $C^1$. The argument therefore uses the barrier globally. By the proper gap of lemma 1.19.7, the difference between the solution and the barrier attains a minimum on the closed set where smoothness fails, and strict positivity of $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho $ turns this minimum into a quadratic separation. Near the minimum, smooth Dirichlet approximants have traces of polynomial growth; the separation improves this growth until a uniform trace bound yields elliptic regularity.

Theorem 14.17.1. Suppose $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ has minimal singularities and is locally Hölder continuous on $\Omega $. Let $c>0$ and let $f$ be smooth and strictly positive on $\Omega $. Assume

\[ (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n=cf\, \omega ^ n\quad \text{on }\Omega . \]

Then $\varphi \in C^\infty (\Omega )$ and $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi >0$ there.

The Hölder hypothesis holds when, in addition, $f\in L^ p(X,\omega ^ n)$ for some $p>1$, by theorem 14.16.1; see corollary 14.17.11. The theorem also applies when local Hölder continuity is obtained by another argument.

The proof is by contradiction and occupies the rest of the section. Let $\mathcal S\subset \Omega $ be the closed set of points near which $\varphi $ is not smooth, suppose that $\mathcal S\neq \varnothing $, and let $p\in \mathcal S$ minimize $\varphi -\rho $ on $\mathcal S$. On a small ball centered at $p$ the argument has four steps.

  1. A comparison principle for continuous solutions (section 14.17.1) and the smooth Dirichlet problem give smooth solutions $u_ j$ of the equation that converge uniformly to $\varphi $ at a polynomial rate in $j$, while the traces of $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j$, measured in Euclidean coordinates, grow at most polynomially in $j$ (section 14.17.3).

  2. Near $p$, the quadratic separation along $\mathcal S$, together with uniform trace bounds on compact sets where $\varphi $ is already smooth, improves the trace growth to $O(j^\gamma )$ for every $\gamma >0$. Interpolation with the convergence rate then gives uniform $C^{1,\alpha }$ bounds for every $\alpha <1$ (section 14.17.4).

  3. A third-order estimate whose constants depend polynomially on the ellipticity turns uniform $C^{1,\alpha }$ bounds, for $\alpha $ close to one, into a uniform trace bound (section 14.17.5).

  4. A continuous solution with bounded trace is smooth (section 14.17.2). Hence $\varphi $ is smooth near $p$, contradicting $p\in \mathcal S$ (section 14.17.6).

14.17.1 Local comparison

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The comparison principle below, for continuous sub- and supersolutions on a bounded domain, identifies the given continuous potential with limits of smooth Dirichlet solutions. Since the background need not be closed, the classical Bedford–Taylor comparison does not apply directly. We freeze the background at a point, apply the closed-current comparison formula (1.8) to quadratic modifications, and control the error by the mixed inequalities of lemma 1.7.6, lemma 1.7.7.

The local arguments of this section take place in coordinate balls, always with compact closure in a larger coordinate chart. There we put $\beta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2$, write $\partial _ i=\partial /\partial z_ i$ and $\partial _{\bar i}=\partial /\partial \bar z_ i$, and take coefficient matrices of real $(1,1)$-forms relative to $\beta $. For such a form $A$, $\operatorname {tr}_\beta A$ and $\det _\beta A$ denote the trace and the determinant of its coefficient matrix, so that $A^ n=(\det _\beta A)\beta ^ n$, and $\Delta _\beta =\operatorname {tr}_\beta \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$. For a positive form $T$, $\operatorname {tr}_ T$ is the trace computed with the inverse of the coefficient matrix of $T$. Indices run from $1$ to $n$, repeated indices in tensor expressions are summed, and $I$ denotes the identity matrix. In local statements, $\vartheta $ denotes a smooth real $(1,1)$-form, not necessarily closed, which plays the role of $\theta $.

Proposition 14.17.2. Let $D\Subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a bounded domain and let $\vartheta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form near $\overline D$. Suppose $u,v\in C(\overline D)$ are $\vartheta $-plurisubharmonic,

\[ (\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n\geq F\beta ^ n, \qquad (\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\leq F\beta ^ n, \]

where $F>0$ is continuous on $D$. If $u\leq v$ on $\partial D$, then $u\leq v$ in $D$.

Proof. Step 1: $v$ admits no strict smooth lower test. Suppose, to the contrary, that a $C^2$ function $q$ touches $v$ from below at a point $p\in D$ and that, at $p$,

\[ \vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q>0, \qquad \det _\beta (\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q)>F. \]

Choose a sufficiently small $a>0$. After replacing $q$ by $q-a|z-p|^2$, which makes the contact strict, and restricting to a small ball $B_ r(p)$, there are fixed $\lambda ,\eta >0$ such that

\[ A:=\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q\geq \lambda \beta , \qquad A^ n\geq (F+\eta )\beta ^ n. \]

To compare with closed currents, freeze the background at $p$: put $\kappa =\sup _{B_ r}\lVert \vartheta -\vartheta (p)\rVert _\beta $ and $B_0=\vartheta (p)+\kappa \beta $. Then $S=B_0-\vartheta $ satisfies $0\leq S\leq 2\kappa \beta $. For small $s>0$ the set $E=\{ v<q+s\} $ is nonempty and relatively compact in $B_ r$. If $Q_0$ is a quadratic potential for the constant form $B_0$, then

\[ P=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v+Q_0)=\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v+S, \qquad Q=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(q+s+Q_0)=A+S\geq \lambda \beta \]

are closed positive Hessians. Applying (1.8), expanding $P$, and using $S\leq 2\kappa \beta \leq 2\kappa \lambda ^{-1}Q$, we obtain

\[ \int _ E P^ n\leq \int _ E F\beta ^ n+e_\kappa \int _ E P^ n, \qquad e_\kappa =\left(1+\frac{2\kappa }{\lambda }\right)^ n-1. \]

On the other hand, (1.8) with $j=n$ gives

\[ \int _ E P^ n\geq \int _ E Q^ n\geq \int _ E(F+\eta )\beta ^ n. \]

If $M$ bounds $F$ on the ball, the two estimates give $(1-e_\kappa )\eta \leq e_\kappa M$. Since $\kappa \to 0$, and hence $e_\kappa \to 0$, as $r\downarrow 0$, this is impossible for small $r$. Thus no such lower test exists.

Step 2: comparison.

Suppose that $\max _ D(u-v)>0$. We regularize $u$ to produce a forbidden lower test for $v$. Let $\chi _\delta $ be a nonnegative smooth mollifier of mass one and radius $\delta >0$. For small $t>0$ and $C\geq \sup _ D|z|^2$, set

\[ u_ t=u+t(|z|^2-C). \]

Then $u_ t\leq v$ on $\partial D$, the positive maximum persists, and $T_ t=\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ t\geq t\beta $. Lemma 1.7.6 gives

\[ T_ t^ n\geq (F^{1/n}+t)^ n\beta ^ n. \]

Choose a relatively compact neighborhood $W$ of the positive maximum set. By lemma 1.7.7,

\[ (T_ t*\chi _\delta )^ n \geq \bigl ((F^{1/n}+t)*\chi _\delta \bigr )^ n\beta ^ n. \]

Since

\[ \vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_ t*\chi _\delta ) =T_ t*\chi _\delta +(\vartheta -\vartheta *\chi _\delta ), \]

the convolution satisfies $T_ t*\chi _\delta \geq t\beta $. If $\epsilon _\delta =\| \vartheta -\vartheta *\chi _\delta \| _{C^0(W)}$, then

\[ \vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_ t*\chi _\delta ) \geq (1-\epsilon _\delta /t)(T_ t*\chi _\delta ). \]

For fixed $t>0$, uniform convergence of the convolved density and $\epsilon _\delta \to 0$ show that the left-hand side is positive and has determinant strictly larger than $F$ on $W$ for small $\delta $. Since $u_ t*\chi _\delta \to u_ t$ uniformly on $W$, the difference $u_ t*\chi _\delta -v$ still attains its maximum over $\overline W$ at an interior point of $W$ for small $\delta $. Subtracting this maximum from $u_ t*\chi _\delta $ produces a smooth function touching $v$ from below with the properties excluded in Step 1, a contradiction. $\square$

14.17.2 Bounded traces and smoothness

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We begin with the criterion used in the last step of the proof. A bounded trace of a positive current bounds all its coefficient measures, so a continuous solution with bounded trace is a strong solution with $W^{2,q}$ regularity for every finite $q$. The equation then bounds the eigenvalues from below, so it becomes uniformly elliptic. Since the solution is not yet known to be $C^2$, the Evans–Krylov theory is applied to it as a viscosity solution of a concave extension of the operator.

Proposition 14.17.3. Let $B\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a coordinate ball, let $\vartheta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form there and let $F\in C^\infty (B)$ be strictly positive. Suppose $u$ is continuous, $T=\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0$, and

14.17
\begin{equation} T^ n=F\beta ^ n. \end{equation}

For a point $p\in B$, the following conditions, each required on some neighborhood of $p$, are equivalent:

  1. $u$ is smooth;

  2. $T\geq \delta \beta $ for some $\delta >0$;

  3. $\operatorname {tr}_\beta T$ has a locally bounded measure density.

Proof. Choose $C$ so that $v=u+C|z|^2$ is plurisubharmonic, and use nonnegative radial mollifiers for which $v_\eta \downarrow v$. The smooth matrix convolutions $T_\eta =T*\chi _\eta $ can be written as

\[ T_\eta =(\vartheta *\chi _\eta -C\beta )+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\eta . \]

Bedford–Taylor monotone continuity and smooth convergence of the background imply $T_\eta ^ k\to T^ k$ for every $k$.

(2)$\Rightarrow $(3). Assume (2). For every Hermitian matrix $M\geq \delta I$,

\[ \det M\geq \delta ^{n-1}\bigl (\operatorname {tr} M-(n-1)\delta \bigr ). \]

Apply this to the regularizations and pass to currents. Equation (14.17) gives

\[ \operatorname {tr}_\beta T\leq \delta ^{1-n}F+(n-1)\delta , \]

which is (3).

(3)$\Rightarrow $(1). Assume (3), say $\operatorname {tr}_\beta T\leq L$. Positivity makes every coefficient of the matrix-valued current $T$ a bounded density. Hence the Euclidean Laplacian of $u$ is locally bounded, and interior Poisson estimates give $u\in W^{2,q}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ for every finite $q$. Mollification converges in $W^{2,q}$; taking $q>2n$ identifies the bounded-potential determinant with the ordinary almost-everywhere determinant. Thus $\det _\beta T=F$ almost everywhere. On a smaller ball with $F\geq m>0$, all eigenvalues of $T$ are at most $L$ and their product is at least $m$, so

\[ \frac{m}{L^{n-1}}I\leq T\leq LI \]

almost everywhere.

It remains to show that this strong solution is smooth. Let $P$ be the real linear map from real symmetric $2n\times 2n$ Hessian matrices to Hermitian $n\times n$ matrices, sending $D^2u$ to the coefficient matrix of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ relative to $\beta $, and let $\Theta (x)$ be the matrix of $\vartheta $. With $\delta =mL^{1-n}$, define the concave extension

\[ G(x,M)=\inf _{\delta I\leq A\leq LI} \left\{ \log \det A+\operatorname {tr}\! \left[A^{-1}\bigl (\Theta (x)+P(M)-A\bigr )\right]\right\} -\log F(x). \]

For fixed $A$, write $\mathcal E_ A(x,M)$ for the expression in braces minus $\log F(x)$. The tangent-plane formula for $\log \det $ gives

\[ G(x,D^2u)=0\quad \text{almost everywhere}. \]

The operator $G$ is concave and uniformly elliptic on real symmetric matrices. The affine operators $\mathcal E_ A$ have constant principal matrices with eigenvalues bounded above and below in terms of $\delta $ and $L$. Their $x$-dependence is smooth uniformly in $A$, so $G$ has a uniform local Lipschitz modulus in $x$.

We claim that $u$ is a viscosity solution of $G(x,D^2u)=0$. Suppose that a smooth function $\psi $ touches $u$ from above at $x_0$ and that $G(x_0,D^2\psi )<0$. Some fixed matrix $A$ then satisfies $\mathcal E_ A(x,D^2\psi )<-\eta $ on a small ball. Since $G(x,D^2u)=0$ almost everywhere, $\mathcal E_ A(x,D^2u)\geq 0$ there. Thus $u-\psi $ has an interior maximum and satisfies

\[ \operatorname {tr}\! \left(A^{-1}P(D^2(u-\psi ))\right)\geq \eta \quad \text{almost everywhere}. \]

After a small quadratic perturbation makes the contact strict, this contradicts the Aleksandrov maximum principle [Chapter 9, Theorem 9.1, GT01]. If instead $\psi $ touches $u$ from below and $G(x_0,D^2\psi )>0$, then $\mathcal E_ A(x,D^2\psi )>\eta $ for every admissible $A$ on a small ball. At almost every point choose an admissible $A(x)$ with $\mathcal E_{A(x)}(x,D^2u)<\eta /2$. A measurable choice is obtained by taking the first qualifying element of a countable dense family. The function $\psi -u$ then violates the same maximum principle for the measurable uniformly elliptic coefficients $A(x)^{-1}$. This proves the claim.

The classical variable-coefficient Evans–Krylov theorem gives $u\in C^{2,\alpha }_{\mathrm{loc}}$ for some $0<\alpha <1$. The original equation is now uniformly elliptic with smooth data. Difference quotients satisfy linear equations whose principal coefficients are averages of inverse matrices along positive definite segments. Interior Schauder estimates first give $u\in C^{3,\alpha }_{\mathrm{loc}}$ and then, by iteration, $u\in C^\infty $. This proves (3)$\Rightarrow $(1).

(1)$\Rightarrow $(2). If $u$ is smooth, then $T$ is a smooth semipositive form with determinant $F>0$, so it is positive definite; on a smaller neighborhood this gives (2). $\square$

14.17.3 Smooth exact approximants

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now construct the approximants of the first step. The smooth Dirichlet theorem, lemma 14.6.1, applies to every smooth positive density $F$. Approximating the Hölder boundary values of $\varphi $ by smooth functions therefore gives smooth solutions, and the point is to control them quantitatively. The estimates below bound the trace of a smooth solution by a power of the $C^4$ norm of its boundary value. The $C^4$ norms of the boundary approximations grow polynomially while their uniform errors decay polynomially, so both the convergence and the trace growth have polynomial rates.

In this subsection balls are centered at the origin. On the boundary sphere, $\nu $ is the outward Euclidean unit normal and $\nabla _{\tan }$ denotes tangential differentiation. For a positive form $T$, inner products $\langle \cdot ,\cdot \rangle _ T$ and norms $|\cdot |_ T$ of $(1,0)$-covectors are computed with the inverse of the coefficient matrix of $T$.

Lemma 14.17.4. Let $B$ be a coordinate ball, let $\vartheta $ and $F>0$ be smooth near $\overline B$, and let $q$ be smooth with $R=\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q\geq \varepsilon \beta $ for a fixed $\varepsilon >0$. Fix a finite $L\geq 0$. If $u\in C^\infty (B)$ satisfies

\[ T=\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>0, \qquad T^ n=F\beta ^ n, \qquad |u-q|\leq L, \]

put $S=\operatorname {tr}_\beta T$ and $v=u-q$. For $A>0$, define

\[ Q_ A=\log S-Av+\mathrm{e}^{-v}. \]

There are $A_0,C$, depending only on the fixed data and $L$, such that at every interior local maximum of $Q_ A$, with $A\geq A_0$,

14.18
\begin{equation} S\leq C\sqrt A. \end{equation}

The constants do not depend on any bound for the gradient of $u$.

Proof. This is the maximum-principle computation behind the classical second-order estimate. The non-closedness of $\vartheta $ produces a first-order error term, which is absorbed by the convex term $\mathrm{e}^{-v}$.

At the point in question make a constant unitary change of coordinates so that the matrix of $T$ is diagonal, with eigenvalues $\lambda _ i$. Write

\[ \Delta _ T=\sum _ i\lambda _ i^{-1}\partial _ i\partial _{\bar i}, \qquad H=\operatorname {tr}_ T\beta . \]

Differentiating $\log \det T=\log F$ and commuting derivatives gives

14.19
\begin{equation} \Delta _ TS=\Delta _\beta \log F+P+E_0, \quad P=\sum _{k,i,j}\frac{|\partial _ kT_{i\bar j}|^2}{\lambda _ i\lambda _ j}, \quad |E_0|\leq C_0H. \end{equation}

The non-closedness of $\vartheta $ enters through the bounded vector

\[ E_ p=\sum _ k(\partial _ p\vartheta _{k\bar k}-\partial _ k\vartheta _{p\bar k}). \]

Since $\partial _ pS-E_ p=\sum _ k\partial _ kT_{p\bar k}$, weighted Cauchy–Schwarz gives $|\partial S-E|_ T^2\leq SP$. From (14.19),

14.20
\begin{equation} \Delta _ T\log S \geq -\frac{C_0(1+H)}S- \frac2S\operatorname {Re}\langle \partial \log S,E\rangle _ T. \end{equation}

Set $g_ A(s)=-As+\mathrm{e}^{-s}$. At a local maximum of $Q_ A$, $\partial \log S=-g_ A'(v)\partial v$ and $\Delta _ Tv=n-\operatorname {tr}_ TR$. Combining these identities with (14.20), then completing the square in $\partial v$, yields

14.21
\begin{equation} 0\geq \left[\varepsilon A-\frac{C_2}{S}-\frac{C_2(A+1)^2}{S^2}\right]H -C_3(A+1)-\frac{C_2}{S}. \end{equation}

Here $|E|_ T^2\leq CH$ and the bounds for $v$ have been used. If $S\geq K\sqrt A$ and $K$ is large, the bracket is at least $\varepsilon A/2$. Thus $H\leq C$ independently of $A$ and $u$. The determinant upper bound then gives $S\leq C'$. Enlarging $K$ makes this incompatible with $S\geq K\sqrt A$, proving (14.18). $\square$

We shall also use the following variant, in which a gradient bound replaces the convex term. If $|\nabla (u-q)|_\beta $ is bounded, then at an interior maximum of $\log S-A(u-q)$ the trace is bounded independently of $A$ for all large $A$. Indeed, at the maximum $\partial \log S=A\partial (u-q)$, and (14.20) gives

\[ 0\geq \left[A\varepsilon -\frac CS-\frac{CA}{S}\right]H-nA-\frac CS. \]

For $S$ larger than a fixed constant the bracket is at least $A\varepsilon /2$; the determinant again controls $S$.

A polynomial boundary trace estimate

Proposition 14.17.5. Let $B$ be the unit ball. Fix smooth $\vartheta $, smooth $F>0$ near $\overline B$, and $M<\infty $. There are $C,N<\infty $, depending only on these fixed data, such that every smooth solution $u$ with boundary value $g\in C^\infty (\partial B)$ of

\[ T=\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>0, \qquad T^ n=F\beta ^ n, \qquad \lVert u\rVert _{L^\infty (B)}\leq M, \qquad u|_{\partial B}=g, \]

satisfies

14.22
