\begin{equation} \sup _{\overline B}\operatorname {tr}_\beta T \leq C\bigl (1+\lVert g\rVert _{C^4(\partial B)}\bigr )^ N. \end{equation}

Proof. We follow the classical boundary estimates for the Dirichlet problem on a ball and keep track of the dependence on $L=1+\lVert g\rVert _{C^4}$. Barriers bound the first derivatives, and differentiation along rotations bounds the mixed normal–tangential second derivatives on the boundary. A lower bound for the tangential block of $T$ then bounds the double normal derivative through the equation, and lemma 14.17.4 reduces the interior bound to the boundary bound.

Step 1: first derivatives. Fix a sufficiently large constant $K>0$, depending only on the fixed data. Extend $g$ to $G$ on $\overline B$ with $\lVert G\rVert _{C^4}\leq CL$. For $A=CL$ large enough,

\[ \underline u=G+A(|z|^2-1), \qquad \underline T=\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\underline u\geq K\beta , \qquad \underline T^ n\geq F\beta ^ n. \]

Proposition 14.17.2 gives $\underline u\leq u$. If $h$ solves $\Delta _\beta h=-C_\vartheta $ with $h=g$ on $\partial B$ and $C_\vartheta \geq \sup \operatorname {tr}_\beta \vartheta $, then $u\leq h$. Boundary differentiation of these barriers gives

14.23
\begin{equation} |\partial _\nu u|+|\nabla _{\tan }u|\leq CL \quad \text{on }\partial B. \end{equation}

Let $H=\operatorname {tr}_ T\beta $, $\mathcal L=\operatorname {tr}_ T\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$, and $w=u-\underline u\geq 0$, the linearized operator and the barrier function used below. Taking $K$ fixed and sufficiently large gives

14.24
\begin{equation} \mathcal Lw=n-\operatorname {tr}_ T\underline T\leq -(1+H). \end{equation}

Let $E=\sum _ k(z_ k\partial _{z_ k}+\bar z_ k\partial _{\bar z_ k})$ be the real radial field. Write $\Theta $ for the coefficient matrix of $\vartheta $. Differentiating $\log \det T=\log F$ and commuting $E$ with complex second derivatives yields

\[ \mathcal L(Eu)=E(\log F)+2n-\operatorname {tr}_ T(E\Theta +2\Theta ). \]

Hence $|\mathcal L(Eu)|\leq C(1+H)$. The same estimate holds for $Zu$ when $Z$ is an infinitesimal unitary rotation. Applying the minimum principle to $CL+C'w\pm Yu$, with $Y=E$ or $Z$, gives

14.25
\begin{equation} |Eu|+|Zu|\leq CL\quad \text{on }B. \end{equation}

Step 2: mixed second derivatives on the boundary. For a rotation $Z$, the function $R_ Z=Z(u-G)$ vanishes on $\partial B$ and satisfies $|\mathcal LR_ Z|\leq CL(1+H)$. The barriers $CLw\pm R_ Z$ and (14.24) imply $|\partial _\nu R_ Z|\leq CL^2$ on the boundary. Rotations span the real tangent space of $\partial B$, so all real mixed normal–tangential second derivatives are $O(L^2)$. Pure tangential second derivatives are $O(L)$ by differentiating $u=g$ twice and using (14.23). Thus all complex Hessian entries except the complex normal–normal entry are $O(L^2)$.

Step 3: the double normal derivative. It remains to bound the tangential block of $T$ from below. For $n\geq 2$, let

\[ \lambda _0=\min \{ T_ p(\xi ,\bar\xi ):p\in \partial B, \ \xi \in T_ p^{1,0}(\partial B),\ |\xi |_\beta =1\} . \]

Choose a minimizing pair $(p,\xi )$ and extend $\xi $ to a smooth unit tangential field near $p$, with uniformly bounded derivatives. On the sphere, any smooth function vanishing there satisfies

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u-G)(\xi ,\bar\xi )=\frac12E(u-G). \]

Define

\[ a(z)=(\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}G)_ z(\xi (z),\overline{\xi (z)})-\frac12EG(z), \qquad P=a+\frac12Eu-\lambda _0. \]

Then $P\geq 0$ on the nearby spherical boundary and $P(p)=0$. The boundary first-derivative bound also gives $\lambda _0\leq CL$ by the tangential identity above. Consequently

\[ |P|\leq CL, \qquad |\mathcal LP|\leq CL(1+H). \]

On a fixed cap $D=B\cap B_ r(p)$ consider

\[ \Psi =P+C_1Lw+C_2L|z-p|^2. \]

Choose $C_2$ so that $\Psi \geq 0$ on the interior spherical part of $\partial D$, and then $C_1$ so that $\mathcal L\Psi \leq 0$ by (14.24). The minimum principle yields $\Psi \geq 0$. At $p$ the normal derivative of $|z-p|^2$ vanishes, and therefore

\[ \partial _\nu P+C_1L\partial _\nu w\leq 0. \]

Equations (14.23)–(14.25) now give $D^2u(\nu ,\nu )(p)\leq CL^2$, hence $\operatorname {tr}_\beta T(p)\leq CL^2$. Positivity and $\det _\beta T=F$ imply

14.26
\begin{equation} \lambda _0\geq cL^{-2(n-1)}. \end{equation}

At any boundary point write

\[ T=\begin{pmatrix} B_0 & b \\ b^* & d \end{pmatrix} \]

in tangential–normal blocks, with $B_0$ an $(n-1)\times (n-1)$ Hermitian matrix, $b\in \mathbb C^{n-1}$ and $d\in \mathbb R$. We have $|b|\leq CL^2$ and $B_0\geq \lambda _0I$. The determinant equation and the Schur complement give

\[ d=\frac{F}{\det B_0}+b^*B_0^{-1}b \leq (\sup F)\lambda _0^{-(n-1)}+CL^4\lambda _0^{-1}, \]

which is polynomial in $L$. For $n=1$, the trace is $F$ and this argument is unnecessary.

Step 4: the interior. Choose a fixed quadratic $q$ with $\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q\geq \varepsilon \beta $. Apply lemma 14.17.4 with one fixed $A$. At an interior maximum of its auxiliary function the trace is uniformly bounded, while at a boundary maximum the preceding estimate applies. Since the remaining terms are uniformly bounded, (14.22) follows. $\square$

Exact approximation

We apply these estimates to smooth approximations of the boundary values of $\varphi $ on a ball in $\Omega $. Recall that $\theta $, $\omega $, $c$ and $f$ are the data of theorem 14.17.1.

Proposition 14.17.6. Let $B\Subset \Omega $ be a coordinate ball, identified with $\{ |z|<r_0\} $, with closure in a larger coordinate chart, and put

\[ F=\frac{cf\, \omega ^ n}{\beta ^ n}. \]

There are $a>0$, $b,C<\infty $, and $u_ j\in C^\infty (\overline B)$ such that

14.27
\begin{equation} \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j>0, \qquad (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n=F\beta ^ n, \end{equation}

and

14.28
\begin{equation} \lVert u_ j-\varphi \rVert _{L^\infty (B)}\leq Cj^{-a}, \qquad \sup _{\overline B}\operatorname {tr}_\beta (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)\leq Cj^ b. \end{equation}

Proof. Since $\varphi $ is Hölder continuous near $\overline B$, mollification at scale $1/j$ gives smooth boundary approximations $g_ j$ with

\[ \lVert g_ j-\varphi \rVert _{L^\infty (\partial B)}\leq Cj^{-a}, \qquad \lVert g_ j\rVert _{C^4(\partial B)}\leq Cj^4. \]

Choose a fixed quadratic $q$ with $\eta =\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q>0$ near $\overline B$. Extend $g_ j$ smoothly to $G_ j$. For a sufficiently large, polynomially bounded $A_ j$, the function

\[ \underline w_ j=G_ j-q+A_ j(|z|^2-r_0^2) \]

has boundary value $g_ j-q$ and satisfies

\[ \eta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\underline w_ j =\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}G_ j+A_ j\beta >0, \qquad (\eta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\underline w_ j)^ n\geq F\beta ^ n. \]

Apply the smooth Dirichlet theorem recalled at the beginning of this subsection, with density $H=F\beta ^ n/\eta ^ n$ to obtain a solution $w_ j$ for the Hermitian background $\eta $; put $u_ j=w_ j+q$. Comparing $u_ j$ with $\varphi \pm \lVert g_ j-\varphi \rVert _\infty $ by proposition 14.17.2 proves the first bound in (14.28). The functions $u_ j$ are therefore uniformly bounded. Proposition 14.17.5 and the polynomial $C^4$ boundary norm give the second bound. $\square$

14.17.4 Localization near the nonregular set

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

In the auxiliary function of lemma 14.17.4, the term $-A(u_ j-q)$ penalizes points where $u_ j-q$ is large. A quadratic gap of $u-q$ along the nonregular set therefore suppresses the contribution of the boundary of a neighborhood of that set. Taking $A$ proportional to $\log j$ makes this suppression beat the polynomial growth of the boundary trace, and near the point of minimal $u-q$ the trace then grows more slowly than every positive power of $j$. Interpolation with the polynomial approximation rate gives uniform $C^{1,\alpha }$ bounds.

Distances and norms below are Euclidean. By a smooth exact solution for $(\vartheta ,F)$ we mean a smooth function $u_ j$ with $\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j>0$ and $(\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n=F\beta ^ n$.

Proposition 14.17.7. For $R>0$, let $B=B_ R(0)$, let $\vartheta $ and $F>0$ be smooth near $\overline B$, and let $u\in C(\overline B)$. Suppose $q$ is smooth and $\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q\geq \varepsilon \beta $ for some $\varepsilon >0$. Put $v=u-q$ and $m=v(0)$, and fix $\kappa >0$. Let $\mathcal S\subset \overline B$ be closed, contain $0$, and satisfy

14.29
\begin{equation} v(0)=m, \qquad v(z)\geq m+\kappa |z|^2\quad (z\in \mathcal S). \end{equation}

Assume that smooth exact solutions $u_ j$, $j\geq 2$, satisfy, for fixed constants $a>0$, $b\geq 0$ and $C>0$,

14.30
\begin{align} \lVert u_ j-u\rVert _\infty & \leq Cj^{-a}, \\ \sup _{\overline B}\operatorname {tr}_\beta (\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)& \leq Cj^ b, \\ \sup _ j\sup _ K\operatorname {tr}_\beta (\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)& <\infty \quad \text{for every }K\Subset \overline B\setminus \mathcal S. \end{align}

Then for every $\gamma >0$ there are a neighborhood $U_\gamma $ of $0$ and $C_\gamma $ such that

14.33
\begin{equation} \operatorname {tr}_\beta (\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)\leq C_\gamma j^\gamma \quad \text{on }U_\gamma . \end{equation}

For every $0<\alpha <1$, there is also a neighborhood $U$ of $0$ on which $u_ j\to u$ in $C^{1,\alpha }$. In particular the approximants are uniformly bounded in that norm and $u\in C^{1,\alpha }(U)$.

Proof. Step 1: the trace bound (14.33). Write $e_ j=Cj^{-a}$, fix $d=\kappa R^2/2$, and choose $D>(b+1)/d$. Choose $\eta >0$ so that $2D\eta <\gamma /2$. For small $\delta >0$, the neighborhood

\[ W_\delta =\{ z\in B:\operatorname {dist}(z,\mathcal S)<\delta \} \]

contains $0$, has $v\geq m-\eta $ on its closure, and satisfies $v\geq m+d$ on $\partial B\cap \overline W_\delta $ with the fixed $d$ above. These properties follow from (14.29), continuity, and compactness. The remaining part of the boundary of $W_\delta $ is a compact subset of $\overline B\setminus \mathcal S$, where (14.32) applies.

Set $A_ j=D\log j$. Apply the maximum principle on $\overline W_\delta $ to

\[ Q_ j=\log \operatorname {tr}_\beta (\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)-A_ j(u_ j-q)+\mathrm{e}^{-(u_ j-q)}. \]

At an interior maximum, lemma 14.17.4 gives $\log \operatorname {tr}_\beta (\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)\leq C+\frac12\log A_ j$. On the part of $\partial W_\delta $ inside $B$, which stays away from $\mathcal S$, the trace has a bound independent of $j$. On the outer part $\partial B\cap \overline W_\delta $, (14.31) and the gap $d$ give

\[ Q_ j\leq C+b\log j-A_ j(m+d-e_ j), \]

and the choice $Dd>b+1$ makes this smaller than the bound below for large $j$. Hence throughout $W_\delta $,

\[ Q_ j\leq C_\delta +\tfrac 12\log A_ j-A_ j(m-\eta -e_ j). \]

On a small ball about $0$ where $v\leq m+\eta $, this implies

\[ \log \operatorname {tr}_\beta (\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j) \leq C_\delta +\tfrac 12\log A_ j+A_ j(2\eta +2e_ j). \]

By the choice of $\eta $, the logarithmic factor and $A_ je_ j$ are absorbed into the remaining power. This proves (14.33).

Step 2: $C^{1,\alpha }$ convergence. On a smaller ball, the real Laplacian of $u_ j$ is bounded by $Cj^\gamma $. Interior Poisson estimates give a $W^{2,q_0}$ bound for every finite $q_0$, and Sobolev embedding with $q_0$ large then gives, for every $\sigma <1$,

\[ \lVert u_ j\rVert _{W^{2,q_0}}+\lVert u_ j\rVert _{C^{1,\sigma }} \leq Cj^\gamma . \]

For $d_ k=u_{2^{k+1}}-u_{2^ k}$,

\[ \lVert d_ k\rVert _\infty \leq C2^{-ka}, \qquad \lVert d_ k\rVert _{C^{1,\sigma }}\leq C2^{k\gamma }. \]

If $\alpha <\sigma $ and $t=(1+\alpha )/(1+\sigma )$, Hölder interpolation gives

\[ \lVert d_ k\rVert _{C^{1,\alpha }} \leq C2^{k[-a(1-t)+\gamma t]}. \]

Since $\gamma $ may be arbitrarily small, the exponent can be made negative. The dyadic series converges in $C^{1,\alpha }$ to its uniform limit $u$. If $2^ k\leq j<2^{k+1}$, interpolation applied to $u_ j-u_{2^ k}$ gives the same decaying bound. Hence the full sequence converges to $u$ in $C^{1,\alpha }$ on a smaller ball. $\square$

Proposition 14.17.8. Suppose $u_ j$ solve the same smooth equation as in proposition 14.17.7, satisfy (14.30)–(14.31), and $u$ is smooth with $\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>0$ on an open set $O\subset B$. Then, for every $K\Subset O$,

14.34
\begin{equation} \sup _ j\sup _ K\operatorname {tr}_\beta (\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)<\infty . \end{equation}

Proof. Where $u$ is smooth and strictly $\vartheta $-plurisubharmonic, it provides its own quadratic separation, so the argument of proposition 14.17.7 applies around every point of $O$. A first application gives trace growth of order $\sqrt{\log j}$; the resulting gradient convergence then allows the variant of lemma 14.17.4 with a gradient bound, which removes this growth.

For $p$ in a fixed compact subset of $O$, take a uniformly sized ball $B_ r(p)\Subset O$ and set

\[ q_ p(z)=u(z)-\kappa |z-p|^2 \]

with $\kappa >0$ small, so $\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q_ p\geq \varepsilon \beta $. If $e_ j=Cj^{-a}$ and $v_{j,p}=u_ j-q_ p$, then

\[ v_{j,p}\geq -e_ j, \quad v_{j,p}(p)\leq e_ j, \quad v_{j,p}\geq \kappa r^2-e_ j\text{ on }\partial B_ r(p). \]

With $A_ j=D\log j$ and $D\kappa r^2>b+1$, the same maximum argument as above gives

14.35
\begin{equation} \operatorname {tr}_\beta (\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)(p)\leq C\sqrt{\log j}. \end{equation}

The constants are uniform on compact subsets of $O$.

Put $T_ j=\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j$ and $w_ j=u_ j-u$. On slightly larger compact sets, (14.35) bounds $|\Delta w_ j|$ by $C\sqrt{\log j}$. Poisson estimates and Sobolev embedding give

\[ \lVert w_ j\rVert _{C^{1,\sigma }}\leq C_\sigma \sqrt{\log j}, \qquad \sigma <1. \]

Interpolating with $\lVert w_ j\rVert _\infty \leq Cj^{-a}$ shows $\nabla w_ j\to 0$ uniformly on smaller compact sets. The gradients of $v_{j,p}$ are therefore uniformly bounded. Repeat the maximum argument with $\log \operatorname {tr}_\beta T_ j-A_ jv_{j,p}$, now using the variant of lemma 14.17.4 with a gradient bound. Its interior trace bound is now independent of $A_ j$, while the boundary term is suppressed as before. Evaluation at $p$ gives

\[ \operatorname {tr}_\beta T_ j(p)\leq C\exp (2A_ je_ j)\leq C, \]

proving (14.34). $\square$

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory
14.31
\[\begin{aligned}\begin{align} \sup _{\overline B}\operatorname {tr}_\beta (\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)& \leq Cj^ b, \\ \end{align}\end{aligned}\]
From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory
14.32
\[\begin{aligned}\begin{align} \sup _ j\sup _ K\operatorname {tr}_\beta (\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)& <\infty \quad \text{for every }K\Subset \overline B\setminus \mathcal S. \end{align}\end{aligned}\]

14.17.5 Derivative estimates and bounded traces

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Uniform $C^{1,\alpha }$ bounds control averages of the trace $\operatorname {tr}_\beta (\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)=\operatorname {tr}_\beta \vartheta +\Delta _\beta u_ j$ over small balls. To pass from averages to pointwise values we need a bound for the gradient of the trace, that is, a third-order estimate, whose dependence on the ellipticity constants is polynomial. We prove it by Calabi's maximum-principle argument, computed with the Chern connection of the solution so that the torsion of the nonclosed background appears explicitly. The resulting averaging inequality is nonlinear in the trace, and it closes under iteration when $\alpha $ is sufficiently close to one.

In this subsection $B$ is the unit ball and $B_ r(p)$ is the Euclidean ball of radius $r$ centered at $p$. For a Hermitian metric $g$, $\partial g$ denotes the ordinary coordinate derivatives of its coefficient matrix, while tensor derivatives in the proofs are taken with respect to the Chern connection of $g$.

Proposition 14.17.9. Fix smooth $\vartheta $ and smooth $F>0$ near the closed unit ball. There are $C,N<\infty $, depending only on finitely many derivatives of the fixed data, such that any smooth solution

\[ g=\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u, \qquad g^ n=F\beta ^ n, \qquad M^{-1}\beta \leq g\leq M\beta , \qquad M\geq 1, \]

satisfies

14.36
\begin{equation} \sup _{B_{1/2}}|\partial g|_\beta \leq CM^ N. \end{equation}

If only $\sup _ B\operatorname {tr}_\beta g\leq L$ is given, the same bound holds with a fixed power of $1+L$. On a ball of radius $r\leq 1$,

14.37
\begin{equation} \sup _{B_{r/2}}|\nabla \operatorname {tr}_\beta g|_\beta \leq Cr^{-1}(1+L)^ N. \end{equation}

Proof. The quantity $\mathcal R$ below is the squared norm of the difference between the Chern connection of $g$ and the flat connection. We derive a differential inequality for $\mathcal R$ in which the torsion enters through fixed derivatives of $\vartheta $, and combine it with the trace in a maximum principle; every constant is kept a power of $M$.

We compare the flat connection of $\beta $ with the Chern connection $\nabla $ of $g$. Write $g^{k\bar\ell }$ for the coefficients of the inverse matrix and $\bar\nabla $ for covariant differentiation in $(0,1)$ directions; tensor norms and inner products without subscript are computed with $g$. The difference of the two connections and its squared norm are

\[ \Psi ^ k_{ij}=g^{k\bar\ell }\partial _ i g_{j\bar\ell }, \qquad \mathcal R=|\Psi |_ g^2. \]

The torsion of $\nabla $ is determined by the fixed background, because the third derivatives of $u$ cancel in it. Its lowered and raised components are

14.38
\begin{equation} C_{ij\bar\ell } =\partial _ i\vartheta _{j\bar\ell }-\partial _ j\vartheta _{i\bar\ell }, \qquad \mathcal T^ k_{ij}=g^{k\bar\ell }C_{ij\bar\ell }. \end{equation}

Consequently, for a fixed integer $N$,

14.39
\begin{align} |\mathcal T|& \leq CM^ N,\nonumber \\ |\nabla \mathcal T|+|\bar\nabla \mathcal T| & \leq CM^ N(1+\sqrt{\mathcal R}),\\ |\nabla ^2\mathcal T|+|\nabla \bar\nabla \mathcal T|+|\bar\nabla ^2\mathcal T| & \leq CM^ N(1+\mathcal R+|\nabla \Psi |+|\bar\nabla \Psi |).\nonumber \end{align}

Every factor here is either a fixed derivative of $\vartheta $, a connection factor $\Psi $, or a factor of $g^{\pm 1}$; this explains the polynomial dependence.

For tensor fields, use $\Delta _ g=g^{p\bar q}\nabla _ p\nabla _{\bar q}$. We need the following curvature identities. With

\[ \operatorname {Rm}^ k{}_{i\bar j l}=-\partial _{\bar j}\Psi ^ k_{il}, \qquad \operatorname {Rm}_{i\bar j l\bar k}=g_{m\bar k}\operatorname {Rm}^ m{}_{i\bar j l}, \]

set

\[ \operatorname {Ric}^{(2)}_{l\bar k}=g^{i\bar j}\operatorname {Rm}_{i\bar j l\bar k}, \qquad \operatorname {Ric}^{(1)}_{l\bar k}=g^{i\bar j}\operatorname {Rm}_{l\bar k i\bar j} =-\partial _ l\partial _{\bar k}\log F. \]

The two curvature interchanges are

\begin{align*} \operatorname {Rm}_{i\bar j l\bar k}-\operatorname {Rm}_{l\bar j i\bar k} & =-\nabla _{\bar j}C_{il\bar k},\\ \operatorname {Rm}_{i\bar j l\bar k}-\operatorname {Rm}_{i\bar k l\bar j} & =-\nabla _ i\overline{C}_{\bar j\bar k l}. \end{align*}

They follow by differentiating the torsion identity and its conjugate. Successive interchange and contraction give

14.40
\begin{equation} \operatorname {Ric}^{(2)}_{l\bar k} =\operatorname {Ric}^{(1)}_{l\bar k} -g^{i\bar j}\bigl ( \nabla _ l\overline C_{\bar j\bar k i} +\nabla _{\bar j}C_{il\bar k} \bigr ). \end{equation}

Metric compatibility, (14.39), and the fixed smooth formula for $\operatorname {Ric}^{(1)}$ imply

14.41
\begin{equation} |\operatorname {Ric}^{(2)}|\leq CM^ N(1+\sqrt{\mathcal R}), \qquad |\nabla \operatorname {Ric}^{(2)}| \leq CM^ N(1+\mathcal R+|\nabla \Psi |+|\bar\nabla \Psi |). \end{equation}

Moreover,

\[ \nabla _{\bar q}\Psi ^ k_{ij}=-\operatorname {Rm}^ k{}_{i\bar q j}, \]

and the Chern–Bianchi identity, including its torsion term, is

\[ \nabla _ p\operatorname {Rm}^ k{}_{i\bar q j}-\nabla _ i\operatorname {Rm}^ k{}_{p\bar q j} =-\mathcal T^ r_{pi}\operatorname {Rm}^ k{}_{r\bar q j}. \]

Consequently,

14.42
\begin{equation} \Delta _ g\Psi ^ k_{ij} =-\nabla _ i(\operatorname {Ric}^{(2)})^ k{}_ j +g^{p\bar q}\mathcal T^ r_{pi}\operatorname {Rm}^ k{}_{r\bar q j}. \end{equation}

If $A^ k{}_ l=(\operatorname {Ric}^{(2)})^ k{}_ l$, define its action on a tensor of type $(1,2)$ by

\[ (A\mathbin {\cdot }\Psi )^ k_{ij} =A^ k{}_ l\Psi ^ l_{ij}-A^ l{}_ i\Psi ^ k_{lj}-A^ l{}_ j\Psi ^ k_{il}. \]

Differentiating the squared norm and using metric compatibility gives

14.43
\begin{equation} \Delta _ g\mathcal R =|\nabla \Psi |^2+|\bar\nabla \Psi |^2 +2\operatorname {Re}\langle \Delta _ g\Psi ,\Psi \rangle -\operatorname {Re}\langle A\mathbin {\cdot }\Psi ,\Psi \rangle . \end{equation}

There is no mixed torsion term because the Chern torsion has type $(2,0)$. Using (14.39), (14.41), and (14.42) in (14.43), Young's inequality absorbs the products of $\sqrt{\mathcal R}$ with $|\nabla \Psi |+|\bar\nabla \Psi |$. We obtain

14.44
\begin{equation} \Delta _ g\mathcal R\geq -CM^ N(\mathcal R^{3/2}+1). \end{equation}

Let $s=\operatorname {tr}_\beta g$. Direct differentiation as in (14.19) gives

14.45
\begin{equation} \Delta _ gs\geq M^{-1}\mathcal R-CM, \qquad |\partial s|_ g^2\leq nM^2\mathcal R. \end{equation}

Take a cutoff $\zeta $ supported in $B$, equal to one on $B_{1/2}$, set $\eta =\zeta ^2$ and $h=2nM-s$, so $nM\leq h\leq 2nM$, and maximize

\[ Q=\frac{\eta \mathcal R}{h}. \]

At a positive interior maximum of $Q$, the identity $\partial \mathcal R/\mathcal R=\partial h/h-\partial \eta /\eta $ gives

\[ \begin{split} 0\geq \frac{\Delta _ gQ}{Q} & =\frac{\Delta _ g\mathcal R}{\mathcal R} +\frac{\Delta _ g\eta }{\eta }-\frac{\Delta _ gh}{h} -\frac{2|\partial \eta |_ g^2}{\eta ^2} +\frac{2\operatorname {Re}\langle \partial \eta ,\partial h\rangle _ g}{\eta h}. \end{split} \]

We have $|\partial \eta |_ g^2\leq CM\eta $, $|\Delta _ g\eta |\leq CM$ and $|\partial h|_ g\leq \sqrt n M\sqrt{\mathcal R}$. Insert (14.44) and (14.45) and multiply by $\eta Q$. The positive term is $M^{-1}Q^2$. The two terms involving $\sqrt{\mathcal R}$ are bounded in absolute value by a fixed power of $M$ times $Q^{3/2}$, since $\mathcal R=hQ/\eta $ and $h\leq 2nM$. The remaining cutoff terms are bounded by $CM^ N(Q+1)$. Therefore

\[ 0\geq M^{-1}Q^2-CM^ N(Q^{3/2}+Q+1). \]

Thus $Q$, and then $\mathcal R$ on $B_{1/2}$, are bounded by a fixed polynomial in $M$. Since $|\partial g|_\beta ^2\leq M^3\mathcal R$, this proves (14.36).

If $s\leq L$, the determinant lower bound gives $\lambda _{\min }(g)\geq (\inf F)L^{1-n}$, so $M$ is a fixed power of $1+L$. For a ball $B_ r(p)$, set $u_ r(z)=r^{-2}u(p+rz)$ and use the coefficient matrices $\vartheta _ r(z)=\vartheta (p+rz)$, $g_ r(z)=g(p+rz)$ and $F_ r(z)=F(p+rz)$. Then $g_ r=\vartheta _ r+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ r$ in the unit coordinates and $\det g_ r=F_ r$. For $r\leq 1$ the derivatives of the fixed data remain uniformly bounded. The unit-ball estimate for the ordinary coefficient derivatives $\partial g_ r$ and their conjugates gives the factor $r^{-1}$ in (14.37). $\square$

Proposition 14.17.10. There is $\alpha _*<1$, depending only on $n$ and the exponent $N$ in proposition 14.17.9, with the following property. Let $B_0$ be a Euclidean ball, fix a smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\vartheta $ and a smooth function $F>0$ near $\overline{B_0}$, and let $u_ j$ be smooth exact solutions of $(\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n=F\beta ^ n$ with $\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j>0$. If

\[ \sup _ j\lVert u_ j\rVert _{C^{1,\alpha }(B_0)}<\infty \qquad \text{for some }\alpha _*<\alpha <1, \]

then $\operatorname {tr}_\beta (\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)$ is uniformly bounded on every smaller ball.

Proof. Fix a center for concentric balls $B_ r\Subset B_0$, put $s_ j=\operatorname {tr}_\beta (\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)$, and set

\[ M_ j(r)=1+\sup _{B_ r}s_ j. \]

For concentric balls $B_ r\Subset B_ R\Subset B_0$, $d=R-r\leq 1$, proposition 14.17.9 gives

14.47
\begin{equation} |\nabla s_ j|\leq Cd^{-1}M_ j(R)^ N \quad \text{on }B_{r+d/2}. \end{equation}

Fix $p\in B_ r$ and let $\chi _ h$ be a nonnegative mollifier of radius $h\leq d/4$. Since

\[ s_ j=\operatorname {tr}_\beta \vartheta +\Delta _\beta u_ j, \]

integration by parts, after subtracting the constant vector $\nabla u_ j(p)$, and the uniform $C^{1,\alpha }$ bound give

\[ (s_ j*\chi _ h)(p)\leq C(1+h^{\alpha -1}). \]

Equation (14.47) gives

\[ |s_ j(p)-(s_ j*\chi _ h)(p)| \leq Cd^{-1}M_ j(R)^ Nh. \]

Choose

\[ h=\frac d4M_ j(R)^{-N/(2-\alpha )}. \]

Taking the supremum over $p\in B_ r$ and absorbing fixed powers of $d^{-1}$, we obtain

14.48
\begin{equation} M_ j(r)\leq Cd^{-1}M_ j(R)^ q, \qquad q=\frac{N(1-\alpha )}{2-\alpha }. \end{equation}

Choose $\alpha _*$ so close to one that $q<1$ for $\alpha >\alpha _*$. Choose $0<r_0<R_0$ with $B_{R_0}\Subset B_0$. Let $r_ k\uparrow R_0$ with $r_{k+1}-r_ k=(R_0-r_0)2^{-k-1}$. Iterating (14.48) gives

\[ M_ j(r_0) \leq \prod _{k=0}^{m-1}\left[C(r_{k+1}-r_ k)^{-1}\right]^{q^ k} M_ j(r_ m)^{q^ m}. \]

The infinite product converges because $\sum q^ k(1+k)<\infty $. For each fixed $j$, the final factor tends to one as $m\to \infty $. The resulting bound is independent of $j$. $\square$

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory
14.39
\[\begin{aligned}\begin{align} |\nabla \mathcal T|+|\bar\nabla \mathcal T| & \leq CM^ N(1+\sqrt{\mathcal R}),\\ \end{align}\end{aligned}\]

14.17.6 Proof of the main theorem

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now assemble the four steps outlined after theorem 14.17.1. The barrier enters only once, but globally: it locates a point of the nonsmooth set at which the quadratic separation required in proposition 14.17.7 holds.

of theorem 14.17.1. If $n=1$, the equation is the linear identity

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi =cf\, \omega -\theta \quad \text{on }\Omega . \]

Its right-hand side is smooth, so interior elliptic regularity gives $\varphi \in C^\infty (\Omega )$, and the original equation gives $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi =cf\, \omega >0$. We henceforth assume $n\geq 2$.

Let

\[ \mathcal S=\{ p\in \Omega :\varphi \text{ is not smooth on any neighborhood of }p\} . \]

This is closed in $\Omega $. Suppose $\mathcal S\neq \varnothing $. By lemma 1.19.7, the restriction of $\varphi -\rho $ to $\mathcal S$ attains its minimum $m$ at some $p\in \mathcal S$.

Choose nested coordinate balls

\[ B\Subset B_0\Subset \Omega \]

centered at $p$. On $B_0$, proposition 14.17.6 gives smooth exact Dirichlet solutions $u_ j$ converging uniformly to $\varphi $ at a polynomial rate and having polynomial global trace growth.

On the open set $\Omega \setminus \mathcal S$ the function $\varphi $ is smooth, and $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ is semipositive with positive determinant, hence positive definite. Proposition 14.17.8 therefore gives a uniform trace bound for the $u_ j$ on every compact subset of $B_0\setminus \mathcal S$.

In coordinates centered at $p$, take $\kappa >0$ small and set

\[ q(z)=\rho (z)-\kappa |z|^2. \]

After shrinking $B$, the lower bound $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \omega $ gives $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q>0$ on $\overline B$. If $v=\varphi -q$, then the minimizing property of $p$ gives

\[ v(p)=m, \qquad v(z)=(\varphi -\rho )(z)+\kappa |z|^2 \geq m+\kappa |z|^2 \quad (z\in \mathcal S\cap \overline B). \]

Thus proposition 14.17.7 applies on $B$ with the closed set $\mathcal S\cap \overline B$: the approximation rate and global polynomial trace bound come from proposition 14.17.6, and the bound away from $\mathcal S$ comes from proposition 14.17.8. Its second assertion gives, for every $0<\alpha <1$, a neighborhood of $p$ on which the approximants have a uniform $C^{1,\alpha }$ bound.

Choose $\alpha >\alpha _*$ as in proposition 14.17.10, and apply that proposition on a ball centered at $p$ inside this neighborhood. It yields

\[ \sup _ j\operatorname {tr}_\beta (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)<\infty \]

on a still smaller neighborhood of $p$. Since $u_ j\to \varphi $ uniformly, $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j\to \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ as currents. The trace bound passes to the limit, so $\operatorname {tr}_\beta (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )$ has a bounded density near $p$. Proposition 14.17.3 now implies that $\varphi $ is smooth near $p$, contradicting $p\in \mathcal S$.

Hence $\mathcal S=\varnothing $ and $\varphi \in C^\infty (\Omega )$. The form $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ is smooth and semipositive there, while

\[ (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n=cf\, \omega ^ n>0. \]

Its nonnegative eigenvalues have positive product, so every eigenvalue is positive. Therefore $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi >0$ on $\Omega $. $\square$

Corollary 14.17.11. Let $0\leq f\in L^ p(X,\omega ^ n)$, $p>1$, be smooth and strictly positive on $\Omega $. Every minimal-singularity solution of $(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )^ n=cf\, \omega ^ n$, $c>0$, is smooth and strictly $\theta $-plurisubharmonic on $\Omega $.

Proof. The analytic complement of $\Omega $ has zero mass for both measures, so it suffices to know the equation in the bounded-potential sense on $\Omega $. Apply theorem 14.16.1 with density $cf$ to obtain local Hölder continuity. All hypotheses of theorem 14.17.1 are now satisfied. $\square$

By theorem 14.15.4, such a solution exists, with a uniquely determined $c>0$. For a smooth closed big representative on a compact Kähler manifold, theorem 28.10.1 applies this conclusion to the classical full-mass solution and identifies the regular open set with the ample locus.

14.18 Degenerating semipositive backgrounds

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Adding a small multiple of a Hermitian form makes a semipositive background strictly positive, and the smooth theory then solves the normalized equation with a smooth positive density. We study these smooth solutions as the added multiple tends to zero. Bounded subsolutions give uniform bounds for the potentials and the normalizing constants, and a strict analytic barrier gives uniform bounds for the trace on compact sets away from its poles. Smooth local convergence then identifies the limiting equation, without any global continuity property of the Monge–Ampère operator.

The main example is a small resolution of a threefold with nodes (14.18.1). There the background is the pullback of a positive form from the singular threefold, and it degenerates exactly along the exceptional curves. When this background is closed, the limit is unique and the whole family converges smoothly away from the exceptional curves. Convergence of the associated metric spaces then reduces to controlling the diameters of neighborhoods of these curves, in the limiting metric and in the approximating metrics (14.18.3).

Throughout this section $n\geq 2$. The background $\theta $, the barrier $\rho $ and its pole set $Z$ are new data, independent of those fixed in section 14.12. Let $\theta \geq 0$ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form, and fix a Hermitian form $\eta $ and a smooth positive probability volume form $\mu $ on $X$. Suppose that there are $\delta >0$ and $\rho \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with analytic singularities such that

14.49
\begin{equation} \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \delta \eta , \qquad \sup _ X\rho =0. \end{equation}

Write $Z=\{ \rho =-\infty \} $ and assume that $\rho $ is smooth on $X\setminus Z$. For $0<\varepsilon \leq 1$, the form $\theta +\varepsilon \eta $ is Hermitian, and theorem 9.1.1 gives $u_\varepsilon \in C^\infty (X)$ and $c_\varepsilon >0$ with

14.50
\begin{equation} \omega _\varepsilon :=\theta +\varepsilon \eta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\varepsilon >0, \qquad \omega _\varepsilon ^ n=c_\varepsilon \mu , \qquad \sup _ Xu_\varepsilon =0. \end{equation}

Lemma 14.18.1. There are constants $b,B,C>0$, independent of $\varepsilon $, such that

\[ b\leq c_\varepsilon \leq B, \qquad -C\leq u_\varepsilon \leq 0. \]

Proof. Write $\mu =f\, \mathrm{d}V$, where $f$ is smooth and positive. The strict barrier makes $\theta $ big. By lemma 14.2.1, there is a bounded $s\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $-1\leq s\leq 0$ and $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (s)\geq b\mu $ for some fixed $b>0$. If $c_\varepsilon <b$, positivity gives

\[ \mathrm{MA}_{\theta +\varepsilon \eta }(u_\varepsilon ) \leq (c_\varepsilon /b)\mathrm{MA}_{\theta +\varepsilon \eta }(s+A) \]

for every constant $A$. Strict bounded domination would imply $u_\varepsilon \geq s+A$ for every $A$, which is absurd. Hence $c_\varepsilon \geq b$.

For an upper bound, choose a fixed Gauduchon form $\gamma $. The arithmetic–geometric mean inequality and Stokes give

\[ c_\varepsilon ^{1/n} \int _ X\left(\frac{\mu }{\gamma ^ n}\right)^{1/n}\gamma ^ n \leq \int _ X\omega _\varepsilon \wedge \gamma ^{n-1} =\int _ X(\theta +\varepsilon \eta )\wedge \gamma ^{n-1} \leq \int _ X(\theta +\eta )\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}. \]

The first integral is fixed and positive, giving $c_\varepsilon \leq B$.

For the lower bound on $u_\varepsilon $, note that all $u_\varepsilon $ are normalized $(\theta +\eta )$-plurisubharmonic functions. Uniform exponential integrability, lemma 1.5.1, gives $a>0$ such that $g_\varepsilon =\mathrm{e}^{-a u_\varepsilon }f$ has a common $L^2$ bound. Also $g_\varepsilon \geq f$. By theorem 14.2.2, the solutions

\[ w_\varepsilon \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ), \qquad \mathrm{MA}_\theta (w_\varepsilon ) =\mathrm{e}^{a w_\varepsilon }g_\varepsilon \, \mathrm{d}V \]

are uniformly bounded. Put $B_0=\max \{ 1,B\} $ and $z_\varepsilon =w_\varepsilon -a^{-1}\log B_0$. Then

\[ \mathrm{MA}_{\theta +\varepsilon \eta }(z_\varepsilon ) \geq \mathrm{e}^{a z_\varepsilon }c_\varepsilon g_\varepsilon \, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad \mathrm{MA}_{\theta +\varepsilon \eta }(u_\varepsilon ) =\mathrm{e}^{a u_\varepsilon }c_\varepsilon g_\varepsilon \, \mathrm{d}V. \]

The comparison principle for the exponential equation in the background $\theta +\varepsilon \eta $ gives $z_\varepsilon \leq u_\varepsilon \leq 0$, and $z_\varepsilon $ is bounded below uniformly in $\varepsilon $. $\square$

Since $\theta $ may degenerate along $Z$, we do not estimate the trace by local arguments. Instead we apply the maximum principle on all of $X\setminus Z$ to the logarithm of the trace minus a multiple of $u_\varepsilon -\rho $. This function tends to $-\infty $ at $Z$, so its maximum is attained off $Z$, where $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \delta \eta $ provides positivity. An additional logarithmic term in the weight absorbs the torsion term without a gradient bound for $\rho $.

Lemma 14.18.2. For every compact $K\Subset X\setminus Z$, there is $C_ K>1$, independent of $\varepsilon $, such that

\[ C_ K^{-1}\eta \leq \omega _\varepsilon \leq C_ K\eta \quad \hbox{on }K. \]

Proof. For the differentiated calculation write $U=u_\varepsilon /(2\pi )$ and $P=\rho /(2\pi )$, so that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\varepsilon =\mathrm{i}\partial \bar\partial U$. Put

\[ S=\operatorname {tr}_\eta \omega _\varepsilon , \quad R=\operatorname {tr}_{\omega _\varepsilon }\eta , \quad D=\omega _\varepsilon ^ n/\eta ^ n. \]

The previous lemma and the smooth positive density give $0<d_1\leq D\leq d_2$. Choose a fixed $C_0$ so that $v=U-P+C_0>1$, and set

\[ H=\log S-\gamma (v),\qquad \gamma (v)=Av+\log v, \]

where $A$ will be fixed below. For each $\varepsilon $, $S$ is smooth on $X$ and $v$ tends to infinity at $Z$. Thus $H\to -\infty $ there, and $H$ attains its maximum on $X\setminus Z$. We bound this maximum independently of $\varepsilon $.

In local holomorphic coordinates, $h$ and $g$ denote the coefficient matrices of $\eta $ and $\omega _\varepsilon $ with respect to $\mathrm{i}\, \mathrm{d}z_ j\wedge \mathrm{d}\bar z_ k$, and commas denote coordinate derivatives, as in $h_{i\bar j,k}=\partial _ kh_{i\bar j}$ and $h_{i\bar j,\bar k}=\partial _{\bar k}h_{i\bar j}$. At a maximum point choose holomorphic coordinates in which

\[ h_{i\bar j}=\delta _{ij},\qquad h_{i\bar i,k}=0, \qquad g_{i\bar j}=\lambda _ i\delta _{ij}. \]

A linear change first normalizes $h$ and diagonalizes $g$. In these coordinates, the quadratic change $z_ i=w_ i+\frac12\sum _{j,k}a^ i_{jk}w_ jw_ k$, with symmetric coefficients chosen so that $a^ i_{ik}=-h_{i\bar i,k}$, then kills $h_{i\bar i,k}$. Its coefficients are uniformly bounded on a fixed finite cover. Write

\[ \Delta _ g=\sum _ i\lambda _ i^{-1}\partial _ i\partial _{\bar i}, \quad T_{ij\bar k}=g_{j\bar k,i}-g_{i\bar k,j}, \quad E_ i=\sum _ jT_{ij\bar j}. \]

The lowered torsion coefficients $T_{ij\bar k}$ and their first derivatives are uniformly bounded: the potential Hessian cancels, leaving only derivatives of $\theta +\varepsilon \eta $.

Let $\mu _{\mathrm{loc}}$ be the density of $\mu $ with respect to the coordinate volume form. Differentiating $S=h^{k\bar\ell }g_{k\bar\ell }$ and the equation $\log \det g=\log c_\varepsilon +\log \mu _{\mathrm{loc}}$ gives

14.51
\begin{equation} \Delta _ g S\geq Q-CRS-CR, \qquad Q=\sum _{i,j}\frac{|g_{i\bar j,j}|^2}{\lambda _ i\lambda _ j}. \end{equation}

The second derivatives of $g$ contribute $\sum _{i,j,k}|g_{i\bar j,k}|^2/(\lambda _ i\lambda _ j)$, up to $-CR-C$, by commuting the potential derivatives. The first derivatives of $h$ contribute $-2\operatorname {Re}\sum _{i,j,k}\lambda _ i^{-1}h_{j\bar k,\bar i}g_{k\bar j,i}$. Its terms with $j=k$ vanish. Replace $g_{k\bar j,i}$ by $g_{i\bar j,k}+T_{ik\bar j}$ and apply weighted Cauchy–Schwarz. This consumes exactly the terms of the preceding square with $j\ne k$ and costs at most $CRS+CR$. The second derivatives of $h^{-1}$ cost $CRS$. Finally the fixed density term $-C$ is absorbed using $SR\geq n^2$.

Since $\partial _ iS=\sum _ jg_{i\bar j,j}+E_ i$ at the maximum point, weighted Cauchy–Schwarz also gives

\[ |\partial S|_ g^2 \leq SQ+2\operatorname {Re}\langle E,\partial S\rangle _ g. \]

Indeed expand around $\partial S-E$ and discard the resulting term $-|E|_ g^2$. Combining with (14.51) yields

\[ \Delta _ g\log S\geq -CR-CR/S -\frac2S\operatorname {Re}\langle E,\partial \log S\rangle _ g. \]

At the maximum, $\partial \log S=\gamma '\partial v$. Moreover (14.49) gives $\Delta _ gv\leq n-\delta R$, and $|E|_ g^2\leq CR$. Young's inequality with the positive coefficient $-\gamma ''=v^{-2}$ therefore gives

\[ 0\geq \Delta _ g H \geq (\delta \gamma '-C)R-n\gamma '-CR/S -\frac{CR}{S^2}\frac{\gamma '^2}{-\gamma ''}. \]

Since $\gamma '=A+v^{-1}$, choose $A$ with $\delta A-C\geq 1$. The resulting inequality is

14.52
\begin{equation} 0\geq R-C\left(1+\frac RS+\frac{R(1+v^2)}{S^2}\right). \end{equation}

All constants are independent of $\varepsilon $.

If the maxima of $H$ were unbounded, then at a sequence of maximizing points

\[ \log (S/v)=H+Av\longrightarrow +\infty . \]

Thus $S/v\to \infty $ and $S\to \infty $. Inequality (14.52) would imply $R\leq 2C$ along this sequence. But

\[ S=D\sum _ i\prod _{j\ne i}\lambda _ j^{-1} \leq nDR^{n-1}\leq nd_2(2C)^{n-1}, \]

a contradiction. Hence $H$ is uniformly bounded. On $K$, both $U$ and $P$ are bounded, so $S\leq C_ K$. The determinant lower bound then gives $\lambda _ i\geq d_1/C_ K^{n-1}$, completing the proof. $\square$

Theorem 14.18.3. In the preceding setting, $c_\varepsilon $ decreases to a positive number $c_0$ as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$. This number is independent of the perturbing Hermitian form $\eta $. For every sequence $\varepsilon _ j\downarrow 0$, a subsequence satisfies

\[ u_{\varepsilon _ j}\longrightarrow u_0 \quad \hbox{in }L^1(X)\cap C^\infty _{\mathrm{loc}}(X\setminus Z), \]

where $u_0\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\cap L^\infty (X)$, $\sup _ Xu_0=0$, and

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u_0)=c_0\mu . \]

If $\theta $ is closed, then $c_0=\int _ X\theta ^ n$.

Proof. Monotonicity of the constants. Let $\alpha \leq \beta $ be Hermitian forms, and let $(u_\alpha ,c_\alpha )$ and $(u_\beta ,c_\beta )$ be the normalized smooth solutions of $(\alpha +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\alpha )^ n=c_\alpha \mu $ and $(\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\beta )^ n=c_\beta \mu $. At a maximum point of $u_\alpha -u_\beta $,

\[ \alpha +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\alpha \leq \beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\beta , \]

so $c_\alpha \leq c_\beta $. Hence $c_\varepsilon $ decreases as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, and lemma 14.18.1 gives $c_0\geq b>0$. Write $c_\eta (\varepsilon )$ for the constant $c_\varepsilon $ defined with the perturbation $\eta $. For two perturbations with $M^{-1}\eta _1\leq \eta _2\leq M\eta _1$, the same comparison gives

\[ c_{\eta _1}(\varepsilon /M) \leq c_{\eta _2}(\varepsilon ) \leq c_{\eta _1}(M\varepsilon ). \]

Letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ shows that $c_0$ does not depend on $\eta $.

Local estimates. The trace bound of lemma 14.18.2 gives uniform ellipticity on compact subsets of $X\setminus Z$. The smooth coefficients of $\theta +\varepsilon \eta $ and all derivatives of $\log \mu _{\mathrm{loc}}$ have common bounds there. Interior complex Evans–Krylov and Schauder estimates, as in the ellipticity argument of proposition 14.17.3, give uniform $C^ k$ bounds on smaller compact subsets for every $k$.

Convergence and the limiting equation. Normalized compactness in the fixed background $\theta +\eta $, followed by a diagonal extraction over an exhaustion of $X\setminus Z$, gives the stated convergence. The uniform bound $-C\leq u_\varepsilon \leq 0$ passes to the canonical limit, and normalized compactness preserves the supremum zero. Current convergence gives $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_0\geq 0$, and smooth convergence identifies the equation on $X\setminus Z$. The bounded non-pluripolar measure $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u_0)$ and the smooth measure $\mu $ both vanish on the analytic set $Z$. This proves the equation on all of $X$. For closed $\theta $, bounded closed-background Stokes gives $\int _ X\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u_0)=\int _ X\theta ^ n$, proving the last assertion. $\square$

Since $\theta \geq 0$ is nef, theorem 26.10.1 identifies $c_0$ with the Gauduchon variational volume $\mathcal V_\mu (\theta )$. The proof uses no bound on Monge–Ampère masses: all estimates concern the solutions for the fixed smooth density $\mu $. If the bounded normalized limiting equation has a unique solution, all cluster limits coincide, and the whole family converges in $L^1(X)\cap C^\infty _{\mathrm{loc}}(X\setminus Z)$.

14.18.1 Small resolutions of nodes

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The pullback of a positive form from a singular space loses positivity along the contracted curves. In the standard small resolution of a node, the logarithm of the norm in the normal bundle of the exceptional curve provides the missing curvature along the curve. Cutting this logarithm off where the pullback is already positive gives a global strict barrier, so the results above apply.

Assume now $n=3$. Let $Y$ be a connected compact normal complex threefold, smooth outside a finite set $\Sigma $ of nodes. A node is locally the hypersurface

\[ \{ x_1x_4-x_2x_3=0\} \subset \mathbb C^4. \]

Let $\pi :X\to Y$ be a proper holomorphic map that is biholomorphic over $Y\setminus \Sigma $. Assume each local resolution is the standard small resolution: a neighborhood of its exceptional curve is identified with a neighborhood of the zero section of

\[ \mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1}(-1)\oplus \mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1}(-1), \]

and the contraction, in bundle coordinates $(z,y_1,y_2)$, is

14.53
\begin{equation} \pi (z,y_1,y_2)=(y_1,zy_1,y_2,zy_2). \end{equation}

Interchanging the two rulings gives the other standard model. Write $E=\pi ^{-1}(\Sigma )$; its components $E_ j$ are disjoint copies of $\mathbb P^1$.

Fix a positive form $\omega _ Y$ on $Y$ in the local embedding sense: in each ambient chart it is induced by a smooth strictly positive real $(1,1)$-form. Set $\theta =\pi ^*\omega _ Y$, and let $\eta $ be an arbitrary Hermitian form on $X$.

Lemma 14.18.4. The form $\theta $ is smooth and semipositive, positive on $X\setminus E$. There are $\delta >0$ and $\rho \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with analytic poles precisely on $E$, smooth on $X\setminus E$, such that

\[ \sup _ X\rho =0,\qquad \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \delta \eta . \]

Proof. Smoothness and positivity off $E$ follow from the pullback and the biholomorphism. In one standard model put

\[ r^2=(1+|z|^2)(|y_1|^2+|y_2|^2),\qquad b=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log (1+|z|^2). \]

The function $r^2$ is the sum of the squared absolute values of the four contracted coordinates. It is therefore globally defined on the bundle; $b$ is the pullback of the Fubini–Study form from its base. In the chart (14.53),

14.54
\begin{equation} \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log r^2 =b+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log (|y_1|^2+|y_2|^2)\geq b. \end{equation}

This is an inequality of currents across the zero section as well as a smooth inequality off it.

Ambient positivity gives $\theta \geq c\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}r^2$ on a sufficiently small tube, for some $c>0$. The smooth form $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}r^2+b$ is positive definite there. Indeed the contraction is an immersion off the zero section, and on that section $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}r^2$ is positive in both fiber directions while $b$ is positive along the base. Compactness of the zero section and a smaller closed tube give a uniform lower bound by a positive multiple of $\eta $. Thus, for every fixed $a>0$,

\[ \theta +a\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log r^2 \geq c\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}r^2+ab \geq \delta _ a\eta \]

near the zero section, with $\delta _ a>0$.

Choose disjoint model tubes with nonempty complement and numbers $0<t_1<t_2<1$ in each. Let $0\leq \chi \leq 1$ be a smooth cutoff of $r^2$, equal to one for $r^2\leq t_1$ and zero for $r^2\geq t_2$. Extend $\chi (r^2)\log r^2$ by zero outside its tube. On the compact transition annuli, the pullback $\theta $ is uniformly positive and the second derivatives of this cutoff potential are bounded. A sufficiently small positive rational $a$ therefore makes

\[ \rho =a\sum _ j\chi _ j(r_ j^2)\log r_ j^2 \]

satisfy $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \delta \eta $ there and near every $E_ j$. On the remaining compact set, $\rho =0$ and $\theta $ is positive, so decreasing $\delta $ proves the global inequality. Near $E_ j$ the function is $a\log (|y_1|^2+|y_2|^2)+a\log (1+|z|^2)$, hence has analytic singularities with rational coefficient and pole set exactly $E_ j$. It is smooth elsewhere and nonpositive; its zero extension gives supremum zero. If $E$ is empty, take $\rho =0$ and use the positivity of $\theta $ on compact $X$. $\square$

Corollary 14.18.5. For the preceding geometric data and every smooth positive probability volume form $\mu $ on $X$, the solutions of (14.50) have the bounds and subsequential convergence of theorem 14.18.3, with $Z=E$. Moreover, for each exceptional curve,

\[ \int _{E_ j}\omega _\varepsilon =\varepsilon \int _{E_ j}\eta \longrightarrow 0. \]

Proof. Apply the theorem using lemma 14.18.4. Since $\pi $ contracts $E_ j$, the restriction of $\theta $ to it is zero. Stokes on the compact smooth curve gives $\int _{E_ j}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\varepsilon =0$, proving the area identity. $\square$

When $K_ X$ is torsion, the density can be chosen so that these metrics are Chern–Ricci flat. Recall that the Chern–Ricci form $\operatorname {Ric}^{\mathrm{Ch}}(\omega )$ of a Hermitian metric $\omega $ is $-\mathrm{i}\partial \bar\partial $ of the logarithm of the density of $\omega ^ n$ with respect to a coordinate volume form. Choose a nowhere vanishing holomorphic section of $K_ X^{\otimes m}$ for some $m\geq 1$. If its local expression is $f(\mathrm{d}z_1\wedge \mathrm{d}z_2\wedge \mathrm{d}z_3)^{\otimes m}$, use $|f|^{2/m}$ as the density relative to the standard coordinate volume form. The coordinate Jacobians cancel, so this defines a smooth positive volume form on $X$; normalize it to total mass one and take it as $\mu $. Its logarithmic density is pluriharmonic. Applying $-\mathrm{i}\partial \bar\partial \log $ to $\omega _\varepsilon ^3=c_\varepsilon \mu $ therefore gives $\operatorname {Ric}^{\mathrm{Ch}}(\omega _\varepsilon )=0$, and the same holds for the smooth limiting metric on $X\setminus E$.

14.18.2 Measures and bounded potentials at a node

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We keep the standard small resolution $\pi :X\to Y$ and a smooth positive probability volume form $\mu $ on $X$. Write $\mu _ Y=\pi _*\mu $, and let $\nu _ Y$ be $\omega _ Y^3$ on the regular locus, extended by zero at the nodes. The pullback of the ambient volume form $\omega _ Y^3$ vanishes along the exceptional curves, and its order of vanishing determines the integrability of the density of $\mu _ Y$ with respect to $\nu _ Y$.

Lemma 14.18.6. The measures $\mu _ Y$ and $\nu _ Y$ are finite and give the nodes zero mass. There is a positive density $F$ with $\mu _ Y=F\nu _ Y$. If $\Sigma $ is nonempty, then for $1\leq p<\infty $,

\[ F\in L^ p(Y,\nu _ Y)\quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad p<3. \]

At each node, $F$ is comparable to $r^{-2}$, where $r^2=\sum _{j=1}^4|x_ j|^2$ in the local model $\{ x_1x_4-x_2x_3=0\} $ of the node. There are $c,C,T_0>0$ such that

\[ cT^{-3}\leq \nu _ Y(\{ F>T\} )\leq CT^{-3} \qquad (T\geq T_0). \]

Thus the endpoint is weak $L^3$, with $F\notin L^3$. If $\Sigma $ is empty, $F$ is smooth and bounded.

Proof. The map is biholomorphic off $E$, and both smooth measures $\mu $ and $\theta ^3$ give $E$ zero mass. Hence $\nu _ Y=\pi _*\theta ^3$ is finite, $\mu _ Y$ has no node atom, and on the regular locus

\[ F\circ \pi =\mu /\theta ^3. \]

In the chart (14.53), put $h=1+|z|^2$ and $s=|y_1|^2+|y_2|^2$, so $r^2=hs$. The fiber block of the complex Hessian of $r^2$ is $h$ times the identity, and its Schur complement is $s/h$. Consequently

14.55
\begin{equation} \det \bigl ((r^2)_{j\bar k}\bigr )=hs=r^2, \qquad \frac{(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}r^2)^3}{3!} =\frac{r^2}{\pi ^3}\, \mathrm{d}\lambda _6, \end{equation}

where $\lambda _6$ is Euclidean volume in $(z,y_1,y_2)$. Ambient positivity bounds $\theta $ above and below by positive multiples of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}r^2$ on a smaller tube. The smooth density of $\mu $ is bounded above and below there. On compact subcharts covering the base $\mathbb P^1$, it follows that

\[ \theta ^3\asymp r^2\, \mathrm{d}\lambda _6, \qquad F\circ \pi \asymp r^{-2}. \]

The other bundle chart gives the same comparison, since $r$ is the norm of the contracted coordinates.

On each bounded base subchart, $r$ is comparable to the Euclidean radius $R$ in the two complex fiber variables. Up to fixed positive constants, the contribution to $\int F^ p\, \mathrm{d}\nu _ Y$ is therefore

\[ \int _0^{R_0}R^{2-2p}R^3\, \mathrm{d}R =\int _0^{R_0}R^{5-2p}\, \mathrm{d}R. \]

Two compact base subcharts give the upper estimate; a fixed open base subchart gives the lower estimate. The integral is finite precisely when $p<3$, and diverges logarithmically at $p=3$. Away from the finitely many nodes the density is smooth. The same comparisons give $\nu _ Y(\{ r<t\} )\asymp t^6$ near a node. The two bounds for $F$ sandwich $\{ F>T\} $ between such tubes of radii comparable to $T^{-1/2}$, proving the endpoint estimate. $\square$

The descent theorem theorem A.2.1 applies to the normal space $Y$ and its resolution $\pi $, also when the ambient background is not closed. Every bounded $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function is therefore constant on each exceptional curve and is the pullback of a unique bounded $\omega _ Y$-plurisubharmonic function. In particular each cluster limit has the form $u_0=\psi _0\circ \pi $, with $\sup _ Y\psi _0=0$. Let $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_{Y,\omega _ Y}$ be the extended Monge–Ampère measure on $Y$, defined in (A.7) as the pushforward of the extended measure of the pullback to a resolution; it does not depend on the resolution. Then

14.56
\begin{equation} \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_{Y,\omega _ Y}(\psi _0) =c_0\mu _ Y=c_0F\nu _ Y. \end{equation}

The equality holds also at the nodes, since both measures give them zero mass. Thus $\psi _0$ is a bounded solution of an equation on the singular space; nothing is claimed about its continuity at a node.

Theorem 14.18.7. Suppose additionally that $\theta $ is closed. The equation

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=c\mu , \qquad u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\cap L^\infty (X), \qquad \sup _ Xu=0, \]

has a unique pair $(c,u)$ with $c>0$. Its scalar is $c=c_0=\int _ X\theta ^3$. The entire family $u_\varepsilon $ converges to $u$ in $L^1(X)$ and smoothly on compact subsets of $X\setminus E$. The limit is independent of $\eta $, and its descended potential is the unique bounded normalized solution of (14.56).

Proof. Existence follows from corollary 14.18.5. For uniqueness we use an energy identity, which is available because $\theta $ is closed. Fix one cluster limit $u_0$ and put $A=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_0$. This is a positive closed current with bounded local potentials, smooth and strictly positive on $X\setminus E$ by the trace bounds. If $v$ is another bounded solution with scalar $c$, bounded closed-background Stokes gives $c=\int _ X\theta ^3=c_0$. Put $B=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ and $w=v-u_0$. The bounded integration-by-parts calculus of section 1.6 gives

\[ 0=\int _ Xw(B^3-A^3) =-\sum _{j=0}^2\int _ X\mathrm{d}w\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}w \wedge B^ j\wedge A^{2-j}. \]

All three energies are nonnegative, so each of them vanishes, in particular the one with $A^2$. On every relatively compact chart of $X\setminus E$, the smooth positive factor $A^2$ controls the weak gradient of $w$, so $\mathrm{d}w=0$ there. The complement of a proper complex analytic subset in the connected manifold $X$ is connected. Thus $w$ is constant almost everywhere off $E$. Since $E$ has ordinary volume zero, canonical almost-everywhere equality of quasi-plurisubharmonic functions, lemma 1.3.3, gives $v=u_0+C$ on all of $X$. Normalization fixes $C=0$.

The compactness and smooth local estimates of theorem 14.18.3 now make every subsequential limit equal to $u_0$, proving convergence of the whole family. The equation and its unique normalized solution do not depend on $\eta $. Descent and the zero exceptional mass identify bounded solutions upstairs and downstairs, proving the final assertion. $\square$

14.18.3 Distances across finitely many contracting regions

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

On the small resolutions above, the potential limit for a closed background gives smooth convergence of the metrics away from the exceptional curves. Global distances, however, are also affected by paths through neighborhoods of these curves, so their convergence requires control of the diameters of such neighborhoods. The criterion of proposition 14.18.11 separates the two controls: uniform convergence on compact subsets of the regular part, and small diameters of neighborhoods of the contracted sets, in the limiting metric and uniformly in the approximating metrics. We first recall the Gromov–Hausdorff distance.

Definition 14.18.8. For nonempty subsets $E,F$ of a metric space $(W,d_ W)$, their Hausdorff distance is

\[ d_{\mathrm H}^ W(E,F)=\max \left\{ \sup _{x\in E}\inf _{y\in F}d_ W(x,y), \sup _{y\in F}\inf _{x\in E}d_ W(x,y)\right\} . \]

For nonempty bounded metric spaces $E,F$, put

\[ d_{\mathrm{GH}}(E,F) =\inf _{W,\iota ,\jmath }d_{\mathrm H}^ W(\iota (E),\jmath (F)), \]

where $\iota ,\jmath $ are isometric embeddings into a common metric space $W$. A correspondence is a subset $R\subset E\times F$ whose two coordinate projections are surjective. Its distortion is

\[ \operatorname {dis}R =\sup _{(x,y),(x',y')\in R} |d_ E(x,x')-d_ F(y,y')|. \]

Lemma 14.18.9. For any correspondence $R$ between nonempty bounded metric spaces,

\[ \frac12|\operatorname {diam}E-\operatorname {diam}F| \leq d_{\mathrm{GH}}(E,F) \leq \frac12\operatorname {dis}R. \]

Proof. Choose $\varepsilon >0$ with $2\varepsilon \geq \operatorname {dis}R$. Give the disjoint union $E\sqcup F$ its original distances within each space, and use the symmetric cross distance

\[ D(x,y)=\inf _{(x_0,y_0)\in R} \bigl (d_ E(x,x_0)+\varepsilon +d_ F(y_0,y)\bigr ), \qquad x\in E,\ y\in F. \]

It is finite and at least $\varepsilon $. The triangle inequalities involving one cross distance follow from the triangle inequality within the corresponding space and the infimum. For two cross distances, take $(x_0,y_0),(x_1,y_1)\in R$. The distortion bound gives

\[ \begin{split} d_ E(x,x') & \leq d_ E(x,x_0)+d_ F(y_0,y_1) +2\varepsilon +d_ E(x_1,x')\\ & \leq d_ E(x,x_0)+d_ F(y_0,y)+\varepsilon +\varepsilon +d_ F(y,y_1)+d_ E(x_1,x'). \end{split} \]

Taking the two infima gives $d_ E(x,x')\leq D(x,y)+D(y,x')$. The same argument with $E,F$ exchanged treats the other case. Hence $D$ is a metric extending both metrics. Each point has a correspondent at cross distance $\varepsilon $, so the Hausdorff distance between $E$ and $F$ in $(E\sqcup F,D)$ is at most $\varepsilon $. Letting $\varepsilon $ decrease to half the distortion proves the upper bound, including zero distortion.

For the lower bound, suppose two isometric images have Hausdorff distance $h$. For every $\delta >0$, two points in the first image have points in the second within $h+\delta $. Their distance is at most $\operatorname {diam}F+2h+2\delta $. Taking the supremum and then $\delta \downarrow 0$ gives $\operatorname {diam}E\leq \operatorname {diam}F+2h$. Exchanging the images and taking the infimum over embeddings proves the lower bound. $\square$

We now work in a purely metric setting, which contains the nodal one. Let $Y$ be a compact metrizable space and let $S=\{ s_1,\ldots ,s_ N\} \subset Y$ be finite. Assume $Y^\circ =Y\setminus S$ is a dense connected smooth manifold. Let $g_0$ be a smooth Riemannian metric on $Y^\circ $, and denote its intrinsic length distance by $d_0$.

Lemma 14.18.10. Suppose every $s_ a$ has a neighborhood basis of open sets $V_{a,r}$ such that

\[ \lim _{r\downarrow 0}\operatorname {diam}_{d_0}(V_{a,r}\setminus S)=0. \]

Then the metric completion $(\widehat Y,\widehat d_0)$ of $(Y^\circ ,d_0)$ is compact, and the identity on $Y^\circ $ extends to a homeomorphism $\iota :Y\to \widehat Y$.

Proof. The neighborhoods can be chosen nested, with closures avoiding the other points of $S$. Every sequence of regular points converging to $s_ a$ is $d_0$-Cauchy: its tail lies in each prescribed neighborhood, whose punctured diameter tends to zero. Interleaving two such sequences shows that they define the same completion point, denoted by $\widehat s_ a$. Density of $Y^\circ $ guarantees the existence of these sequences.

For each prescribed positive diameter, remove sufficiently small neighborhoods of all the points of $S$. Their complement is a compact subset of $Y^\circ $ and hence is totally bounded for $d_0$. The removed punctured neighborhoods each have the prescribed small diameter. Thus $Y^\circ $ is totally bounded, and its completion is compact.

The points $\widehat s_ a$ are distinct from one another and from the regular points. To see this, fix $s_ a$ and a different point $y\in Y$. Choose an open neighborhood $V$ of $s_ a$ whose closure avoids $y$ and $S\setminus \{ s_ a\} $, and choose $W\Subset V$ containing $s_ a$. There is a smooth function $\chi $ on $Y^\circ $, equal to one on $W\setminus S$ and zero outside $V$, whose derivative has compact support in $Y^\circ $. Indeed the transition takes place in the compact regular set $\overline V\setminus W$, where smooth cutoffs exist. Therefore $|\mathrm{d}\chi |_{g_0}\leq C$ for some finite $C$. Integration along curves gives

\[ |\chi (p)-\chi (q)|\leq C d_0(p,q),\qquad p,q\in Y^\circ . \]

The Lipschitz extension of $\chi $ to the completion has value one at $\widehat s_ a$ and zero at the regular point $y$, or at $\widehat s_ b$ if $y=s_ b$. This proves the assertion.

Define $\iota $ by the identity on the regular locus and by $\iota (s_ a)=\widehat s_ a$. The intrinsic distance induces the smooth topology at each regular point. At $s_ a$, adjoining $\widehat s_ a$ to a punctured neighborhood does not increase its diameter, so the diameter hypothesis proves continuity of $\iota $. Every completion point is represented by a Cauchy sequence in $Y^\circ $. Compact metrizability of $Y$ gives a subsequence converging topologically either to a regular point or to some $s_ a$; the preceding arguments give its metric limit. Hence $\iota $ is surjective as well as injective. A continuous bijection from compact $Y$ to Hausdorff $\widehat Y$ is a homeomorphism. $\square$

Let $X$ be a compact connected smooth manifold and let $\pi :X\to Y$ be a continuous surjection, with $E=\pi ^{-1}(S)$ and with $\pi :X\setminus E\to Y^\circ $ a diffeomorphism. Assume that the identity of $Y^\circ $ extends to a homeomorphism $\iota :Y\to \widehat Y$, for instance under the hypothesis of lemma 14.18.10, and write $G=\iota \circ \pi $. For a smooth Riemannian metric $g$ on $X$ and an open subset $U$, let $\operatorname {diam}^{\mathrm{int}}(U,g)$ denote the intrinsic diameter of $U$, computed with the lengths of piecewise smooth curves contained in $U$.

Proposition 14.18.11. Let $g_ j$ be smooth Riemannian metrics on $X$ such that $g_ j\to \pi ^*g_0$ uniformly on every compact subset of $X\setminus E$. Suppose each $s_ a$ has a nested neighborhood basis $V_{a,r}$, with $U_{a,r}=\pi ^{-1}(V_{a,r})$, satisfying

\[ \lim _{r\downarrow 0}\operatorname {diam}_{\widehat d_0}(\iota (V_{a,r}))=0, \qquad \lim _{r\downarrow 0}\limsup _{j\to \infty } \operatorname {diam}^{\mathrm{int}}(U_{a,r},g_ j)=0. \]

Then, for the global length distances $d_ j$ of $g_ j$,

\[ \lim _{j\to \infty }\sup _{x,x'\in X} |d_ j(x,x')-\widehat d_0(G(x),G(x'))|=0. \]

Consequently $(X,d_ j)$ converges to $(\widehat Y,\widehat d_0)$ in Gromov–Hausdorff distance.

Proof. If $S$ is empty, uniform metric comparison on compact $X$ proves the distance convergence. Assume $N\geq 1$. Fix $\tau >0$. Choose pairwise disjoint neighborhoods $V_ a$ from the specified bases with

\[ \operatorname {diam}_{\widehat d_0}(\iota (V_ a))<\tau , \qquad \operatorname {diam}^{\mathrm{int}}(U_ a,g_ j)<\tau \]

for all sufficiently large $j$, where $U_ a=\pi ^{-1}(V_ a)$. Pick $q_ a\in V_ a\setminus S$, and then choose open neighborhoods $W_ a$ of $s_ a$ with $\overline W_ a\subset V_ a$ and $q_ a\notin \overline W_ a$. The regular cores

\[ K_ Y=Y\setminus \bigcup _ a W_ a, \qquad K_ X=\pi ^{-1}(K_ Y) \]

are compact and identified by $\pi $. Uniform convergence and positivity of $g_0$ on this core give numbers $A_ j\geq 1$, with $A_ j\to 1$, such that lengths of curves contained in the cores compare in both directions by the factor $A_ j$.

Consider a piecewise smooth path in $Y^\circ $ joining two points of $K_ Y$. The preimages of the closed sets $\overline W_ a$ are compact subsets of its parameter interval. At its first visit to any such set, delete the portion from that visit through its last visit to the same set. Resume with the remaining tail. That set is never visited again; thus there are at most $N$ deleted portions. Each retained segment lies in $K_ Y$, and each deleted portion has endpoints in a single $\overline W_ a\subset V_ a$. The lifts of those endpoints can be joined in $U_ a$ with $g_ j$-length less than $\tau $, up to an arbitrarily small error. Applying this procedure to almost minimizing paths, comparing retained lengths, and letting the errors tend to zero gives, for $x,x'\in K_ X$,

\[ d_ j(x,x')\leq A_ j\widehat d_0(G(x),G(x'))+N\tau . \]

Conversely start with an almost minimizing $g_ j$-path in $X$ and apply the same deletion procedure to the closed sets $\pi ^{-1}(\overline W_ a)$. Project its retained segments, which avoid $E$, to $K_ Y$. The two images of each deleted portion have completion distance less than $\tau $. The triangle inequality in $\widehat Y$ therefore gives

\[ \widehat d_0(G(x),G(x'))\leq A_ j d_ j(x,x')+N\tau , \qquad x,x'\in K_ X. \]

For any $x\notin K_ X$ with $\pi (x)\in W_ a$, replace $x$ by $p=\pi ^{-1}(q_ a)\in U_ a\cap K_ X$. Then $d_ j(x,p)<\tau $ and $\widehat d_0(G(x),G(p))<\tau $. Leave points of $K_ X$ fixed. Replacing both endpoints extends the two inequalities to all $x,x'\in X$, with the same error

\[ C_ j=(N+2+2A_ j)\tau . \]

If $D=\operatorname {diam}(\widehat Y,\widehat d_0)$, the first inequality gives $\operatorname {diam}(X,d_ j)\leq A_ jD+C_ j$. The positive and negative distance errors are consequently bounded by

\[ (A_ j-1)D+C_ j, \qquad (A_ j-1)(A_ jD+C_ j)+C_ j, \]

respectively. Their upper limits are at most $(N+4)\tau $. Letting $\tau \downarrow 0$ proves uniform convergence.

The graph of $G$ is a correspondence between $X$ and $\widehat Y$, and its distortion $b_ j$ for the distances $d_ j$ and $\widehat d_0$ is the supremum in the statement. By lemma 14.18.9,

\[ d_{\mathrm{GH}}((X,d_ j),(\widehat Y,\widehat d_0))\leq b_ j/2\longrightarrow 0. \]
$\square$

For the standard nodal small resolutions of 14.18.1 with closed $\theta $, the smooth convergence of the whole family off $E$ in theorem 14.18.7 gives the uniform convergence on compact subsets of $X\setminus E$ required in proposition 14.18.11. What remains are the two conditions on the diameters of neighborhoods. The next estimates control the approximating neighborhoods by metric bounds along and normal to the exceptional curves.

Curve areas and radial tube lengths

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The approximating metrics give each exceptional curve an area tending to zero, by corollary 14.18.5. Along the curve, small area forces small diameter as long as the metrics are bounded above; in the normal directions, an integrable bound for the speed of radial rays has the same effect.

Let $C$ be a connected compact Riemann surface. Fix a smooth Hermitian metric $h$ of area one on $C$, and use the associated Riemannian metrics for lengths and areas.

Lemma 14.18.12. There is $K_ h>0$ such that every smooth Hermitian metric $g\leq M h$, with $M>0$ and $A=\operatorname {Area}_ g(C)$, satisfies

\[ \operatorname {diam}(C,g) \leq K_ h\sqrt{A\bigl (1+\log (M/A)\bigr )}. \]

Here $0<A\leq M$. In particular, for a fixed upper bound $M$, area tending to zero implies diameter tending to zero.

Proof. Replace $g$ by $g/M$. Its area is $a=A/M\leq 1$, and it is bounded above by $h$. We prove the estimate for this normalized metric.

Choose a finite cover by holomorphic coordinate disks $U_ i$ represented by $\{ |z|<1\} $, with each coordinate defined on the larger disk $\{ |z|<2\} $. Write $g=\rho _ i|\mathrm{d}z|^2$ there. There is a common $L>0$ such that $0<\rho _ i\leq L$ on all the closed unit disks, and

\[ \int _{U_ i}\rho _ i\, \mathrm{d}\lambda _2\leq a, \]

where $\lambda _2$ is Euclidean area. For $p,z\in U_ i$, let $\ell _ p(z)$ be the $g$-length of their straight coordinate segment, which stays in $U_ i$. Polar coordinates about $p$ and Fubini give

\[ \int _{U_ i}\ell _ p(z)\, \mathrm{d}\lambda _2(z) \leq 2\int _{U_ i}\frac{\sqrt{\rho _ i(w)}}{|w-p|} \, \mathrm{d}\lambda _2(w). \]

Indeed each ray meets the convex unit disk in an interval of length $R\leq 2$. Interchanging the radial integrals multiplies the length integrand at radius $s$ by $(R^2-s^2)/2\leq 2$.

For $0<a\leq 1/4$, put $t=\sqrt a$. The integral on the right, without its factor two, is bounded by

\[ \begin{split} \sqrt L\int _{\{ |w-p|<t\} }\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda _2(w)}{|w-p|} & +\left(\int _{U_ i}\rho _ i\, \mathrm{d}\lambda _2\right)^{1/2} \left(\int _{\{ t<|w-p|<2\} }\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda _2(w)}{|w-p|^2}\right)^{1/2}\\ & \leq 2\pi \sqrt L\, t+\sqrt{2\pi a\log (2/t)} \leq K\sqrt{a\bigl (1+\log (1/a)\bigr )}. \end{split} \]

Thus for any $p,q\in U_ i$, averaging $\ell _ p(z)+\ell _ q(z)$ over $z\in U_ i$ gives a two-segment path of length at most $K\sqrt{a(1+\log (1/a))}$. The constant is common to the finite cover.

The overlap graph of this cover is connected because $C$ is connected. Choose points in consecutive overlaps along a simple path in this finite graph. The local estimates join any two points of $C$ through at most the number of covering disks, proving the global bound for $a\leq 1/4$. For $1/4\leq a\leq 1$, the comparison $g\leq h$ gives $\operatorname {diam}(C,g)\leq \operatorname {diam}(C,h)$, and increasing $K_ h$ gives the same estimate. Restoring the factor $M$ scales lengths by $\sqrt M$ and proves the assertion. $\square$

Example 14.18.13. On every such $(C,h)$ there are smooth Hermitian metrics $g_\varepsilon \leq h$, for $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, such that

\[ \begin{aligned} A_\varepsilon :=\operatorname {Area}_{g_\varepsilon }(C) & \asymp \varepsilon ^2\log (1/\varepsilon ),\\ \operatorname {diam}(C,g_\varepsilon ) & \asymp \varepsilon \log (1/\varepsilon ). \end{aligned} \]

Consequently the preceding diameter bound has sharp order under its uniform upper bound. Without that bound, area can tend to zero while diameter stays bounded below by a positive constant.

Proof. Choose a coordinate disk $\{ |z|<R\} $ with $0<R<1$, on whose closure $h=\lambda (z)|\mathrm{d}z|^2$ with $\lambda >0$ smooth. Choose a smooth cutoff $0\leq \chi \leq 1$, supported in this disk and equal to one for $|z|\leq R/2$, and a constant $c>0$ with $c\leq \min \lambda $. For sufficiently small $\varepsilon >0$, set

\[ g_\varepsilon = \left(\chi \frac{c\varepsilon ^2}{\lambda (z)(\varepsilon ^2+|z|^2)} +(1-\chi )\varepsilon ^2\right)h \]

in the disk, and set $g_\varepsilon =\varepsilon ^2h$ outside it. The cutoff makes this a smooth positive metric, and both coefficients in its convex combination are at most one.

On $|z|\leq R/2$ its metric density is $c\varepsilon ^2/(\varepsilon ^2+|z|^2)$, whose area is

\[ \pi c\varepsilon ^2 \log \left(1+\frac{R^2}{4\varepsilon ^2}\right). \]

The remaining area is $O(\varepsilon ^2)$, proving the asserted area comparison. Any path from the origin to a point outside this smaller disk must first cross its boundary. Radial variation is bounded by Euclidean speed, so that initial portion has length at least

\[ \sqrt c\, \varepsilon \int _0^{R/2} \frac{\mathrm{d}r}{\sqrt{\varepsilon ^2+r^2}} =\sqrt c\, \varepsilon \operatorname {arsinh}(R/(2\varepsilon )) \asymp \varepsilon \log (1/\varepsilon ). \]

This gives the diameter lower bound. The upper bound follows from lemma 14.18.12, since $A_\varepsilon (1+\log (1/A_\varepsilon ))\asymp \varepsilon ^2\log ^2(1/\varepsilon )$. Finally rescale by

\[ \widetilde g_\varepsilon =\frac{g_\varepsilon }{\varepsilon ^2\log ^2(1/\varepsilon )}. \]

Its area is comparable to $1/\log (1/\varepsilon )$ and tends to zero, whereas its diameter is comparable to one. At the origin its ratio to $h$ tends to infinity, so it has no uniform upper bound. $\square$

Let $V\to C$ be a holomorphic vector bundle of positive rank with a fixed smooth Hermitian fiber norm. Write $U_\delta =\{ v\in V:|v|<\delta \} $ and identify its zero section with $C$.

Proposition 14.18.14. Let $g_ j$ be smooth Hermitian metrics on $U_{\delta _0}$. Suppose their restrictions to $C$ satisfy

\[ g_ j|_ C\leq M h,\qquad A_ j=\operatorname {Area}_{g_ j|_ C}(C)\longrightarrow 0 \]

for a fixed $M>0$. Suppose also that there are $B>0$ and $0<\alpha \leq 1$ such that, for every unit fiber vector $v$ over any $x\in C$, the ray $\gamma _{x,v}(s)=sv$ satisfies

\[ g_ j(\gamma _{x,v}'(s),\gamma _{x,v}'(s)) \leq B s^{2\alpha -2}, \qquad 0<s<\delta _0, \]

uniformly in $j,x,v$. Then

\[ \operatorname {diam}^{\mathrm{int}}(U_\delta ,g_ j) \leq \frac{2\sqrt B}{\alpha }\delta ^\alpha +K_ h\sqrt{A_ j\bigl (1+\log (M/A_ j)\bigr )}, \qquad 0<\delta <\delta _0. \]

In particular,

\[ \lim _{\delta \downarrow 0}\limsup _{j\to \infty } \operatorname {diam}^{\mathrm{int}}(U_\delta ,g_ j)=0. \]

Proof. A point of fiber radius $r<\delta $ joins its base point on the zero section along the radial segment, entirely inside $U_\delta $. Its length is at most

\[ \sqrt B\int _0^ r s^{\alpha -1}\, \mathrm{d}s =\frac{\sqrt B}{\alpha }r^\alpha . \]

The metric is smooth at the zero section for each $j$; the integrable uniform bound controls the whole segment. Join the two base points by an almost minimizing path in $(C,g_ j|_ C)$. This path also lies in $U_\delta $, and up to an arbitrarily small excess its length is bounded by lemma 14.18.12. Together with the two radial segments, this gives the asserted bound for the intrinsic diameter. Since $A_ j(1+\log (M/A_ j))\to 0$, taking $j\to \infty $ and then $\delta \downarrow 0$ proves the final assertion. $\square$

Return to the standard nodal small resolutions of 14.18.1. On a nodal tube put $r^2=\sum _{k=1}^4|x_ k\circ \pi |^2$, the squared bundle norm used in lemma 14.18.4. Let $V_\delta \subset Y$ be the nodal coordinate ball $\sum _ k|x_ k|^2<\delta ^2$. For sufficiently small $\delta $, properness gives $\pi ^{-1}(V_\delta )=\{ r<\delta \} $. Write $g_\varepsilon $ for the Riemannian metric associated to $\omega _\varepsilon $. Its area on $E_ a$ is $\varepsilon \int _{E_ a}\eta $ by corollary 14.18.5. Fix an area-one Hermitian metric $h_ a$ on $E_ a$. If $g_\varepsilon |_{E_ a}\leq M h_ a$ uniformly and the radial bound of proposition 14.18.14 holds with $\alpha =1/2$, substitution in that proposition gives

\[ \operatorname {diam}^{\mathrm{int}}(\{ r<\delta \} ,g_\varepsilon ) \leq K\delta ^{1/2} +K\sqrt{\varepsilon \bigl (1+\log (1/\varepsilon )\bigr )} \]

for sufficiently small $\varepsilon $ and $\delta $, with $K$ independent of both. Under these two pointwise metric bounds, the condition on approximating neighborhoods in proposition 14.18.11 therefore holds. The condition on punctured neighborhoods in the limiting metric does not follow from it, as the next example shows.

In the following surface example the volume form is fixed and the approximating tubes shrink, but the punctured neighborhoods of the contracted point keep a positive diameter in the limiting metric.

Example 14.18.15. There are a compact complex surface $X$, a holomorphic map $\pi :X\to Y=\mathbb P^2$ contracting a curve $E$ to a point $p$, and smooth Hermitian metrics $g_ j,g_*$ on $X$ with the following properties. The map is biholomorphic from $X\setminus E$ to $Y\setminus \{ p\} $, and $g_ j=g_*$ eventually on each compact subset of $X\setminus E$. Their associated Hermitian forms satisfy

\[ \omega _ j^2=\omega _*^2. \]

The curve areas tend to zero, and the metrics have a uniform integrable radial bound near $E$. For a neighborhood basis $V_\delta $ of $p$, with $U_\delta =\pi ^{-1}(V_\delta )$,

\[ \lim _{\delta \downarrow 0}\limsup _{j\to \infty } \operatorname {diam}^{\mathrm{int}}(U_\delta ,g_ j)=0. \]

Nevertheless, for the intrinsic distance $d_0$ of $g_0=(\pi ^{-1})^*g_*$ on $Y\setminus \{ p\} $, there is $c>0$ such that

\[ \operatorname {diam}_{d_0}(V_\delta \setminus \{ p\} )\geq c \]

for every sufficiently small $\delta $.

Proof. Put $p=[1:0:0]$ and take the incidence surface

\[ \begin{gathered} X=\bigl \{ ([z_0:z_1:z_2],[u:v])\in \mathbb P^2\times \mathbb P^1: z_1v=z_2u\bigr \} ,\\ \pi ([z],[u:v])=[z]. \end{gathered} \]

Off $p$, the second coordinate is $[z_1:z_2]$, so $\pi $ is biholomorphic there. Its exceptional curve is $E=\{ p\} \times \mathbb P^1$. In the affine chart $z_0=1$, a chart of $X$ is

\[ (t,w)\longmapsto ([1:w:tw],[1:t]). \]

The other chart is $([1:t'w':w'],[t':1])$, with $t'=1/t$ and $w'=tw$ on their overlap. These are the charts of the tautological line bundle $L=\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1}(-1)$. They also show smoothness along $E$; smoothness elsewhere follows from the biholomorphism. Thus $X$ is a compact smooth complex surface. Its connected dense complement $X\setminus E$ makes it connected.

Let $q:L\to \mathbb P^1$ be the bundle projection. Give $L$ the norm induced by its tautological inclusion into $\mathbb P^1\times \mathbb C^2$. In the first chart its squared norm is

\[ r^2=(1+|t|^2)|w|^2=|z_1|^2+|z_2|^2. \]

Fix an area-one Hermitian metric $h$ on $\mathbb P^1$. The Chern connection of the fiber norm defines a smooth semipositive Hermitian tensor $k$ on $L$ by the squared norm of the covariant fiber differential. Explicitly, with $\lambda =1+|t|^2$,

\[ k=\lambda \left|\mathrm{d}w+w\partial \log \lambda \right|^2. \]

Under $t'=1/t$, $w'=tw$, the differential in this formula multiplies by $t$ and $\lambda $ divides by $|t|^2$, so the tensor is globally defined. It has complex rank one, is positive on nonzero vertical tangent vectors, and restricts to zero on $TE$. Consequently $q^*h+k$ is a smooth positive Hermitian metric on $L$.

Choose $0<\delta _0<1$ and extend this metric from $U_{\delta _0}=\{ r<\delta _0\} $ to a smooth Hermitian metric $g_*$ on $X$. Such an extension follows by a smooth cutoff supported in a slightly larger bundle tube and a convex combination with any global Hermitian metric. It agrees exactly with $q^*h+k$ on $U_{\delta _0}$.

Choose $0<\varepsilon _ j<1$ tending to zero, with $2\varepsilon _ j^2<\delta _0$, and a smooth function $0\leq \chi \leq 1$ on $[0,\infty )$, equal to zero on $[0,1]$ and to one on $[4,\infty )$. Set

\[ \begin{aligned} f_ j(r)& =\varepsilon _ j^2+(1-\varepsilon _ j^2) \chi (r^2/\varepsilon _ j^4),\\ g_ j& =f_ jq^*h+f_ j^{-1}k, \end{aligned} \]

on $U_{\delta _0}$, and set $g_ j=g_*$ elsewhere. The function $f_ j$ is smooth at $E$, is at least $\varepsilon _ j^2$, and equals one for $r\geq 2\varepsilon _ j^2$. Hence the metrics glue smoothly and are positive. They equal $g_*$ eventually on every compact subset of $X\setminus E$.

Let $\beta ,\kappa $ be the Hermitian forms of $q^*h,k$. Their complex rank one gives $\beta ^2=\kappa ^2=0$. On the tube,

\[ (f_ j\beta +f_ j^{-1}\kappa )^2 =2\beta \wedge \kappa =(\beta +\kappa )^2. \]

Outside it the metrics coincide. This proves the global volume identity. On $E$, the restriction is $g_ j|_ E=\varepsilon _ j^2h$, so its area is $\varepsilon _ j^2$.

For any unit fiber vector $a$, a radial ray $\gamma (s)=sa$ has $k$-squared speed one and zero base component. Thus its $g_ j$-squared speed is $f_ j(s)^{-1}$. If $s<2\varepsilon _ j^2$, this is at most $\varepsilon _ j^{-2}\leq 2/s$; otherwise it is one, still at most $2/s$ for $s<\delta _0$. This is the uniform integrable radial bound with exponent $1/2$.

A radial segment of radius $r<\delta $ has length at most $r+2\varepsilon _ j$: its portion below $2\varepsilon _ j^2$ has speed at most $\varepsilon _ j^{-1}$ and length at most $2\varepsilon _ j$, and its remaining speed is one. Connect two points by their radial segments and an almost minimizing path on $E$. All three segments stay inside $U_\delta $, giving

\[ \operatorname {diam}^{\mathrm{int}}(U_\delta ,g_ j) \leq 2\delta +\varepsilon _ j\bigl (4+\operatorname {diam}(\mathbb P^1,h)\bigr ). \]

Taking $j\to \infty $ before $\delta \downarrow 0$ proves the asserted shrinking condition. The affine balls

\[ V_\delta =\{ [1:z_1:z_2]:|z_1|^2+|z_2|^2<\delta ^2\} \]

have precisely these tubes as their preimages.

Let $d_*$ be the global distance of the fixed smooth metric $g_*$. Choose distinct points $a,b\in E$, and put $c=d_*(a,b)>0$. Choose points $a_ r,b_ r$ of fiber norm $r>0$ over these base points. They tend to $a,b$ as $r\downarrow 0$. Every path in $Y\setminus \{ p\} $ lifts to a path in $X\setminus E$ with the same $g_0$-length as its $g_*$-length. Therefore

\[ d_0(\pi (a_ r),\pi (b_ r))\geq d_*(a_ r,b_ r)\longrightarrow c. \]
