For any fixed $\delta >0$, these two images lie in $V_\delta $ when $r<\delta $. Taking the supremum and then $r\downarrow 0$ gives its punctured diameter at least $c$. $\square$

14.19 Symmetry and the selected projective limit

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Let $\omega $ be a Hermitian form and $0\leq f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$, $p>1$, a nonzero density. When the normalized equation $\mathrm{MA}_\omega (u)=cf\, \mathrm{d}V$ has several solutions, the positive-exponent equations $\mathrm{MA}_\omega (u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}f\, \mathrm{d}V$ still have unique solutions, and their normalized limits as $\lambda \downarrow 0$ single out particular solutions of the normalized equation. We study this selection in the projective example of theorem 11.6.1, whose density vanishes on an open band. The starting point is a general observation: uniqueness at positive exponent preserves the symmetries of the data, and uniform compactness carries them to every normalized cluster limit. For the projective data this already gives a solution distinct from the two smooth solutions exchanged by the involution. The radial form of the equation then identifies the selected limit (theorem 14.19.3) and shows that the positive-exponent solutions fail to be $C^2$ along the same hypersurfaces (proposition 14.19.5).

Proposition 14.19.1. Let $\omega $ be a Hermitian form on $X$, let $\mathrm{d}V$ be a smooth positive volume form, and let $0\leq f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$, $p>1$, be nonzero. Let $c>0$ be the unique scalar for the bounded equation $\mathrm{MA}_\omega (u)=cf\, \mathrm{d}V$ (theorem 10.1.1). For $\lambda >0$ denote by $u_\lambda $ the unique bounded solution of

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\omega (u_\lambda )=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_\lambda }f\, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad a_\lambda =u_\lambda -\sup _ Xu_\lambda . \]

The family $\{ a_\lambda :0<\lambda \leq 1\} $ is relatively compact in $C(X)$. Every cluster limit as $\lambda \downarrow 0$ is a normalized bounded solution of $\mathrm{MA}_\omega (a)=cf\, \mathrm{d}V$.

Suppose a group $G$ of biholomorphisms of $X$ preserves $\omega $ and the measure $f\, \mathrm{d}V$. Then every $a_\lambda $ and every such cluster limit is $G$-invariant. In particular, the zero-exponent equation has a $G$-invariant normalized solution.

Proof. The barriers of proposition 10.6.1 give a uniform oscillation bound for $u_\lambda $ and uniform convergence $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_\lambda }\to c$. Thus the equation densities are uniformly bounded in $L^ p$ for $0<\lambda \leq 1$ and tend to $cf$ in $L^ p$ as $\lambda \downarrow 0$. Apply theorem 10.3.4 to obtain relative compactness of $a_\lambda $ in $C(X)$ and the asserted equation for every cluster limit.

For each $g\in G$, pullback of the positive-exponent equation shows that $u_\lambda \circ g$ solves the same equation. Its uniqueness gives $u_\lambda \circ g=u_\lambda $. Surjectivity of $g$ preserves the supremum, so $a_\lambda \circ g=a_\lambda $. Uniform convergence preserves these equalities for every $g$. Compactness gives at least one cluster limit as $\lambda \downarrow 0$, yielding the invariant solution. $\square$

Corollary 14.19.2. On $\mathbb P(\mathbb C^2\oplus \mathbb C^2)$, with block homogeneous coordinates $[A:B]$, the data in theorem 11.6.1 can be chosen so that the same zero-exponent equation has at least three distinct normalized bounded solutions. Two are smooth, and a third is Hölder continuous and fixed by the involution $\iota [A:B]=[B:A]$.

Proof. Use the radial coordinate $r$, rank-one forms $P,Q,R$, Fubini–Study form $\omega _ X$, and scalar functions $F,x,y,z$ from 11.6.1, 11.6.2. Set $w=F(r)$, $\omega _0=xP+yQ+zR$, $H=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w/2$, $A_0=\omega _0-H$ and $B_0=\omega _0+H$ as in 11.6.3. The Hermitian form is $\omega =\omega _0+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho $, with $\rho =\varepsilon (r^2-1)\kappa (r)$ from 11.6.4. Choose the cutoff $\kappa $ even; all its required support and plateau properties are preserved by this choice. Then $\rho $ is even, while $w=F(r)$ is odd. The involution sends $r$ to $-r$, interchanges $P,Q$, and fixes $R$. The coefficient identities $x(-r)=y(r)$ and $z(-r)=z(r)$ show that $\iota ^*\omega _0=\omega _0$. Thus $\iota ^*\omega =\omega $, and $\iota ^*\omega _ X=\omega _ X$.

Since $\iota ^*H=-H$, the involution interchanges $A_0$ and $B_0$. Their cubes are equal, so $f\omega _ X^3$ is invariant. It also interchanges $\widetilde u=-w/2-\rho $ and $\widetilde v=w/2-\rho $. These functions therefore have the same supremum, and the normalized solutions $u,v$ are interchanged. They are distinct, so neither is invariant.

Apply proposition 14.19.1 to the group generated by $\iota $. Here $c=1$, as the two constructed solutions already have that scalar. An invariant cluster solution $a$ cannot equal either $u$ or $v$. Its bounded equation with smooth density gives Hölder regularity by theorem 14.4.1. This gives a third solution. Its regularity is determined in section 14.19.1, section 14.19.2: it is $C^{1,1}$ but not $C^2$. $\square$

To identify the whole normalized limit for these symmetric data, we now use the radial form of the equation. It determines the invariant solution even among bounded potentials whose differentiability is not known in advance.

14.19.1 The invariant projective solution

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We keep the symmetric projective data of corollary 14.19.2. Thus $X=\mathbb P^3$ with block coordinates $[A:B]$ and Fubini–Study form $\omega _ X$; on $U=\{ A\ne 0,\ B\ne 0\} $ we use the radial coordinate $r$, the rank-one forms $P,Q,R$ and the coefficients $x,y,z,p,z_0$ of 11.6.1, 11.6.2. The function $\rho (r)$ is the even correction of 11.6.4, not a barrier, and $\omega =\omega _0+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho $ is the resulting Hermitian form, with $\omega _0=xP+yQ+zR$; the density $f$ is that of theorem 11.6.1. Let $K$ be the group generated by the block unitary group $U(2)\times U(2)$ and the exchange $\iota [A:B]=[B:A]$. Both preserve $\omega $ and $f\omega _ X^3$. Denote by $u_*$ the normalized $\iota $-invariant solution of proposition 11.6.2, given by the symmetric transition pair $(-t_*,t_*)$.

Theorem 14.19.3. For the symmetric projective data, $u_*$ is the unique normalized bounded $K$-invariant solution of

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\omega (u)=f\omega _ X^3. \]

If $u_\lambda $ is the unique solution of

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\omega (u_\lambda ) =\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_\lambda }f\omega _ X^3,\qquad \lambda >0, \]

then

\[ u_\lambda -\sup _ Xu_\lambda \longrightarrow u_* \quad \hbox{uniformly as }\lambda \downarrow 0. \]

The selected limit $u_*$ is $C^{1,1}$.

Uniqueness holds only within the $K$-invariant class: by proposition 11.6.2, the same equation has uncountably many normalized solutions. Since a cluster limit is a priori only bounded, the proof needs the radial form of the equation for bounded invariant solutions, which we derive first. For a real scalar $s$, define the coefficient polynomial $A(r,s)$ and its primitive $\Phi (r,s)$ by

\[ A(r,s)=(x(r)+s)(y(r)-s),\qquad \Phi (r,s)=\int _0^ s A(r,t)\, \mathrm{d}t. \]

Partial derivatives of $\Phi $ with respect to $r$ below keep $s$ fixed.

Lemma 14.19.4. Suppose $u$ is a bounded block-unitary-invariant solution of the zero-exponent equation. On $U$ write $u+\rho =G(r)$. Then $G$ is locally Lipschitz, its derivative $h=G'$ has locally bounded variation, and its one-sided representatives satisfy

\[ -x(r)\leq h(r\pm )\leq y(r),\qquad \mathrm{d}h+z\, \mathrm{d}r\geq 0. \]

In fact $h$ is continuous. With

\[ k(r)=\begin{cases} 0,& |r|\leq 1,\\ x(r)y(r)z(r),& |r|>1,\end{cases} \]

it satisfies the distributional identity on $\mathbb R$

14.67
\begin{equation} \mathrm{d}\bigl (\Phi (r,h(r))\bigr ) =\bigl (k(r)-A(r,h(r))z(r) +\partial _ r\Phi (r,h(r))\bigr )\, \mathrm{d}r. \end{equation}

On every open interval where $A(r,h(r))>0$, $h$ is smooth and

\[ h'=\frac{k(r)}{A(r,h)}-z. \]

Proof. Reduction to one variable. Block-unitary orbits in $U$ are exactly the level sets of $r$: normalize the sum of the squared norms of the two coordinate blocks, and map the two vectors of the same respective lengths by their unitary groups. Thus invariance gives a function $G$ of one real variable.

On a compact $r$-interval, the radial direction annihilates $P,Q$, while $R$ is strictly positive in that direction. Testing positivity of $\omega _0+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}G(r)$ in this direction gives $G''+z\geq 0$ as a one-dimensional distribution. One may obtain this first by smooth approximation of the scalar distribution and then by testing with nonnegative functions. Adding a twice integrated primitive of $z$ makes $G$ convex. A finite bounded convex function on an interval is locally Lipschitz, and its derivative is locally of bounded variation. The quasi-plurisubharmonic representative is the corresponding continuous representative. The horizontal directions give $x+h\geq 0$, $y-h\geq 0$ almost everywhere. Continuity of $x,y$ and the one-sided limits of a function of bounded variation give these bounds for both one-sided representatives. The radial inequality is precisely $\mathrm{d}h+z\, \mathrm{d}r\geq 0$; in particular jumps of $h$ can only be upward.

The radial form of the measure. We next justify the formula for the measure, including possible jumps. Identify $U$ smoothly with $\mathbb R\times Y$, where $Y=\{ r=0\} $, by real scaling of one block. The forms $P,Q$ are independent of $r$ in this identification. So is $\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}r$: in local coordinates it is the angular differential of the relative complex scale plus fixed base terms. Therefore

\[ P\wedge Q\wedge R=\mathrm{d}r\wedge \nu , \]

where $\nu $ is a fixed smooth positive volume form on the compact orbit $Y$, with the orientation chosen by this equality.

For smooth $G$, the rank-one wedge formula gives the radial coefficient of its Monge–Ampère measure, after dividing by $6\nu $, as

\[ A(r,h)(z+h')\, \mathrm{d}r =A(r,h)z\, \mathrm{d}r+\mathrm{d}\bigl (\Phi (r,h)\bigr ) -\partial _ r\Phi (r,h)\, \mathrm{d}r. \]

This identity passes to a locally Lipschitz $G$ with the preceding lower Hessian bound. Indeed, convolve $G$ on a slightly larger compact interval. The derivatives $h_\epsilon $ are uniformly bounded and converge to $h$ almost everywhere and in every finite $L^ q$ space. Their second derivatives have a uniform lower bound. Consequently the functions $G_\epsilon (r)$ have a common quasi-plurisubharmonic lower Hessian bound on compact subsets of $U$, and converge uniformly to $G(r)$. A fixed smooth local correction and bounded Bedford–Taylor continuity give convergence of their signed products. On the right-hand side of the formula, the two ordinary terms converge in $L^1$, and $\Phi (r,h_\epsilon )\to \Phi (r,h)$ in $L^1$, giving distributional convergence of its derivative. This proves the same formula for the bounded product of $G$.

Continuity of $h$. After the same division by $6\nu $, the right-hand side $f\omega _ X^3$ has radial coefficient $k(r)\, \mathrm{d}r$. This gives (14.67), initially for the almost-everywhere representative of $h$. If $h$ jumped at $r_0$, the atom of its left side would be

\[ \Phi (r_0,h(r_0+))-\Phi (r_0,h(r_0-)) =\int _{h(r_0-)}^{h(r_0+)}(x(r_0)+s)(y(r_0)-s)\, \mathrm{d}s. \]

The right side has no atom. An upward jump of positive size lies in $[-x(r_0),y(r_0)]$, where the integrand is strictly positive in the interior. Since $x+y>0$ everywhere, such an integral is strictly positive. Thus there are no jumps. A locally bounded-variation function whose jumps all vanish has a continuous representative here: its distributional derivative consists of an absolutely continuous measure and a nonnegative singular measure, so its one-sided representatives coincide at every point. We henceforth use this representative. In particular $G$ is $C^1$ and $G'=h$ everywhere.

All coefficients on the right of (14.67) are now continuous, so $\Phi (r,h(r))$ is $C^1$. Where $\partial _ s\Phi =A>0$, the implicit function theorem gives $h\in C^1$ and the stated ordinary differential equation. Smoothness then follows by the smooth coefficients of that equation. The function $k$ is smooth, since the original density and its flat joins are smooth. $\square$

of theorem 14.19.3. We first show that a normalized bounded $K$-invariant solution $u$ coincides with $u_*$, by computing its slope $h$ on the band $|r|\leq 1$ and outside it. Apply lemma 14.19.4. Because $\rho $ and $u$ are invariant under block exchange, $G$ is even, so $h$ is odd and $h(0)=0$.

The slope on the band. On $0<r<1$ we have $x=p>0$ and $y=p+b\geq p>0$. Starting from zero, as long as $-p<h<y$, the equation is $h'=-z_0$. Thus $h(r)=-\int _0^ r z_0(s)\, \mathrm{d}s$. This decreasing function cannot meet the positive upper boundary $y$. Its first meeting with the lower boundary $-p$ occurs at the unique $t_*\in (0,1)$ satisfying

\[ \int _0^{t_*}z_0(s)\, \mathrm{d}s=p(t_*), \]

whose existence and uniqueness were proved in proposition 11.6.2.

After that meeting, $h$ must stay on $-p$ until $r=1$. To prove it, suppose a connected component $(a_0,b_0)$ of $\{ h+p>0\} $ lies to its right. Its left endpoint has $a_0\geq t_*$ and $h(a_0)=-p(a_0)<0$. Near that endpoint, $h<0<y$; hence on the component the interior equation applies for as long as $h<0$. It forces $h'=-z_0<0$, so $h$ cannot first reach zero. The equation therefore holds throughout the component. But then

\[ (h+p)'=p'-z_0<0, \]

contradicting positivity of $h+p$ immediately to the right of its zero at $a_0$. Thus $h=-p$ on $[t_*,1]$. Oddness determines the entire negative half of the band. The slope on $[-1,1]$ is exactly that of the symmetric explicit solution.

The slope outside the band. Here boundedness of $u$ at the projective ends matters. On $r>1$, $k=xyz>0$. Neither horizontal coefficient can vanish at a finite point of this interval. For the lower one define

\[ \Psi (r)=\int _{-x(r)}^{h(r)}A(r,s)\, \mathrm{d}s\geq 0. \]

Equation (14.67) and the smooth lower endpoint show that $\Psi $ is $C^1$. If $h(r_0)=-x(r_0)$, its derivative at that point is $k(r_0)>0$: the $Az$ term vanishes, and the two partial-$r$ terms agree at the common endpoint. This contradicts that the nonnegative $C^1$ function $\Psi $ has an interior minimum zero there. The upper-endpoint integral $\int _{h(r)}^{y(r)}A(r,s)\, \mathrm{d}s$ gives the same contradiction, with derivative $-k(r_0)$, when $h=y$. Thus $-x<h<y$ everywhere on $r>1$, and the smooth ordinary differential equation of the lemma applies.

For $r\geq 2$ the background is Fubini–Study:

\[ x=\sigma ,\qquad y=1-\sigma ,\qquad z=\sigma ', \qquad \sigma =\frac{\mathrm{e}^ r}{1+\mathrm{e}^ r}. \]

Set $a=\sigma +h\in (0,1)$. Its equation is

\[ a(1-a)a'=\sigma (1-\sigma )\sigma '>0. \]

Hence $a$ is increasing and has a limit in $(0,1]$ at $+\infty $. If that limit were less than one, $h=a-\sigma $ would approach a negative constant and its primitive $G$ would be unbounded below, contrary to boundedness of $u$ and $\rho $. The limit is therefore one. Integrating the equation gives

\[ \frac{a^2}{2}-\frac{a^3}{3} =\frac{\sigma ^2}{2}-\frac{\sigma ^3}{3}+C. \]

Taking the limit at infinity gives $C=0$. The scalar function $s^2/2-s^3/3$ is strictly increasing on $(0,1)$, so $a=\sigma $ and $h=0$ for $r\geq 2$. Ordinary uniqueness propagates this zero solution throughout $r>1$, since the coefficients are smooth and the two horizontal coefficients stay positive there. Oddness gives $h=0$ on $r<-1$ as well.

We have proved that $G'$ agrees everywhere with the symmetric explicit slope. Thus $u$ differs from $u_*$ by a constant on $U$. The two quasi-plurisubharmonic functions then agree up to that constant on $X$ by their canonical representatives. Their supremum normalization removes the constant. This proves uniqueness within the bounded $K$-invariant class.

Convergence. Finally proposition 14.19.1 gives uniform precompactness of the normalized positive-exponent family. Every cluster limit solves the zero-exponent equation and is $K$-invariant, because the data are $K$-invariant. The scalar is one for these data. The uniqueness just proved identifies every cluster limit with $u_*$. If convergence of the whole family failed, a sequence staying a fixed positive uniform distance from $u_*$ would have a convergent subsequence, contradicting that identification. This proves uniform convergence and the asserted $C^{1,1}$ regularity of the limit. $\square$

14.19.2 Second derivatives at positive exponent

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

On the band where the density vanishes, the exponential factor plays no role, and the radial equation determines the slope of the solution independently of the exponent. Consequently the positive-exponent solutions have the same failure of second differentiability as the selected limit $u_*$. We keep the notation $A$, $\Phi $, $k$ and the transition radius $t_*$ of section 14.19.1.

Proposition 14.19.5. For the symmetric projective data of theorem 14.19.3, let $u_\lambda $ solve

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\omega (u_\lambda ) =\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_\lambda }f\omega _ X^3, \qquad \lambda >0. \]

On the open band $\{ -1<r<1\} $, $u_\lambda -u_*$ is constant. Both potentials are $C^{1,1}$ there and fail to be $C^2$ at every point of the two hypersurfaces $r=\pm t_*$. In particular, none of these positive-exponent solutions is a globally $C^2$ potential.

Proof. Uniqueness and invariance of the data imply that $u_\lambda $ is $K$-invariant. Write $u_\lambda +\rho =G_\lambda (r)$ on $U$. The positivity argument in lemma 14.19.4 uses only boundedness and invariance, so $G_\lambda $ is locally Lipschitz and $h_\lambda =G_\lambda '$ is locally of bounded variation, with

\[ -x\leq h_\lambda (r\pm )\leq y, \qquad \mathrm{d}h_\lambda +z\, \mathrm{d}r\geq 0. \]

Its weak flux equation is the same one, with the prescribed coefficient replaced by

\[ k_\lambda (r)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda (G_\lambda (r)-\rho (r))}k(r). \]

Indeed, the scalar convolution and signed-product argument of that lemma is unchanged, and $k_\lambda $ is exactly the radial coefficient of the right-hand measure. Since $G_\lambda $ is bounded on compact $r$-intervals, $k_\lambda $ is locally bounded. Hence the right side of the flux equation has no atoms. The same strictly positive jump integral eliminates every jump of $h_\lambda $. Thus $h_\lambda $ is continuous and $G_\lambda $ is $C^1$. The coefficient $k_\lambda $ is consequently continuous. Where $A(r,h_\lambda )>0$, inversion of the $C^1$ flux gives

\[ h_\lambda '=\frac{k_\lambda (r)}{A(r,h_\lambda )}-z. \]

On $-1<r<1$ the coefficient $k_\lambda $ is identically zero. Exchange invariance gives $h_\lambda (0)=0$ and oddness. On the positive half-band, the first-contact and no-departure arguments in the proof of theorem 14.19.3 apply verbatim: the first branch solves $h'=-z_0$, reaches $-p$ at $t_*$, and cannot leave that lower boundary to its right because $(h+p)'=p'-z_0<0$ on every proposed departure interval. Therefore

\[ h_\lambda (r)= \begin{cases} p(r),& -1<r\leq -t_*,\\ -\displaystyle \int _0^ r z_0(s)\, \mathrm{d}s,& -t_*\leq r\leq t_*,\\ -p(r),& t_*\leq r<1. \end{cases} \]

This is the derivative of $u_*+\rho $. Its primitive differs from $G_\lambda $ by one constant on the connected band. The claimed local $C^{1,1}$ regularity follows from the explicit slope.

At $t_*\in (0,1)$ the two one-sided derivatives of this slope are

\[ h_\lambda '(t_*-)=-z_0(t_*)<0, \qquad h_\lambda '(t_*+)=-p'(t_*)>0. \]

These unequal derivatives prevent the scalar primitive from having a second derivative at $t_*$. Through every point of that level set, the submersion $r$ provides a smooth curve parametrized by $r$, transferring the failure to $u_\lambda $. Smooth $\rho $ cannot cancel the jump. Oddness gives the conclusion at $-t_*$, and the identical slope proves it for $u_*$. $\square$

The zero set of $f$ contains the whole band. This example therefore does not concern the smooth strictly positive densities in the Hermitian Calabi–Yau theorem. It does show why smoothness of a nonnegative density alone is insufficient for second-order regularity, even when the equation includes a strictly increasing exponential factor.

14.20 Local density and boundary control

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Theorem 14.17.1 requires a smooth positive density on the whole regular locus of a global strict barrier, and the global minimum of the difference between the solution and the barrier drives its proof. If the density is smooth and positive only on a smaller open set, smoothness can fail there. A bounded solution with minimal singularities can be discontinuous on a ball where its density is constant (theorem 14.20.4), and a locally Lipschitz plurisubharmonic function with constant density need not be $C^1$ (example 14.20.5). Boundary data give a different route to regularity: if the solution has smooth boundary values on small balls, uniqueness for the bounded Dirichlet problem identifies it with the smooth Dirichlet solution.

In this section $\theta $ is a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $X$, and $\rho \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfies $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \varepsilon \omega $ for some $\varepsilon >0$; in particular $\theta $ is big. We fix an open set $\Omega \subset X$ on which $\rho $ is smooth and $\mathrm{d}\theta =0$.

14.20.1 Smooth boundary traces

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Let $B\Subset \Omega $ be a coordinate ball. A function $u$ on $B$ has boundary trace $g$, a function on $\partial B$, if $u(z)\to g(\xi )$ as $B\ni z\to \xi $, for every $\xi \in \partial B$.

Theorem 14.20.1. Suppose that $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ has minimal ordinary singularities and

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=f\, \mathrm{d}V\quad \text{on }\Omega , \qquad f\in C^\infty (\Omega ),\quad f>0. \]

Assume that $\Omega $ is covered by coordinate Euclidean balls $B_ j\Subset \Omega $ such that $\theta $ has a smooth local potential near $\overline{B_ j}$ and $u$ has a smooth boundary trace on $\partial B_ j$. Then $u\in C^\infty (\Omega )$ and $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>0$ there.

Proof. Since $\rho -\sup _ X\rho \leq V_\theta $, minimal singularities give $u\geq \rho -C$ for a constant $C$. Thus $u$ is locally bounded on $\Omega $. Fix one of the balls, write it as $B=B_ R(z_0)$, let $g$ be the boundary trace of $u$ on $\partial B$, and choose a smooth function $h$ near $\overline B$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h=\theta $. By proposition 1.7.1, the bounded plurisubharmonic function $v=u+h$ has Bedford–Taylor measure $f\, \mathrm{d}V$ and smooth boundary trace $\varphi =g+h|_{\partial B}$.

For a smooth function $V$ and the Euclidean volume $\mathrm{d}\Lambda $,

14.71
\begin{equation} (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V)^ n=\frac{n!}{\pi ^ n}\det (V_{j\bar k})\, \mathrm{d}\Lambda . \end{equation}

The smooth Dirichlet theorem recalled in section 14.17.3, with zero background and density $f\, \mathrm{d}V$, gives a smooth strictly plurisubharmonic $V$ on $\overline B$ with $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V)^ n=f\, \mathrm{d}V$ and boundary value $\varphi $; a smooth extension $\Phi $ of $\varphi $ gives the required strict subsolution $\Phi +A(|z-z_0|^2-R^2)$ for large $A$. In dimension one this is the Dirichlet problem for the Poisson equation.

The difference $v-V$ tends to zero at every boundary point. Compactness of $\partial B$ makes this convergence uniform near the boundary: otherwise a sequence approaching the boundary with difference bounded away from zero would have a subsequence approaching one fixed boundary point. Uniqueness for the bounded Dirichlet problem, that is, the Bedford–Taylor comparison principle, gives $v=V$. Therefore $u=V-h$ is smooth on $B$, and $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V>0$ there. Since the balls cover $\Omega $, the theorem follows. $\square$

The proof uses minimal singularities only to obtain the local lower bound by $\rho $. Consequently the same conclusion holds for any solution already known to be locally bounded on $\Omega $ and satisfying the same trace condition. Closedness of $\theta $ on $\Omega $ provides the local potentials $h$ once the coordinate balls are small enough; the background may be nonclosed elsewhere.

Example 14.20.2. For every $n\geq 1$, the trace criterion applies to a bounded continuous solution whose global density belongs to $L^1$ but to no $L^ p$ with $p>1$, and which is not Hölder continuous at the points of $X\setminus \Omega $.

Let $E=\mathbb C/(\mathbb Z+\mathrm{i}\mathbb Z)$, $X=E^ n$, and

\[ \omega _ j=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\pi }\, \mathrm{d}z_ j\wedge \mathrm{d}\bar z_ j, \qquad \theta =\sum _{j=1}^ n\omega _ j, \qquad D=\{ 0\} \times E^{n-1}. \]

Take $\mathrm{d}V=\theta ^ n$ and $\rho =0$. Choose a nonnegative smooth cutoff $\chi $ supported in a coordinate disk $|z|<r_0<1$ of $E$ and equal to one near zero. For $\beta >0$, define

\[ \psi (z)=\chi (z)(-\log |z|^2)^{-\beta }\quad (z\ne 0), \qquad \psi (0)=0, \]

and extend by zero. On the smaller punctured disk put $t=|z|^2$ and $L=-\log t$. Differentiation gives

14.72
\begin{equation} \psi _{z\bar z}=\frac{\beta (\beta +1)}{tL^{\beta +2}}, \qquad t\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}L^{-\beta } =\beta L^{-\beta -1}\longrightarrow 0. \end{equation}

The first expression is integrable and the second is the radial flux. Integration by parts on punctured disks, using also $\psi \to 0$, shows that the distributional Hessian has no atom at zero. On the cutoff annulus its negative part is bounded. Hence, for a sufficiently small fixed $\eta >0$, $u=\eta \psi (z_1)$ satisfies $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq \theta /2$.

Only one coordinate contributes to $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$. Locally approximate the bounded subharmonic potential in that coordinate from above and use theorem 1.6.1; the smooth diagonal determinant identity passes to the limit and yields

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=f\theta ^ n, \qquad f=1+\eta \psi _{z\bar z}(z_1). \]

Give $f$ any positive value on the null set $D$. It is smooth and positive on $\Omega =X\setminus D$, where $u$ is smooth and has smooth traces on all relatively compact coordinate balls. Near $D$ its singular term is comparable to $|z_1|^{-2}(-\log |z_1|^2)^{-\beta -2}$. Its radial $L^1$ integral converges, whereas

\[ \int _0^{r_0}s^{1-2p}(-\log s^2)^{-p(\beta +2)}\, \mathrm{d}s=+\infty \qquad (p>1). \]

Also $(-\log |z|^2)^{-\beta }/|z|^\alpha \to +\infty $ for every $\alpha >0$, so $u$ is not Hölder at $D$. Finally, boundedness gives minimal ordinary singularities, since every global $\theta $-plurisubharmonic competitor is bounded above.

14.20.2 A bounded discontinuous potential

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We construct a bounded plurisubharmonic function on a bidisk whose Monge–Ampère measure is a constant multiple of Lebesgue measure, but which is discontinuous. The construction places logarithmic poles with summable weights at points accumulating at the origin; the resulting weight $b$ vanishes at the poles but not at their limit. It is the local model for theorem 14.20.4.

Let $U=W=\{ |z|<1/4\} \subset \mathbb C$, with coordinates $z$ and $w$ on the two factors. Write $\mathrm{d}A$ for planar Lebesgue measure and set

\[ a_ k=\alpha _ k=2^{-k-4},\qquad A=\sum _{k\geq 1}\alpha _ k=\frac1{16},\qquad S=\{ 0\} \cup \{ a_ k:k\geq 1\} . \]

Define on $U$

14.73
\begin{equation} h(z)=\sum _{k\geq 1}\alpha _ k\log |z-a_ k|^2, \qquad b(z)=\mathrm{e}^{h(z)}, \end{equation}

with $b=0$ where $h=-\infty $.

Lemma 14.20.3. The function

\[ a(z)=\frac1\pi \int _ U\log |z-\zeta |^2\, b(\zeta )^{-1}\, \mathrm{d}A(\zeta ) \]

is bounded and continuous on $U$. The bounded function

\[ P(z,w)=a(z)+b(z)|w|^2 \]

is plurisubharmonic on $U\times W$, is smooth off $S\times W$, and satisfies

14.74
\begin{equation} (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}P)^2=\frac2{\pi ^2}\, \mathrm{d}A(z)\, \mathrm{d}A(w). \end{equation}

It is discontinuous at every $(0,w_0)$ with $w_0\ne 0$.

Proof. The summands defining $h$ are negative on $U$, so their partial sums decrease. Since $h(0)=2\sum _ k\alpha _ k\log a_ k> -\infty $, the limit is subharmonic. On compact subsets of $U\setminus S$, the series and its derivatives converge uniformly, making $h$ harmonic there. Moreover $0\leq b\leq 1$, $b(a_ k)=0$, and $b(0)>0$.

Weighted arithmetic–geometric mean gives, almost everywhere,

14.75
\begin{equation} b(z)^{-2}\leq \sum _{k\geq 1}\frac{\alpha _ k}{A}|z-a_ k|^{-4A}. \end{equation}

For the countable inequality, first use the first $N$ weights and assign the remaining weight to the factor $1$; then let $N$ increase. Since $4A=1/4<2$, the integrals of the terms $|z-a_ k|^{-4A}$ on $U$ are bounded uniformly in $k$. Thus $b^{-1}\in L^2(U)$.

The logarithmic kernel has uniformly bounded $L^2$ norm on the translates of $U$ that occur in the definition of $a$. Cauchy–Schwarz bounds $a$ uniformly. Choose a compactly supported $L^2(\mathbb C)$ kernel agreeing with $\log |z|^2$ on $U-U$. Translation continuity in $L^2$, paired with $b^{-1}\mathbf1_ U\in L^2$, proves continuity of $a$. The distribution identity $\partial _ z\partial _{\bar z}\log |z|^2=\pi \delta _0$ gives $a_{z\bar z}=b^{-1}$. In particular $a$ is subharmonic and, by local elliptic regularity, smooth off $S$.

The second summand of $P$ is $\exp (h(z)+\log |w|^2)$, a convex increasing composition of a plurisubharmonic function, extended by zero at its poles. Thus $P$ is bounded and plurisubharmonic. At smooth points the harmonicity of $h=\log b$ gives $bb_{z\bar z}=|b_ z|^2$, and hence

\[ \det (P_{j\bar k}) =ba_{z\bar z}+|w|^2\bigl (bb_{z\bar z}-|b_ z|^2\bigr )=1. \]

The set $S\times W$ is contained in the pole set of $h(z)+\log |z|$, a plurisubharmonic function not identically $-\infty $. It is also Lebesgue-null. Pluripolar nullity in theorem 1.6.1 and (14.71) prove (14.74) throughout the bidisk. Finally,

\[ P(a_ k,w_0)=a(a_ k)\longrightarrow a(0) <a(0)+b(0)|w_0|^2=P(0,w_0) \]

when $w_0\ne 0$. $\square$

14.20.3 Compact gluing

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now glue the local example into a compact torus by a regularized maximum with a quadratic function. The result is a bounded solution with minimal singularities whose density is constant on a ball, where the solution is discontinuous; in the transition region the density acquires singularities that destroy every $L^ p$ bound with $p>1$.

We work on the flat complex torus $X=(\mathbb C/(\mathbb Z+\mathrm{i}\mathbb Z))^2$, identifying a small coordinate ball with a ball in $U\times W$. For a smooth function $v$ on the ball write $H_ v=(v_{j\bar k})$ for its complex Hessian and $I$ for the two-dimensional identity matrix.

Theorem 14.20.4. There are a positive flat Kähler form $\theta $ on $X$, a bounded $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with minimal ordinary singularities, and a density $F>0$ almost everywhere such that

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=F\theta ^2,\qquad F\in L^1(X,\theta ^2),\qquad F\notin L^ p(X,\theta ^2)\quad (p>1). \]

On an open coordinate ball $\Omega $, $F$ is a positive constant and $u$ is discontinuous. The constant function $\rho =0$ is a smooth strict barrier on $X$.

Proof. Set $R=1/8$ and choose $M$ so that $P_0=P+M$ satisfies $0\leq P_0\leq L$ on $B_ R\Subset U\times W$ for some finite $L$. Choose $C>0$ with

14.76
\begin{equation} \frac{3CR^2}{16}\geq 2,\qquad \frac{5CR^2}{16}>L+2, \end{equation}

and put $Q(Z)=C(|Z|^2-R^2/4)$ for $Z=(z,w)$. Let $\chi $ be the convolution of $|t|$ with an even nonnegative smooth probability kernel supported in $(-1,1)$. Then $\chi $ is smooth and convex, $|\chi '|\leq 1$, $\chi '(0)=0$, and $\chi (t)=|t|$ for $|t|\geq 1$. The function

\[ m(s,t)=\frac{s+t+\chi (s-t)}2,\qquad v=m(P_0,Q) \]

is bounded and plurisubharmonic, because $m$ is convex and nondecreasing in each variable. The collar inequalities imply

\[ v=P_0\quad \text{on }B_{R/4},\qquad v=Q\quad \text{when }3R/4\leq |Z|<R. \]

Indeed, $Q\leq -2\leq P_0-2$ on the first ball and $Q>L+2\geq P_0+2$ on the collar. Define

\[ \theta =\frac{\mathrm{i}C}{2\pi } (\mathrm{d}z\wedge \mathrm{d}\bar z+\mathrm{d}w\wedge \mathrm{d}\bar w), \qquad u=\begin{cases} v-Q& \text{on }B_ R,\\ 0& \text{outside }B_ R.\end{cases} \]

The collar identity makes $u$ identically zero near $\partial B_ R$ and gives a global bounded potential with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ on the ball and $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=\theta $ outside. Thus $u$ is admissible and has minimal ordinary singularities.

Outside the pluripolar set $(S\times W)\cap B_ R$, set $\lambda =(1+\chi '(P_0-Q))/2$. Differentiating the regularized maximum gives

14.79
\begin{equation} H_ v=\lambda H_ P+(1-\lambda )CI +\frac{\chi ''(P_0-Q)}2 \partial (P_0-Q)\otimes \overline{\partial (P_0-Q)}. \end{equation}

Both $H_ P$ and $CI$ are positive definite there; the final term is positive semidefinite. Hence the classical density is positive. Bounded products charge no pluripolar sets, so there is no further measure on the exceptional set. Their mass on compact $X$ is finite by proposition 1.7.1. This proves $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=F\theta ^2$ for an almost-everywhere positive $F\in L^1$. On $\Omega =B_{R/4}$, (14.74) gives the representative $F=C^{-2}$. There $u=P+M-Q$ is discontinuous at $(0,w_0)$ for $0<|w_0|<R/4$.

We prove failure of every global $L^ p$ directly in the transition region. For large $k$, set

\[ m_ k=a(a_ k)+M\in [0,L],\qquad w_ k=\bigl (R^2/4+m_ k/C-|a_ k|^2\bigr )^{1/2}, \qquad Z_ k=(a_ k,w_ k). \]

Then $w_ k>0$, $P_0(Z_ k)=Q(Z_ k)=m_ k$, and (14.76) gives $|Z_ k|^2<9R^2/16$. Near the isolated pole $a_ k$,

\[ h(z)=\alpha _ k\log |z-a_ k|^2+H_ k(z), \]

where $H_ k$ is smooth and harmonic. Consequently $b$ and $P_0$ are continuous near $Z_ k$. There is a product neighborhood $V_ k\Subset B_ R$ of $Z_ k$, with $|w|$ bounded below by a positive number, on which $1/4\leq \lambda \leq 3/4$.

At smooth points of $V_ k$, positivity and the two-dimensional determinant expansion give

\[ \begin{split} \det H_ v& \geq \det \bigl (\lambda H_ P+(1-\lambda )CI\bigr )\\ & \geq \lambda (1-\lambda )C\operatorname {tr}H_ P \geq c_ k b_{z\bar z}, \end{split} \]

where $c_ k>0$ may depend on $k$ and $C$. Here $P_{z\bar z}=b^{-1}+|w|^2b_{z\bar z}$. With $\zeta =z-a_ k$, shrink the first factor of $V_ k$ so that

\[ b_{z\bar z}=|\zeta |^{2\alpha _ k}\mathrm{e}^{H_ k} \left|\frac{\alpha _ k}{\zeta }+\partial _ zH_ k\right|^2 \geq c'_ k|\zeta |^{2\alpha _ k-2} \]

for some $c'_ k>0$. Since $F=C^{-2}\det H_ v$, this lower bound implies $F\notin L^ p$ whenever $p(1-\alpha _ k)\geq 1$: the $w$-disk has positive area and the radial integral in $z$ contains

\[ \int _0^{\delta _ k}r^{1-2p(1-\alpha _ k)}\, \mathrm{d}r=+\infty . \]

For each $p>1$ such a $k$ exists because $\alpha _ k\downarrow 0$. $\square$

The bounded-product capacity estimate in section 1.6 also holds for the discontinuous potential $P$. Thus a first-order capacity bound, even together with a constant positive density, cannot supply the missing boundary control.

Example 14.20.5. On the bidisk in $\mathbb C^2$ with coordinates $(z,w)$, the function

\[ H(z,w)=2|z|(1+|w|^2) \]

is locally Lipschitz, plurisubharmonic and not $C^1$ along $\{ z=0\} $. Plurisubharmonicity follows by writing its two summands as convex exponential compositions of $\log |z|$ and $\log |z|+\log |w|^2$. At $z\ne 0$,

\[ H_{z\bar z}=\frac{1+|w|^2}{2|z|},\qquad H_{w\bar w}=2|z|,\qquad |H_{z\bar w}|^2=|w|^2. \]

Hence $\det (H_{j\bar k})=1$, and pluripolar nullity gives $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H)^2=2\pi ^{-2}\, \mathrm{d}\Lambda $ on the entire bidisk.

The last example rules out a purely local implication from Hölder continuity and a smooth positive density to smoothness; being local, it says nothing about the global hypotheses of theorem 14.17.1. Nor does theorem 14.20.4 contradict that theorem: there the solution is not locally Hölder continuous on the ball $\Omega $, and its density is not smooth on the rest of $X$, which is the regular locus of the barrier $\rho =0$. The trace criterion of theorem 14.20.1 avoids both global hypotheses by identifying the bounded solution with a smooth Dirichlet solution on each ball.

14.21 Equations with resolved logarithmic poles

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now prescribe a singularity type with logarithmic poles. If the poles become a divisor on a modification, the singular part can be removed there, and what remains is a smooth residual form. When this residual form is strictly positive, the bounded Hermitian theory on the modification provides the remaining bounded potential, and pushing forward solves the equation in the prescribed type.

In this section $n\geq 2$, and the background is a Hermitian form $h$ on $X$. Let $\psi \leq 0$ be a quasi-plurisubharmonic function that is smooth outside a nonempty analytic set $A$, its pole set. Assume that there is a modification $\pi :Y\to X$, biholomorphic over $X\setminus A$, such that $\pi ^{-1}(A)$ is the support $|D|$ of a simple normal crossing divisor $D=\sum _ jD_ j$ and

14.82
\begin{equation} \psi \circ \pi =\sum _ j c_ j\log |s_ j|^2+b, \qquad \beta =\pi ^*h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b-\sum _ jc_ j\Theta _ j>0, \end{equation}

where $c_ j>0$, $b$ is smooth on $Y$, $s_ j$ is a holomorphic section of $\mathcal O(D_ j)$ defining $D_ j$, and $\Theta _ j$ is the curvature form of a smooth Hermitian metric on $\mathcal O(D_ j)$, so that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log |s_ j|^2=[D_ j]-\Theta _ j$. Thus $\beta $ is the smooth part of the current $\pi ^*h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\psi \circ \pi )$, which we call the residual form.

Theorem 14.21.1. For every nonzero nonnegative $f\in L^ p(X,h^ n)$, $p>1$, there are $\kappa >0$ and a normalized $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ such that

14.83
\begin{equation} \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)=\kappa f h^ n, \qquad u=\psi +O(1). \end{equation}

The lifted remainder $v=(u-\psi )\circ \pi $ extends Hölder continuously over $Y$, so $u$ is locally Hölder on $X\setminus A$. Its current is

14.84
\begin{equation} \pi ^*h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u\circ \pi ) =\sum _ jc_ j[D_ j]+\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v. \end{equation}

The positive residual current $\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ has zero Lelong numbers. If $h$ is Kähler, then $\kappa =\int _ Y\beta ^ n/\int _ X f h^ n$, and the normalized solution is unique among all normalized solutions $u'$ of (14.83) with $u'=\psi +O(1)$, continuous or not. If the density $g=(f\circ \pi )\, \pi ^*h^ n/\beta ^ n$ is smooth and strictly positive on $Y$, the residual current $\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ may be chosen smooth and strictly positive.

Proof. Define $J=\pi ^*h^ n/\beta ^ n$ and $g=(f\circ \pi )J$ off the exceptional set, assigning any value to $g$ on that volume-zero set. The smooth nonnegative $J$ is bounded. Change of variables on the biholomorphic locus gives

14.85
\begin{equation} \int _ Yg\beta ^ n=\int _ Xfh^ n, \qquad \int _ Yg^ p\beta ^ n\leq \| J\| _\infty ^{p-1}\int _ Xf^ ph^ n. \end{equation}

Thus $g$ is a nonzero $L^ p$ density. Theorem 10.1.1, Theorem 14.4.1 give a bounded Hölder $\beta $-plurisubharmonic $v$ and $\kappa >0$ with $(\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n=\kappa g\beta ^ n$. If $h$ is Kähler, then $\beta $ is Kähler and the mass identity fixes the stated scalar. For smooth positive $g$, theorem 9.1.1 gives a smooth solution instead.

Set $u=\psi +v\circ \pi ^{-1}$ on $X\setminus A$ and $u=-\infty $ on $A$. Push forward the positive current $\sum c_ j[D_ j]+\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$. Pushforward commutes with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$, $\pi _*\pi ^*h=h$, and the pushed locally integrable potential is $u$ almost everywhere. Its quasi-plurisubharmonic representative equals $\psi +v\circ \pi ^{-1}$ off $A$ and tends to $-\infty $ at $A$ because $v$ is bounded. Hence $h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0$ and $u=\psi +O(1)$ in the two-sided ordinary sense. The pulled-back identity is (14.84); bounded residuals have zero Lelong number. The equation holds off $A$ by (14.85), and theorem 6.1.2 extends it as (14.83). Subtracting $\sup _ Xu$ changes neither the equation nor the ordinary type.

For uniqueness when $h$ is Kähler, let $u'$ be a normalized solution with $u'=\psi +O(1)$. On $Y\setminus D$, $w=(u'-\psi )\circ \pi $ is bounded and $\beta $-plurisubharmonic. Since $w$ is bounded, lemma 1.3.7 extends it across $D$ to a bounded $\beta $-plurisubharmonic function on $Y$. On each relatively compact subset of $Y\setminus D$, sufficiently deep truncations of $u'$ equal $u'$. Thus locality in theorem 6.1.2 gives the bounded equation $\kappa g\beta ^ n$ there; both sides give $D$ zero mass. The global equation follows, its mass fixes $\kappa $, and closed full-mass uniqueness gives $w=v+$constant as recalled in section 34.19. Normalization fixes that constant. $\square$

The Hölder constants depend on the fixed geometry and divisor metrics, the $c_ j$, a lower bound for the positivity of $\beta $, $p$, and the $L^ p$ norm of the density $\kappa g$ itself. In particular, the argument gives no estimate that is uniform as $\beta $ degenerates or as $\kappa $ becomes unbounded. In simple normal crossing coordinates a density comparable to $\prod _ j|z_ j|^{-2a_ j}$ belongs locally to $L^ p$ exactly when

14.86
\begin{equation} pa_ j<1\quad \hbox{for every }j, \end{equation}

by the factors $\int _0^\epsilon r_ j^{1-2pa_ j}\, \mathrm{d} r_ j$. A smooth positive density $f$ on $X$ need not give a strictly positive $g$ on $Y$, since the Jacobian factor $J=\pi ^*h^ n/\beta ^ n$ of the proof can vanish on the exceptional set.

Example 14.21.2. Take $X=\mathbb P^ n$, $h=\lambda \omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$, $\int _ X\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}^ n=1$, and $0<c<\lambda $. Put

\[ \psi _ c=c\log \frac{\sum _{j=1}^ n|Z_ j|^2}{\sum _{j=0}^ n|Z_ j|^2}. \]

Its pole is $P=[1:0:\cdots :0]$. On the point blowup $\pi :Y\to X$, let $q:Y\to \mathbb P^{n-1}$ be the direction map. In affine blowup coordinates $x=(z,zw)$, Poincaré–Lelong gives

\[ \pi ^*h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\psi _ c\circ \pi ) =c[E]+(\lambda -c)\pi ^*\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}+cq^*\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}. \]

The residual form $\beta =(\lambda -c)\pi ^*\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}+cq^*\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$ is positive: $\pi ^*\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$ is positive in the directions normal to $E$, and $q^*\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$ in the directions tangent to $E$. Write $H=\pi ^*[\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}]$. Since $H|_ E=0$ and $E^ n=(-1)^{n-1}$, its mass is

14.87
\begin{equation} \int _ Y\bigl ((\lambda -c)\pi ^*\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}+cq^*\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}\bigr )^ n=\lambda ^ n-c^ n. \end{equation}

Hence, for every nonzero nonnegative $f\in L^ p$ with $\int _ Xfh^ n=\lambda ^ n-c^ n$, theorem 14.21.1 gives $\kappa =1$ and a solution of $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)=fh^ n$ with the nonminimal type $u=\psi _ c+O(1)$.

For an explicit solution, choose a smooth function $v$ on $Y$ with $v|_ E$ nonconstant, small enough that $S=\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ is still positive, and put $u=\psi _ c+v\circ \pi ^{-1}$ off $P$. The density $f$ of its measure, given by $f\circ \pi =S^ n/\pi ^*h^ n$ off $E$, lies in every $L^ p$ with $1<p<n/(n-1)$: $\pi ^*h^ n$ vanishes to order $|z|^{2(n-1)}$ along $E$, so the local integral of $f^ p$ is finite precisely when $(n-1)(p-1)<1$. Different directions of approach to $P$ give different limits of $u-\psi _ c$. The divisor $c[E]$ of the current upstairs leaves no divisor downstairs, since $E$ is contracted to a point. On $X\setminus \{ P\} $, where $u$ is smooth and strictly $h$-plurisubharmonic, the Hermitian metric $h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ is isometric to $(Y\setminus E,S)$. Its metric completion is $Y$ with the metric $S$, so the whole divisor $E$ is restored: removing the smooth real codimension-two submanifold $E$ does not change the infimum of path lengths in $Y$. Finally, if $h$ is replaced by a sufficiently small nonclosed perturbation, both $h$ and the residual form remain positive, so the existence part of theorem 14.21.1 still applies.

14.21.1 Residual mass

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

When $h$ is not Kähler, the scalar in theorem 14.21.1 is still determined by masses if $h$ satisfies the power condition $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(h^ k)=0$, $1\leq k\leq n-1$, of theorem 2.2.2, which fixes the mass of every bounded solution. The condition persists under pullback and under addition of a smooth closed form: expand each power and apply the original identities to its terms. For $n=2$ the condition is simply $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h=0$, so it includes the nonclosed Hopf metric of proposition 20.6.1. The residual form then has strictly smaller mass than $h$, the difference being carried by the divisor.

Proposition 14.21.3. Under (14.82), assume $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(h^ k)=0$ for $1\leq k\leq n-1$, and put $H=\pi ^*h$. Then

14.88
\begin{equation} \int _ Xh^ n-\int _ Y\beta ^ n =\sum _ jc_ j\sum _{i=0}^{n-1} \int _{D_ j}H^ i\wedge \beta ^{n-1-i}>0. \end{equation}

Thus the constructed solution of theorem 14.21.1 has $\kappa =\int _ Y\beta ^ n/\int _ Xfh^ n$. If $\int _ Xfh^ n=\int _ Xh^ n$, its scalar is strictly less than one.

Proof. Let $C=\sum _ jc_ j\Theta _ j-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b$, so $\beta =H-C$ with $C$ closed. The mixed forms $Q_ i=H^ i\wedge \beta ^{n-1-i}$ satisfy $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}Q_ i=0$: expand $\beta =H-C$ and apply the power condition to each $H^ k$. With $\Psi =\psi \circ \pi $, $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Psi =\sum _ jc_ j[D_ j]-C$. Distributional integration by parts against the smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-closed $Q_ i$ gives

\[ \int _ Y(H^ n-\beta ^ n)=\sum _ i\int _ Y C\wedge Q_ i =\sum _ jc_ j\sum _ i\int _{D_ j}Q_ i. \]

Change of variables gives $\int _ YH^ n=\int _ Xh^ n$. Every integrand on the right is nonnegative, since $H$ is semipositive and $\beta $ positive; the $i=0$ term on each nonempty $D_ j$ is strictly positive. This includes exceptional divisors. By theorem 2.2.2, the residual solution has mass $\int _ Y\beta ^ n$, giving the scalar. $\square$

14.22 Minimal singularities on blowups

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

On a blowup, the background itself can force an exceptional divisor into every positive current. The coefficient of this divisor is then part of the minimal ordinary type, while the rest of the potential descends to the target of the blowup, so the equation reduces to one on the target. The density changes by the Jacobian of the blowup, which determines the loss of integrability.

In this section the roles of the letters differ from those in section 14.21: $X$ is a blowup and $Y$ its target. Let $(Y,h)$ be a connected compact Hermitian $n$-fold, $n\geq 2$, let $Z\subset Y$ be a connected smooth center of codimension $r\geq 2$, and write $\pi :X=\operatorname {Bl}_ ZY\to Y$ for the blowup, with exceptional divisor $E$. Let $s_ E$ be the canonical section of $\mathcal O(E)$, choose a smooth Hermitian metric on $\mathcal O(E)$ with curvature form $\Theta _ E$, so that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log |s_ E|^2=[E]-\Theta _ E$, put $L=\log |s_ E|^2$, and fix $c>0$. The smooth background

14.89
\begin{equation} \theta =\pi ^*h+c\Theta _ E \end{equation}

can be indefinite, and it is nonclosed when $h$ is nonclosed. Positivity is imposed on $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$, not on $\theta $.

Proposition 14.22.1. Every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ has, up to an additive constant, a unique $h$-plurisubharmonic target potential $q$ such that

14.90
\begin{equation} u=cL+q\circ \pi +C, \qquad \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=\pi ^*(h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q)+c[E]. \end{equation}

The potentials with minimal ordinary singularities are exactly those of the form $cL+O(1)$, and $u$ is one of them if and only if $q$ is bounded. There is a strict $\theta $-plurisubharmonic barrier with logarithmic coefficient $c+\delta $ for some $\delta >0$.

Proof. Put $T=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ and $q=\pi _*(u-cL)$ as a scalar distribution. The latter is locally integrable: off $Z$ it is the inverse-coordinate function, and a smooth target volume pulls back with bounded Jacobian, so its absolute integral is controlled by that of $u-cL$ on $X$. Since $\pi _*[E]=0$,

\[ S=\pi _*T=h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q\geq 0. \]

A smooth local strictly plurisubharmonic shift gives a quasi-plurisubharmonic representative of $q$. Its pullback $q\circ \pi $ is not identically $-\infty $ and satisfies $\pi ^*(h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q)\geq 0$, by lemma 1.22.1: since $\pi $ is dominant, the composition of a local plurisubharmonic function with $\pi $ is again plurisubharmonic and not identically $-\infty $.

Off $E$, $T=\pi ^*S$. The difference is an order-zero current supported on $E$, and it is closed: both derivatives equal $\pi ^*\mathrm{d} h$. To identify it, write $R=T-\pi ^*S$. In a chart where $E=\{ z=0\} $, order zero gives $zR=\bar zR=0$. Closedness then gives $\mathrm{d}z\wedge R=\mathrm{d}\bar z\wedge R=0$. Thus only its normal $(1,1)$ coefficient remains, a measure on $E$; the tangential equations in $\mathrm{d}R=0$ make that measure a constant multiple of integration on $E$. Connectedness of $E$ identifies one constant $a$ globally, so $R=a[E]$. Its cohomology class is $c[E]$. On a line in an exceptional fiber, $\mathcal O(E)$ has degree $-1$, so $[E]$ is nonzero and $a=c$. The difference between the two scalar potentials in (14.90) has $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ zero. Local elliptic regularity makes it pluriharmonic; compactness and connectedness make it constant.

Since $h>0$, $q=0$ is admissible, and every $h$-plurisubharmonic $q$ is bounded above on compact $Y$. Hence $cL$ is minimal and two-sided bounded comparison with it is exactly boundedness of $q$. For the barrier choose another divisor metric, with logarithmic norm $L'=L+\sigma $ and curvature $\Theta '_ E=\Theta _ E-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\sigma $ for a smooth real function $\sigma $, so that $-\Theta '_ E$ is positive on the vertical tangent bundle of $E\to Z$, using the tautological $\mathcal O(-1)$. In adapted coordinates

14.91
\begin{equation} \pi (y,z,w)=(y,z,zw), \end{equation}

$\pi ^*h$ controls the nonvertical directions and $|z|^2|\mathrm{d} w|^2$. For small $\delta >0$, the vertical positivity of $-\delta \Theta '_ E$ dominates its mixed errors by completing the square; away from $E$, compactness allows the same $\delta $. Thus $\pi ^*h-\delta \Theta '_ E>0$. For the fixed background $\theta $ in (14.89), take

\[ w=(c+\delta )L'-c\sigma =(c+\delta )L+\delta \sigma . \]

Then $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w=(c+\delta )[E]+\pi ^*h-\delta \Theta '_ E$ dominates a positive Hermitian form. The smooth correction $\delta \sigma $ preserves the stated logarithmic coefficient. $\square$

Theorem 14.22.2. Fix a smooth positive volume form $\mu $ on $X$ and a nonzero nonnegative $f\in L^ p(X,\mu )$, $p>1$. There exist $\kappa >0$ and a normalized minimal-type solution of

14.92
\begin{equation} \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)=\kappa f\mu , \qquad u=cL+q\circ \pi +C, \end{equation}

where $q$ is Hölder on $Y$. The divisor coefficient is exactly $c$. In (14.91), $u-cL=q(y,0)+C+O(|z|^\alpha )$ on smaller charts for some $\alpha >0$, so its limit is constant along each exceptional fiber.

If $\pi _*(f\mu )=Fh^ n$, then $F\in L^ s(Y,h^ n)$ for every

14.93
\begin{equation} 1<s<\frac{rp}{(r-1)p+1}. \end{equation}

For a point center, data bounded above and below by positive constants near $E$ attain the strict threshold $s<n/(n-1)$, and for finite $p$ no strictly larger universal exponent than $np/((n-1)p+1)$ can be guaranteed. If $h$ is Kähler, the class $[\theta ]$ is big and non-nef, has volume $\int _ Yh^ n$, and $\kappa =\int _ Yh^ n/\int _ Xf\mu $; the normalized solution is unique among all normalized solutions of (14.92) with minimal ordinary singularities. Under $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(h^ j)=0$, $1\leq j\leq n-1$, the scalar of the constructed solution has the same integral formula.

Proof. Integrability and existence. In (14.91) the complex Jacobian is $z^{r-1}$. Put $J=\pi ^*h^ n/\mu $, so $J\asymp |z|^{2(r-1)}$ on nested charts and $F\circ \pi =f/J$ off $E$. For $1<s<p$, change of variables and Hölder's inequality give

14.94
\begin{align} \int _ YF^ s h^ n & =\int _ X f^ sJ^{1-s}\mu \\ & \leq \| f\| _{L^ p(\mu )}^ s \left(\int _ XJ^{-(s-1)p/(p-s)}\mu \right)^{(p-s)/p}. \end{align}

The normal complex integral is finite precisely if $(r-1)(s-1)p/(p-s)<1$, which is (14.93). Theorem 10.1.1, Theorem 14.4.1 on $(Y,h)$ supply $q$ and $\kappa $ with $(h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q)^ n=\kappa Fh^ n$. Use (14.90) off $E$ and theorem 6.1.2 across it. Hölder continuity gives $q(\pi (y,z,w))=q(y,0)+O(|z|^\alpha )$ uniformly on smaller charts.

Sharpness of the exponent. For $f$ bounded above and below by positive constants near the exceptional divisor of a point center, $F$ is comparable to $\operatorname {dist}_ h(\cdot ,Z)^{-2(n-1)}$ near $Z$, giving the strict threshold. More generally, $f\asymp |s_ E|^{-2a}$ with $0\leq a<1/p$ belongs to $L^ p$ upstairs, while its pushforward has threshold $s<n/(n-1+a)$. Letting $a\uparrow 1/p$ proves the stated limitation; it makes no finite-$p$ endpoint assertion.

The Kähler case. When $h$ is Kähler, the strict barrier current $(c+\delta )[E]+\pi ^*h-\delta \Theta '_ E$ constructed in proposition 14.22.1 makes $[\theta ]$ big. Its degree on an exceptional-fiber line is $-c$, so it is not nef. The minimal current $c[E]+\pi ^*h$ has non-pluripolar top mass $\int _ Yh^ n$, and the minimal mass theorem identifies this with $\operatorname {vol}([\theta ])$ as recalled in section 34.16. Full-mass uniqueness and mass integration fix the scalar and normalized current as recalled in section 34.19. A normalized solution of minimal ordinary type, possibly discontinuous, still has bounded $q$ by proposition 14.22.1; on every relatively compact set off $E$, deep truncations equal that competitor, so locality in theorem 6.1.2 gives the bounded target equation off $Z$. Both target measures give $Z$ zero mass, so closed uniqueness applies globally. If only the power conditions hold, theorem 2.2.2 fixes the scalar of the constructed solution. Quantitative Hölder bounds depend on $\kappa F$, not on an unnormalized $F$ alone. $\square$

For the prescribed pullback measure $\pi ^*(h^ n)$, choose $q=0$ and $\kappa =1$. The current is $c[E]+\pi ^*h$. Its smooth part $\pi ^*h$ is degenerate on $E$ exactly in the directions tangent to the exceptional fibers, and its density relative to a smooth volume form is $|z|^{2(r-1)}$ times a smooth positive factor. On $X\setminus E$, where the solution $u=cL$ is smooth and strictly $\theta $-plurisubharmonic, the metric $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=\pi ^*h$ is isometric to $(Y\setminus Z,h)$; its metric completion is $Y$, so each exceptional fiber collapses to a point of $Z$. The distance to $E$ is comparable to $|z|$ on bounded adapted charts. Neither the density order nor this distance exponent depends on $c$.

Example 14.22.3. If $n=2$ and $Z$ is a point on a compact Kähler surface, $[\theta ]=\pi ^*[h]+c[E]$ has Zariski positive part $\pi ^*[h]$ and negative part $cE$: $E^2=-1$ and $\pi ^*[h]\cdot E=0$. Its volume is $\int _ Yh^2$, although it is non-nef. The solution with $\kappa =1$ for the measure $\pi ^*(h^2)$ has logarithmic coefficient exactly $c$, its density vanishes to order $|z|^2$ along $E$, and the completion of its metric on $X\setminus E$ collapses $E$ to the point $Z$. The stronger barrier from proposition 14.22.1 has coefficient $c+\delta $.

Theorem 14.22.4. Let $\pi :Y_ m\to Y_0$ be a finite nonempty sequence of blowups of connected smooth centers of codimensions $r_ j\geq 2$ over a compact Hermitian $(Y_0,h_0)$. Let $E_ j$ be the exceptional divisor at stage $j$, let $\pi _{>j}$ denote the subsequent map, choose $a_ j>0$, and set

\[ \Delta =\sum _ j a_ j\pi _{>j}^*E_ j, \qquad \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}L_\Delta =[\Delta ]-\Theta _\Delta , \qquad \theta =\pi ^*h_0+\Theta _\Delta , \]

where $L_\Delta =\sum _ ja_ j\log |s_ j|^2$, with $s_ j$ the canonical section of $\pi _{>j}^*\mathcal O(E_ j)$ for a smooth Hermitian metric, and $\Theta _\Delta $ is the corresponding combination of curvature forms. Every $\theta $-positive current is $\pi ^*(h_0+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q)+[\Delta ]$ and every potential is $L_\Delta +q\circ \pi +C$, with $q\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y_0,h_0)$. Minimal ordinary type is exactly $L_\Delta +O(1)$, equivalent to bounded $q$. For potentials of this type, every prime exceptional coefficient is the one prescribed by the effective total transform $\Delta $. There is a strict barrier with divisor $\Delta +B$ for an effective exceptional $B$ positive on every exceptional component. For nonzero nonnegative upstairs $L^ p$ data, $p>1$, a minimal solution has Hölder $q$, with pushed density in every $L^ s$ satisfying

14.96
\begin{equation} 1<s<\frac{Rp}{(R-1)p+1},\qquad R=\prod _ jr_ j. \end{equation}

For Kähler $h_0$ the mass, scalar, and closed full-mass uniqueness are those of the target; the power conditions on $h_0$ fix the scalar for the constructed nonclosed solution. For the pullback measure $\pi ^*(h_0^ n)$, the metric $\pi ^*h_0$ on the complement of the exceptional set has metric completion $Y_0$, each fiber collapsing to a point, and the zero divisor of the density is that of the squared modulus of the holomorphic Jacobian, independently of the $a_ j$.

Proof. Apply proposition 14.22.1 successively, allowing an intermediate smooth background to be indefinite. The difference between each current and the pullback of its pushforward is closed of order zero, and exceptional-fiber degree fixes its coefficient. Pullback of earlier divisor currents produces the total transforms, proving the representation without an SNC requirement. The initial $q=0$ is admissible; upper bounds on compact targets prove minimality and the bounded-$q$ criterion. At SNC charts this is the usual sum of logarithms.

At each stage choose an auxiliary divisor metric as in proposition 14.22.1, with curvature $\Theta '_{E_ j}$, so that $h_ j=\pi _ j^*h_{j-1}-\delta _ j\Theta '_{E_ j}>0$ for a sufficiently small $\delta _ j>0$. Expanding yields $h_ m=\pi ^*h_0-\Theta _ B$ for $B=\sum _ j\delta _ j\pi _{>j}^*E_ j$, effective with a positive coefficient on every exceptional component. If $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}L_ B=[B]-\Theta _ B$ uses these auxiliary metrics, then the fixed background satisfies $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(L_\Delta +L_ B)=h_ m+[\Delta +B]$, which is the strict barrier current. The Jacobian calculation and Hölder estimate apply stagewise. If $s=rp/((r-1)p+1)$, then $1-1/s=(1-1/p)/r$; iteration over the stages gives (14.96), with strict inequality. The target Hölder equation, mass and uniqueness are obtained as in theorem 14.22.2. For the pullback measure take $q=0$ and use the target path comparison away from the exceptional set. The Jacobian fixes the density, while $[\Delta ]$ fixes the divisor current; these are independent pieces of the equation. $\square$

14.23 Representing measures in an ordinary type

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We return to the general setting of the chapter, with the Hermitian form $\omega $ fixed at its beginning. Locally, every finite non-pluripolar measure is absolutely continuous with respect to the Monge–Ampère measure of a bounded plurisubharmonic function (lemma 1.6.5). We globalize this statement in a prescribed ordinary type. The local potentials are glued to a strictly $\theta $-plurisubharmonic potential lying below the reference and are then truncated relative to the reference. A decreasing convex sum of the resulting countable family has a Monge–Ampère measure that dominates every local piece in the sense of absolute continuity. Its potential has the prescribed ordinary type, and the given measure has an integrable density with respect to its Monge–Ampère measure.

Proposition 14.23.1. Let $\theta \geq 0$ be a smooth big real $(1,1)$-form, and let $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ be such that the envelope $P_{\epsilon \theta }(\varphi )$ of section 1.19 is not identically $-\infty $ for some $0<\epsilon <1$. For every finite non-pluripolar measure $\mu $ there are

\[ \psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ),\qquad \psi \sim \varphi , \qquad 0\leq f\in L^1\bigl (\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (\psi )\bigr ) \]

such that

\[ \mu =f\, \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (\psi ). \]

The measure $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (\psi )$ may have infinite mass, but the density $f$ is integrable with respect to it.

Proof. Translate $\varphi $ below zero. Put $q=P_{\epsilon \theta }(\varphi )\leq \varphi $, and choose a strict big potential $\rho \leq 0$ with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq a\omega $, where $a>0$. Then

\[ s=q+(1-\epsilon )\rho \leq \varphi ,\qquad \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}s\geq (1-\epsilon )a\omega . \]

In particular $s$ is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic and not identically $-\infty $. Its pole set is pluripolar, as is that of $\varphi $, so both are negligible for $\mu $.

Cover $X$ by finitely many closed coordinate balls $K_ i$, each contained in a larger coordinate ball $B_ i$. Apply lemma 1.6.5 to $\mu _ i=\mathbf1_{K_ i}\mu $. This gives bounded local plurisubharmonic $v_ i$, normalized by $1\leq v_ i\leq 2$, with

\[ \mu _ i\ll \nu _ i:=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ i)^ n. \]

Take a smooth cutoff $\chi _ i$ which is zero near $K_ i$ and $-3$ on a boundary collar of $B_ i$. Choose $\delta _ i>0$ so small that

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\bigl (s+\delta _ i(v_ i+\chi _ i)\bigr ) \geq \tfrac 12(1-\epsilon )a\omega +\delta _ i\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ i. \]

Let $S_ i$ equal $\max (s,s+\delta _ i(v_ i+\chi _ i))$ on $B_ i$ and $s$ elsewhere. The boundary collar makes this a global $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function. Since $\chi _ i\leq 0$ may be arranged,

\[ s\leq S_ i\leq s+2\delta _ i\leq \varphi +2\delta _ i. \]

For every positive integer $m$, set

\[ q_{i,m}=\max (\varphi -m,S_ i)-2\delta _ i. \]

These potentials have the same ordinary type as $\varphi $, and

14.97
\begin{equation} \varphi -m-2\delta _ i\leq q_{i,m}\leq \varphi . \end{equation}

On a neighborhood of $K_ i$, $S_ i=s+\delta _ i v_ i$ away from the pole set of $s$. Let

\[ D_{i,m}=\{ s+\delta _ i v_ i>\varphi -m\} \]

in that neighborhood, restricting to points where the potentials are finite. This is a plurifinely open set there. Locality and positive mixed products give

14.98
\begin{equation} \mathbf1_{D_{i,m}}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (q_{i,m}) \geq \delta _ i^ n\mathbf1_{D_{i,m}}\nu _ i. \end{equation}

Here $s$ need not be bounded. After a local smooth correction, the positive product inequality holds at every common finite truncation level of $s$ and the bounded potential $v_ i$. Exhaust the complement of the pole set of $s$. The measure $\nu _ i$ is non-pluripolar, so it loses no mass in that exhaustion. Then restrict the resulting inequality by plurifine locality. This proves (14.98) for the extended measures.

For $\mu _ i$-almost every point of $K_ i$, both $s$ and $\varphi $ are finite, and hence that point belongs to $D_{i,m}$ for some $m$. Consequently, a Borel set which is null for every $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (q_{i,m})$ is null for every $\mu _ i$, and therefore for $\mu $.

Enumerate the countable family $q_{i,m}$ as $q_ k$, and write its bounds as $\varphi -C_ k\leq q_ k\leq \varphi $ with $C_ k<\infty $. Choose positive $t_ k$ such that

\[ \sum _ k t_ k<1,\qquad B:=\sum _ k t_ kC_ k<\infty . \]

The finite convex combinations

\[ \psi _ N=\sum _{k=1}^ N t_ kq_ k +\left(1-\sum _{k=1}^ N t_ k\right)\varphi \]

decrease and lie between $\varphi -B$ and $\varphi $. Their limit $\psi $ is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic, satisfies $\psi \geq \varphi -B$, and hence $\psi \sim \varphi $. For any fixed $k$, separate its summand to write

\[ \psi =t_ kq_ k+(1-t_ k)w_ k, \qquad w_ k\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ),\qquad w_ k\sim \varphi . \]

The remainder is obtained by the same decreasing convex-sum construction. Positive non-pluripolar mixed products give

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (\psi ) \geq t_ k^ n\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (q_ k). \]

This is valid also for infinite measures: first restrict away from the common pole set, which is the pole set of $\varphi $ by the ordinary-type bounds, and then exhaust by finite truncations. All measures ignore that pole set. The preceding null-set conclusion now gives $\mu \ll \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (\psi )$.

By theorem 6.1.2, the measure $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (\psi )$ is $\sigma $-finite. The Radon–Nikodym theorem applies to this $\sigma $-finite measure and the finite $\mu $. It gives a nonnegative density $f$ with

\[ \int _ X f\, \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (\psi )=\mu (X)<\infty , \]

as claimed. If $\mu =0$, one may simply take $\psi =\varphi $ and $f=0$. $\square$

Lemma 14.23.2. Let $\theta \geq 0$ be a smooth big real $(1,1)$-form, and let $\mu $ be a finite non-pluripolar measure on $X$. There are a bounded $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and a nonnegative $f\in L^1(\mathrm{MA}_\theta (\psi ))$ such that

\[ \mu =f\, \mathrm{MA}_\theta (\psi ). \]

Proof. Apply proposition 14.23.1 with $\varphi =0$. Semipositivity of $\theta $ gives $P_{\epsilon \theta }(0)=0$ for every $0<\epsilon <1$, and $\psi \sim 0$ means that $\psi $ is bounded. $\square$

These representations give only an $L^1$ density, whereas theorem 14.9.1 requires a bounded density with respect to the measure of a bounded potential; for an unbounded prescribed type, moreover, the representing potential is itself unbounded. The existence argument for general measures in section 31.6 therefore uses a bounded Hermitian reference and relative compactness instead.

14.24 The homogeneous equation with a Hölder reference

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

A bounded density against a Hölder reference measure gives an exact homogeneous equation. The coefficient is obtained with the solution. Continuous approximation of the density is needed before passing to smooth reference measures, since weak convergence does not preserve integrals of a fixed Borel function.

Theorem 14.24.1. Let $h$ be a Hermitian form on a connected compact complex manifold $X$. Suppose $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)\cap C^{0,\alpha }(X)$ for some $0<\alpha \leq 1$, and let $\nu $ be a finite nonzero positive measure with

\[ \nu \leq A\mathrm{MA}_ h(u),\qquad A>0. \]

There are a constant $c>0$ and a Hölder continuous $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ such that

\[ \sup _ Xw=0,\qquad \mathrm{MA}_ h(w)=c\nu . \]

In particular, its mass is finite and positive. No mass bound over all bounded $h$-plurisubharmonic functions is assumed.

Proof. Step 1: approximate the density and reference. Write $\sigma =\mathrm{MA}_ h(u)$ and $\nu =A g\sigma $, with $0\leq g\leq 1$. Choose continuous $g_ l\in [0,1]$ tending to $g$ in $L^1(\sigma )$. Regularity of the finite measure supplies this approximation. The inequality $|s^{1/n}-t^{1/n}|^ n\leq |s-t|$ on $[0,1]$, followed by Hölder's inequality, gives $g_ l^{1/n}\to g^{1/n}$ in $L^1(\sigma )$.

By lemma 1.21.5, there are smooth $v_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ tending uniformly to $u$ with a common Hölder norm. Their bounded products $\sigma _ j=h_{v_ j}^ n$ tend weakly to $\sigma $. Choose a dense countable family of continuous tests, including the constant one. For each $l$, choose $j(l)\geq l$ so that integration of $g_ l^{1/n}$, and of $g_ l$ multiplied by the first $l$ tests, against $\sigma _{j(l)}-\sigma $ has absolute value at most $1/l$. Write $\widehat v_ l=v_{j(l)}$ and

\[ \nu _ l=A g_ l h_{\widehat v_ l}^ n. \]

The common bounded-reference mass and $0\leq g_ l\leq 1$ give uniformly bounded masses. The diagonal and density approximation imply

\[ \nu _ l\rightharpoonup \nu ,\qquad \lim _ l\int _ Xg_ l^{1/n}h_{\widehat v_ l}^ n =\int _ Xg^{1/n}\, \mathrm d\sigma >0. \]

Discard finitely many terms if needed. Each $\nu _ l$ has a continuous density with respect to smooth volume and positive mass. Thus theorem 10.1.1 gives continuous normalized $h$-plurisubharmonic $\varphi _ l$ and $c_ l>0$ with $h_{\varphi _ l}^ n=c_ l\nu _ l$.

Step 2: control the coefficients and potentials. Since both right-hand sides have smooth-volume densities, lemma 1.7.6 gives

\[ h_{\varphi _ l}\wedge h_{\widehat v_ l}^{n-1} \geq (c_ lA)^{1/n}g_ l^{1/n}h_{\widehat v_ l}^ n. \]

The trace bound in lemma 1.8.13 is uniform for references with a fixed supremum norm, as its proof shows. Integrating therefore bounds $c_ l$ above. By proposition 1.8.7, the solution measures $\mu _ l=h_{\varphi _ l}^ n\leq C h_{\widehat v_ l}^ n$ have a common square-capacity bound.

Fix $\varepsilon =1/2$, the zero competitor, and $D>0$ with $2D<\delta _{1/2}$. The backwards estimate of lemma 10.3.2 gives

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_ h\{ \varphi _ l<\inf _ X\varphi _ l+D\} \geq a_0>0. \]

The normalized tail estimate (1.26) then bounds $-\inf _ X\varphi _ l$ uniformly. Applying lemma 10.3.1 at $s=t=D$ also gives

\[ D^ na_0\leq C\mu _ l(X)=Cc_ l\nu _ l(X). \]

Since the masses of $\nu _ l$ are uniformly bounded above, $c_ l$ is bounded away from zero.

Step 3: take a uniform limit. Extract a subsequence with $c_ l\to c>0$ and $\varphi _ l$ converging in $L^1(\mathrm dV)$, using theorem 1.4.2. All potentials now have common upper and lower bounds. The common Hölder norms of $\widehat v_ l$ and corollary 1.8.4 give, uniformly in $j,k$,

\[ \int _ X|\varphi _ j-\varphi _ k|\, \mathrm d\mu _ j \leq C\| \varphi _ j-\varphi _ k\| _{L^1(\mathrm dV)}^{\alpha /(\alpha +2n)} \longrightarrow 0. \]

Use proposition 10.3.5 in both orders to see that this subsequence is uniformly Cauchy. Its continuous limit $w$ is $h$-plurisubharmonic and has supremum zero. Uniform bounded-product continuity and $\nu _ l\rightharpoonup \nu $ yield $\mathrm{MA}_ h(w)=c\nu $. Finally theorem 14.4.2, with reference $u$ and domination constant $cA$, gives the Hölder assertion. Its mass is $c\nu (X)$. $\square$

14.25 Singular measures on shrinking spheres

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

A bounded density with respect to the measure of a bounded potential gives an exponential equation by theorem 14.9.1. The following construction turns this analytic hypothesis into a test against a probability measure fixed by a coordinate ball. It applies to singular data on every Hermitian background, including backgrounds with zero lower mass.

Fix a Hermitian form $h$ on $X$, with $n\geq 2$, a holomorphic coordinate ball centered at a point $p$, and a smooth cutoff $0\leq \chi \leq 1$ supported in that ball and equal to one near a smaller closed ball. Choose $r_0>0$ so small that all spheres of radii $r_ j=r_0 2^{-j}$, $j\geq 1$, lie in the smaller ball. Let $\sigma _ j$ denote their normalized Euclidean surface probabilities, extended by zero to $X$, and put

14.99
\begin{equation} S_ n=\sum _{j\geq 1}r_ j^{n/2},\qquad \rho =\frac1{S_ n}\sum _{j\geq 1}r_ j^{n/2}\sigma _ j. \end{equation}

For a finite positive measure $\nu $, define

\[ M_\rho (\nu )=\left\| \frac{\mathrm d\nu }{\mathrm d\rho }\right\| _{L^\infty (\rho )}, \]

with value $+\infty $ when $\nu \not\ll \rho $. The measure $\rho $ is chosen before $\nu $ or the exponent in the equation. Transporting the same measure under a change of coordinates leaves the test unchanged. Allowing the existence of some coordinate ball with this construction gives a sufficient condition independent of a specified chart.

Proposition 14.25.1. The measure $\rho $ is a singular non-pluripolar probability. There are $k>0$ and $u_\rho \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)\cap C^{0,1/2}(X)$ with $\sup _ Xu_\rho =0$ such that

\[ h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\rho \geq h/2,\qquad \mathrm{MA}_ h(u_\rho )\geq k\rho ,\qquad 0<\mathrm{MA}_ h(u_\rho )(X)<\infty . \]

Proof. In the coordinate ball write $R=|z|$ and set

\[ \psi _ j=\chi \log \max \{ R^2,r_ j^2\} +(1-\chi ), \]

extending $\psi _ j$ by one outside it. On the transition region of $\chi $, $R$ is bounded away from zero and exceeds all $r_ j$. Hence all $\psi _ j$ agree there with the same smooth function. Where $\chi =1$ they are plurisubharmonic, and outside its support they are constant. One fixed $K\geq 1$ therefore satisfies $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi _ j\geq -Kh$ for every $j$. Put

\[ Z=2K\sum _{j\geq 1}\sqrt{r_ j},\qquad d_ j=\frac{\sqrt{r_ j}}Z,\qquad u_0=\sum _{j\geq 1}d_ j\psi _ j. \]

The estimate $\| \psi _ j\| _\infty \leq C(1+|\log r_ j|)$ gives uniform convergence, and $\sum d_ j=1/(2K)$ yields $h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_0\geq h/2$.

For two points of the inner ball at Euclidean distance $t$, the reverse triangle inequality gives

\[ |\log \max \{ |z|^2,r_ j^2\} -\log \max \{ |z'|^2,r_ j^2\} | \leq 2\log (1+t/r_ j). \]

The contribution of $r_ j\geq t$ is at most $Ct\sum _{r_ j\geq t}r_ j^{-1/2}\leq C\sqrt t$. For $r_ j<t$, summing the geometric tail gives at most $C\sqrt t\sum _{\ell \geq 0}2^{-\ell /2}(1+\ell )$. On the cutoff transition and outside, the series is a fixed smooth function. These estimates give a local $1/2$-Hölder bound near every point. A finite cover and the bound on the oscillation give the global bound.

On the smaller ball, the currents $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi _ j$ are closed and positive. Expand the product of $h+\sum _{j\leq N}d_ j\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi _ j$ there and retain its positive diagonal terms. By lemma 1.6.15, these are $d_ j^ n\sigma _ j$. Uniform convergence of the corrected bounded potentials gives weak convergence of their products. Testing against nonnegative functions and first retaining any fixed finite number of diagonal terms therefore gives

\[ \mathrm{MA}_ h(u_0)\geq \sum _{j\geq 1}d_ j^ n\sigma _ j =\frac{S_ n}{Z^ n}\rho . \]

Here the right-hand side is supported strictly inside the smaller ball, so the inequality extends to $X$. Each $\sigma _ j$ is non-pluripolar by the same lemma; countable additivity gives that property for $\rho $. The countable union of the spheres has zero smooth volume, which proves singularity. Local bounded-product estimates on a finite cover make the mass of $u_0$ finite, and the retained inequality makes it positive. Set $k=S_ n/Z^ n$ and subtract $\sup _ Xu_0$ to obtain $u_\rho $. $\square$

Corollary 14.25.2. Let $\nu $ be a finite nonzero positive measure with $M_\rho (\nu )<\infty $. There are $c>0$ and a Hölder continuous $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ satisfying

\[ \sup _ Xw=0,\qquad \mathrm{MA}_ h(w)=c\nu , \qquad 0<\mathrm{MA}_ h(w)(X)<\infty . \]

Proof. By proposition 14.25.1, $\nu \leq (M_\rho (\nu )/k)\mathrm{MA}_ h(u_\rho )$, where $u_\rho $ is Hölder continuous with exponent $1/2$. Apply theorem 14.24.1. The equation and $0<c<\infty $ give its positive finite mass. $\square$

Theorem 14.25.3. Let $\nu $ be a finite nonzero positive measure with $M_\rho (\nu )<\infty $. Then $\nu $ is non-pluripolar and

\[ \mathrm{MA}_ h(u_\rho )\geq \frac{k}{M_\rho (\nu )}\nu . \]

For every $\lambda >0$ there is a unique bounded $U_\lambda \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ satisfying

\[ \mathrm{MA}_ h(U_\lambda )=\mathrm e^{\lambda U_\lambda }\nu , \qquad 0<\mathrm{MA}_ h(U_\lambda )(X)<\infty . \]

Proof. Since $\nu \leq M_\rho (\nu )\rho $, the reference inequality proves the first assertion. Write $\alpha =\mathrm{MA}_ h(u_\rho )$. Then $\nu =f\alpha $ with $0\leq f\leq M_\rho (\nu )/k$. The form $h>0$ is semipositive and big, so theorem 14.9.1 gives the unique bounded solution. Its boundedness and the equation give positive finite mass. $\square$

The scalar in the exponential equation is fixed: adding a constant to $U_\lambda $ changes its right-hand side. The theorem imposes no supremum normalization and does not assert uniqueness among unbounded potentials. For example, it applies to every bounded nonzero nonnegative density on any one of the chosen spheres. Failure of the test gives no obstruction to solvability. The homogeneous equation is supplied separately by corollary 14.25.2, with a positive coefficient obtained as part of the solution. The construction does not identify a largest admissible coefficient or establish uniqueness for that equation.

14.26 Lower mass and solvability for all measures

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Throughout the rest of the chapter, $h$ denotes a Hermitian form on $X$, used as the background, and densities are taken with respect to the fixed smooth positive probability volume form $\mathrm{d}V$; the sections that use another background say so explicitly. No singularity type is prescribed: the unknown potentials range over all of $\mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$. Constants belong to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$, and every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ is bounded above, so $u\preceq v$ for every bounded $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$.

The masses of bounded potentials are measured by the lower and upper masses of definition 6.5.1,

\[ v_-(h)=\inf _ v\int _ X(h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n,\qquad v_+(h)=\sup _ v\int _ X(h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n, \]

where $v$ ranges over the bounded functions in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$. The form $h$ has positive lower mass if $v_-(h)>0$. If $h$ is Kähler, Stokes' theorem gives $v_-(h)=v_+(h)=\int _ Xh^ n$; on a non-Kähler manifold the lower mass can vanish (theorem 26.18.1).

For a nonzero nonnegative $q\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$ with $p>1$, theorem 10.1.1 gives a constant $c>0$ and a continuous $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ with $\mathrm{MA}_ h(u)=cq\, \mathrm{d}V$, and proposition 10.6.1 shows that $c$ is determined by $q$. We call $c_ h(q)=c$ the normalization scalar of $q$, and for the measure $\nu =q\, \mathrm{d}V$ we also write $c_ h(\nu )=c_ h(q)$. We say that a finite measure $\nu $ admits a positive scaled subsolution if there are $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ and a constant $a>0$ such that $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)\geq a\nu $.

If $\nu $ admits a solution of $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)=c\nu $ with $c>0$, then it admits in particular a positive scaled subsolution. We show that every nonzero finite non-pluripolar measure admits such a solution if and only if $h$ has positive lower mass. The key estimate is a consequence of strict domination: if $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)\geq aq\, \mathrm{d}V$, then $a\leq c_ h(q)$. When $v_-(h)=0$, there are smooth probability densities with arbitrarily small normalization scalars, and a weighted sum of such densities, with scalars decreasing fast enough, is a single probability density admitting no positive scaled subsolution.

Lemma 14.26.1. Let $0\leq q\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$, $p>1$, be nonzero. If $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ and $a>0$ satisfy

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)\geq aq\, \mathrm{d}V, \]

then $a\leq c_ h(q)$. Consequently, for nonzero nonnegative bounded densities $q,r$,

\[ q\leq r\ \Longrightarrow \ c_ h(q)\geq c_ h(r), \qquad c_ h(tq)=t^{-1}c_ h(q)\quad (t>0). \]

Proof. Let $v$ be a continuous solution of $\mathrm{MA}_ h(v)=c_ h(q)q\, \mathrm{d}V$, and suppose, to reach a contradiction, that $a>c_ h(q)$. Then $\tau =c_ h(q)/a<1$ and, for every real $A$,

\[ \mathrm{MA}_ h(v+A)=c_ h(q)q\, \mathrm{d}V \leq \tau \, \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u). \]

The translate $v+A$ is bounded, so $P_{h/2}(v+A)\geq \inf _ Xv+A$ is not identically $-\infty $, and $u\preceq v+A$. Theorem 14.1.2, with $\delta =1/2$ and $c=\tau $, therefore gives $v+A\geq u$ for every real $A$. Letting $A\to -\infty $ at a point where $u$ is finite gives a contradiction. Hence $a\leq c_ h(q)$.
