If $q\leq r$, a continuous solution of $\mathrm{MA}_ h(w)=c_ h(r)r\, \mathrm{d}V$ satisfies $\mathrm{MA}_ h(w)\geq c_ h(r)q\, \mathrm{d}V$, so the first assertion gives $c_ h(r)\leq c_ h(q)$. Finally, the equation $\mathrm{MA}_ h(v)=c_ h(q)q\, \mathrm{d}V$ can be written as $\mathrm{MA}_ h(v)=t^{-1}c_ h(q)\, (tq)\, \mathrm{d}V$, and uniqueness of the scalar gives $c_ h(tq)=t^{-1}c_ h(q)$. $\square$

The proof uses only that $v$ is bounded. Hence, more generally, if a nonnegative density $g$ satisfies $\mathrm{MA}_ h(v)=c\, g\, \mathrm{d}V$ for some bounded $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ and some $c>0$, then every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ and $a>0$ with $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)\geq ag\, \mathrm{d}V$ satisfy $a\leq c$.

Lemma 14.26.2. Fix $p>1$. If nonzero nonnegative densities $q_ j,q\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$ satisfy $q_ j\to q$ in $L^ p$, then $c_ h(q_ j)\to c_ h(q)$.

Proof. We first show that the scalars $c_ h(q_ j)$ lie in a compact subset of $(0,+\infty )$. Fix a Gauduchon metric $\gamma $ on $X$ (definition 4.1.1; in dimension one, any Hermitian metric). Pairing the equation for $q_ j$ with $\gamma ^{n-1}$, as in proposition 14.13.2 with $\theta =h$ and barrier $\rho =0$, gives

\[ c_ h(q_ j)^{1/n}\int _ X\left(\frac{q_ j\, \mathrm{d}V}{\gamma ^ n}\right)^{1/n}\gamma ^ n \leq \int _ Xh\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}. \]

Since $q_ j^{1/n}\to q^{1/n}$ in $L^ n$, the integrals on the left converge to the corresponding integral for $q$, which is positive. Hence the scalars are bounded above. The lower-density estimate of theorem 10.3.3 gives $c_ h(q_ j)\| q_ j\| _{L^ p}\geq c_ p>0$, so they are also bounded away from zero.

Consequently, the normalized continuous solutions $u_ j$ of $\mathrm{MA}_ h(u_ j)=c_ h(q_ j)q_ j\, \mathrm{d}V$ have uniformly bounded $L^ p$ densities. By theorem 10.3.4, every subsequence has a further subsequence along which $u_ j$ converges uniformly to some $u$ and $c_ h(q_ j)$ converges to some $c>0$. Continuity of bounded Monge–Ampère products under uniform convergence gives $\mathrm{MA}_ h(u)=cq\, \mathrm{d}V$, and uniqueness of the scalar gives $c=c_ h(q)$. Since every subsequence of $(c_ h(q_ j))$ has a further subsequence converging to $c_ h(q)$, the whole sequence converges to $c_ h(q)$. $\square$

Theorem 14.26.3. Suppose $v_-(h)=0$. There is a probability measure $\mu =f\, \mathrm{d}V$, with $f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$ and $f\geq m$ almost everywhere for a constant $m>0$, such that no $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ and $a>0$ satisfy

14.100
\begin{equation} \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)\geq a\mu . \end{equation}

In particular, the equation $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)=c\mu $ has no solution $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ for any $c>0$; neither a singularity type nor finiteness of the mass of $u$ is imposed.

Proof. By theorem 26.14.3, theorem 26.1.3, $v_-(h)$ is the infimum of the normalization scalars $c_ h(\nu )$ over smooth strictly positive probability measures $\nu $. Since $v_-(h)=0$, we can choose such measures $\nu _ j=f_ j\, \mathrm{d}V$ with

\[ c_ h(\nu _ j)\leq 2^{-2j}\qquad (j\geq 1). \]

Set

\[ \mu =\sum _{j=1}^{\infty }2^{-j}\nu _ j, \qquad f=\sum _{j=1}^{\infty }2^{-j}f_ j. \]

By monotone convergence, $\int _ Xf\, \mathrm{d}V=1$, so $f\in L^1$. Moreover $f\geq \tfrac 12\min _ X f_1=:m>0$, and $\mu $ is non-pluripolar, being absolutely continuous with respect to $\mathrm{d}V$.

Suppose that (14.100) held for some $u$ and $a>0$. Since $\mu \geq 2^{-j}\nu _ j$, we would have

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)\geq a2^{-j}\nu _ j \]

for every $j$, and lemma 14.26.1 would give $a2^{-j}\leq c_ h(\nu _ j)\leq 2^{-2j}$. Thus $a\leq 2^{-j}$ for every $j$, contradicting $a>0$. $\square$

For the reference $0$, definition 13.4.1 says that $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ belongs to the relative envelope class $\mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(X,h,0)$ if, for every $b\geq 1$, some $g\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ satisfies $g\leq bu$.

Corollary 14.26.4. The following conditions are equivalent:

  1. $v_-(h)>0$.

  2. For every nonzero finite non-pluripolar measure $\mu $ there are $c>0$ and $u\in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(X,h,0)$ such that $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)=c\mu $.

  3. For every probability density $f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$ bounded below by a positive constant, there are $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ and $a>0$ such that $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)\geq af\, \mathrm{d}V$.

Proof. The Hermitian form $h$ is semipositive and big, and the reference $0$ satisfies (13.26), since $P_ h[0]=0$ and $P_{\varepsilon h}(0)=0$ for every $0<\varepsilon <1$. Hence, if $v_-(h)>0$, theorem 31.1.1, applied with $\theta =h$ and $\varphi =0$, gives (2). Condition (2) implies (3), because a finite measure that is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mathrm{d}V$ is non-pluripolar. Finally, if $v_-(h)=0$, the density of theorem 14.26.3 violates (3); thus (3) implies (1). $\square$

Corollary 14.26.5. Assume $v_-(h)=0$, and let $\mu =f\, \mathrm{d}V$ be the measure of theorem 14.26.3. Then $f\notin L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$ for every $p>1$. Moreover, for every probability density $g\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$ and every $0<t<1$, the probability density

\[ g_ t=(1-t)g+tf \]

admits no positive scaled subsolution, although $\| g_ t-g\| _{L^1}\leq 2t$. In particular, the probability densities admitting no positive scaled subsolution are dense among all probability densities in $L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$.

Proof. If $f\in L^ p$ for some $p>1$, theorem 10.1.1 would give a continuous solution of $\mathrm{MA}_ h(u)=cf\, \mathrm{d}V$ with $c>0$, contradicting the theorem. Since $g_ t\, \mathrm{d}V\geq t\mu $, a positive scaled subsolution for $g_ t\, \mathrm{d}V$ would also give one for $\mu $. Finally, $\| g_ t-g\| _{L^1}=t\| f-g\| _{L^1}\leq 2t$. $\square$

Smooth strictly positive probability densities are also dense in $L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$, and each of them admits a smooth solution of the normalized equation. Thus, when the lower mass vanishes, solvability is not preserved under $L^1$ approximation. The obstruction comes from the way the density concentrates, not from its size: the density in theorem 14.26.3 is bounded below by a positive constant. It occurs, for instance, on the compact threefold of theorem 26.18.1, where every Hermitian form has zero lower mass.

Theorem 14.26.3 does not exclude unbounded solutions of the exponential equation $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}\mu $ with $\lambda >0$, because the factor $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}$ need not have a positive lower bound. It does exclude bounded solutions, since a bounded solution would satisfy $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)\geq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda \inf _ Xu}\mu $. On suitable products, theorem 14.34.6 chooses the concentrating components of such a density so that its exponential equation has a solution for every positive exponent. We do not know whether this holds for every density admitting no positive scaled subsolution, nor which individual measures admit a solution of the normalized equation when $v_-(h)=0$.

14.27 Subsolutions for an individual density

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

When $v_-(h)=0$, some densities admit no positive scaled subsolution. For one fixed density $f$, we now determine the largest coefficient $a$ for which $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)\geq af\, \mathrm{d}V$ has a solution. The bounded truncations of $f$ have normalization scalars, and their limit is the answer. Moreover, the largest coefficient is attained: the normalized continuous solutions for the truncations have an $L^1$ cluster limit, and a lower bound for Monge–Ampère densities passes to $L^1$ limits of potentials by means of monotone upper envelopes.

Let $0\leq f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$ have positive integral. Its truncations are bounded and nonzero, and we put

14.101
\begin{equation} f_ k=\min (f,k),\qquad b_ h(f)=\lim _{k\to \infty }c_ h(f_ k). \end{equation}

By lemma 14.26.1, the scalars $c_ h(f_ k)$ decrease as $k$ increases, so the limit exists in $[0,c_ h(f_1)]$. We call $b_ h(f)$ the subsolution threshold of $f$; theorem 14.27.2 below justifies the name. The same lemma gives

14.102
\begin{equation} b_ h(f)=\inf \{ c_ h(q):0\leq q\leq f,\ q\in L^\infty , \ \textstyle \int _ Xq\, \mathrm{d}V>0\} . \end{equation}

Indeed, every such $q$ satisfies $q\leq f_ k$ for $k\geq \| q\| _{L^\infty }$, hence $c_ h(q)\geq c_ h(f_ k)\geq b_ h(f)$, while the truncations $f_ k$ are themselves admissible.

The Monge–Ampère operator is not continuous under $L^1$ convergence of potentials, but a lower bound for a Monge–Ampère density survives such convergence. The proof replaces the sequence by monotone upper envelopes, which converge in capacity, and uses the continuity of damped measures, theorem 7.3.1, applied with the Hermitian form $h$ and the weight $t\mapsto \mathrm{e}^{-t}$. For nonpositive $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$, the corresponding damped measure is $\mathrm{e}^{v}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(v)$, with weight zero on the pole set; it has finite mass and depends weakly continuously on $v$ under convergence in capacity.

Lemma 14.27.1. Let $s_ j,s\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ be bounded above by a common constant and satisfy $s_ j\to s$ in $L^1(X)$. Suppose $g_ j,g\geq 0$, $g_ j\to g$ in $L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$, and

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(s_ j)\geq g_ j\, \mathrm{d}V \quad \text{for every }j. \]

Then $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(s)\geq g\, \mathrm{d}V$.

Proof. Subtracting a common constant, which does not change the extended measures, we may assume that all potentials are nonpositive. Pass to a subsequence along which $s_ j\to s$ and $g_ j\to g$ almost everywhere, and set

\[ a_ j=\inf _{\ell \geq j}g_\ell ,\qquad w_ j=\left(\sup _{\ell \geq j}s_\ell \right)^*. \]

Then $0\leq a_ j\uparrow g$ almost everywhere, and $w_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ decreases to $s$.

Step 1: $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(w_ j)\geq a_ j\, \mathrm{d}V$. Fix $j$. For $N\geq j$, the finite maximum $m_ N=\max _{j\leq \ell \leq N}s_\ell $ satisfies $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(m_ N)\geq a_ j\, \mathrm{d}V$: on the set $\{ m_ N=s_\ell \} $, proposition 6.2.1 gives $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(m_ N)\geq \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(s_\ell )\geq g_\ell \, \mathrm{d}V\geq a_ j\, \mathrm{d}V$, and these sets cover $X$. As $N\to \infty $, the functions $m_ N$ increase, are bounded above by zero, and have regularized limit $w_ j$. By lemma 1.8.12, $m_ N\to w_ j$ in capacity, so the damped measures $\mathrm{e}^{m_ N}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(m_ N)$ converge weakly to $\mathrm{e}^{w_ j}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(w_ j)$. Since $m_ N\to w_ j$ almost everywhere and $a_ j\leq g$, dominated convergence gives $\mathrm{e}^{m_ N}a_ j\to \mathrm{e}^{w_ j}a_ j$ in $L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$. Passing to the limit in $\mathrm{e}^{m_ N}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(m_ N)\geq \mathrm{e}^{m_ N}a_ j\, \mathrm{d}V$ gives

\[ \mathrm{e}^{w_ j}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(w_ j) \geq \mathrm{e}^{w_ j}a_ j\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

The pole set of $w_ j$ has zero volume and is not charged by $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(w_ j)$. Dividing by the positive weight off this set gives $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(w_ j)\geq a_ j\, \mathrm{d}V$.

Step 2: passage to $s$. Since $w_ j$ decreases to $s\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$, lemma 1.8.12 gives $w_ j\to s$ in capacity, and the damped measures of $w_ j$ converge weakly to $\mathrm{e}^ s\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(s)$. Moreover $0\leq \mathrm{e}^{w_ j}a_ j\leq g$ and $\mathrm{e}^{w_ j}a_ j\to \mathrm{e}^ sg$ almost everywhere, so dominated convergence and Step 1 give

\[ \mathrm{e}^ s\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(s) \geq \mathrm{e}^ s g\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Dividing by $\mathrm{e}^ s$ on $\{ s>-N\} $ and letting $N\to \infty $ gives $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(s)\geq g\, \mathrm{d}V$ off the pole set of $s$. Both measures give zero mass to the pole set, which proves the lemma. $\square$

Theorem 14.27.2. For every nonnegative $f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$ with positive integral,

\[ b_ h(f)=\max \{ a\geq 0:\text{there exists }u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h) \text{ with }\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u) \geq af\, \mathrm{d}V\} . \]

In particular, $f\, \mathrm{d}V$ admits a positive scaled subsolution if and only if $b_ h(f)>0$.

Proof. If $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)\geq af\, \mathrm{d}V$ with $a>0$, then also $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)\geq af_ k\, \mathrm{d}V$ for every $k$, and lemma 14.26.1 gives $a\leq c_ h(f_ k)$. Letting $k\to \infty $ gives $a\leq b_ h(f)$. If $b_ h(f)=0$, the coefficient zero is attained by the zero potential.

Suppose now that $b=b_ h(f)>0$, and let $u_ k$ be continuous solutions of

\[ \mathrm{MA}_ h(u_ k)=c_ h(f_ k)f_ k\, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad \sup _ Xu_ k=0. \]

By theorem 1.4.2, a subsequence $u_{k_ j}$ converges in $L^1$ to some $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ with $\sup _ Xu=0$. The densities $c_ h(f_{k_ j})f_{k_ j}$ are dominated by $c_ h(f_1)f$ and converge to $bf$ almost everywhere, hence in $L^1$. Lemma 14.27.1 now gives $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)\geq bf\, \mathrm{d}V$, so the maximum is attained at $a=b$. $\square$

When $b_ h(f)>0$, a function $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ with $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)\geq b_ h(f)f\, \mathrm{d}V$ is called a threshold subsolution for $f$, and a threshold subsolution with $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)=b_ h(f)f\, \mathrm{d}V$ is called a threshold solution; the theorem shows that threshold subsolutions exist. Its proof preserves only a lower bound for the density. It shows neither that the normalized solutions $u_ k$ converge in capacity, nor that their limit has finite mass, nor that equality holds in the limit; these are the additional difficulties of the exact equation for an individual density. The threshold does not depend on the choice of truncations. If $(q_ k)$ is any increasing sequence of nonzero bounded densities converging to $f$ almost everywhere, then $c_ h(q_ k)\geq b_ h(f)$ by (14.102); conversely, the second half of the proof, applied to normalized solutions for $q_ k$, gives a subsolution with coefficient $\lim _ kc_ h(q_ k)$, which is therefore at most $b_ h(f)$. Hence $\lim _ kc_ h(q_ k)=b_ h(f)$.

14.27.1 The greatest normalized threshold subsolution

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Suppose $b_ h(f)>0$. The threshold subsolutions with supremum zero form a family that is stable under finite maxima and under $L^1$ limits. Its upper envelope is therefore again a normalized threshold subsolution, and it dominates all the others.

Proposition 14.27.3. Suppose $b=b_ h(f)>0$. The family

\[ \mathcal S_ f=\{ s\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h):\sup _ Xs=0, \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(s)\geq bf\, \mathrm{d}V\} \]

is nonempty and compact in $L^1(X)$. Its upper envelope

\[ \Phi _ f=\left(\sup _{s\in \mathcal S_ f}s\right)^* \]

belongs to $\mathcal S_ f$ and is therefore its greatest member. Moreover, let $u_\lambda $ be the greatest exponential solutions of corollary 14.34.2 and $v_\lambda =u_\lambda -\sup _ Xu_\lambda $. Every $L^1$ limit of a sequence $v_{\lambda _ j}$ with $\lambda _ j\downarrow 0$ belongs to $\mathcal S_ f$, and hence lies below $\Phi _ f$.

Proof. The family is nonempty by theorem 14.27.2. By theorem 1.4.2, an $L^1$ limit of members of $\mathcal S_ f$ is $h$-plurisubharmonic with supremum zero, and lemma 14.27.1, with $g_ j=g=bf$, shows that it satisfies the measure inequality. Thus $\mathcal S_ f$ is a closed subset of the compact set of normalized $h$-plurisubharmonic functions, hence compact. It is also stable under finite maxima: if $s_1,s_2\in \mathcal S_ f$, then $\max (s_1,s_2)$ has supremum zero, and proposition 6.2.1, applied on the sets where the maximum equals $s_1$ or $s_2$, gives $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(\max (s_1,s_2))\geq bf\, \mathrm{d}V$.

By Choquet's lemma there is a sequence $(s_ i)_{i\geq 1}$ in $\mathcal S_ f$ with $(\sup _ is_ i)^*=\Phi _ f$. Fix $s_0\in \mathcal S_ f$. The finite maxima $t_ N=\max (s_0,s_1,\ldots ,s_ N)$ belong to $\mathcal S_ f$, increase with $N$, and satisfy $s_0\leq t_ N\leq 0$; their regularized limit is $\Phi _ f$. Hence $t_ N\to \Phi _ f$ almost everywhere and, by dominated convergence, in $L^1$. Lemma 14.27.1 gives $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(\Phi _ f)\geq bf\, \mathrm{d}V$, and $s_0\leq \Phi _ f\leq 0$ forces $\sup _ X\Phi _ f=0$. Thus $\Phi _ f\in \mathcal S_ f$, and by construction it dominates every member of $\mathcal S_ f$.

Finally, suppose $v_{\lambda _ j}\to v$ in $L^1$ with $\lambda _ j\downarrow 0$. The measures $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(v_{\lambda _ j})=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda _ ju_{\lambda _ j}}f\, \mathrm{d}V$ have densities converging in $L^1$ to $bf$, by corollary 14.34.2. Applying lemma 14.27.1 to these densities and potentials shows that $v\in \mathcal S_ f$. $\square$

The proposition gives a greatest normalized threshold subsolution, but not the equation $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(\Phi _ f)=bf\, \mathrm{d}V$; in particular, it does not show that $\Phi _ f$ has finite mass.

Proposition 14.27.4. Let $0\leq f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$ have positive integral. If $f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$ for some $p>1$, then $b_ h(f)=c_ h(f)$. In general,

\[ b_ h(f)\geq \frac{v_-(h)}{\int _ Xf\, \mathrm{d}V}. \]

If $h$ is Kähler, then $b_ h(f)=\int _ Xh^ n/\int _ Xf\, \mathrm{d}V$.

Proof. Suppose that $f\in L^ p$ with $p>1$, and let $v$ be a continuous solution of $\mathrm{MA}_ h(v)=c_ h(f)f\, \mathrm{d}V$. It is a subsolution with coefficient $c_ h(f)$, so theorem 14.27.2 gives $c_ h(f)\leq b_ h(f)$. Conversely, lemma 14.26.1, applied to a threshold subsolution, gives $b_ h(f)\leq c_ h(f)$.

For general $f$, the continuous solution for $f_ k$ has total mass $c_ h(f_ k)\int _ Xf_ k\, \mathrm{d}V$, which is at least $v_-(h)$ by the definition of the lower mass. Letting $k\to \infty $ and using monotone convergence gives the lower bound for $b_ h(f)$. If $h$ is Kähler, Stokes' theorem for bounded potentials gives instead the identity $c_ h(f_ k)\int _ Xf_ k\, \mathrm{d}V=\int _ Xh^ n$, and the same limit proves the last assertion. $\square$

14.27.2 Smooth comparison tests

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The infimum (14.102) runs over bounded measurable densities below $f$. One may ask whether smooth densities suffice to compute the threshold. A smooth test $g$ is in general not below $f$, so we compare it with $f$ through the ratio $\| g/f\| _{L^\infty }$. Thus let $f$ be a probability density bounded below by a positive constant, and define

\[ C_ h^{\mathrm{sm}}(f)=\inf _ g c_ h(g)\| g/f\| _{L^\infty }, \]

where $g$ ranges over smooth strictly positive probability densities. For each such $g$, with $R=\| g/f\| _{L^\infty }$, a subsolution with coefficient $a$ for $f\, \mathrm{d}V$ is a subsolution with coefficient $a/R$ for $g\, \mathrm{d}V$, so $a\leq Rc_ h(g)$ by lemma 14.26.1. Hence $b_ h(f)\leq C_ h^{\mathrm{sm}}(f)$, with equality if $f$ is smooth (take $g=f$). When the lower mass vanishes, the two quantities can differ even in their positivity.

Theorem 14.27.5. If $v_-(h)=0$, there is a probability density $f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$ bounded below by a positive constant such that

\[ b_ h(f)=0<C_ h^{\mathrm{sm}}(f). \]

Thus $f\, \mathrm{d}V$ admits no positive scaled subsolution, although $c_ h(g)\| g/f\| _{L^\infty }$ is bounded below by a positive constant for all smooth strictly positive probability densities $g$.

Proof. We construct $f=(1+Q)/M$, where $Q\geq 0$ concentrates on closed nowhere dense sets in such a way that it has essential infimum zero on every nonempty open set.

Step 1: the pieces. Let $(U_ i)_{i\geq 1}$ be a countable basis of nonempty open subsets of $X$. We construct inductively numbers $\alpha _ j>0$, nonzero bounded densities $q_ j$, and closed nowhere dense sets $K_ j\subset X$ such that

\[ \int _ Xq_ j\, \mathrm{d}V\leq 1,\qquad q_ j=0\text{ outside }K_ j,\qquad c_ h(q_ j)\leq \alpha _ j2^{-j}. \]

Start with $\alpha _1=1/4$. Once $\alpha _ j$ has been chosen, theorem 26.14.3, theorem 26.1.3 and $v_-(h)=0$ give a smooth strictly positive probability density $g_ j$ with $c_ h(g_ j)<\alpha _ j2^{-j}$. Removing from $X$ an open dense set of small volume, we obtain a closed nowhere dense set $K_ j$ for which $q_ j=g_ j\mathbf1_{K_ j}$ is nonzero and, since $g_ j$ is bounded, arbitrarily close to $g_ j$ in $L^2$. By lemma 14.26.2 with $p=2$, we can therefore choose $K_ j$ so that $c_ h(q_ j)\leq \alpha _ j2^{-j}$.

After $K_1,\ldots ,K_ N$ have been chosen, put

\[ m_ N=\min _{1\leq i\leq N} \mathrm{d}V\left(U_ i\setminus \bigcup _{j=1}^ NK_ j\right)>0; \]

each set inside the minimum is open and nonempty, since a finite union of closed nowhere dense sets is closed and nowhere dense. Choose

\[ 0<\alpha _{N+1}\leq \min \{ \alpha _ N/2,\ 2^{-N-2}m_ N\} , \]

and continue. In particular,

\[ \sum _{j>N}\alpha _ j\leq 2\alpha _{N+1} \leq 2^{-N-1}m_ N. \]

Step 2: the concentrating part. The function $Q=\sum _ j\alpha _ jq_ j$ is nonnegative and integrable. We claim that its essential infimum on every nonempty open set is zero. Since every nonempty open set contains some $U_ i$, it suffices to consider $U_ i$. Take $N\geq i$ and remove $K_1,\ldots ,K_ N$ from $U_ i$. On the remaining set, whose volume is at least $m_ N$, the first $N$ summands of $Q$ vanish. By Markov's inequality, the set where the tail $\sum _{j>N}\alpha _ jq_ j$ exceeds $2^{-N}$ has volume at most

\[ 2^ N\sum _{j>N}\alpha _ j\int _ Xq_ j\, \mathrm{d}V \leq m_ N/2. \]

Hence $Q\leq 2^{-N}$ on a subset of $U_ i$ of positive volume. Letting $N\to \infty $ proves the claim.

Step 3: $b_ h(f)=0$. Set $M=\int _ X(1+Q)\, \mathrm{d}V$ and $f=(1+Q)/M$, a probability density with $f\geq 1/M$. If $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)\geq af\, \mathrm{d}V$ with $a>0$, then $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)\geq (a\alpha _ j/M)q_ j\, \mathrm{d}V$, and lemma 14.26.1 gives

\[ \frac{a\alpha _ j}{M}\leq c_ h(q_ j)\leq \alpha _ j2^{-j} \quad \text{for every }j. \]

This is impossible. Hence $b_ h(f)=0$ by theorem 14.27.2.

Step 4: $C_ h^{\mathrm{sm}}(f)>0$. Let $g$ be a smooth strictly positive probability density and put $R=\| g/f\| _{L^\infty }$. Then $g\leq R(1+Q)/M$ almost everywhere, and this forces $g\leq R/M$ everywhere. Otherwise, by continuity, $g>R(1+\varepsilon )/M$ on a nonempty open set for some $\varepsilon >0$, while $Q<\varepsilon $ on a subset of positive volume of this set by Step 2, a contradiction. The order and homogeneity of the scalar in lemma 14.26.1 now give $c_ h(g)\geq c_ h(R/M)=(M/R)c_ h(1)$, that is,

\[ R c_ h(g)\geq M c_ h(1). \]

The constant density $g=1$ attains this value, since $\operatorname *{ess\, inf}_ XQ=0$ gives $\| 1/f\| _{L^\infty }=M$. Thus $C_ h^{\mathrm{sm}}(f)=M c_ h(1)>0$, as required. $\square$

The point is that a smooth test $g$ with $\| g/f\| _{L^\infty }\leq R$ is controlled by the essential infimum of $f$ on open sets. It therefore cannot detect an increase of $f$ that leaves this essential infimum unchanged on every open set, whereas bounded measurable tests below $f$ do detect it. The theorem concerns only the existence of subsolutions. Exact solutions of $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)=b_ h(f)f\, \mathrm{d}V$, the singularity class in which they should be sought, and compactness of their normalized solutions are further problems; see 33.1.1.

14.28 Gauduchon tests for integrable densities

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

For a smooth strictly positive probability density, the normalization scalar is an infimum of Gauduchon quotients by theorem 26.1.3. We extend this dual formula to the threshold of an arbitrary integrable density. A uniform bound for all Gauduchon tests in a single $L^ r$ norm allows the passage from smooth to bounded densities, and monotone truncation then reaches all integrable densities, including those with zero threshold. The formula is most naturally written for the $n$th root of the density.

Recall that a Hermitian form $\gamma $ is Gauduchon if $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\gamma ^{n-1})=0$ (definition 4.1.1); in dimension one every Hermitian form is Gauduchon. For a Gauduchon form $\gamma $ and a nonnegative measurable function $p$, put

\[ L_{h,\gamma }(p)= \frac{\displaystyle \int _ Xp(\mathrm{d}V/\gamma ^ n)^{1/n}\gamma ^ n}{\displaystyle \int _ Xh\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}} =\int _ Xp\, a_\gamma \, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad a_\gamma =\frac{(\gamma ^ n/\mathrm{d}V)^{(n-1)/n}}{\int _ Xh\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}}. \]

Since the coefficient $a_\gamma $ is smooth and strictly positive, each individual test $L_{h,\gamma }$ is finite on $L^ n$; the supremum over $\gamma $ may be infinite. In dimension one the test is $\int _ Xp\, \mathrm{d}V/\int _ Xh$. For real or complex $p\in L^ n(\mathrm{d}V)$, define the root functional

\[ \rho _ h(p)=b_ h(|p|^ n)^{-1/n}\quad (p\ne 0),\qquad \rho _ h(0)=0, \]

with the convention $0^{-1/n}=+\infty $.

Theorem 14.28.1. For every real or complex $p\in L^ n(\mathrm{d}V)$,

14.103
\begin{equation} \rho _ h(p)=\sup _{\gamma \text{ Gauduchon}}L_{h,\gamma }(|p|). \end{equation}

Equivalently, for every nonzero nonnegative $f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$,

14.104
\begin{equation} b_ h(f)^{1/n}= \inf _{\gamma \text{ Gauduchon}} \frac{\displaystyle \int _ Xh\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}}{\displaystyle \int _ X(f\, \mathrm{d}V/\gamma ^ n)^{1/n}\gamma ^ n}. \end{equation}

Both sides of (14.103) may be $+\infty $, and both sides of (14.104) may vanish.

Proof. It suffices to prove (14.103) for $p\geq 0$, since both sides depend only on $|p|$; the case $p=0$ is immediate.

Step 1: smooth strictly positive $p$. Apply theorem 26.1.3, with background $h$, to the probability measure $\mu =p^ n\mathrm{d}V/Z$, where $Z=\int _ Xp^ n\, \mathrm{d}V$. By homogeneity (lemma 14.26.1) its scalar is $c_ h(\mu )=Zc_ h(p^ n)$, while the denominator of each Gauduchon quotient is $Z^{-1/n}\int _ Xp(\mathrm{d}V/\gamma ^ n)^{1/n}\gamma ^ n$. The factors of $Z$ cancel, and the dual formula becomes

\[ c_ h(p^ n)^{-1/n}=\sup _\gamma L_{h,\gamma }(p). \]

Since $b_ h(p^ n)=c_ h(p^ n)$ by proposition 14.27.4, this proves (14.103) for such $p$.

Step 2: a uniform bound for the tests. The lower-density estimate of theorem 10.3.3, with exponent two, gives a constant $m_ h>0$ such that

\[ c_ h(p^ n)\| p^ n\| _{L^2}\geq m_ h \]

for smooth strictly positive $p$. By Step 1, therefore,

14.105
\begin{equation} L_{h,\gamma }(p)\leq m_ h^{-1/n}\| p\| _{L^{2n}} \quad \text{for every }\gamma . \end{equation}

Approximating nonnegative functions in $L^{2n}$ by smooth strictly positive ones extends this bound to all nonnegative $p\in L^{2n}$. Since $a_\gamma >0$, duality gives $\| a_\gamma \| _{L^{2n/(2n-1)}}\leq m_ h^{-1/n}$ for every $\gamma $. In particular, $p\mapsto \sup _\gamma L_{h,\gamma }(p)$ is Lipschitz on the cone of nonnegative functions in $L^{2n}$, with the same constant.

Step 3: bounded $p$. Let $p\geq 0$ be bounded and nonzero, and approximate it in $L^{2n}$ by smooth strictly positive functions $p_ j$. By Hölder's inequality, $p_ j^ n\to p^ n$ in $L^2$, so lemma 14.26.2 gives

\[ c_ h(p_ j^ n)\longrightarrow c_ h(p^ n)>0. \]

By the Lipschitz bound derived from (14.105),

\[ \sup _\gamma L_{h,\gamma }(p_ j) \longrightarrow \sup _\gamma L_{h,\gamma }(p). \]

Passing to the limit in the identity of Step 1 for $p_ j$, and using $b_ h(p^ n)=c_ h(p^ n)$ (proposition 14.27.4), we obtain (14.103) for bounded $p$.

Step 4: general $p$. For nonnegative $p\in L^ n$, put $p_ k=\min (p,k)$. Then $p_ k^ n=\min (p^ n,k^ n)$ are truncations of $p^ n$, so the definition of $b_ h$ and Step 3 give

\[ \rho _ h(p)=\lim _{k\to \infty }c_ h(p_ k^ n)^{-1/n} =\sup _ k\sup _\gamma L_{h,\gamma }(p_ k). \]

For each fixed $\gamma $, monotone convergence gives $\sup _ kL_{h,\gamma }(p_ k)=L_{h,\gamma }(p)$. Interchanging the two suprema proves (14.103). Finally, every denominator in (14.104) is positive; taking reciprocals in (14.103) with $p=f^{1/n}$ proves (14.104), including the case $b_ h(f)=0$. $\square$

For a nonsmooth density we do not claim that the infimum in (14.104) is attained by a Gauduchon form; attainment by Gauduchon currents is studied in section 26.9.

For $n\geq 2$, the coefficient of the test has norm

\[ \| a_\gamma \| _{L^{n/(n-1)}} =\frac{(\int _ X\gamma ^ n)^{(n-1)/n}}{\int _ Xh\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}} \]

in the exponent dual to $n$. By (26.89) in theorem 26.14.3, positive lower mass is therefore equivalent to a uniform bound for these norms, that is, for all tests on $L^ n$ simultaneously. Without positive lower mass, Step 2 of the proof still bounds the coefficients uniformly in the weaker norm of $L^{2n/(2n-1)}$, on every compact Hermitian manifold. This suffices to pass from smooth to bounded densities, and monotone truncation then treats all integrable densities.

Proposition 14.28.2. Let $f$ be a nonzero nonnegative function in $L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$ and let $p\in L^ n(\mathrm{d}V)$ be real or complex. If $h_1,h_2$ are Hermitian forms on $X$ and $m h_1\leq h_2\leq M h_1$ for positive constants $m,M$, then

\[ m^ n b_{h_1}(f)\leq b_{h_2}(f)\leq M^ n b_{h_1}(f), \qquad M^{-1}\rho _{h_1}(p)\leq \rho _{h_2}(p)\leq m^{-1}\rho _{h_1}(p). \]

If $h_2-h_1=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a$ for a smooth real function $a$, then $b_{h_1}(f)=b_{h_2}(f)$ and $\rho _{h_1}(p)=\rho _{h_2}(p)$.

Proof. For every Gauduchon form $\gamma $, the numerators in (14.104) satisfy

\[ m\int _ Xh_1\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}\leq \int _ Xh_2\wedge \gamma ^{n-1} \leq M\int _ Xh_1\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}, \]

while the denominators do not depend on the background. Taking the infimum over $\gamma $ and then $n$th powers proves the bounds for the thresholds; taking reciprocal $n$th roots gives the bounds for $\rho $, with the stated conventions at zero and infinity. If $h_2-h_1=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a$, the numerators agree by Stokes' theorem, since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\gamma ^{n-1})=0$. $\square$

Any two Hermitian metrics on the compact manifold $X$ are comparable. Hence the positivity of $b_ h(f)$, and the set of real or complex $p\in L^ n$ with $\rho _ h(p)<+\infty $, do not depend on the choice of $h$. The dependence on the reference volume is equally simple. If $\mathrm{d}V$ is replaced by $\mathrm{d}V'=\sigma \, \mathrm{d}V$ with a smooth function $\sigma >0$, the measure $f\, \mathrm{d}V$ has density $f'=f/\sigma $ with respect to $\mathrm{d}V'$, and theorem 14.27.2 shows that the threshold of $f'$ relative to $\mathrm{d}V'$ equals $b_ h(f)$. Correspondingly, the map $p\mapsto \sigma ^{-1/n}p$ from $L^ n(\mathrm{d}V)$ to $L^ n(\mathrm{d}V')$ preserves both the $L^ n$ norm and the root functional.

14.29 Exact equations on products with zero lower mass

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Positive lower mass guarantees solvability for all measures, but an individual density can have a positive threshold and an exact equation even when the lower mass vanishes. We construct explicit examples on products. Let $(X_0,h_0)$ be a connected compact Hermitian manifold of dimension $m\geq 1$ with $v_-(h_0)=0$. On its product with a projective space, a singular potential pulled back from the projective factor solves an explicit equation whose density has a sublogarithmic singularity. The coefficient is computed from the normalization scalars of bounded truncations, the equation itself follows from the product structure of the Monge–Ampère measure, and the product background again has zero lower mass.

Throughout this section, $Y=\mathbb P^ k$ with $k\geq 1$, $\kappa $ is the Fubini–Study form normalized by $\int _ Y\kappa ^ k=1$, and $X=X_0\times Y$, a compact complex manifold of dimension $N=m+k$ with the Hermitian background $H=h_0+\kappa $. Forms and functions on the factors are identified with their pullbacks to $X$. Put

\[ V_0=\int _{X_0}h_0^ m,\qquad C=\binom Nm,\qquad \mu =V_0^{-1}h_0^ m\wedge \kappa ^ k,\qquad R=\frac{\mu }{\mathrm{d}V}, \]

where $\mathrm{d}V$ is any smooth positive probability volume form on $X$. Then $\mu $ is a smooth positive probability volume form, and $R$ is smooth and bounded above and below by positive constants. The normalization scalar $c_ H$ and the threshold $b_ H$ are those of section 14.26, section 14.27, now on the $N$-dimensional manifold $X$ with background $H$.

For $0<\alpha <1$ define, on $Y$,

\[ \psi ([z_0:\cdots :z_ k])=\log \frac{\sum _{i=1}^ k|z_ i|^2}{\sum _{i=0}^ k|z_ i|^2}, \qquad u_\alpha =1-(1-\psi )^\alpha . \]

The function $\psi $ is smooth on $Y\setminus \{ o\} $, where $o=[1:0:\cdots :0]$, and tends to $-\infty $ at $o$; moreover $\psi \leq 0$, with equality exactly on the hyperplane $\{ z_0=0\} $. Thus $u_\alpha \leq 0$, and $u_\alpha $ has a single pole at $o$, where $u_\alpha (o)=-\infty $.

Lemma 14.29.1. The function $u_\alpha $ is $\kappa $-plurisubharmonic with supremum zero, and

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\kappa (u_\alpha )=q_\alpha \kappa ^ k, \qquad \int _ Yq_\alpha \kappa ^ k=1. \]

The density $q_\alpha $ is smooth and strictly positive on $Y\setminus \{ o\} $, belongs to $L^1(\kappa ^ k)$, and belongs to no $L^ p(\kappa ^ k)$ with $p>1$. If we set $q_\alpha (o)=+\infty $, then every truncation $\min (q_\alpha ,s)$, $s>0$, is strictly positive and continuous on $Y$.

Proof. In homogeneous coordinates, $\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log \sum _{i=1}^ k|z_ i|^2\geq 0$ on $Y\setminus \{ o\} $. The function $\chi (t)=1-(1-t)^\alpha $, $t\leq 0$, is increasing and convex, with $0<\chi '\leq \alpha $. Hence, off $o$,

\[ \kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\alpha \geq (1-\chi '(\psi ))\kappa +\chi '(\psi )(\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi ) \geq (1-\alpha )\kappa . \]

Since $u_\alpha $ is bounded above, it extends across $o$ as a $\kappa $-plurisubharmonic function, with value $-\infty $ at $o$. On the hyperplane $\{ z_0=0\} $ we have $\psi =0$, hence $u_\alpha =0$, so $\sup _ Yu_\alpha =0$.

To compute the measure, work in the affine chart $z_0=1$ and put $r=\sum _{i=1}^ k|z_ i|^2$. On this chart $\kappa =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log (1+r)$ and $\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\alpha =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F$, where $s(r)=1-\log (r/(1+r))$ and

\[ F(r)=\log (1+r)+1-s(r)^\alpha ,\qquad Q(r)=rF'(r)=\frac{r+\alpha s(r)^{\alpha -1}}{1+r}. \]

For a radial function, the Levi form has the tangential eigenvalue $F'(r)=Q(r)/r$, with multiplicity $k-1$, and the radial eigenvalue

\[ Q'(r)=\frac{1-\alpha s(r)^{\alpha -1}+\alpha (1-\alpha )r^{-1}s(r)^{\alpha -2}}{(1+r)^2}>0. \]

Integrating in the radial variable gives $\int _{\{ a<r<b\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^ k=Q(b)^ k-Q(a)^ k$; the constant of proportionality is fixed by the case $F=\log (1+r)$, for which $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^ k=\kappa ^ k$ has mass one on the chart and $Q(r)=r/(1+r)$. Since again $Q(0)=0$ and $Q(\infty )=1$, the punctured chart has mass one. The complement of the chart is a hyperplane, which has zero volume, and the non-pluripolar measure does not charge $o$. Hence $\int _ Yq_\alpha \kappa ^ k=1$.

Relative to $\kappa ^ k$, whose tangential and radial eigenvalues are $1/(1+r)$ and $1/(1+r)^2$, the density on this chart is

\[ q_\alpha (r)=(1+r)^{k+1}(Q(r)/r)^{k-1}Q'(r) \asymp r^{-k}(1-\log r)^{-1-k(1-\alpha )}\quad (r\downarrow 0). \]

Near $r=0$ the smooth volume is comparable to $r^{k-1}\mathrm{d}r$ in the radial variable, so $q_\alpha $ is integrable. For $p>1$, the substitution $L=1-\log r$ turns the integral of $q_\alpha ^ p$ into the integral of $\mathrm{e}^{k(p-1)L}$ times a power of $L$, which diverges. The same asymptotics show that $q_\alpha (r)\to +\infty $ uniformly as $r\downarrow 0$, so every truncation $\min (q_\alpha ,s)$ is continuous at $o$. Finally, $q_\alpha \geq (1-\alpha )^ k>0$ by the lower bound $\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\alpha \geq (1-\alpha )\kappa $. $\square$

Lemma 14.29.2. Let $g$ be a strictly positive continuous function on $Y$, and put $M_ g=\int _ Yg\kappa ^ k$. Then

\[ c_ H(Rg)=\frac{CV_0}{M_ g}. \]

Proof. Suppose first that $g$ is smooth. Since $\kappa $ is Kähler, theorem 9.1.1 on $Y$ gives a smooth $\phi _ g$ with $A_ g=\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi _ g>0$ and $A_ g^ k=(g/M_ g)\kappa ^ k$; the scalar is determined by integration, since both sides have mass one. On $X$, since $h_0^{m+1}=0$ and $A_ g^{k+1}=0$ for dimensional reasons,

\[ (H+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi _ g)^ N=C h_0^ m\wedge A_ g^ k =(CV_0/M_ g)Rg\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Uniqueness of the scalar gives $c_ H(Rg)=CV_0/M_ g$. For continuous $g>0$, choose smooth $g_ l>0$ converging uniformly to $g$. Since $g$ has a positive minimum, $(1-\varepsilon _ l)g\leq g_ l\leq (1+\varepsilon _ l)g$ for some $\varepsilon _ l\in (0,1)$ tending to zero. The order and homogeneity of the scalar in lemma 14.26.1 give

\[ (1-\varepsilon _ l)c_ H(Rg_ l)\leq c_ H(Rg) \leq (1+\varepsilon _ l)c_ H(Rg_ l). \]

Since $M_{g_ l}\to M_ g$, the smooth case proves the formula. $\square$

Theorem 14.29.3. The Hermitian form $H$ is not closed and satisfies $v_-(H)=0$. For every $0<\alpha <1$, the function $f=Rq_\alpha $ is a probability density in $L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$, belonging to no $L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$ with $p>1$, and

\[ b_ H(f)=CV_0>0,\qquad \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(u_\alpha )=b_ H(f)f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

The potential $u_\alpha $, pulled back to $X$, has supremum zero and finite mass $CV_0$. It belongs to $\mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(X,H,0)$, and $P_{\delta H}(u_\alpha )\not\equiv -\infty $ for every $0<\delta <1$.

Proof. Step 1: the threshold. Write $a=\min _ XR>0$, $A=\max _ XR$, and $M_ s=\int _ Y\min (q_\alpha ,s)\kappa ^ k$ for $s>0$. By lemma 14.29.1, the truncations $\min (q_\alpha ,s)$ are continuous and strictly positive, and $M_ s\uparrow 1$ as $s\to \infty $. The truncations of $f$ satisfy

\[ R\min (q_\alpha ,j/A)\leq \min (Rq_\alpha ,j) \leq R\min (q_\alpha ,j/a). \]

Hence lemma 14.29.2 and the monotonicity of the scalar give

\[ \frac{CV_0}{M_{j/a}}\leq c_ H(\min (f,j)) \leq \frac{CV_0}{M_{j/A}}. \]

Both bounds tend to $CV_0$ as $j\to \infty $, so $b_ H(f)=CV_0$, whatever the reference volume $\mathrm{d}V$.

Step 2: the equation. For $u_{\alpha ,j}=\max (u_\alpha ,-j)$, the binomial expansion of bounded local products, with factor forms of degrees $m$ and $k$, gives

\[ \mathrm{MA}_ H(u_{\alpha ,j}) =C h_0^ m\wedge \mathrm{MA}_\kappa (u_{\alpha ,j}). \]

Multiplying by $\mathbf1_{\{ u_\alpha >-j\} }$ and letting $j\to \infty $, as in the definition (6.1) of the extended measure, we obtain

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(u_\alpha )=Ch_0^ m\wedge q_\alpha \kappa ^ k =CV_0\, q_\alpha \mu =CV_0f\, \mathrm{d}V, \]

a measure of mass $CV_0$. The bounds $a\leq R\leq A$ transfer the integrability properties of $q_\alpha $ to $f$.

Step 3: lower mass and nonclosedness. Pull back bounded $h_0$-plurisubharmonic functions whose masses on $X_0$ tend to zero. The same expansion shows that their $H$-masses are $C$ times their masses on $X_0$, so $v_-(H)=0$. If $h_0$ were closed, Stokes' theorem would give every bounded $h_0$-plurisubharmonic function the mass $V_0>0$, contradicting $v_-(h_0)=0$. Hence $h_0$, and therefore $H$, is not closed.

Step 4: the envelopes. Choose $\alpha <\beta <1$. For $b\geq 1$ and $\varepsilon >0$ with $\varepsilon \beta \leq 1$, the number

\[ K_ b=\sup _{s\geq 1}\{ b s^\alpha -\varepsilon s^\beta \} -b \]

is finite, since $\beta >\alpha $. The function $g_ b=-\varepsilon (1-\psi )^\beta -K_ b$ on $Y$ satisfies $\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g_ b\geq (1-\varepsilon \beta )\kappa \geq 0$, by the chain-rule computation in the proof of lemma 14.29.1, applied to the increasing convex function $t\mapsto -\varepsilon (1-t)^\beta $, whose derivative is at most $\varepsilon \beta $ for $t\leq 0$. With $L=1-\psi \geq 1$, the inequality $g_ b\leq bu_\alpha $ reads $bL^\alpha -\varepsilon L^\beta -b\leq K_ b$, so it holds on $Y\setminus \{ o\} $, and also at $o$, where both sides are $-\infty $. Thus the pullback of $g_ b$ belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,H)$ and lies below $bu_\alpha $. Since $b\geq 1$ is arbitrary, $u_\alpha \in \mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(X,H,0)$ by definition 13.4.1. For $0<\delta <1$, take $b=1$ and $\varepsilon \beta \leq \delta $. Then $g_1$ is $\delta H$-plurisubharmonic and lies below $u_\alpha $, so $P_{\delta H}(u_\alpha )\geq g_1$ is not identically $-\infty $. $\square$

Corollary 14.29.4. Let $X_0$ be the elliptic threefold of theorem 26.18.1, with the balanced metric $\omega $ of proposition 26.18.7. On $X_0\times \mathbb P^2$, with background $H=\omega +\kappa $, the densities $f=Rq_\alpha $ of the preceding theorem satisfy

\[ v_-(H)=0,\qquad b_ H(f)=240\pi , \qquad \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(u_\alpha )=240\pi f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Proof. Theorem 26.18.1 gives $v_-(\omega )=0$, and proposition 26.18.7 gives $V_0=24\pi $. The product theorem applies with $m=3$, $k=2$ and $C=\binom 53=10$. $\square$

These examples show that an individual density can have a positive threshold and an exact equation even though the background has zero lower mass. The exactness comes from the product structure. For $k\geq 2$, the radial flux also identifies the normalized exponential limit in theorem 14.35.2; the first moment determines whether scalar centering has a finite limit. 33.1.1 asks for an exact equation for every integrable density with positive threshold.

14.29.1 Closed factors and arbitrary integrable densities

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The preceding examples use an explicit potential on projective space. On a closed factor, the mass bound from Stokes' theorem instead makes every threshold subsolution exact. This gives equations for arbitrary integrable densities on that factor, with no assumption that they are bounded below.

For this subsection let $(X_0,h_0)$ be any connected compact Hermitian manifold of dimension $m\geq 1$, and let $(Y,\kappa )$ be connected compact Kähler of dimension $k\geq 2$, normalized by $\int _ Y\kappa ^ k=1$. On $X=X_0\times Y$ put

\[ N=m+k,\qquad H=h_0+\kappa ,\qquad C=\binom Nm,\qquad V_0=\int _{X_0}h_0^ m,\qquad c_0=CV_0. \]

Forms and functions on the factors are pulled back when used on $X$. For any smooth positive probability volume form $\mathrm dV$ on $X$, put

\[ \mu =V_0^{-1}h_0^ m\wedge \kappa ^ k,\qquad R=\frac\mu {\mathrm dV},\qquad a=\min _ XR>0,\qquad A=\max _ XR. \]

Theorem 14.29.5. For every nonzero nonnegative $g\in L^1(Y,\kappa ^ k)$, with $M_ g=\int _ Yg\kappa ^ k$, one has

\[ b_ H(Rg)=\frac{c_0}{M_ g}. \]

There is $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\kappa )$ with $\sup _ Yv=0$ and $\int _ Y\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\kappa (v)=1$ whose pullback $u$ satisfies

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(u) =\frac{c_0}{M_ g}Rg\, \mathrm dV,\qquad \int _ X\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(u)=c_0. \]

If $v_-(h_0)=0$, then $v_-(H)=0$ and $H$ is not closed.

Proof. We first compute the scalars of bounded densities, then use the closed factor's mass bound to identify the equation for an unbounded density.

Step 1: bounded densities. If $g$ is bounded and nonzero, theorem 10.1.1 on $Y$ gives a bounded continuous $v$ with $\mathrm{MA}_\kappa (v)=c_\kappa (g)g\kappa ^ k$. Closed Stokes gives $c_\kappa (g)=1/M_ g$. The bounded product expansion gives

\[ \mathrm{MA}_ H(v)=C h_0^ m\wedge \mathrm{MA}_\kappa (v) =\frac{c_0}{M_ g}Rg\, \mathrm dV. \]

Uniqueness of the bounded normalization scalar therefore gives $c_ H(Rg)=c_0/M_ g$, including densities with zeros.

Step 2: the threshold. For general $g\in L^1$, the original truncations on $X$ satisfy

\[ R\min (g,j/A)\leq \min (Rg,j)\leq R\min (g,j/a). \]

By Step 1 and lemma 14.26.1,

\[ \frac{c_0}{\int _ Y\min (g,j/a)\kappa ^ k} \leq c_ H(\min (Rg,j)) \leq \frac{c_0}{\int _ Y\min (g,j/A)\kappa ^ k}. \]

Both bounds tend to $c_0/M_ g$.

Step 3: the exact equation. By proposition 14.27.4, $b_\kappa (g)=1/M_ g$. Thus theorem 14.27.2 on $Y$ gives $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\kappa )$ with supremum zero and

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\kappa (v)\geq (g/M_ g)\kappa ^ k. \]

For $v_ j=\max (v,-j)$, closed Stokes gives $\int _ Y\mathrm{MA}_\kappa (v_ j)=1$. The defining non-pluripolar truncations consequently give $\int _ Y\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\kappa (v)\leq 1$. The prescribed lower measure has mass one, so equality holds both in mass and in the measure inequality.

The pullback $u$ is $H$-plurisubharmonic, and $\max (u,-j)$ is the pullback of $v_ j$. Multiplying the bounded product expansion by the pullback of $\mathbf1_{\{ v>-j\} }$ and passing to the defining non-pluripolar limit gives

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(u) =C h_0^ m\wedge \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\kappa (v) =\frac{c_0}{M_ g}Rg\, \mathrm dV. \]

Its mass is $c_0$. Finally, when $v_-(h_0)=0$, pulling back bounded potentials from $X_0$ whose masses tend to zero proves $v_-(H)=0$ by the same expansion. Closedness of $h_0$ would contradict that vanishing by Stokes' theorem, so $H$ is not closed. $\square$

On the elliptic threefold of theorem 26.18.1, with the balanced metric of proposition 26.18.7, the product with $\mathbb P^2$ has $c_0=240\pi $. Thus every probability density from the projective factor has this threshold and a finite-mass threshold solution. The theorem concerns factor densities; it does not give an exact equation for an arbitrary density on $X$ or identify limits of normalized exponential solutions.

14.30 Singular measures on products with a curve

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The closed-factor equations above concern integrable densities. A curve factor also allows a direct test on a measure: its logarithmic potential determines an exact equation, and continuity of that potential supplies the energy control needed for every positive exponent. The resulting product measure may be singular with respect to smooth volume, even when the solution is continuous.

Let $Y=\mathbb P^1$ and let $\kappa $ be the Fubini–Study form of mass one. For the chordal distance $d_{\mathrm{ch}}$, put

\[ G(x,y)=\log d_{\mathrm{ch}}(x,y)^2+1,\qquad K(x,y)=1-G(x,y)\geq 0. \]

For a Borel probability $\mu $ on $Y$, define

14.106
\begin{equation} Q_\mu (x)=\int _ YG(x,y)\, \mathrm d\mu (y). \end{equation}

We say that $\mu $ is non-polar if it gives every polar Borel set mass zero.

14.30.1 The logarithmic potential and its measure trace

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Lemma 14.30.1. For every probability $\mu $, the function $Q_\mu $ is $\kappa $-plurisubharmonic, has $\kappa $-mean zero, and satisfies

\[ \kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}Q_\mu =\mu ,\qquad Q_\mu \leq 1. \]

If $\mu $ is non-polar, then $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\kappa (Q_\mu )=\mu $. Every $p\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\kappa )$ with $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\kappa (p)\geq \mu $ is of the form $Q_\mu +C$.

Proof. The Poincaré–Lelong formula gives $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_ xG(x,y)=\delta _ y-\kappa $. After a rotation sending $y$ to $[1:0]$, the variable $t=d_{\mathrm{ch}}(x,y)^2$ is uniformly distributed on $[0,1]$ for the measure $\kappa $. Thus

\[ \int _ YG(x,y)\kappa (x)=\int _0^1(\log t+1)\, \mathrm dt=0, \qquad \sup _ y\int _ Y|G(x,y)|\kappa (x)<\infty . \]

The continuous potentials $\int \max (G(x,y),-L)\, \mathrm d\mu (y)$ are $\kappa $-plurisubharmonic by lemma 1.3.1 and averaging, and decrease to $Q_\mu $. Tonelli's theorem gives its integrability and mean zero; the decreasing-limit property gives its canonical values. Distributional Fubini proves the curvature identity.

In degree one the extended measure of $p$ is

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\kappa (p) =\mathbf1_{\{ p>-\infty \} }(\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p). \]

The pole set of $Q_\mu $ is polar, so non-polarity gives its asserted measure. For any $p$, distributional Stokes gives the full current $\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p$ mass one, and its extended measure has mass at most one. Domination of the probability $\mu $ therefore forces both measures to equal $\mu $. The distribution $p-Q_\mu $ is harmonic on the compact connected curve, hence constant, and canonical equality gives the assertion everywhere. $\square$

For real Sobolev functions on $Y$ use the Dirichlet form

\[ E(p,q)=\int _ Y\mathrm dp\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}q. \]

It is symmetric and nonnegative on the diagonal. The space $H^1(Y)$ and its norm are taken for the Fubini–Study Riemannian metric.

Lemma 14.30.2. Suppose $Q=Q_\mu $ is finite and continuous. Then $\mu $ is non-polar, $Q\in H^1(Y)$, and smooth restriction extends to a continuous linear map

\[ \operatorname {Tr}_\mu :H^1(Y)\longrightarrow L^1(\mu ). \]

For every $h\in H^1(Y)$,

14.107
\begin{equation} \int _ Y\operatorname {Tr}_\mu h\, \mathrm d\mu =\int _ Yh\kappa -E(h,Q). \end{equation}

For $h\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\kappa )\cap H^1(Y)$ this trace agrees $\mu $-almost everywhere with its canonical values. The trace commutes with Lipschitz real compositions.

Proof. Continuity gives the uniform kernel bound

14.108
\begin{equation} A_\mu :=\sup _ x\int _ YK(x,y)\, \mathrm d\mu (y) =1-\inf _ YQ<\infty . \end{equation}

To prove non-polarity, take a nontrivial subharmonic function on a coordinate disk. Cut off its positive Hessian measure on a slightly larger disk. The logarithmic potential of this finite measure has the same Hessian on the smaller disk, so their difference is harmonic there. This is the local Riesz decomposition: it also follows directly from the distributional fundamental solution and harmonic regularity. On compact subdisks the kernel $-\log |z-a|^2$ differs from $K(z,a)$ by a bounded smooth term. Symmetry of $K$, Tonelli's theorem and (14.108) make the logarithmic potential integrable against $\mu $; its harmonic remainder is bounded. The pole set consequently has $\mu $-mass zero. A countable disk cover proves the claim for polar Borel sets.

The group $\mathrm{SU}(2)$ acts on $Y$ by Fubini–Study isometries. Average with smooth nonnegative conjugation-invariant kernels of mass one supported near the identity. This gives smooth $Q_\epsilon $, probability measures $\mu _\epsilon $, and

\[ Q_\epsilon \longrightarrow Q\ \text{uniformly},\qquad \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}Q_\epsilon =\mu _\epsilon -\kappa ,\qquad \int _ YQ_\epsilon \kappa =0. \]

Smooth Stokes gives $E(Q_\epsilon ,Q_\epsilon )=-\int Q_\epsilon \, \mathrm d\mu _\epsilon \leq \| Q\| _\infty $. Poincaré's inequality and weak Hilbert compactness show that $Q\in H^1$. Its distributional curvature identity gives (14.107) first for smooth $h$. Apply this identity to $\sqrt{h^2+\epsilon ^2}$, whose gradient has magnitude at most $|\mathrm dh|$. Letting $\epsilon \downarrow 0$ yields

\[ \int _ Y|h|\, \mathrm d\mu \leq \| h\| _{L^1(\kappa )}+E(h,h)^{1/2}E(Q,Q)^{1/2} \leq C_\mu \| h\| _{H^1}. \]

Completion defines the trace and extends the identity.

These group averages converge strongly in $H^1$: rotations act continuously and isometrically on that space, and the averaging kernels approach the identity. They also converge pointwise to every canonical quasi-subharmonic function. Indeed their induced averages about a point are radial by conjugation invariance. In a coordinate centered at that point they are positive averages of circular means; after a smooth quadratic correction, the submean inequality and upper semicontinuity give the required limit. The correction error tends to zero, and upper semicontinuity gives the limit also at a pole. For a $\kappa $-plurisubharmonic $H^1$ function, strong convergence and the trace bound give $L^1(\mu )$ convergence, while a subsequence converges $\mu $-almost everywhere to the trace. Non-polarity makes the canonical value finite there and identifies the two. Finally, the Sobolev chain rule and smooth approximation show that Lipschitz compositions are continuous in $H^1$; the corresponding $L^1(\mu )$ limits prove the composition assertion. $\square$

14.30.2 Exponential equations and uniform selection on the curve

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Theorem 14.30.3. Suppose $Q=Q_\mu $ is finite and continuous. For every $\lambda >0$ there is a unique bounded $w_\lambda \in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\kappa )$ satisfying

14.109
\begin{equation} \kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_\lambda =\mathrm e^{\lambda w_\lambda }\mu , \qquad \int _ Y\mathrm e^{\lambda w_\lambda }\, \mathrm d\mu =1. \end{equation}

It is continuous and obeys

14.110
\begin{equation} Q-\sup _ YQ\leq w_\lambda \leq Q-\inf _ YQ. \end{equation}

The entire family satisfies, as $\lambda \downarrow 0$,

\[ \left\| w_\lambda -Q+\int _ YQ\, \mathrm d\mu \right\| _\infty =O(\lambda ). \]

The limit is the greatest $p\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\kappa )$ with $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\kappa (p)\geq \mu $ and $\int _ Yp\, \mathrm d\mu \leq 0$.

Proof. Variational existence. On the real Hilbert space

\[ \mathcal H=\{ q\in H^1(Y):\int _ Yq\kappa =0\} \]

consider the extended functional

\[ J_\lambda (q)=\tfrac 12E(q,q) +\lambda ^{-1}\log \int _ Y\mathrm e^{\lambda \operatorname {Tr}_\mu q}\, \mathrm d\mu . \]

It is finite at zero. Jensen's inequality and (14.107) give

\[ J_\lambda (q)\geq \tfrac 12E(q,q)-E(q,Q), \]

so it is coercive and bounded below. Strong $H^1$ convergence gives $L^1(\mu )$ convergence of traces. A subsequence realizing the lower limit converges almost everywhere for $\mu $, and Fatou's lemma proves strong lower semicontinuity of the exponential term. Jensen excludes a zero integral or a logarithm tending to $-\infty $. Hölder's inequality makes the logarithmic term convex, while the Dirichlet term is strictly convex on $\mathcal H$. Separation of a closed convex epigraph makes $J_\lambda $ weakly lower semicontinuous. Weak compactness therefore gives a unique minimizer $q_\lambda $, with

\[ 0<Z_\lambda :=\int _ Y\mathrm e^{\lambda \operatorname {Tr}_\mu q_\lambda }\, \mathrm d\mu <\infty . \]

Variation in a smooth mean-zero direction $h$ is justified by its boundedness, which dominates the differentiated integrand by a constant times the integrable exponential. Put $\rho _\lambda =\mathrm e^{\lambda \operatorname {Tr}_\mu q_\lambda }/Z_\lambda $. The Euler equation is

\[ E(q_\lambda ,h)+\int _ Yh\rho _\lambda \, \mathrm d\mu =0, \qquad \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q_\lambda =\rho _\lambda \mu -\kappa . \]

The second identity follows for arbitrary smooth tests by subtracting their $\kappa $-mean; $\int \rho _\lambda \, \mathrm d\mu =1$ fixes the constant term. The minimizer has a canonical $\kappa $-plurisubharmonic representative. Its mean-zero Green representation and lemma 14.30.2 give

\[ q_\lambda (x)=\int _ YG(x,y)\rho _\lambda (y)\, \mathrm d\mu (y)\leq 1, \qquad \rho _\lambda \leq \mathrm e^\lambda /Z_\lambda \quad \mu \text{-a.e.} \]

The kernel bound then gives $q_\lambda \geq 1-(\mathrm e^\lambda /Z_\lambda )A_\mu $.

To obtain continuity, the continuous functions $\int \min (K(x,y),L)\, \mathrm d\mu (y)$ increase to the continuous function $1-Q(x)$. Dini's theorem gives

14.111
\begin{equation} \sup _ x\int _ Y(K(x,y)-L)_+\, \mathrm d\mu (y)\longrightarrow 0. \end{equation}

Multiplication by the bounded density $\rho _\lambda $ preserves this estimate. Its truncated Green potentials converge uniformly, so $q_\lambda $ is continuous. Now

\[ w_\lambda =q_\lambda -\lambda ^{-1}\log Z_\lambda \]

solves (14.109) with precisely the displayed constant.

Uniqueness and the barriers. Every bounded $\kappa $-plurisubharmonic function is in $H^1$, by the same smoothing and energy bound used above after subtracting its $\kappa $-mean. For two bounded solutions $w_1,w_2$, their right-hand sides have bounded densities relative to $\mu $. The trace inequality extends distributional Stokes to their difference, giving

\[ E(w_1-w_2,w_1-w_2) =-\int _ Y(w_1-w_2)(\mathrm e^{\lambda w_1}-\mathrm e^{\lambda w_2})\, \mathrm d\mu \leq 0. \]

The difference is constant; the unit exponential integrals force that constant to be zero.

Set $L_0=Q-\sup Q\leq 0$, $R_0=Q-\inf Q\geq 0$, and $z=(L_0-w_\lambda )_+$. The $H^1$ chain rule, canonical trace and extended Stokes give

\[ E(z,z)=-\int _ Yz(1-\mathrm e^{\lambda w_\lambda })\, \mathrm d\mu \leq 0. \]

Indeed $z>0$ implies $w_\lambda <L_0\leq 0$. Thus $z$ is constant. If it were a positive constant, continuity would give $w_\lambda =L_0-z\leq -z$ everywhere, contradicting its unit exponential integral. This proves the lower barrier. Testing $(w_\lambda -R_0)_+$ gives the upper barrier with the same argument and the reversed exponential difference. In particular $\| w_\lambda \| _\infty \leq \operatorname {osc}Q$.

Selection. Write $q_\lambda =w_\lambda -\int _ Yw_\lambda \kappa $, consistent with the variational normalization. The barriers give $\mathrm e^{\lambda w_\lambda }=1+O(\lambda )$ uniformly, and the Green identity gives

\[ \| q_\lambda -Q\| _\infty \leq \| \mathrm e^{\lambda w_\lambda }-1\| _\infty \sup _ x\int _ Y|G(x,y)|\, \mathrm d\mu (y)=O(\lambda ). \]

The last kernel integral is bounded by $1+A_\mu $. The $q_\lambda $ are uniformly bounded, so Taylor's formula gives

\[ \lambda ^{-1}\log Z_\lambda =\int _ Yq_\lambda \, \mathrm d\mu +O(\lambda ) =\int _ YQ\, \mathrm d\mu +O(\lambda ). \]

This proves the whole-family estimate. Finally, lemma 14.30.1 identifies every critical subsolution as $Q+C$. Its $\mu $-mean is at most zero exactly when $C\leq -\int Q\, \mathrm d\mu $, proving the order characterization. $\square$

14.30.3 The product equation and its maximal coefficient

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Let $(X_0,h_0)$ be a connected compact Hermitian manifold of dimension $m\geq 1$. Set

\[ \begin{gathered} X=X_0\times Y,\qquad N=m+1,\qquad H=h_0+\kappa ,\\ V_0=\int _{X_0}h_0^ m,\qquad \nu =M\frac{h_0^ m}{V_0}\otimes \mu ,\qquad c=\frac{NV_0}{M}, \end{gathered} \]

where $M>0$ and factor forms and potentials are pulled back to $X$ when used there. Thus $\nu (X)=M$.

Proposition 14.30.4. If $\mu $ is non-polar, then $\nu $ is non-pluripolar and

\[ u=(Q_\mu -\sup _ YQ_\mu )\circ \pi _ Y,\qquad \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(u)=c\nu ,\qquad \int _ X\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(u)=NV_0. \]

This is the unique supremum-zero solution among pullbacks of $\kappa $-plurisubharmonic functions. It is bounded exactly when $Q_\mu $ is bounded, and continuous exactly when $Q_\mu $ is continuous. If $v_-(h_0)=0$, then $v_-(H)=0$ and $H$ is not closed.

Proof. A nontrivial local plurisubharmonic function on a product chart has a nontrivial subharmonic restriction to the curve for smooth-volume-almost every point of $X_0$, by local integrability and Fubini's theorem. Non-polarity of $\mu $ and a second application of Fubini give its pole set product measure zero. A countable cover proves that $\nu $ is non-pluripolar.

For bounded $p\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\kappa )$, bounded multilinearity and lemma 1.7.3 give

14.112
\begin{equation} \mathrm{MA}_ H(p\circ \pi _ Y) =N h_0^ m\otimes (\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p). \end{equation}

Only one curve factor survives in the expansion. For arbitrary $p$, apply this to $p_ j=\max (p,-j)$, restrict to the pullback of $\{ p>-j\} $ and increase $j$. The defining extended limit gives the same formula with $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\kappa (p)$ on the right. Lemma 14.30.1 now proves the equation, finite mass and separated uniqueness. Boundedness and continuity follow from the same constant-difference identity.

If $v_-(h_0)=0$, pull back bounded potentials whose $h_0$-masses tend to zero. Their $H$-masses are $N$ times those masses, because $\int _ Y\kappa =1$, so $v_-(H)=0$. A closed $h_0$ would give every bounded potential mass $V_0>0$ by Stokes. Hence $h_0$ is not closed; restricting $H$ to a factor slice shows that $H$ is not closed either. $\square$

For this fixed product measure define its subsolution threshold by

\[ b_ H(\nu )=\sup \{ a\geq 0:\exists p\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,H), \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(p)\geq a\nu \} . \]

For $\nu =f\, \mathrm dV$ this agrees with the individual-density threshold of theorem 14.27.2.

Theorem 14.30.5. Suppose $Q=Q_\mu $ is finite and continuous. Then $b_ H(\nu )=c$, attained by the bounded continuous $u$ of proposition 14.30.4. For every $\lambda >0$, the continuous potential

\[ U_\lambda =\lambda ^{-1}\log c+w_\lambda \circ \pi _ Y \]

satisfies

\[ \mathrm{MA}_ H(U_\lambda )=\mathrm e^{\lambda U_\lambda }\nu , \qquad \int _ X\mathrm{MA}_ H(U_\lambda )=NV_0. \]

It dominates every $p\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,H)$ with $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(p)\geq \mathrm e^{\lambda p}\nu $, and it is the unique bounded solution. For $v_\lambda =U_\lambda -\sup _ XU_\lambda $ and $c_\lambda =\mathrm e^{\lambda \sup _ XU_\lambda }$,

\[ \begin{gathered} \mathrm{MA}_ H(v_\lambda )=c_\lambda \mathrm e^{\lambda v_\lambda }\nu , \qquad c_\lambda =c\, \mathrm e^{\lambda \sup _ Yw_\lambda },\\ \| v_\lambda -u\| _\infty =O(\lambda ),\qquad \lambda ^{-1}\log (c_\lambda /c)\longrightarrow \sup _ YQ-\int _ YQ\, \mathrm d\mu . \end{gathered} \]

All the constructed potentials belong to $\mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(X,H,0)$. The same coefficient $c$ is attained if the defining supremum for $b_ H(\nu )$ is restricted to bounded potentials or to this envelope class.

Proof. Lemma 14.30.2 gives non-polarity, and theorem 14.30.3 constructs $w_\lambda $. Substituting it in (14.112) proves the exponential equation and its finite mass. Supremum normalization gives the displayed $c_\lambda $, and the uniform curve estimate gives both limits. For any bounded $H$-plurisubharmonic $p$ and $t\geq 1$, the constant $a_ t=t\inf _ Xp$ is an $H$-plurisubharmonic competitor below $tp$. Hence $P_ H(tp)$ is genuine for every $t$, which proves the envelope-class assertion.

Greatestness. Write $s=U_\lambda $. If $b$ is a bounded exponential subsolution, then on $\{ s<b-\epsilon \} $,

\[ \mathrm{MA}_ H(s)\leq \mathrm e^{-\lambda \epsilon }\mathrm{MA}_ H(b-\epsilon ). \]

The bounded strict domination theorem, theorem 10.2.2, gives $s\geq b-\epsilon $. Letting $\epsilon \downarrow 0$ proves $s\geq b$, and applying the argument in both directions proves bounded uniqueness.

For an arbitrary exponential subsolution $r$, fix $A>0$ and put $p=s-A$, $W=\max (r,p)$. These maxima are $H$-plurisubharmonic by lemma 1.3.1, and $W$ is bounded. Choose $j$ with $-j<\inf _ Xp$ and set $r_ j=\max (r,-j)$; then $W=\max (r_ j,p)$. Plurifine locality on $\{ r_ j>p\} $ and lemma 1.7.2, applied to $p\leq W$ on their contact set, give

\[ \mathrm{MA}_ H(W)\geq \mathbf1_{\{ r_ j>p\} }\mathrm{MA}_ H(r_ j) +\mathbf1_{\{ r_ j\leq p\} }\mathrm{MA}_ H(p). \]

On the first set $r_ j=r>-j$, so strict-superlevel locality identifies the measure with $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(r)$. On the second, $W=p$ and $\mathrm{MA}_ H(p)=\mathrm e^{\lambda s}\nu \geq \mathrm e^{\lambda p}\nu $. Thus $W$ is a bounded exponential subsolution, and $r\leq W\leq s$. This uses bounded products before every restriction and applies also when the measure of $r$ has infinite mass.

The maximal coefficient. Suppose $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(r)\geq a\nu $ with $a>0$, and put $B=\sup _ Xr$. For every $\lambda >0$ the potential $r+\lambda ^{-1}\log a-B$ is an exponential subsolution, since its exponential is at most $a$. Greatestness gives

\[ r+\lambda ^{-1}\log a-B \leq \lambda ^{-1}\log c+w_\lambda \circ \pi _ Y. \]

Evaluate at a fixed finite point of $r$, multiply by $\lambda $, and let $\lambda \downarrow 0$. The uniform barriers for $w_\lambda $ give $a\leq c$. The equation for $u$ attains $c$ in each stated class. $\square$

The order characterization of the zero-exponent limit is confined to the separated sector. Bounded uniqueness at a positive exponent and maximality of the coefficient do not determine all normalized critical potentials on the nonclosed product.

Proposition 14.30.6. Suppose, for some $C>0$ and $\delta >0$,

\[ \mu (B_{\mathrm{ch}}(x,r))\leq Cr^\delta \qquad (x\in Y,\ 0<r\leq 1). \]

Then $Q_\mu $ is finite and continuous, so theorem 14.30.5 applies. In particular it applies to

\[ \mathrm d\mu (\vartheta )=(1+\epsilon \cos \vartheta ) \frac{\mathrm d\vartheta }{2\pi },\qquad 0<\epsilon <1, \]

on the circle $[1:\mathrm e^{\mathrm i\vartheta }]$. This measure and its product $\nu $ are singular with respect to smooth volume, and $Q_\mu $ is nonconstant on its support.

Proof. Since $K=-\log d_{\mathrm{ch}}^2$, layer-cake integration gives

\[ \sup _ x\int _{\{ K(x,y)>L\} }K(x,y)\, \mathrm d\mu (y) \leq C\left(L+\frac2\delta \right)\mathrm e^{-\delta L/2}. \]

Consequently the continuous truncated-kernel potentials converge uniformly to $Q_\mu $. Weighted arclength on the circle has the ball bound with $\delta =1$. On that circle the kernel is $1+\log \sin ^2((\vartheta -\varphi )/2)$, whose first cosine coefficient is $-2$. Integrating its $L^1$ Fourier series against $1+\epsilon \cos \varphi $ gives

\[ Q_\mu ([1:\mathrm e^{\mathrm i\vartheta }]) =1-2\log 2-\epsilon \cos \vartheta . \]

This proves nonconstancy, and the support has smooth curve volume zero. Its product with $X_0$ also has smooth volume zero. $\square$

14.31 Stability of the subsolution threshold

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now study how the threshold $b_ h(f)$ depends on $f$. By (14.104), $b_ h^{1/n}$ is an infimum of functions that are continuous on $L^1$, and is therefore upper semicontinuous. It is not continuous in general: when $v_-(h)=0$, every density with positive threshold is an $L^1$ limit of densities with zero threshold. For density roots, (14.103) expresses $\rho _ h$ as a supremum of positive linear functionals. Hence $\rho _ h$ is a norm on the space where it is finite, this space is complete for a suitable norm, and the threshold depends continuously on the root in that norm.

Theorem 14.31.1. Suppose $f_ j,f$ are nonzero nonnegative $L^1$ densities and $f_ j\to f$ in $L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$. Then

\[ \limsup _{j\to \infty }b_ h(f_ j)\leq b_ h(f). \]

Moreover,

14.113
\begin{equation} b_ h(f)=\lim _{\delta \downarrow 0}\sup \{ c_ h(q):q\in L^\infty ,\ q\geq 0,\ q\not\equiv 0, \ \| q-f\| _{L^1}<\delta \} , \end{equation}

and the suprema are finite for sufficiently small $\delta $.

Proof. For a Gauduchon form $\gamma $ on $X$, write $A_\gamma (f)=\int _ X(f\, \mathrm{d}V/\gamma ^ n)^{1/n}\gamma ^ n$. Since $|s^{1/n}-t^{1/n}|\leq |s-t|^{1/n}$ for $s,t\geq 0$, Hölder's inequality shows that $A_\gamma $ is continuous on $L^1$; it is positive at every nonzero nonnegative density. By theorem 14.28.1, $b_ h^{1/n}$ is the infimum over $\gamma $ of the continuous positive functions $f\mapsto \int _ Xh\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}/A_\gamma (f)$. It is therefore upper semicontinuous, and testing a single $\gamma $ bounds it above on an $L^1$ neighborhood of $f$.

For the second assertion, recall that $b_ h(q)=c_ h(q)$ for bounded $q$ (proposition 14.27.4). By the first assertion, $\limsup _ jc_ h(q_ j)\leq b_ h(f)$ for every sequence of nonzero nonnegative bounded densities $q_ j\to f$ in $L^1$. Hence the limit in (14.113) is at most $b_ h(f)$, and the local upper bound makes the suprema finite for small $\delta $. Conversely, the truncations $f_ k$ converge to $f$ in $L^1$ and satisfy $c_ h(f_ k)\geq b_ h(f)$, so every supremum in (14.113) is at least $b_ h(f)$. $\square$

Since $b_ h\geq 0$, upper semicontinuity makes $b_ h$ continuous at every density where it vanishes. If $v_-(h)=0$, these are its only points of continuity. Indeed, let $b_ h(f)>0$ and $g=f/\int _ Xf\, \mathrm{d}V$. By corollary 14.26.5 and theorem 14.27.2, the densities $(1-t)g+tf_0$, where $f_0$ is the density of theorem 14.26.3, have zero threshold and converge to $g$ in $L^1$ as $t\downarrow 0$; multiplying them by $\int _ Xf\, \mathrm{d}V$ gives densities with zero threshold converging to $f$. These statements concern only the scalar $b_ h$; they say nothing about capacity convergence of the corresponding potentials or about the limits of their Monge–Ampère measures.

14.31.1 Finite sums of densities

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Since (14.103) expresses $\rho _ h(p)$ as a supremum of positive linear functionals evaluated at $|p|$, the root functional is homogeneous, monotone and subadditive. Subadditivity shows that the sum of two densities with positive threshold again has positive threshold. In the statements below, $f$ and $g$ are nonzero nonnegative $L^1$ densities, and $p$ and $q$ are real or complex functions in $L^ n$.

Proposition 14.31.2. The functional $\rho _ h$ satisfies $\rho _ h(tp)=|t|\rho _ h(p)$ for scalars $t\ne 0$, $\rho _ h(p)\leq \rho _ h(q)$ whenever $|p|\leq |q|$, and

\[ \rho _ h(p+q)\leq \rho _ h(p)+\rho _ h(q). \]

In particular, if $b_ h(f),b_ h(g)>0$, then

\[ b_ h(f+g)\geq \bigl (b_ h(f)^{-1/n}+b_ h(g)^{-1/n}\bigr )^{-n}>0. \]

Proof. By (14.103), $\rho _ h(p)=\sup _\gamma L_{h,\gamma }(|p|)$ is a supremum of positive linear functionals evaluated at $|p|$. This gives homogeneity and monotonicity, and, together with $|p+q|\leq |p|+|q|$, the triangle inequality. Finally, $(f+g)^{1/n}\leq f^{1/n}+g^{1/n}$ gives

\[ b_ h(f+g)^{-1/n}=\rho _ h\bigl ((f+g)^{1/n}\bigr )\leq \rho _ h(f^{1/n})+\rho _ h(g^{1/n}) =b_ h(f)^{-1/n}+b_ h(g)^{-1/n}, \]

which is equivalent to the asserted lower bound for $b_ h(f+g)$. $\square$

Proposition 14.31.3. The real or complex vector space

\[ \mathcal D_ h=\{ p\in L^ n(\mathrm{d}V):\rho _ h(p)<+\infty \} \]

is complete for the norm $\| p\| _{\mathcal D_ h}=\| p\| _{L^ n}+\rho _ h(p)$. It is an order ideal in $L^ n$: if $q\in \mathcal D_ h$ and $|p|\leq |q|$, then $p\in \mathcal D_ h$. It contains $L^\infty $ and is therefore dense in $L^ n$ for the $L^ n$ norm.

Moreover, $\mathcal D_ h=L^ n$ if and only if $v_-(h)>0$, and in that case the norm $\| \cdot \| _{\mathcal D_ h}$ is equivalent to the $L^ n$ norm. If $h$ is Kähler, then

\[ \rho _ h(p)=\left(\int _ Xh^ n\right)^{-1/n}\| p\| _{L^ n}. \]

Proof. By proposition 14.31.2, $\mathcal D_ h$ is a vector space and an order ideal, and $\| \cdot \| _{\mathcal D_ h}$ is a norm on it. Bounded functions belong to $\mathcal D_ h$, because $b_ h(|p|^ n)=c_ h(|p|^ n)>0$ for bounded nonzero $p$.

We next show that $\rho _ h$ is lower semicontinuous on $L^ n$. If $p_ j\to p$ in $L^ n$, then $|p_ j|^ n\to |p|^ n$ in $L^1$, and the upper semicontinuity of $b_ h$ in theorem 14.31.1 gives

\[ \rho _ h(p)\leq \liminf _ j\rho _ h(p_ j). \]

This includes the case $b_ h(|p|^ n)=0$, in which the right side must be infinite; for $p=0$ the inequality is trivial. Now let $(p_ j)$ be a Cauchy sequence in $\mathcal D_ h$. It converges in $L^ n$ to some $p$, and lower semicontinuity gives $\rho _ h(p)\leq \liminf _ j\rho _ h(p_ j)<+\infty $, so $p\in \mathcal D_ h$. Applied to $p_ j-p_ k$ as $k\to \infty $, lower semicontinuity also gives

\[ \rho _ h(p_ j-p)\leq \liminf _{k\to \infty }\rho _ h(p_ j-p_ k), \]

which tends to zero as $j\to \infty $. Together with $L^ n$ convergence, this proves completeness.

If $v_-(h)>0$, proposition 14.27.4 gives $\rho _ h(p)\leq v_-(h)^{-1/n}\| p\| _{L^ n}$, so $\mathcal D_ h=L^ n$ with an equivalent norm. If $v_-(h)=0$, the density $f$ of theorem 14.26.3 has $b_ h(f)=0$ by theorem 14.27.2; thus $f^{1/n}$ belongs to $L^ n$ but not to $\mathcal D_ h$. The Kähler formula follows from the same proposition. $\square$

Thus, when the lower mass vanishes, $\mathcal D_ h$ is a proper dense subspace of $L^ n$, complete for the stronger norm $\| \cdot \| _{\mathcal D_ h}$; its nonnegative elements are the $n$th roots of the densities admitting a positive scaled subsolution. A finite sum of such densities again admits a positive scaled subsolution, but a convergent infinite sum need not: the smooth summands in theorem 14.26.3 give an example. By proposition 14.28.2, the space $\mathcal D_ h$ does not depend on the Hermitian form $h$, and the norms $\| \cdot \| _{\mathcal D_ h}$ for different Hermitian forms are equivalent. The closure of the bounded functions for this norm is studied in 14.31.2.

The restriction of $\rho _ h$ to $\mathcal D_ h$ is itself a norm: if $p\ne 0$, then $\rho _ h(p)\geq L_{h,\gamma }(|p|)>0$ for any Gauduchon form $\gamma $. Whether this weaker norm is complete depends on the upper mass.

Proposition 14.31.4. The norm $\rho _ h$ is complete on $\mathcal D_ h$ if and only if $v_+(h)<+\infty $. When the upper mass is finite,

\[ \| p\| _{L^ n}\leq v_+(h)^{1/n}\rho _ h(p)\qquad (p\in \mathcal D_ h), \]

so $\rho _ h$ and $\| \cdot \| _{\mathcal D_ h}$ are equivalent norms.

Proof. Suppose $v_+(h)<+\infty $. A continuous solution for a bounded density $q$ has mass $c_ h(q)\int _ Xq\, \mathrm{d}V\leq v_+(h)$. Applying this to the truncations of $|p|^ n$ and letting the truncation level increase gives $b_ h(|p|^ n)\| p\| _{L^ n}^ n\leq v_+(h)$, which is the asserted inequality. Hence $\rho _ h$ and $\| \cdot \| _{\mathcal D_ h}$ are equivalent, and completeness follows from proposition 14.31.3.

Suppose instead that $v_+(h)=+\infty $. By theorem 26.14.3, theorem 26.1.3, the normalization scalars of smooth strictly positive probability densities are unbounded above. For each $j$, choose such a density $f_ j$ with

\[ c_ h(f_ j)\geq (j2^ j)^ n, \qquad \text{and put}\qquad p_ j=jf_ j^{1/n}. \]

Then $\| p_ j\| _{L^ n}=j$ and $\rho _ h(p_ j)=jc_ h(f_ j)^{-1/n}\leq 2^{-j}$, so the partial sums $P_ N=\sum _{j\leq N}p_ j$ form a Cauchy sequence for $\rho _ h$.

Fix a Gauduchon form $\gamma $. Its coefficient $a_\gamma $ is bounded below by a positive constant, so (14.103) gives $\rho _ h(p)\geq c\| p\| _{L^1}$ with a constant $c>0$. Hence $\sum _ jp_ j$ converges in $L^1$ to a nonnegative function $P$. If $P_ N$ converged for $\rho _ h$ to an element of $\mathcal D_ h$, that element would also be the $L^1$ limit, hence equal to $P$ almost everywhere. But $P\geq p_ j$ gives $\| P\| _{L^ n}\geq j$ for every $j$, so $P\notin L^ n$. Therefore $\rho _ h$ is not complete. $\square$

The two mass properties play separate roles: positive lower mass makes every $L^ n$ function admissible, whereas finite upper mass makes the root norm control the $L^ n$ norm. The norm $\| \cdot \| _{\mathcal D_ h}$ is complete without either assumption.

All four combinations occur. On a compact Kähler manifold both mass properties hold. The Hopf threefold has positive lower mass and infinite upper mass (corollary 8.7.2). The elliptic threefold of theorem 26.18.1 has zero lower mass, and finite upper mass for its balanced metric (proposition 26.18.7). The product manifolds of corollary 26.18.6 have zero lower mass and infinite upper mass. Since neither $\mathcal D_ h$ nor the completeness of $\rho _ h$ depends on the choice of $h$ (proposition 14.28.2, proposition 6.5.2), we obtain the following table.

Manifold

Domain $\mathcal D_ h$

Is $\rho _ h$ complete?

Kähler manifold

$L^ n$

Yes

Hopf threefold

$L^ n$

No

Elliptic threefold

$\mathcal D_ h\subsetneq L^ n$

Yes

Product example

$\mathcal D_ h\subsetneq L^ n$

No

14.31.2 Approximation by bounded density roots

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Bounded functions are dense in $\mathcal D_ h$ for the $L^ n$ norm, but not obviously for the norm $\| \cdot \| _{\mathcal D_ h}$. Let $\mathcal D_ h^0$ denote the closure of $L^\infty $ in $\mathcal D_ h$ for this norm. We show that a function belongs to $\mathcal D_ h^0$ exactly when its tails are small uniformly over all Gauduchon tests. The basic tool is a subcritical estimate. For every $r>n$,

14.114
\begin{equation} \rho _ h(p)\leq C_ r\| p\| _{L^ r}\qquad (p\in L^ r), \end{equation}

where the constant $C_ r$ depends on $h$, the reference volume and $r$, but not on $p$. Indeed, for nonzero $p\in L^ r$ the density $|p|^ n$ belongs to $L^{r/n}$, so $b_ h(|p|^ n)=c_ h(|p|^ n)$ by proposition 14.27.4, and the lower-density estimate of theorem 10.3.3, with exponent $r/n$, gives $c_ h(|p|^ n)\| |p|^ n\| _{L^{r/n}}\geq c_{r/n}>0$.

Proposition 14.31.5. For $p\in \mathcal D_ h$, the following conditions are equivalent:

  1. $p\in \mathcal D_ h^0$;

  2. $\rho _ h(|p|\mathbf1_{\{ |p|>K\} })\to 0$ as $K\to \infty $;

  3. whenever $|q_ j|\leq |p|$ and $q_ j\to 0$ in measure, one has $\| q_ j\| _{\mathcal D_ h}\to 0$.

By (14.103), the second condition can be written as

\[ \sup _{\gamma \text{ Gauduchon}} L_{h,\gamma }(|p|\mathbf1_{\{ |p|>K\} })\longrightarrow 0 \qquad (K\to \infty ). \]

The space $\mathcal D_ h^0$ is a closed order ideal in $\mathcal D_ h$. It contains $L^ r$ for every $r>n$, and smooth functions are dense in it. If $v_-(h)>0$, then $\mathcal D_ h^0=\mathcal D_ h=L^ n$.

Proof. For $K>0$, set $p_ K=p\min (1,K/|p|)$ where $p\ne 0$, and $p_ K=0$ where $p=0$. Then $|p-p_ K|=(|p|-K)_+$ and

\[ (|p|-K)_+\leq |p|\mathbf1_{\{ |p|>K\} },\qquad |p|\mathbf1_{\{ |p|>2K\} }\leq 2(|p|-K)_+. \]

Since the $L^ n$ norms of the tails tend to zero, these inequalities and the monotonicity of $\rho _ h$ show that the second condition is equivalent to $p_ K\to p$ in $\mathcal D_ h$; in particular, it implies the first. Conversely, if $a$ is bounded and $K\geq \| a\| _{L^\infty }$, then $(|p|-K)_+\leq |p-a|$, so $\| p-p_ K\| _{\mathcal D_ h}\leq \| p-a\| _{\mathcal D_ h}$. Approximating $p$ by bounded functions $a$ shows that the first condition implies $p_ K\to p$, hence the second.

Assume the second condition, and let $q_ j$ be as in the third. Fix $K$. On $\{ |p|\leq K\} $, the functions $q_ j$ are bounded by $K$ and tend to zero in measure, hence in $L^{2n}$; by (14.114) with $r=2n$, the root functional of $q_ j\mathbf1_{\{ |p|\leq K\} }$ tends to zero. On the complementary set, $\rho _ h(q_ j\mathbf1_{\{ |p|>K\} })\leq \rho _ h(|p|\mathbf1_{\{ |p|>K\} })$. Letting first $j$ and then $K$ tend to infinity gives $\rho _ h(q_ j)\to 0$, and dominated convergence gives $\| q_ j\| _{L^ n}\to 0$. Conversely, the third condition applied to $q_ j=|p|\mathbf1_{\{ |p|>j\} }$ gives the second.

Since the second condition passes from $p$ to every function dominated by $|p|$, the space $\mathcal D_ h^0$ is an order ideal; it is closed by definition. For $p\in L^ r$ with $r>n$, the truncations $p_ K$ converge to $p$ in $L^ r$, hence in $\mathcal D_ h$ by (14.114). Bounded functions can be approximated in $L^{2n}$ by smooth functions, which then converge in $\mathcal D_ h$ by the same estimate; hence smooth functions are dense in $\mathcal D_ h^0$. Under positive lower mass, proposition 14.31.3 shows that $\mathcal D_ h=L^ n$ with an equivalent norm, and bounded functions are dense in $L^ n$; hence $\mathcal D_ h^0=\mathcal D_ h=L^ n$. $\square$

By proposition 14.28.2, the space $\mathcal D_ h^0$ does not depend on the choice of the Hermitian form $h$. Convergence in $\mathcal D_ h$ controls the threshold: if $p_ j\to p\ne 0$ in $\mathcal D_ h$, then

\[ |\rho _ h(p_ j)-\rho _ h(p)|\leq \rho _ h(p_ j-p)\longrightarrow 0, \qquad b_ h(|p_ j|^ n)\longrightarrow b_ h(|p|^ n)>0. \]

For $p\in \mathcal D_ h^0$, the uniform tail condition also gives a uniform modulus for exponential damping and error bounds for the scalars of the exponential equations; see proposition 14.34.3. It does not by itself give capacity convergence of the solutions. We do not know whether $\mathcal D_ h^0=\mathcal D_ h$ when $v_-(h)=0$.

14.31.3 Density roots from a closed factor

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The product formula of theorem 14.29.5 identifies a complete subspace of density roots with uniformly vanishing tails even when the ambient lower mass is zero. Use the product data $X_0,Y,N,H,c_0,\mu ,R$ of 14.29.1. The spaces $\mathcal D_ H$ and $\mathcal D_ H^0$ are now taken on the $N$-dimensional product with reference volume $\mathrm dV$.

Proposition 14.31.6. For real or complex $\xi \in L^ N(Y,\kappa ^ k)$, put $J\xi =R^{1/N}\pi _ Y^*\xi $. Then

\[ \| J\xi \| _{L^ N(\mathrm dV)}=\| \xi \| _{L^ N(\kappa ^ k)},\qquad \rho _ H(J\xi )=c_0^{-1/N}\| \xi \| _{L^ N(\kappa ^ k)}. \]

The image of $J$ is a closed linear subspace of $\mathcal D_ H^0$. If $g=|\xi |^ N$ and $A=\max _ XR$, then
