\[ \rho _ H\bigl (J\xi \mathbf1_{\{ |J\xi |>K\} }\bigr ) \leq \left(\frac1{c_0}\int _{\{ g>K^ N/A\} }g\kappa ^ k\right)^{1/N} \longrightarrow 0. \]

For $\mathrm dV=\mu $, so that $R=1$, the exact tail formula is

\[ \rho _ H\bigl (\pi _ Y^*\xi \mathbf1_{\{ |\xi |>K\} }\bigr ) =\left(\frac1{c_0}\int _{\{ |\xi |>K\} }|\xi |^ N\kappa ^ k\right)^{1/N}. \]

Proof. Since $|J\xi |^ N=R|\xi |^ N$, integration against $\mathrm dV$ gives the first norm identity. For nonzero $\xi $, theorem 14.29.5 applied to $g=|\xi |^ N$ gives the root-functional identity; for $\xi =0$ it holds by definition. Applying the two identities to differences yields

\[ \| J\xi -J\eta \| _{\mathcal D_ H} =(1+c_0^{-1/N})\| \xi -\eta \| _{L^ N(\kappa ^ k)}. \]

Bounded approximations of $\xi $ in $L^ N$ have bounded images because $R$ is bounded, so $J\xi \in \mathcal D_ H^0$. Completeness of $L^ N(Y,\kappa ^ k)$ and the displayed identity make the image closed.

On $\{ |J\xi |>K\} $ one has $g>K^ N/A$, and hence

\[ |J\xi |\mathbf1_{\{ |J\xi |>K\} } \leq \bigl (Rg\mathbf1_{\{ g>K^ N/A\} }\bigr )^{1/N}. \]

Monotonicity of the root functional and its identity for factor roots give the estimate, with the value zero when the tail vanishes. Integrability of $g$ makes the bound tend to zero; the $L^ N$ tails also vanish. When $R=1$, the amplitude cutoff itself is pulled back from $Y$, so the same norm identity gives equality. $\square$

For $Y=\mathbb P^ k$ and $\mathrm dV=\mu $, take $p_\alpha =\pi _ Y^*(q_\alpha ^{1/N})$ with $q_\alpha $ from lemma 14.29.1. Then $p_\alpha \in \mathcal D_ H^0$ but $p_\alpha \notin L^ r(\mathrm dV)$ for every $r>N$. Moreover,

\[ \rho _ H\bigl (p_\alpha \mathbf1_{\{ p_\alpha >K\} }\bigr ) \asymp (\log K)^{-k(1-\alpha )/N}\qquad (K\to +\infty ). \]

Indeed, the radial estimate of that lemma gives $q_\alpha \asymp r^{-k}(1-\log r)^{-1-k(1-\alpha )}$. With $L=1-\log r$, the density level set $\{ q_\alpha >K^ N\} $ lies between two sets $\{ L>c\log K\} $ for fixed positive constants $c$, once $K$ is large. Integrating the radial density $L^{-1-k(1-\alpha )}\, \mathrm dL$ and taking its $N$th root gives the claimed tail. The integrability exclusion follows from $q_\alpha \notin L^ s$ for $s>1$.

These roots enlarge the examples in $\mathcal D_ H^0$ beyond the subcritical $L^ r$ classes. They give neither $\mathcal D_ H^0=\mathcal D_ H$ nor a general criterion for an exact equation at zero lower mass.

14.32 Power capacity bounds and threshold equations

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

In this section, $X$ is a connected compact complex manifold of dimension $n\geq 3$, with a Hermitian form $h$ and a smooth positive probability volume $\mathrm dV$. The root-tail condition of proposition 14.31.5 controls the scalar threshold. A power bound for one density's measure supplies the missing control of its approximating potentials: the capacity estimates give a common bound, and stability against the solution measure gives uniform convergence.

Theorem 14.32.1. Let $0\leq f\in L^1(\mathrm dV)$ have positive integral. Suppose that $A,\alpha >0$ satisfy

14.115
\begin{equation} \int _ Ef\, \mathrm dV\leq A\operatorname {Cap}_ h(E)^{1+\alpha } \qquad (E\subset X\text{ Borel}). \end{equation}

There is $C>0$, depending only on $(X,h),A,\alpha $, such that every measurable $0\leq g\leq f$ with positive integral satisfies

14.116
\begin{equation} b_ h(g)\geq C\left(\int _ Xg\, \mathrm dV\right)^{-\alpha /(1+\alpha )}, \qquad \rho _ h(g^{1/n})\leq C^{-1/n} \left(\int _ Xg\, \mathrm dV\right)^{\alpha /(n(1+\alpha ))}. \end{equation}

In particular, $f^{1/n}\in \mathcal D_ h^0$. There is $\phi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)\cap C(X)$, with $\sup _ X\phi =0$, such that

14.117
\begin{equation} \mathrm{MA}_ h(\phi )=b_ h(f)f\, \mathrm dV. \end{equation}

The coefficient $b_ h(f)$ and the mass of this measure are finite and strictly positive.

Proof. Step 1: the coefficient and root tails. Put $g_ j=\min (g,j)$ and let $\phi _ j$ be normalized continuous solutions of

\[ \mathrm{MA}_ h(\phi _ j)=c_ jg_ j\, \mathrm dV, \qquad c_ j=c_ h(g_ j),\qquad \sup _ X\phi _ j=0, \]

given by theorem 10.1.1. The definition (14.101) gives $c_ j\downarrow b_ h(g)$. Their measures satisfy a power capacity bound with constant $c_ jA$.

Take competitor zero, $H=1$ and $\varepsilon =1/2$ in lemma 10.3.2, lemma 10.3.1. Choose $D>0$ fixed with $2D<\delta _{1/2}$ and put $U_ D=\{ \phi _ j<\inf _ X\phi _ j+D\} $. These estimates give

\[ D\leq C_\alpha (C_1c_ jA)^{1/n} \operatorname {Cap}_ h(U_ D)^{\alpha /n}, \qquad D^ n\operatorname {Cap}_ h(U_ D) \leq C_1c_ j\int _ Xg_ j\, \mathrm dV. \]

Combining them yields

\[ c_ j^{1+\alpha }\left(\int _ Xg_ j\, \mathrm dV\right)^\alpha \geq c>0. \]

Taking the limit proves the lower bound for $b_ h(g)$. The identity (14.103), with its full family of Gauduchon tests, gives the root estimate. Apply it to $g=f\mathbf1_{\{ f>K^ n\} }$ whenever this density is nonzero. Its mass tends to zero; the zero density has root norm zero. Thus the tails of $f^{1/n}$ tend to zero in both $L^ n$ and $\rho _ h$, and proposition 14.31.5 gives $f^{1/n}\in \mathcal D_ h^0$.

Step 2: uniform convergence of a subsequence. Now use $f_ j=\min (f,j)$, $c_ j=c_ h(f_ j)$ and normalized continuous solutions $\phi _ j$. Step 1 gives $b_ h(f)>0$, while $c_ j\leq c_1<\infty $. Hence

\[ \mathrm{MA}_ h(\phi _ j)(E) \leq Ac_1\operatorname {Cap}_ h(E)^{1+\alpha }. \]

The first part of the proof of proposition 10.3.5, using the capacity tail estimate, gives one $M\geq 1$ with $-M\leq \phi _ j\leq 0$ for all $j$.

By theorem 1.4.2, extract a subsequence converging in $L^1(\mathrm dV)$ and almost everywhere. Since $f\, \mathrm dV$ is a fixed finite absolutely continuous measure and the potentials have a common bound, dominated convergence gives

\[ \int _ X|\phi _ j-\phi _ k|\, \mathrm{MA}_ h(\phi _ j) \leq c_1\int _ X|\phi _ j-\phi _ k|f\, \mathrm dV\longrightarrow 0 \]

along this subsequence as both indices tend to infinity. Apply proposition 10.3.5 in both orders, with $H=M$. The subsequence is uniformly Cauchy. Its limit $\phi $ is continuous, $h$-plurisubharmonic by distributional positivity, and has supremum zero.

Step 3: passage of the products. On a coordinate ball choose a fixed smooth $q$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q\geq h$, and put $S_ j=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\phi _ j+q)$ and $S=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\phi +q)$. For every $\ell \geq 1$, the calculus of bounded products gives

\[ S_ j^\ell -S^\ell =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\left[(\phi _ j-\phi ) \sum _{a=0}^{\ell -1}S_ j^ a\wedge S^{\ell -1-a}\right]. \]

The local Chern–Levine–Nirenberg estimate bounds its pairing with a compactly supported smooth test form by $C\| \phi _ j-\phi \| _\infty $. Expand the Hermitian products as in proposition 1.7.1:

\[ \mathrm{MA}_ h(\phi _ j) =\sum _{\ell =0}^ n\binom n\ell S_ j^\ell \wedge (h-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q)^{n-\ell }. \]

Every term converges, with its smooth correction factor, to the corresponding term for $\phi $. On the other hand, $c_ jf_ j\to b_ h(f)f$ in $L^1(\mathrm dV)$. The two limits prove (14.117); its prescribed mass is $b_ h(f)\int _ Xf\, \mathrm dV$. $\square$

The capacity assumption concerns the fixed datum and implies the root-tail condition. Whether root tails alone suffice for the exact equation when $v_-(h)=0$ remains part of 33.1.1; whether every positive-threshold root has these tails is the question following proposition 14.31.5.

14.32.1 A logarithmic density with critical coefficient one

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The power capacity condition can hold for a density outside every $L^ p$ class with $p>1$. A bounded continuous potential with logarithmic modulus at one point gives such a datum on each fixed Hermitian background.

Theorem 14.32.2. In the setting of theorem 14.32.1, fix $\alpha >0$. There is $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)\cap C(X)$ with $h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq h/2$ whose measure $\mathrm{MA}_ h(u)=f\, \mathrm dV$ satisfies

\[ f\in L^1(\mathrm dV),\qquad f\notin L^ p(\mathrm dV)\quad \text{for every }p>1. \]

For some $A>0$ it satisfies (14.115), and $b_ h(f)=1$. For all sufficiently large $K$,

14.118
\begin{equation} \rho _ h\bigl (f^{1/n}\mathbf1_{\{ f>K\} }\bigr ) \leq C(\log K)^{-\alpha }. \end{equation}

Proof. Choose a holomorphic ball with coordinates centred at $x_0$ and $R=|z|<1$, and a smooth cutoff $0\leq \chi \leq 1$ supported in it and equal to one on a smaller ball. Put

\[ q(s)=(1-s)^{-\alpha },\qquad s=\log R^2, \qquad \psi =\chi q(\log R^2)+1-\chi . \]

Set $\psi (x_0)=0$ and extend $\psi =1$ outside the ball. In the smaller ball, $q'=\alpha (1-s)^{-\alpha -1}>0$ and $q''=\alpha (\alpha +1)(1-s)^{-\alpha -2}>0$, so $\psi $ is plurisubharmonic there, including its continuous extension at $x_0$. The transition is smooth and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi \geq -K_0h$ globally for a fixed $K_0$. For sufficiently small $\delta >0$, the normalized potential $u=\delta \psi -\sup _ X\delta \psi $ satisfies $h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq h/2$.

The bounded product gives zero mass to $x_0$. Indeed, after a smooth local correction, its expansion is dominated in total variation by a constant times the top product of a bounded plurisubharmonic function plus a strictly plurisubharmonic quadratic function. Rescaling this sum into $[0,1]$ bounds that top product by local capacity, and a pluripolar point has capacity zero. Off $x_0$ the potential is smooth. Thus the measure is absolutely continuous; write it as $f\, \mathrm dV$. The strict lower bound implies $f\geq c>0$ almost everywhere.

In the smaller ball the tangential and radial eigenvalues of the Hessian of $q(\log R^2)$ are $q'/R^2$ and $q''/R^2$. In the operator convention of the book this gives

14.119
\begin{equation} (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n =\frac{\delta ^ nn!\alpha ^ n(\alpha +1)}{\pi ^ n} R^{-2n}(1-\log R^2)^{-n(\alpha +1)-1}\, \mathrm d\lambda , \end{equation}

where $\mathrm d\lambda $ is Euclidean volume.

Integration over the ball gives

\[ \int _{B_ r}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n =\delta ^ n\alpha ^ n(1-\log r^2)^{-n(\alpha +1)}. \]

For $k<n$, the degree-$k$ mixed terms with the smooth positive form $h$ have density bounded by $CR^{-2k}(1-\log R^2)^{-k(\alpha +1)}$ and mass $O(r^2)$ on $B_ r$. The expansion has positive terms in this ball. Hence $f\in L^1$. For $p>1$, the pure top term gives a positive multiple of the divergent integral

\[ \int _0^ r R^{2n(1-p)-1} (1-\log R^2)^{-p(n(\alpha +1)+1)}\, \mathrm dR \]

as a lower bound for $\int _{B_ r}f^ p\, \mathrm dV$, since smooth volume densities are comparable. Thus $f\notin L^ p$. For all sufficiently small $r$,

14.120
\begin{equation} \int _{B_ r}f\, \mathrm dV \leq C\bigl [(1-\log r^2)^{-n(\alpha +1)}+r^2\bigr ], \qquad \mathop{\rm ess\, sup}_{X\setminus B_ r}f\leq Cr^{-2n}. \end{equation}

By (1.25),

\[ \mathrm dV(E)\leq C\exp \bigl (-a\operatorname {Cap}_ h(E)^{-1/n}\bigr ). \]

Let $s=\operatorname {Cap}_ h(E)>0$ be small and choose $r=\exp [-as^{-1/n}/(4n)]$. Splitting $E$ inside and outside $B_ r$ and applying (14.120), we obtain

\[ \int _ Ef\, \mathrm dV \leq Cs^{1+\alpha } +C\exp [-as^{-1/n}/(2n)] +C\exp [-as^{-1/n}/2] \leq C's^{1+\alpha }. \]

For larger $s$ use the finite total mass; for $s=0$ the volume estimate gives $\mathrm dV(E)=0$. This proves (14.115) for every Borel set.

By theorem 14.32.1, there is a normalized continuous $\phi $ with $\mathrm{MA}_ h(\phi )=b_ h(f)f\, \mathrm dV$. The explicit potential $u$ is a subsolution with coefficient one, so theorem 14.27.2 gives $b_ h(f)\geq 1$. Suppose $b_ h(f)>1$, and choose $0<\varepsilon <1$ with $(1-\varepsilon )^ nb_ h(f)>1$. Positive multilinearity gives

\[ \mathrm{MA}_ h((1-\varepsilon )\phi ) \geq (1-\varepsilon )^ nb_ h(f)f\, \mathrm dV. \]

Apply theorem 10.2.1 to $u,\phi $ on

\[ U_ s=\{ u<(1-\varepsilon )\phi + \inf _ X(u-(1-\varepsilon )\phi )+s\} . \]

For sufficiently small $s>0$ its reverse factor $Q_\varepsilon (s)$ is defined, tends to one as $s\downarrow 0$, and satisfies

\[ (1-\varepsilon )^ nb_ h(f)\int _{U_ s}f\, \mathrm dV \leq Q_\varepsilon (s)\int _{U_ s}f\, \mathrm dV. \]

Choose $s$ so that $Q_\varepsilon (s)<(1-\varepsilon )^ nb_ h(f)$. The set $U_ s$ is open and nonempty, and $f\geq c>0$ almost everywhere, so its $f\, \mathrm dV$ measure is positive. This contradiction proves $b_ h(f)=1$.

Finally take $r_ K=K^{-1/(4n)}$. The outside bound in (14.120) gives $f\leq CK^{1/2}<K$ almost everywhere on $X\setminus B_{r_ K}$ for large $K$. Therefore

\[ \int _{\{ f>K\} }f\, \mathrm dV \leq C(\log K)^{-n(\alpha +1)}+CK^{-1/(2n)} \leq C'(\log K)^{-n(\alpha +1)}. \]

Apply (14.116) to $g=f\mathbf1_{\{ f>K\} }$ to get (14.118). $\square$

The potential has logarithmic modulus at $x_0$ and is not Hölder there. Its root-tail estimate follows from the capacity bound despite the failure of higher $L^ p$ integrability. The construction applies to each fixed background, including one with $v_-(h)=0$.

14.33 Limits of exponential equations

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

In this section $\theta $ is an arbitrary smooth real $(1,1)$-form and $\mu $ a finite non-pluripolar measure. We consider solutions $u_\lambda \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ of

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (u_\lambda )=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_\lambda }\mu \qquad (\lambda >0). \]

As $\lambda \downarrow 0$, one expects suitably normalized solutions to converge to a solution of $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=c\mu $ with a scalar $c>0$. For an $L^ p$ density with $p>1$ and a Hermitian background, the continuous solutions satisfy $\lambda u_\lambda \to \log c$ uniformly (proposition 10.6.1). For solutions in a fixed unbounded ordinary type, no such uniform convergence is possible: by proposition 1.23.2, the $L^\infty $ distance from $\lambda u_\lambda $ to every finite constant is infinite for each $\lambda >0$. The limit must instead be formulated through normalized potentials and their measures. Write

\[ v_\lambda =u_\lambda -\sup _ Xu_\lambda ,\qquad c_\lambda =\mathrm{e}^{\lambda \sup _ Xu_\lambda }, \]

so that $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (v_\lambda )=c_\lambda \mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda }\mu $. By lemma 1.23.3, $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda }\to 1$ in $L^1(\mu )$ as $\lambda \downarrow 0$; hence, if $c_\lambda \to c<+\infty $, the right-hand sides converge in total variation to $c\mu $. If $\mu =f\, \mathrm{d}V$ with $f\in L^ p$ for some $p>1$, lemma 1.23.3 gives an error linear in $\lambda $.

To identify the Monge–Ampère measure of an $L^1$ limit of the $v_\lambda $, one needs in addition a continuity theorem for the Monge–Ampère operator. For potentials of a fixed ordinary type, corollary 14.8.3 identifies the limiting measure under a common barrier and a domination hypothesis. In a relative envelope class, theorem 31.3.3 instead constructs a common lower envelope under positive lower mass: for a semipositive big $\theta $ and a reference satisfying (13.26), an $L^1$-convergent sequence in the class whose measures are dominated by one finite non-pluripolar measure converges in capacity, and its measures converge weakly. That argument, with its mass hypothesis, is developed in chapter 31.

14.33.1 A positive limiting exponent

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

When the exponents converge to a positive limit, the factor $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}$ makes the Monge–Ampère mass of deep sublevels uniformly small. If the potentials converge in capacity, the tail criterion of theorem 7.4.1 then identifies the limit of their Monge–Ampère measures, and convergence of the right-hand sides identifies the equation.

Theorem 14.33.1. Let $\theta $ be any smooth real $(1,1)$-form and $\mu $ a finite non-pluripolar measure. Suppose $f_ j\geq 0$, $f_ j\to f$ in $L^1(\mu )$, and $\lambda _ j>0$ with $\lambda _ j\to \lambda >0$. Let $u_ j,u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ be nonpositive, suppose $u_ j\to u$ in capacity, and assume

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u_ j) =\mathrm{e}^{\lambda _ j u_ j}f_ j\, \mu . \]

Then $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)$ is finite and

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}f\, \mu . \]

Moreover, $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u_ j)\rightharpoonup \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)$ weakly, and the right-hand sides converge in total variation.

Proof. Discarding finitely many indices, we may assume that $\lambda _ j\geq \lambda /2$. The measures $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u_ j)$ have masses at most $\| f_ j\| _{L^1(\mu )}$, which are uniformly bounded, and since $u_ j\leq 0$, their deep sublevels satisfy

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u_ j)(\{ u_ j<-T\} ) \leq \mathrm{e}^{-\lambda T/2}\| f_ j\| _{L^1(\mu )}. \]

The tail criterion theorem 7.4.1, which holds for an arbitrary smooth background by theorem 7.6.1, therefore shows that $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)$ is finite and that $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u_ j)\rightharpoonup \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)$.

It remains to identify this limit with $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}f\, \mu $. By lemma 1.8.11, $u_ j\to u$ in $\mu $-measure. Since $t\mapsto \mathrm{e}^{\lambda t}$ is $\lambda $-Lipschitz for $t\leq 0$, the functions $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_ j}$, which are bounded by one, converge in $\mu $-measure to $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}$. The change of exponent is uniform for nonpositive arguments, because the mean value theorem gives

\[ \sup _{t\leq 0}|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda _ jt}-\mathrm{e}^{\lambda t}| \leq |\lambda _ j-\lambda | \sup _{s\geq 0}s \mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda /2)s}\longrightarrow 0. \]

Thus $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda _ j u_ j}\to \mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}$ in $\mu $-measure, and hence in $f\mu $-measure, since the finite measure $f\mu $ is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu $. As these functions are bounded by one, the convergence also holds in $L^1(f\mu )$. Therefore

\[ \begin{split} \int _ X|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda _ j u_ j}f_ j-\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}f|\, \mathrm{d}\mu & \leq \| f_ j-f\| _{L^1(\mu )}\\ & \quad +\int _ X|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda _ j u_ j}-\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}|f\, \mathrm{d}\mu \longrightarrow 0. \end{split} \]

This proves the total-variation convergence of the right-hand sides, and comparing the two limits gives the equation for $u$. $\square$

Additive normalizations of the potentials can be absorbed into the densities: translating each $u_ j$ by a constant multiplies $f_ j$ by an exponential factor, and the theorem applies as long as the modified densities still converge in $L^1(\mu )$. Convergence in capacity is a hypothesis; the theorem says nothing about sequences that converge only in $L^1$, and it does not keep the limit in a prescribed envelope class.

The proof also shows where the difficulty lies as $\lambda _ j\downarrow 0$: the tail bound $\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda _ jT}\| f_ j\| _{L^1(\mu )}$ no longer tends to zero uniformly in $j$ as $T\to \infty $. A zero-exponent limit therefore needs another source of uniform tail control, such as the integrability condition of corollary 7.4.2; a uniform bound on the total masses does not by itself control these tails.

14.34 Exponential equations for an individual density

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We return to the Hermitian background $h$ and a fixed nonzero density $0\leq f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$, and consider, for $\lambda >0$, the exponential equation

14.123
\begin{equation} \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}f\, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h). \end{equation}

A solution is a function $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ satisfying (14.123), and an exponential subsolution at exponent $\lambda $ is a function $s\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ with $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(s)\geq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda s}f\, \mathrm{d}V$. For the truncations $f_ k=\min (f,k)$, theorem 10.1.2 gives continuous solutions $u_ k$ of the truncated equations, and these decrease as $k$ increases. Strict domination shows that every exponential subsolution is a common lower bound for the $u_ k$. Hence, as soon as one subsolution exists, the decreasing limit is not identically $-\infty $, and theorem 14.33.1 shows that it solves (14.123); it is the greatest subsolution. A positive threshold $b_ h(f)$ provides exponential subsolutions for every exponent, and the normalized greatest solutions then recover $b_ h(f)$ as $\lambda \downarrow 0$.

Theorem 14.34.1. Fix $\lambda >0$, and suppose that (14.123) has an exponential subsolution $s\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$. For $k\geq 1$, let $u_ k$ be the continuous solution of

\[ \mathrm{MA}_ h(u_ k)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_ k}f_ k\, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad f_ k=\min (f,k), \]

given by theorem 10.1.2. Then $u_ k$ decreases to a solution $u_\lambda $ of (14.123), and $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u_\lambda )$ has finite mass. Moreover, $u_\lambda $ is the greatest exponential subsolution: every exponential subsolution $v$ at exponent $\lambda $ satisfies $v\leq u_\lambda $. In particular, $u_\lambda $ is the greatest solution of (14.123).

Proof. Since $f_ k\leq f_{k+1}$, lemma 10.4.2 gives $u_{k+1}\leq u_ k$.

We first show that every exponential subsolution $v$ lies below each $u_ k$. Let $t>0$. On the set $\{ u_ k<v-t\} $,

\[ \mathrm{MA}_ h(u_ k)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_ k}f_ k\, \mathrm{d}V \leq \mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v}f\, \mathrm{d}V \leq \mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(v-t). \]

Since $u_ k$ is bounded, $P_{h/2}(u_ k)\geq \inf _ Xu_ k$ is not identically $-\infty $, and $v-t\preceq u_ k$. Strict domination, theorem 14.1.2, therefore gives $u_ k\geq v-t$. Letting $t\downarrow 0$ gives $u_ k\geq v$.

In particular $s\leq u_ k\leq u_1$ for every $k$, so the decreasing limit $u_\lambda \geq s$ is not identically $-\infty $ and belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$. By lemma 1.8.12, $u_ k\to u_\lambda $ in capacity. Choose a constant $A\geq \sup _ Xu_1$. The nonpositive functions $u_ k-A$ satisfy

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u_ k-A)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda (u_ k-A)}\, \mathrm{e}^{\lambda A}f_ k\, \mathrm{d}V, \]

and $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda A}f_ k\to \mathrm{e}^{\lambda A}f$ in $L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$. Theorem 14.33.1, applied with $\mu =\mathrm{d}V$ and constant exponents, shows that $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u_\lambda )$ is finite and that $u_\lambda $ solves (14.123). Finally, every exponential subsolution lies below all $u_ k$, hence below $u_\lambda $, which is itself a subsolution. $\square$

Being the greatest subsolution, $u_\lambda $ does not depend on the approximation used to construct it. The equation may have other, smaller solutions with different singularities. If $f\in L^ p$ for some $p>1$, the continuous solution of theorem 10.1.2 is a subsolution and therefore lies below $u_\lambda $. Thus $u_\lambda $ is bounded, and bounded exponential comparison (corollary 10.2.4) shows that it coincides with the continuous solution.

Corollary 14.34.2. Suppose $b=b_ h(f)>0$. Then (14.123) has a greatest solution $u_\lambda $ for every $\lambda >0$. If $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ satisfies $\sup _ Xv=0$ and $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(v)\geq bf\, \mathrm{d}V$, then

\[ u_\lambda \geq v+\lambda ^{-1}\log b. \]

Put

\[ M_\lambda =\sup _ Xu_\lambda ,\qquad v_\lambda =u_\lambda -M_\lambda ,\qquad c_\lambda =\mathrm{e}^{\lambda M_\lambda }. \]

Then, as $\lambda \downarrow 0$,

\[ c_\lambda \longrightarrow b_ h(f),\qquad \mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_\lambda }f\longrightarrow b_ h(f)f \quad \text{in }L^1(\mathrm{d}V). \]

Proof. By theorem 14.27.2, a function $v$ as in the statement exists. Since $v\leq 0$, the function $s=v+\lambda ^{-1}\log b$ satisfies

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(s)\geq bf\, \mathrm{d}V \geq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda s}f\, \mathrm{d}V, \]

so it is an exponential subsolution. Theorem 14.34.1 gives the greatest solution $u_\lambda $ and the bound $u_\lambda \geq s$; in particular, $c_\lambda \geq b$.

Set $g_\lambda =\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda }f$. By lemma 1.23.3, applied to the finite measure $f\, \mathrm{d}V$, we have $g_\lambda \to f$ in $L^1$ as $\lambda \downarrow 0$. Each $g_\lambda $ is nonzero, since $v_\lambda $ is finite almost everywhere. The equation for $u_\lambda $ reads $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(v_\lambda )=c_\lambda g_\lambda \, \mathrm{d}V$, so theorem 14.27.2, applied to the density $g_\lambda $, gives

\[ c_\lambda \leq b_ h(g_\lambda ). \]

The upper semicontinuity of the threshold (theorem 14.31.1) therefore gives

\[ b\leq \liminf _{\lambda \downarrow 0}c_\lambda \leq \limsup _{\lambda \downarrow 0}c_\lambda \leq b_ h(f)=b. \]

This proves $c_\lambda \to b$. Since $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_\lambda }f=c_\lambda g_\lambda $ and $g_\lambda \to f$ in $L^1$, it also proves $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_\lambda }f\to bf$ in $L^1$. $\square$

Since $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(v_\lambda )=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_\lambda }f\, \mathrm{d}V$, the Monge–Ampère measures of the normalized greatest solutions converge in total variation to $b_ h(f)f\, \mathrm{d}V$. This answers the questions about the scalar and the measure raised in section 14.33; the potentials $v_\lambda $ themselves are harder to control. Under positive lower mass, corollary 31.7.1 shows that every $L^1$-convergent sequence $v_{\lambda _ j}$ with $\lambda _ j\downarrow 0$ converges in capacity to a solution of $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(v)=b_ h(f)f\, \mathrm{d}V$. For $L^ p$ data with $p>1$, proposition 14.19.1 gives uniform precompactness. In both cases, convergence of the whole family $(v_\lambda )$ requires that all its cluster limits coincide. This holds when the dual problem for $f^{1/n}$ over Gauduchon currents, studied in section 26.9, has a smooth strictly positive maximizer: the whole family then converges uniformly (corollary 26.9.7). If the maximizer is smooth and semipositive, and strictly positive outside a proper analytic set, proposition 26.9.11 gives $L^1$ convergence of the whole family and a fixed lower barrier, and corollary 26.9.18 strengthens this to uniform convergence for $L^ p$ data with $p>1$.

For a general positive threshold, the corollary gives the lower bound

\[ v_\lambda \geq v-\lambda ^{-1}\log (c_\lambda /b_ h(f)) \]

for every normalized threshold subsolution $v$. The next subsection estimates the scalar error $\log (c_\lambda /b_ h(f))$ when $f^{1/n}$ can be approximated by bounded functions in the norm of $\mathcal D_ h$. When $b_ h(f)=0$, theorem 14.34.1 still applies whenever an exponential subsolution exists; examples are constructed in 14.34.2.

14.34.1 An error bound for the scalar limit

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Suppose that $f^{1/n}$ belongs to the space $\mathcal D_ h^0$ of 14.31.2. The uniform tail condition of proposition 14.31.5 then gives a rate for the convergence $c_\lambda \to b_ h(f)$ in corollary 14.34.2. Split the root $p=f^{1/n}$ at a height $K$. On $\{ p\leq K\} $, the error $p(1-\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v/n})$ of the damped root has norm $O(K\lambda )$ in $\mathcal D_ h$, uniformly over normalized potentials $v$, while on $\{ p>K\} $ it is bounded by the tail of $p$, which is small in that norm. Optimizing over $K$ gives a modulus valid for every normalized potential.

Proposition 14.34.3. Let $0\leq p\in \mathcal D_ h^0$ be nonzero, and put $f=p^ n$, $R=\rho _ h(p)$ and $b=R^{-n}=b_ h(f)$. There is a constant $C_ h$, depending only on $h$ and $\mathrm{d}V$, such that the function

\[ \eta _ p(\lambda )=\inf _{K>0} \left(\| p\mathbf1_{\{ p>K\} }\| _{\mathcal D_ h}+C_ hK\lambda \right) \]

tends to zero as $\lambda \downarrow 0$ and satisfies

14.124
\begin{equation} \big \| p(1-\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v/n})\big \| _{\mathcal D_ h} \leq \eta _ p(\lambda ) \qquad \bigl (v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h),\ \sup _ Xv=0\bigr ). \end{equation}

Let $u_\lambda $, $v_\lambda $ and $c_\lambda $ be the greatest exponential solutions and their normalizations from corollary 14.34.2. If $\eta _ p(\lambda )<R$, then

\[ b\leq c_\lambda \leq \bigl (R-\eta _ p(\lambda )\bigr )^{-n} \]

and

\begin{align*} \big \| \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(v_\lambda ) -bf\, \mathrm{d}V\big \| _{\mathrm{TV}} & \leq (c_\lambda -b)\| p\| _{L^ n}^{n} +nb\| p\| _{L^ n}^{n-1}\eta _ p(\lambda ). \end{align*}

If $p\in L^ r$ for some finite $r>n$, the same bounds hold with $\eta _ p(\lambda )$ replaced by $C_{h,r}\| p\| _{L^ r}\lambda $, where $C_{h,r}$ depends only on $h$, $\mathrm{d}V$ and $r$. In particular, for each such $p$ the scalar and total-variation errors are $O(\lambda )$.

Proof. By lemma 1.5.1, normalized $h$-plurisubharmonic functions are uniformly bounded in every $L^ s$ with $s<+\infty $. By (14.114) with $r=2n$, their norms $\| v\| _{\mathcal D_ h}$ are therefore uniformly bounded; let $C_ h$ be $1/n$ times this bound. Let $v$ be normalized and $K>0$. On $\{ p\leq K\} $ we have

\[ 0\leq p(1-\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v/n}) \leq K\lambda (-v)/n, \]

since $1-\mathrm{e}^{-x}\leq x$ for $x\geq 0$, while on $\{ p>K\} $ we use the bound $p(1-\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v/n})\leq p$. The monotonicity of $\| \cdot \| _{\mathcal D_ h}$ and the triangle inequality give

\[ \big \| p(1-\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v/n})\big \| _{\mathcal D_ h} \leq \| p\mathbf1_{\{ p>K\} }\| _{\mathcal D_ h}+C_ hK\lambda , \]

and taking the infimum over $K$ proves (14.124). By proposition 14.31.5, and since the $L^ n$ tails of $p$ tend to zero, $\| p\mathbf1_{\{ p>K\} }\| _{\mathcal D_ h}\to 0$ as $K\to \infty $. Choosing $K$ first and then letting $\lambda \downarrow 0$ shows that $\eta _ p(\lambda )\to 0$.

Now set $q_\lambda =p\, \mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda /n}$, so that $q_\lambda ^ n=g_\lambda =\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda }f$, with $g_\lambda $ as in the proof of corollary 14.34.2. Since $0\leq q_\lambda \leq p$ and $\rho _ h(p-q_\lambda )\leq \eta _ p(\lambda )$ by (14.124), the triangle inequality and monotonicity give

\[ R-\eta _ p(\lambda )\leq \rho _ h(q_\lambda )\leq R. \]

The same proof gives $b\leq c_\lambda \leq b_ h(g_\lambda )=\rho _ h(q_\lambda )^{-n}$, which yields the scalar bound. For the measures, the factorization of $p^ n-q_\lambda ^ n$ gives $0\leq p^ n-q_\lambda ^ n\leq np^{n-1}(p-q_\lambda )$, and Hölder's inequality implies

\[ \| f-g_\lambda \| _{L^1} \leq n\| p\| _{L^ n}^{n-1}\| p-q_\lambda \| _{L^ n} \leq n\| p\| _{L^ n}^{n-1}\eta _ p(\lambda ). \]

Since $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(v_\lambda )=c_\lambda g_\lambda \, \mathrm{d}V$, the identity $c_\lambda g_\lambda -bf=(c_\lambda -b)g_\lambda -b(f-g_\lambda )$, together with $\| g_\lambda \| _{L^1}\leq \| f\| _{L^1}=\| p\| _{L^ n}^ n$, proves the total-variation bound.

Finally, suppose $p\in L^ r$ with $n<r<+\infty $, and choose $n<s<r$. By (14.114) with exponent $s$, and since $\| \cdot \| _{L^ n}\leq \| \cdot \| _{L^ s}$, we have $\| \cdot \| _{\mathcal D_ h}\leq (1+C_ s)\| \cdot \| _{L^ s}$. Hölder's inequality and lemma 1.5.1 then give, for every normalized $v$,

\[ \| p(-v)\| _{\mathcal D_ h} \leq (1+C_ s)\| p(-v)\| _{L^ s} \leq (1+C_ s)\| p\| _{L^ r}\| v\| _{L^{rs/(r-s)}} \leq nC_{h,r}\| p\| _{L^ r}. \]

Since $0\leq 1-\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v/n}\leq \lambda (-v)/n$, the left side of (14.124) is therefore at most $C_{h,r}\| p\| _{L^ r}\lambda $, and the two estimates above can be repeated with this bound in place of $\eta _ p(\lambda )$. For $\lambda $ so small that $C_{h,r}\| p\| _{L^ r}\lambda \leq R/2$, the function $t\mapsto (R-t)^{-n}$ has bounded derivative on $[0,R/2]$, so the scalar error $c_\lambda -b$, and hence the total-variation error, is $O(\lambda )$. $\square$

For a general $p\in \mathcal D_ h^0$ the modulus $\eta _ p(\lambda )$ tends to zero, but we do not know whether it is $O(\lambda )$. Combined with the lower bound $v_\lambda \geq v-\lambda ^{-1}\log (c_\lambda /b)$ for normalized threshold subsolutions $v$, the proposition gives

\[ 0\leq \lambda ^{-1}\log (c_\lambda /b) \leq -\frac n\lambda \log \bigl (1-\eta _ p(\lambda )/R\bigr ), \]

which stays bounded as $\lambda \downarrow 0$ when $\eta _ p(\lambda )=O(\lambda )$, in particular for $p\in L^ r$ with $r>n$; root tails alone do not give such a bound. Even when a uniform lower barrier for $v_\lambda $ is available, passing to the limit in capacity requires the singularity-type or lower-mass hypotheses of a continuity theorem. The proposition estimates only scalars and measures; when the lower mass vanishes, it does not give an exact equation at the threshold.

14.34.2 Exponential equations at zero threshold

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

When $b_ h(f)=0$, no positive multiple of $f\, \mathrm{d}V$ is dominated by a Monge–Ampère measure. An exponential subsolution $s$ only needs $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(s)\geq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda s}f\, \mathrm{d}V$, however, and the factor $\mathrm{e}^{\lambda s}$ is small where $s$ is very negative. A density concentrating near the pole of a singular potential can therefore admit exponential subsolutions although its threshold vanishes. We construct such densities on the products of section 14.29.

We keep the notation of that section: $X=X_0\times Y$ with $Y=\mathbb P^ k$, the background $H=h_0+\kappa $, the constants $N$, $C$ and $V_0$, the probability volume form $\mu =V_0^{-1}h_0^ m\wedge \kappa ^ k$ with density $R=\mu /\mathrm{d}V$, and the potential $u_\alpha $ with density $q_\alpha $ from lemma 14.29.1, for a fixed $0<\alpha <1$. On $Y\setminus \{ o\} $ put $L=1-\psi \geq 1$, so that $u_\alpha =1-L^\alpha $, and $t=\mathrm e^{1-L}$. As functions of $L$, define

\[ a=\alpha L^{\alpha -1},\qquad Q=t+(1-t)a,\qquad D=t(1-a)+(1-t)\alpha (1-\alpha )L^{\alpha -2}, \]
\[ W=kQ^{k-1}D,\qquad B_\tau =q_\alpha \mathrm e^{\tau (L^\alpha -1)}\quad (\tau >0),\qquad b_0=(1-\alpha )^ k. \]

Here $Q$ is the function $Q(r)$ of lemma 14.29.1, written in terms of $L$, and $D=-\mathrm{d}Q/\mathrm{d}L$. The weight $B_\tau $ is chosen so that $\mathrm e^{\tau u_\alpha }B_\tau =q_\alpha $ off $o$.

Lemma 14.34.4. As functions of $L$,

\[ q_\alpha =Q^{k-1}D\, \mathrm e^{k(L-1)},\qquad q_\alpha \geq b_0, \]

and $W(L)\sim k\alpha ^ k(1-\alpha )L^{-1-k(1-\alpha )}$ as $L\to +\infty $. For every $\tau >0$, $\mathrm e^{\tau (L^\alpha -1)}W(L)\to +\infty $ as $L\to +\infty $. In particular, $B_\tau \kappa ^ k$ has infinite mass on every punctured neighborhood of $o$.

Proof. In the notation of the proof of lemma 14.29.1, $t=r/(1+r)$ and $s(r)=L$. Since the mass of $q_\alpha \kappa ^ k$ on $\{ r_1<r<r_2\} $ is $Q(r_2)^ k-Q(r_1)^ k$, this measure has radial distribution $-\mathrm{d}(Q^ k)/\mathrm{d}L=W(L)\, \mathrm{d}L$. The Fubini–Study measure $\kappa ^ k$ corresponds to $Q=t$, so its radial distribution is $k\mathrm e^{-k(L-1)}\mathrm{d}L$. Both measures are invariant under the unitary group of the chart, so $q_\alpha $ is the ratio of the two distributions, which is the stated formula. The lower bound $q_\alpha \geq b_0$ follows from $\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\alpha \geq (1-\alpha )\kappa $ off $o$. As $L\to +\infty $, $t$ tends to zero exponentially, so $Q\sim \alpha L^{\alpha -1}$ and $D\sim \alpha (1-\alpha )L^{\alpha -2}$; this gives the asymptotics of $W$. Consequently,

\[ \log \bigl (\mathrm e^{\tau (L^\alpha -1)}W(L)\bigr )=\tau L^\alpha -[1+k(1-\alpha )]\log L+O(1)\longrightarrow +\infty , \]

and the mass of $B_\tau \kappa ^ k$ on $\{ L>T\} $, which equals $\int _ T^\infty \mathrm e^{\tau (L^\alpha -1)}W(L)\, \mathrm{d}L$, is infinite for every $T$. $\square$

Theorem 14.34.5. Let $f\geq 0$ be a nonzero integrable function on $X$, and put $F=f/R$.

  1. If $\lambda >0$ and $F\leq AB_\lambda $ almost everywhere for a constant $A>0$, then

    \[ s_\lambda =u_\alpha +\lambda ^{-1}\log (CV_0/A) \]

    is an exponential subsolution at exponent $\lambda $, of mass $CV_0$, and the equation $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}f\, \mathrm{d}V$ has a greatest solution, whose mass is finite and positive.

  2. Suppose that $F$ is locally essentially bounded on $X\setminus (X_0\times \{ o\} )$ and

    \[ \lim _{T\to +\infty }\operatorname *{ess\, sup}_{x\in X_0,\ L(y)>T} \frac{\log ^+(F(x,y)/q_\alpha (y))}{L(y)^\alpha }=0, \]

    where $\log ^+r=\log \max (1,r)$ for $r\geq 0$. Then the hypothesis of (1), and hence its conclusion, holds for every $\lambda >0$.

Proof. (1) By theorem 14.29.3, $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(u_\alpha )=CV_0q_\alpha \mu $. Off the pole fiber $X_0\times \{ o\} $ we have $\mathrm e^{\lambda u_\alpha }B_\lambda =q_\alpha $ and $f\, \mathrm{d}V=F\mu \leq AB_\lambda \mu $, hence

\[ \mathrm e^{\lambda s_\lambda }f\, \mathrm dV \leq (CV_0/A)\mathrm e^{\lambda u_\alpha }AB_\lambda \mu =CV_0q_\alpha \mu =\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(s_\lambda ), \]

and both measures vanish on the pole fiber. Thus $s_\lambda $ is an exponential subsolution, and its mass is $CV_0$, since adding a constant does not change the Monge–Ampère measure. Theorem 14.34.1 now gives the greatest solution $u$ and the finiteness of its mass. The mass $\int _ X\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}f\, \mathrm{d}V$ is positive because $u$ is finite almost everywhere and $f$ has positive integral.

(2) Fix $\lambda >0$. By hypothesis, there is $T$ such that $F/q_\alpha \leq \mathrm e^{(\lambda /2)L^\alpha }$ almost everywhere on $\{ L>T\} $. Since $\mathrm e^{(\lambda /2)L^\alpha }\leq \mathrm e^{\lambda }\mathrm e^{\lambda (L^\alpha -1)}$ for $L\geq 1$, we have $F\leq \mathrm e^{\lambda }B_\lambda $ there. On the complement $\{ L\leq T\} $, which is compact and disjoint from the pole fiber, $F$ is essentially bounded, while $B_\lambda \geq q_\alpha \geq b_0>0$. Increasing the constant, we obtain $F\leq AB_\lambda $ almost everywhere on $X$. $\square$

Theorem 14.34.6. Let $F_ j$, $j\geq 1$, be smooth strictly positive probability densities on $X_0$ with respect to $\mathrm dV_0=V_0^{-1}h_0^ m$, whose normalization scalars on $X_0$, relative to $h_0$ and $\mathrm dV_0$, satisfy $c_{h_0}(F_ j)\leq 2^{-2j}/C$. There are smooth strictly positive probability densities $g_ j$ on $Y$ with respect to $\kappa ^ k$ such that the probability densities

\[ f_ j=RF_ jg_ j,\qquad f=\sum _{j\geq 1}2^{-j}f_ j \]

on $X$ satisfy $c_ H(f_ j)\leq 2^{-2j}$ and $b_ H(f)=0$. The function $f$ is finite everywhere, smooth off the pole fiber $X_0\times \{ o\} $, has integral one and is bounded below by a positive constant. For every $\lambda >0$, the equation $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}f\, \mathrm{d}V$ has an exponential subsolution and a greatest solution, of finite mass, and every solution is unbounded.

Proof. Put $w_ j=2^{-j}$ and $M_ j=\max _{X_0}F_ j$.

Step 1: a smooth baseline. Fix $C^ j$ norms $\| \cdot \| _{C^ j}$ on $X_0$, computed in a fixed finite atlas, so that they increase with $j$. Choose $0<\varepsilon _ j\leq 1/2$ with

\[ w_ j\varepsilon _ jM_ j\leq b_0 2^{-j-2},\qquad w_ j\varepsilon _ j\| F_ j\| _{C^ j}\leq 2^{-j}. \]

Then $E=\sum _ jw_ j\varepsilon _ jF_ j$ converges in every $C^ l$ norm, so $E$ is smooth on $X_0$, and $0<E\leq b_0/4$.

Step 2: bumps escaping to the pole. Choose a smooth function $\chi :\mathbb R\to [0,1]$, supported in $[0,1]$, positive on $(0,1)$ and flat at $0$ and $1$. Starting with $T_0=0$, choose inductively $T_ j\geq 2$ with $T_ j>T_{j-1}+2$ and

\[ Z_ j=\int _{T_ j}^{T_ j+1}\chi (L-T_ j) \mathrm e^{(L^\alpha -1)/j}W(L)\, \mathrm dL \geq 2w_ jM_ j. \]

This is possible because, by lemma 14.34.4 with $\tau =1/j$, the factor $\mathrm e^{(L^\alpha -1)/j}W(L)$ is uniformly large on sufficiently distant intervals. On the annulus $\{ T_ j\leq L\leq T_ j+1\} $ put $\beta _ j=\chi (L-T_ j)B_{1/j}/Z_ j$, extend $\beta _ j$ by zero, and set $g_ j=\varepsilon _ j+(1-\varepsilon _ j)\beta _ j$. Since $\chi $ is flat at the endpoints, $\beta _ j$ is smooth; it vanishes near $o$ and near the hyperplane $\{ L=1\} $. Its integral against $\kappa ^ k$ is one, so $g_ j$ is a smooth strictly positive probability density with respect to $\kappa ^ k$. Moreover,

\[ w_ jM_ j(1-\varepsilon _ j)\beta _ j\leq B_{1/j}/2. \]

Step 3: the scalars $c_ H(f_ j)$. By theorem 9.1.1 on $Y$, there is a smooth $\phi _ j$ with $\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi _ j>0$ and $(\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi _ j)^ k=g_ j\kappa ^ k$; the scalar is one because both sides have mass one. On $X_0$, the same theorem gives a smooth $v_ j$ with $h_0+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j>0$ and $(h_0+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j)^ m=c_{h_0}(F_ j)F_ j\, \mathrm dV_0$. Since only one term of the binomial expansion survives for dimensional reasons,

\[ (H+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v_ j+\phi _ j))^ N =C c_{h_0}(F_ j)F_ jg_ j\mu =C c_{h_0}(F_ j)f_ j\, \mathrm dV. \]

Uniqueness of the scalar gives $c_ H(f_ j)=C c_{h_0}(F_ j)\leq 2^{-2j}$.

Step 4: properties of $f$. By Tonelli's theorem, $\int _ Xf\, \mathrm dV=\sum _ jw_ j=1$. The density of $f\, \mathrm{d}V$ with respect to $\mu $ is

\[ F=f/R=\sum _ jw_ jF_ jg_ j=E+\sum _ jw_ j(1-\varepsilon _ j)F_ j\beta _ j. \]

The annuli are disjoint and escape to the pole, so only finitely many bumps meet any compact subset of $Y\setminus \{ o\} $; hence $F$, and therefore $f$, is smooth off the pole fiber. On the pole fiber all bumps vanish and $F=E$, so $f$ is finite everywhere. Also $f\geq w_1f_1\geq \frac12\min _ Xf_1>0$. If $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(u)\geq af\, \mathrm{d}V$ with $a>0$, then $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(u)\geq aw_ jf_ j\, \mathrm{d}V$, and lemma 14.26.1 gives

\[ aw_ j\leq c_ H(f_ j)\leq 2^{-2j},\quad \text{that is,}\quad a\leq 2^{-j}, \quad \text{for every }j. \]

This is impossible, so $b_ H(f)=0$ by theorem 14.27.2.

Step 5: exponential subsolutions. Fix $\lambda >0$, and choose $J$ with $1/j\leq \lambda $ for $j\geq J$. Since $L^\alpha -1\geq 0$, we have $B_{1/j}\leq B_\lambda $ for these $j$. As the bumps have disjoint supports, Step 2 bounds their total contribution $\sum _{j\geq J}w_ j(1-\varepsilon _ j)F_ j\beta _ j$ by $B_\lambda /2$. The baseline satisfies $E\leq b_0/4\leq B_\lambda /4$, because $B_\lambda \geq q_\alpha \geq b_0$. The remaining finitely many bumps are bounded by the constant

\[ K_\lambda =\max _ Y\sum _{j<J}w_ j(1-\varepsilon _ j)M_ j\beta _ j, \]

and $K_\lambda \leq (K_\lambda /b_0)B_\lambda $. Hence $F\leq A_\lambda B_\lambda $ with $A_\lambda =3/4+K_\lambda /b_0$, and theorem 14.34.5 gives the exponential subsolution $u_\alpha +\lambda ^{-1}\log (CV_0/A_\lambda )$ and the greatest solution. Finally, a bounded solution $u$ would satisfy $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(u)\geq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda \inf _ Xu}f\, \mathrm{d}V$, a positive scaled subsolution, contradicting $b_ H(f)=0$. Hence every solution is unbounded. $\square$

The density just constructed also satisfies the criterion in part (2) of theorem 14.34.5: on the $j$th annulus, $F/q_\alpha \leq 1/4+\frac12\mathrm e^{(L^\alpha -1)/j}$, and away from all annuli the ratio is at most $1/4$. Approaching the pole through the annuli forces $j\to \infty $, so the required limit is zero. Since $b_ H(f)=0$, proposition 14.27.4 also shows that $f$ belongs to no $L^ p$ with $p>1$.

Densities $F_ j$ as in the theorem exist whenever $v_-(h_0)=0$, by theorem 26.14.3, theorem 26.1.3. For instance, on the elliptic threefold, theorem 26.18.1 gives smooth functions $v_\ell $ with $h_0+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\ell >0$ and masses $\delta _\ell =\int _{X_0}(h_0+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\ell )^3\to 0$. The probability densities $F_\ell =(h_0+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\ell )^3/(\delta _\ell \, \mathrm dV_0)$ satisfy $c_{h_0}(F_\ell )=\delta _\ell $ by uniqueness of the scalar, and a subsequence with $\delta _{\ell _ j}\leq 2^{-2j}/C$ gives the required densities. Thus the construction applies to this threefold times $\mathbb P^ k$ for every $k\geq 1$. With the balanced metric of proposition 26.18.7, $V_0=24\pi $, so the explicit subsolutions have mass $CV_0$, equal to $96\pi $ on the fourfold ($k=1$) and $240\pi $ on the fivefold ($k=2$). These values concern the subsolutions; the greatest solutions have their own finite positive masses.

In this construction the projective components $g_ j$ are chosen after the densities $F_ j$, to fit them. We do not know whether the exponential equation is solvable for every prescribed concentration family of this kind, and we have no necessary criterion for solvability at zero threshold.

14.34.3 Solvable exponents and collapse at zero threshold

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We return to a general Hermitian form $h$ on $X$ and a nonzero density $0\leq f\in L^1(\mathrm dV)$. Let $\Lambda _ h(f)$ be the set of exponents $\lambda >0$ for which (14.123) has a solution. By theorem 14.34.1, $\lambda \in \Lambda _ h(f)$ if and only if the equation has an exponential subsolution at exponent $\lambda $.

Proposition 14.34.7. If $\lambda _0\in \Lambda _ h(f)$, then $[\lambda _0,+\infty )\subset \Lambda _ h(f)$. Consequently, $\Lambda _ h(f)$ is empty, or equals $(a,+\infty )$ for some $a\geq 0$, or equals $[a,+\infty )$ for some $a>0$. If $b_ h(f)>0$, then $\Lambda _ h(f)=(0,+\infty )$.

Proof. Let $u$ be a solution at $\lambda _0$, and put $s=u-\max (0,\sup _ Xu)$. Then $s\leq \min (u,0)$, so for $\lambda \geq \lambda _0$,

\begin{align*} \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(s) & =\mathrm e^{\lambda _0u}f\, \mathrm dV\\ & \geq \mathrm e^{\lambda _0s}f\, \mathrm dV \geq \mathrm e^{\lambda s}f\, \mathrm dV. \end{align*}

Thus $s$ is an exponential subsolution at every $\lambda \geq \lambda _0$, and theorem 14.34.1 gives a solution. Hence $\Lambda _ h(f)$ contains $[\lambda _0,+\infty )$ together with each of its points $\lambda _0$. A nonempty subset of $(0,+\infty )$ with this property is determined by its infimum $a$ and by whether $a$ belongs to it, which gives the stated forms. If $b_ h(f)>0$, corollary 14.34.2 gives $\Lambda _ h(f)=(0,+\infty )$. $\square$

Proposition 14.34.8. Suppose $b_ h(f)=0$. Let $\lambda _ j>0$ decrease to zero, and let $u_ j$ be any solution of (14.123) at exponent $\lambda _ j$. Put

\[ M_ j=\sup _ Xu_ j,\qquad v_ j=u_ j-M_ j,\qquad c_ j=\mathrm e^{\lambda _ jM_ j}. \]

Then

\begin{gather*} c_ j\longrightarrow 0,\qquad \lambda _ jM_ j\longrightarrow -\infty ,\\ \| \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u_ j)\| _{\mathrm{TV}} =\| \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(v_ j)\| _{\mathrm{TV}} \longrightarrow 0. \end{gather*}

Moreover, $\mathrm e^{\lambda _ ju_ j}\to 0$ uniformly on $X$, where the exponential is zero on the pole set, and $u_ j\to -\infty $ uniformly, that is, $\sup _ Xu_ j\to -\infty $.

Proof. Set $g_ j=\mathrm e^{\lambda _ jv_ j}f$. By lemma 1.23.3, applied to the finite non-pluripolar measure $f\, \mathrm dV$, $g_ j\to f$ in $L^1$; each $g_ j$ is nonzero, since $v_ j$ is finite almost everywhere. Since the Monge–Ampère measure is unchanged by adding constants,

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(v_ j)=c_ jg_ j\, \mathrm dV, \qquad \text{hence}\qquad 0<c_ j\leq b_ h(g_ j) \]

by theorem 14.27.2. The upper semicontinuity of the threshold, theorem 14.31.1, gives $\limsup _ jc_ j\leq b_ h(f)=0$. Since $0\leq g_ j\leq f$, the total masses are at most $c_ j\| f\| _{L^1}$ and tend to zero. Moreover $\lambda _ jM_ j=\log c_ j\to -\infty $, and $0\leq \mathrm e^{\lambda _ ju_ j}\leq \mathrm e^{\lambda _ jM_ j}=c_ j$. Finally, once $\lambda _ j<1$ and $\log c_ j<0$, we have $M_ j=\lambda _ j^{-1}\log c_ j\leq \log c_ j$, so $M_ j\to -\infty $. $\square$

For the density of theorem 14.34.6, solutions exist for every positive exponent, so the collapse applies as $\lambda \downarrow 0$ to every choice of solutions, in particular to the greatest ones. By contrast, the subsolutions of theorem 14.34.5 keep the mass $CV_0$ for every exponent, while the masses of the solutions above them tend to zero: on a nonclosed background, the order of two potentials does not give an order of their total Monge–Ampère masses. Whether the normalized potentials converge in capacity, and which equation a normalized cluster limit satisfies at exponent zero, are not decided by this argument. Positive endpoints of $\Lambda _ h(f)$ are governed instead by the compactness criterion below.

14.34.4 Positive endpoints and one-sided limits

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

For $\lambda \in \Lambda _ h(f)$, write $U_\lambda $ for the greatest solution given by theorem 14.34.1. When the exponents stay above a fixed $\tau >0$, the solution of a bounded truncated equation at exponent $\tau $ is a common upper barrier for all subsolutions. Together with the maximality of greatest solutions, this barrier gives an order estimate between $U_{\lambda _1}$ and $U_{\lambda _2}$, which controls the positive endpoint of $\Lambda _ h(f)$ and the limits of $U_\lambda $ from the right.

Lemma 14.34.9. For every $\tau >0$, there is a nonnegative $t_\tau \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)\cap C(X)$ such that every exponential subsolution at an exponent $\lambda \geq \tau $ lies below $t_\tau $. Put $K_\tau =\sup _ Xt_\tau $. If $\tau \leq \lambda _1\leq \lambda _2$ belong to $\Lambda _ h(f)$, then

\[ U_{\lambda _2}\geq U_{\lambda _1} -K_\tau \frac{\lambda _2-\lambda _1}{\lambda _2}. \]

Consequently, $\lambda \mapsto U_\lambda +K_\tau \log (\lambda /\tau )$ is nondecreasing on $\Lambda _ h(f)\cap [\tau ,+\infty )$.

Proof. Set $q=\min (f,1)$, a nonzero bounded density. By theorem 10.1.2, there is a continuous $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ with $\mathrm{MA}_ h(w)=\mathrm e^{\tau w}q\, \mathrm dV$. Put $t_\tau =w+\max (0,-\inf _ Xw)$. Then $t_\tau \geq 0$ and $t_\tau \geq w$, so for $\lambda \geq \tau $,

\[ \mathrm{MA}_ h(t_\tau )=\mathrm e^{\tau w}q\, \mathrm dV \leq \mathrm e^{\lambda t_\tau }f\, \mathrm dV. \]

Let $u$ be an exponential subsolution at an exponent $\lambda \geq \tau $, and let $\epsilon >0$. On $\{ t_\tau <u-\epsilon \} $,

\[ \mathrm{MA}_ h(t_\tau ) \leq \mathrm e^{\lambda (u-\epsilon )}f\, \mathrm dV \leq \mathrm e^{-\lambda \epsilon } \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u-\epsilon ). \]

The bounded function $t_\tau $ satisfies $P_{h/2}(t_\tau )\not\equiv -\infty $, and $u-\epsilon \preceq t_\tau $. Thus theorem 14.1.2 gives $t_\tau \geq u-\epsilon $, and letting $\epsilon \downarrow 0$ gives $t_\tau \geq u$.

For the order estimate, put $\delta =K_\tau (\lambda _2-\lambda _1)/\lambda _2$. By the first part, $U_{\lambda _1}\leq t_\tau \leq K_\tau $, so $(\lambda _2-\lambda _1)U_{\lambda _1}\leq \lambda _2\delta $, that is, $\lambda _1U_{\lambda _1}\geq \lambda _2(U_{\lambda _1}-\delta )$. Since the Monge–Ampère measure is unchanged by adding constants,

\begin{align*} \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(U_{\lambda _1}-\delta ) & =\mathrm e^{\lambda _1U_{\lambda _1}}f\, \mathrm dV\\ & \geq \mathrm e^{\lambda _2(U_{\lambda _1}-\delta )}f\, \mathrm dV. \end{align*}

Thus $U_{\lambda _1}-\delta $ is an exponential subsolution at $\lambda _2$, and the maximality of $U_{\lambda _2}$ gives $U_{\lambda _2}\geq U_{\lambda _1}-\delta $. Finally, $1-x^{-1}\leq \log x$ for $x\geq 1$; with $x=\lambda _2/\lambda _1$ this gives $\delta \leq K_\tau \log (\lambda _2/\lambda _1)$, which proves the monotonicity. $\square$

Proposition 14.34.10. Suppose $\Lambda _ h(f)\ne \varnothing $ and $a=\inf \Lambda _ h(f)>0$. Then $a\in \Lambda _ h(f)$ if and only if there are exponents $\lambda _ j>a$ decreasing to $a$ and solutions $u_ j$ at exponent $\lambda _ j$ with $\inf _ j\sup _ Xu_ j> -\infty $.

If $a\notin \Lambda _ h(f)$, then every sequence of solutions $u_ j$ at exponents $\lambda _ j>a$ decreasing to $a$ satisfies

\begin{gather*} \sup _ Xu_ j\longrightarrow -\infty ,\qquad \mathrm e^{\lambda _ ju_ j}\longrightarrow 0\ \text{uniformly on }X,\\ \| \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u_ j)\| _{\mathrm{TV}} \longrightarrow 0, \end{gather*}

where the exponential is zero on the pole sets.

Proof. Suppose first that $a\in \Lambda _ h(f)$, let $u_ a$ be a solution at $a$, and put $s=u_ a-\max (0,\sup _ Xu_ a)$. As in the proof of proposition 14.34.7, $s$ is an exponential subsolution at every $\lambda \geq a$, so $U_\lambda \geq s$. Hence the greatest solutions at any exponents $\lambda _ j\downarrow a$ have suprema at least $\sup _ Xs>-\infty $.

Conversely, let $\lambda _ j$ and $u_ j$ be as in the statement. The lemma with $\tau =a$ gives the common upper bound $u_ j\leq K_ a$. Write $M_ j=\sup _ Xu_ j$. After extraction, $M_ j\to M\in \mathbb R$ and, by theorem 1.4.2, $u_ j-M_ j\to v$ in $L^1$ for some $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$. Then $u_ j\to u=v+M$ in $L^1$ and, after a further extraction, almost everywhere. Since $a\leq \lambda _ j\leq \lambda _1$, $u_ j\leq K_ a$ and $K_ a\geq 0$, the densities $\mathrm e^{\lambda _ ju_ j}f$ are bounded by $\mathrm e^{\lambda _1K_ a}f$, and dominated convergence gives

\[ \mathrm e^{\lambda _ ju_ j}f\longrightarrow \mathrm e^{au}f \quad \text{in }L^1(\mathrm dV). \]

Lemma 14.27.1 now gives $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(u)\geq \mathrm e^{au}f\, \mathrm dV$. Thus $u$ is an exponential subsolution at $a$, and theorem 14.34.1 gives a solution at $a$.

Finally, suppose $a\notin \Lambda _ h(f)$, and let $u_ j$ be solutions at exponents $\lambda _ j\downarrow a$. If $\sup _ Xu_ j$ did not tend to $-\infty $, a subsequence would satisfy the condition just shown to imply $a\in \Lambda _ h(f)$. Hence $M_ j\to -\infty $. Once $M_ j<0$, since $\lambda _ j\geq a>0$,

\[ 0\leq \mathrm e^{\lambda _ ju_ j}\leq \mathrm e^{\lambda _ jM_ j}\leq \mathrm e^{aM_ j} \longrightarrow 0, \]

and the total mass $\int _ X\mathrm e^{\lambda _ ju_ j}f\, \mathrm dV$ is at most $\mathrm e^{aM_ j}\| f\| _{L^1}$. $\square$

Proposition 14.34.11. For every $a\in \Lambda _ h(f)$, the greatest solutions satisfy

\[ U_\lambda \longrightarrow U_ a \quad \text{in }L^1\text{ and in capacity}\quad (\lambda \downarrow a), \]

and their Monge–Ampère measures converge in total variation to $\mathrm e^{aU_ a}f\, \mathrm dV$.

Proof. Let $K=K_ a$ be the constant of the lemma with $\tau =a$, and let $\lambda _ j>a$ decrease to $a$. By the lemma, the functions

\[ V_ j=U_{\lambda _ j}+K\log (\lambda _ j/a) \]

decrease and satisfy $V_ j\geq U_ a$. Their limit $V$ therefore belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ and satisfies $V\geq U_ a$. Dominated convergence between the integrable bounds $U_ a$ and $V_1$ gives $V_ j\to V$ in $L^1$, and lemma 1.8.12 gives convergence in capacity. Since the added constants tend to zero, $U_{\lambda _ j}\to V$ in both senses.

The nonpositive functions $U_{\lambda _ j}-K$ satisfy

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(U_{\lambda _ j}-K)=\mathrm e^{\lambda _ j(U_{\lambda _ j}-K)}\, \mathrm e^{\lambda _ jK}f\, \mathrm dV, \]

and $\mathrm e^{\lambda _ jK}f\to \mathrm e^{aK}f$ in $L^1$. Theorem 14.33.1 therefore gives $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(V)=\mathrm e^{aV}f\, \mathrm dV$, together with total-variation convergence of the measures $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(U_{\lambda _ j})$. Hence $V$ is a solution at $a$, so $V\leq U_ a$ by maximality, while $V\geq U_ a$ by construction. Thus $V=U_ a$. Since every sequence of exponents converging to $a$ from above has a decreasing subsequence, this proves the assertions. $\square$

The corrected order also controls approach from the left. The limit solves the equation at the limiting exponent; maximality gives an inequality with the greatest solution there, but does not force equality.

Proposition 14.34.12. Suppose $\Lambda _ h(f)\ne \varnothing $, and put $a_0=\inf \Lambda _ h(f)$. For every $a>a_0$, there is $L_ a\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h)$ such that

\[ U_\lambda \longrightarrow L_ a \quad \text{in }L^1\text{ and in capacity}\quad (\lambda \uparrow a). \]

It satisfies

\[ L_ a\leq U_ a,\qquad \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ h(L_ a)=\mathrm e^{aL_ a}f\, \mathrm dV. \]

The equation measures converge in total variation to this finite measure. The left limit is independent of the auxiliary exponent and of the cofinal sequence used to construct it.

Proof. Choose $\tau \in \Lambda _ h(f)$ with $0<\tau <a$; the upper-interval property gives such a choice. Put $K=K_\tau $ from lemma 14.34.9 and

\[ V_\lambda =U_\lambda +K\log (\lambda /\tau ) \qquad (\lambda \in \Lambda _ h(f),\ \lambda \geq \tau ). \]

This family is increasing. For a sequence $\lambda _ j\uparrow a$ with $\tau \leq \lambda _ j<a$, its members lie between the integrable potentials $V_\tau $ and $V_ a$. The upper-envelope construction in section 1.19 gives

\[ V^-_ a=\left(\sup _{\tau \leq \lambda <a}V_\lambda \right)^* =\left(\lim _ jV_{\lambda _ j}\right)^*\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,h). \]

Indeed cofinality and the order give the same pointwise supremum for every such sequence. Regularization changes it only on a pluripolar set. Dominated convergence between the integrable bounds gives $L^1$ convergence to $V^-_ a$, and lemma 1.8.12 gives convergence in capacity. Since the added constants converge, set

\[ L_ a=V^-_ a-K\log (a/\tau ). \]

Then $U_{\lambda _ j}\to L_ a$ in both topologies and $L_ a\leq U_ a$; the latter inequality survives regularization because $V_ a$ is upper semicontinuous.

The potentials $U_{\lambda _ j}-K$ and their limit $L_ a-K$ are nonpositive. Apply theorem 14.33.1 with exponents tending to $a>0$ and densities $\mathrm e^{\lambda _ jK}f\to \mathrm e^{aK}f$ in $L^1$. It proves the exact equation and total-variation convergence.

Every sequence approaching $a$ from the left has a cofinal increasing subsequence. If potential convergence in either topology, or total-variation convergence of the equation measures, failed, a sequence staying outside a fixed neighbourhood of the proposed limit would therefore have an increasing subsequence, contrary to the preceding argument. This proves the full left limit and measure convergence. Repeating the construction with another $\tau $ gives the same $L^1$ limit; canonical quasi-plurisubharmonic representatives that agree almost everywhere agree everywhere. Thus the auxiliary choice does not change $L_ a$. $\square$

Proposition 14.34.13. Suppose $\Lambda _ h(f)\ne \varnothing $, put $a_0=\inf \Lambda _ h(f)$, and let $L_ a$ be the left limit of proposition 14.34.12. The set

\[ D=\{ a>a_0:L_ a\ne U_ a\} \]

is at most countable. At every $a>a_0$ with $a\notin D$, the greatest solutions are continuous in $L^1$ and in capacity as $\lambda \to a$, and their equation measures converge in total variation to $\mathrm e^{aU_ a}f\, \mathrm dV$.

Proof. Fix $a_0<\alpha <\beta $, choose an admitted $0<\tau <\alpha $, and use the corrected family $V_\lambda $ from the preceding proof on $[\alpha ,\beta ]$. Its integrals

\[ I(\lambda )=\int _ XV_\lambda \, \mathrm dV \]

form a finite bounded increasing real function. For $a\in (\alpha ,\beta ]$, the left-limit proposition gives

\[ I(a)-I(a-)=\int _ X(U_ a-L_ a)\, \mathrm dV. \]

If $L_ a\ne U_ a$, this quantity is positive: the integrable nonnegative difference cannot vanish almost everywhere without equality of the canonical potentials. For any finite ordered collection of such points, the sum of their jumps is at most $I(\beta )-I(\alpha )$. Consequently there are only finitely many jumps at least $1/m$ for each positive integer $m$, and at most countably many positive jumps. A countable cover by intervals with rational endpoints inside $(a_0,+\infty )$ proves the assertion about $D$.

If $a\notin D$, the left limit is $U_ a$, and proposition 14.34.11 supplies the same right limit in both topologies and for the equation measures. Splitting an arbitrary approaching sequence into its two sides proves the stated two-sided convergence. $\square$

Whether an actual jump occurs, or all left limits equal the greatest solution, remains undecided. An intrinsic criterion on $f$ and the existence or nonexistence of a positive excluded endpoint remain separate questions. These one-sided limits concern greatest solutions at positive exponents; they do not identify limits of arbitrary solutions with other singularities or supply a zero-exponent limit.

14.35 Radial selection for integrable densities

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The scalar and measure limits in corollary 14.34.2 do not by themselves determine a limit potential. For the sublogarithmic densities of lemma 14.29.1, the radial flux supplies the missing information. It also distinguishes convergence after subtracting the supremum from convergence after subtracting $\lambda ^{-1}\log b$, where $b$ is the critical scalar: the latter depends on a first moment of the limiting measure.

Use the product $X=X_0\times Y$, $Y=\mathbb P^ k$, $H=h_0+\kappa $, and the probability volume $\mu =V_0^{-1}h_0^ m\wedge \kappa ^ k$ of section 14.29, with $\dim X_0=m$, $v_-(h_0)=0$ and $k\geq 2$. Put $b=\binom NmV_0$, where $N=m+k$. For $0<\alpha <1$, let $U_{\lambda ,\alpha }$ be the greatest solution of

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(U_{\lambda ,\alpha }) =\mathrm e^{\lambda U_{\lambda ,\alpha }}q_\alpha \mu , \qquad \lambda >0. \]

The threshold of this forcing measure is $b>0$, by theorem 14.29.3, so the solutions exist by corollary 14.34.2.

14.35.1 Separation and the radial flux

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Lemma 14.35.1. There is a $\kappa $-plurisubharmonic function $w_{\lambda ,\alpha }$ on $Y$ such that

\[ U_{\lambda ,\alpha }=\lambda ^{-1}\log b+w_{\lambda ,\alpha },\qquad w_{\lambda ,\alpha }\geq u_\alpha , \qquad \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\kappa (w_{\lambda ,\alpha }) =\mathrm e^{\lambda w_{\lambda ,\alpha }}q_\alpha \kappa ^ k. \]

The last measure has mass one. On the affine chart centered at $o$, set $s=\log |z|^2$, $\rho (s)=\log (1+\mathrm e^ s)$ and $m(s)=\rho '(s)$. The function $w=w_{\lambda ,\alpha }$ is radial and satisfies

14.125
\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} Q_{\lambda ,\alpha }(s) & =\int _{-\infty }^ s\mathrm e^{\lambda w(r)}q_\alpha (r)\, \mathrm d(m(r)^ k),\\ w'(s)& =Q_{\lambda ,\alpha }(s)^{1/k}-m(s), \qquad Q_{\lambda ,\alpha }(-\infty )=0, \quad Q_{\lambda ,\alpha }(+\infty )=1. \end{aligned} \end{equation}

Proof. Choose a smooth nondecreasing concave function $\chi $ on $[0,+\infty )$, equal to the identity on $[0,1]$ and constant on $[2,+\infty )$, and put $q_ j=j\chi (q_\alpha /j)$. Concavity gives $q_ j\uparrow q_\alpha $ and

\[ \min (q_\alpha ,j)\leq q_ j\leq \min (q_\alpha ,2j). \]

The functions $q_ j$ are smooth and positive on $Y$: near $o$ they are constant, and elsewhere the density is smooth. Let $w_ j$ be the continuous solution of $\mathrm{MA}_\kappa (w_ j)=\mathrm e^{\lambda w_ j}q_ j\kappa ^ k$ from theorem 10.1.2. It is the greatest subsolution by theorem 14.34.1 and bounded uniqueness. Since $u_\alpha \leq 0$ and $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\kappa (u_\alpha )=q_\alpha \kappa ^ k$, it is a subsolution for every $q_ j$, whence $u_\alpha \leq w_ j$. Density comparison gives $w_ j\downarrow w$.

The pullback $W_ j=\lambda ^{-1}\log b+w_ j$ is a bounded solution on $X$, by the product expansion in theorem 14.29.3. For the smooth density $q_ j\mu $ it is therefore also the greatest subsolution. Every subsolution for $q_\alpha \mu $ is a subsolution for $q_ j\mu $, so $U_{\lambda ,\alpha }\leq W_ j$.

Monotone convergence in capacity and theorem 14.33.1 on $Y$ identify the equation for $w\geq u_\alpha $. Moreover, Stokes gives $\int _ Y\mathrm e^{\lambda w_ j}q_ j\kappa ^ k=1$. The integrands are dominated by $\mathrm e^{\lambda \sup _ Yw_1}q_\alpha $, so dominated convergence gives the same identity for $w$. Its pullback is a product solution, hence lies below the greatest solution on $X$. Together with $U_{\lambda ,\alpha }\leq \lim _ jW_ j$, this proves separation and all measure assertions.

Uniqueness makes each $w_ j$ invariant under the unitary action on the affine coordinates $z\in \mathbb C^ k$, and the limit has the same invariance. A radial $\kappa $-plurisubharmonic function has a convex nondecreasing profile $\rho +w$. Symmetric convolution of this convex profile on compact $s$ intervals, decreasing as the scale decreases, and bounded-product continuity give its radial measure $\mathrm d((\rho +w)'^ k)$, as in lemma 14.29.1. The bound $w\geq u_\alpha $, whose growth is $o(|s|)$ at $-\infty $, forces the initial derivative to vanish. The positive density makes the flux continuous and positive at finite $s$, yielding (14.125). Its final value is the mass one already proved. The profile is consequently smooth on finite $s$ intervals. $\square$

14.35.2 The normalized limit

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Set

\[ a_{\lambda ,\alpha }=\sup _ Yw_{\lambda ,\alpha },\qquad v_{\lambda ,\alpha }=w_{\lambda ,\alpha }-a_{\lambda ,\alpha },\qquad c_{\lambda ,\alpha }=\mathrm e^{\lambda \sup _ XU_{\lambda ,\alpha }} =b\mathrm e^{\lambda a_{\lambda ,\alpha }}. \]

In particular $a_{\lambda ,\alpha }\geq 0$.

Theorem 14.35.2. Let $\alpha _ j\to \alpha \in (0,1)$ and $\lambda _ j\to 0$ with $\lambda _ j>0$, at arbitrary relative rates. Then

\[ v_{\lambda _ j,\alpha _ j}\longrightarrow u_\alpha \quad \text{in }L^1(Y),\text{ in capacity, and locally uniformly on }Y\setminus \{ o\} . \]

Their pullbacks converge in $L^1(X)$, in capacity on $X$, and locally uniformly off $X_0\times \{ o\} $. Furthermore $q_{\alpha _ j}\to q_\alpha $ in $L^1(\kappa ^ k)$, $c_{\lambda _ j,\alpha _ j}\to b$, and the equation measures converge in total variation to $bq_\alpha \mu $. The limiting pullback has finite mass $b$ and belongs to $\mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(X,H,0)$.

Proof. Choose $[\alpha _-,\alpha _+]\Subset (0,1)$ containing all late parameters. The density formula in lemma 14.29.1 gives, uniformly for $\delta $ in this interval and $t=-s$ large,

\[ q_\delta (s)\leq C\mathrm e^{kt}(1+t)^{-1-k(1-\alpha _+)}. \]

Away from $o$ the densities are jointly smooth, including on the hyperplane at infinity. Thus one integrable density $g$ dominates all $q_\delta $, and dominated convergence gives $q_{\alpha _ j}\to q_\alpha $ in $L^1$.

For every normalized $\kappa $-plurisubharmonic $v$, theorem 1.4.2 gives $\int _ Y(-v)\kappa ^ k\leq C_ k$. Absolute continuity of the integral of $g$ therefore implies

\[ \sup _{\sup _ Yv=0}\int _{\{ v<-T\} }g\kappa ^ k\longrightarrow 0 \quad (T\to \infty ). \]

Taking $T=\lambda ^{-1/2}$ shows that

\[ \int _ Y(1-\mathrm e^{\lambda v})q_\delta \kappa ^ k \leq \lambda T+\int _{\{ v<-T\} }g\kappa ^ k\longrightarrow 0 \]

uniformly in $\delta $ and $v$. Write $v_ j=v_{\lambda _ j,\alpha _ j}$ and $a_ j=a_{\lambda _ j,\alpha _ j}$. The exact mass identity of the lemma is

14.128
\begin{equation} 1=\mathrm e^{\lambda _ ja_ j} \int _ Y\mathrm e^{\lambda _ jv_ j}q_{\alpha _ j}\kappa ^ k. \end{equation}

It gives $\mathrm e^{\lambda _ ja_ j}\to 1$, hence $\lambda _ ja_ j\to 0$. The actual densities $\mathrm e^{\lambda _ ja_ j}\mathrm e^{\lambda _ jv_ j}q_{\alpha _ j}$ converge in $L^1$ to $q_\alpha $ and are bounded by $2g$ for all late $j$.

Their cumulative radial fluxes $Q_ j$ consequently converge uniformly to the flux $Q_\alpha $ of $u_\alpha $. By (14.125), $v_ j'\to u_\alpha '$ uniformly on compact $s$ intervals. The normalized $L^1$ bound controls one value of each profile on a fixed annulus; thus every sequence has a subsequence converging uniformly on compact $s$ intervals to $u_\alpha +d$ for some finite constant $d$.

Near infinity the common bound for the densities is smooth and bounded. Hence $1-Q_ j(s)\leq C\mathrm e^{-s}$ and $|v_ j'(s)|\leq C\mathrm e^{-s}$ for large $s$. Integrating gives uniform convergence up to the hyperplane at infinity. At the other end, monotonicity of $\rho +v_ j$ gives, for $s<s_0$,

\[ v_ j(s)\leq v_ j(s_0)+\rho (s_0). \]

Choose $s_0$ so negative that the right side is negative for all late members of the subsequence. The supremum zero is then attained outside this inner ball. Uniform convergence on its compact complement gives $\sup _ Y(u_\alpha +d)=0$, so $d=0$. Normalized compactness identifies every $L^1$ cluster limit with $u_\alpha $; this proves convergence of the entire family and its local uniform convergence off $o$.

For capacity convergence, fix $T>0$. The sublevel $\{ u_\alpha <-T\} $ is a neighborhood of $o$, and its complement is a compact set on which convergence is uniform. Its capacity is at most $C/T$ by (1.26). Let $j\to \infty $ and then $T\to \infty $. The same argument on $X$ uses the normalized pullback of $u_\alpha $ and proves convergence in Hermitian capacity there; comparison sets omit pole sets. Pullback also preserves the $L^1$ and local uniform limits. The product expansion proves total-variation convergence of the measures, and theorem 14.29.3 supplies the exact limiting mass and envelope class. $\square$

14.35.3 Scalar centering and its moment boundary

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Proposition 14.35.3. The first moment

\[ M_\alpha =\int _ Yu_\alpha q_\alpha \kappa ^ k \]

is finite exactly when $\alpha <k/(k+1)$. In this range,

14.129
\begin{equation} -M_\alpha =\alpha \int _1^\infty x^{\alpha -1} \left[\alpha x^{\alpha -1}+(1-\alpha x^{\alpha -1})\mathrm e^{1-x}\right]^ k\, \mathrm dx. \end{equation}

For the joint limits of theorem 14.35.2, if $\alpha <k/(k+1)$, then

\[ a_{\lambda _ j,\alpha _ j}\to -M_\alpha ,\qquad U_{\lambda _ j,\alpha _ j}-\lambda _ j^{-1}\log b \to u_\alpha -M_\alpha \]

in $L^1$, in capacity, and locally uniformly away from the pole fiber. If $\alpha \geq k/(k+1)$, then $a_{\lambda _ j,\alpha _ j}\to +\infty $ and the latter translated potentials tend to $+\infty $ locally uniformly off the pole fiber, although $\lambda _ ja_{\lambda _ j,\alpha _ j}\to 0$. In both cases the exact scalar error is

\[ \lambda _ j^{-1}\log (c_{\lambda _ j,\alpha _ j}/b) =a_{\lambda _ j,\alpha _ j}. \]

Proof. As $t=-s\to \infty $, the radial profile of lemma 14.29.1 satisfies

\[ u_\alpha \sim -t^\alpha ,\qquad Q_\alpha '(s)\sim k(1-\alpha )\alpha ^ k t^{-1-k(1-\alpha )}. \]

Thus the first moment is finite precisely when $(k+1)\alpha <k$. In that case, write $x=1-\psi $. Its cumulative flux is

\[ Q_\alpha =\left[\alpha x^{\alpha -1} +(1-\alpha x^{\alpha -1})\mathrm e^{1-x}\right]^ k. \]

Integration by parts gives (14.129); the pole boundary term vanishes since $(k+1)\alpha -k<0$.

Suppose $\alpha <k/(k+1)$ and choose the parameter interval in the preceding proof with $\alpha _+<k/(k+1)$. Its dominating density $g$ satisfies $(-u_{\alpha _+})g\in L^1$. Write $w_ j=w_{\lambda _ j,\alpha _ j}=v_ j+a_ j$. Since $w_ j\geq u_{\alpha _ j}\geq u_{\alpha _+}$, the mass identity and $\mathrm e^ r\geq 1+r$ give $\int _ Yw_ jq_{\alpha _ j}\kappa ^ k\leq 0$. On a fixed annulus $A$ away from $o$, the normalized smooth-volume $L^1$ bound gives

\[ \int _ Aw_ jq_{\alpha _ j}\kappa ^ k \geq a_ j\int _ Aq_{\alpha _ j}\kappa ^ k-C. \]

On the complement, use $w_ j\geq u_{\alpha _+}$ and the integrable lower bound. The annulus has uniformly positive measure, so $0\leq a_ j\leq C_*$. Along any subsequence with $a_ j\to a$, local uniform convergence gives $w_ j\to u_\alpha +a$ off $o$. In the exact identity

\[ \int _ Y\frac{\mathrm e^{\lambda _ jw_ j}-1}{\lambda _ j} q_{\alpha _ j}\kappa ^ k=0, \]

the negative part is dominated by $(-u_{\alpha _+})g$ and the positive part by $C_*\mathrm e^{C_*}g$. Dominated convergence gives $M_\alpha +a=0$. Every subsequence therefore has the asserted scalar limit. Adding these convergent constants to the normalized potentials gives all three claimed topologies.

If $\alpha \geq k/(k+1)$, use instead

\[ \int _ Y\frac{1-\mathrm e^{\lambda _ jv_ j}}{\lambda _ j} q_{\alpha _ j}\kappa ^ k =\frac{1-\mathrm e^{-\lambda _ ja_ j}}{\lambda _ j}\leq a_ j. \]

The integrands are nonnegative and tend off $o$ to $(-u_\alpha )q_\alpha $. Fatou's lemma forces $a_ j\to +\infty $. The preceding theorem gives $\lambda _ ja_ j\to 0$; local uniform convergence of $v_ j$ gives the stated divergence of $w_ j$. This includes sequences approaching the moment boundary from below. $\square$

For the elliptic fivefold of corollary 14.29.4, $b=240\pi $ and the moment boundary is $\alpha =2/3$. The choice $\alpha =1/2$ has a finite centering constant. For $2/3\leq \alpha <1$, the supremum-normalized family still converges to the exact threshold solution, while its scalar-centered version diverges off the pole fiber. Thus convergence of the normalization scalars alone does not give a bounded first-order scalar error.

14.35.4 Order in the separated class

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We use here the closed-class uniqueness theorem: on a compact connected Kähler manifold, two potentials whose non-pluripolar measures have full mass and are equal differ by a constant. This is an external input; its source is identified in 34.11.

Corollary 14.35.4. Every separated threshold subsolution $r=r(y)$ with $r\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\kappa )$ and $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_ H(r)\geq bq_\alpha \mu $ equals $u_\alpha +d$ for some constant $d$. In particular $u_\alpha $ is its unique supremum-zero member. If $\alpha <k/(k+1)$, then $u_\alpha -M_\alpha $ is the greatest such subsolution whose $q_\alpha $-mean on $Y$ is nonpositive.

Proof. The product formula reduces the inequality to $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\kappa (r)\geq q_\alpha \kappa ^ k$. Closed Stokes gives each bounded truncation mass one, so the extended measure of $r$ has mass at most one. The inequality therefore gives equality and full mass. Closed-class uniqueness yields $r=u_\alpha +d$. In the finite-moment range its mean is $M_\alpha +d$, which is nonpositive exactly when $d\leq -M_\alpha $. $\square$

At and beyond the moment boundary every constant translate has mean $-\infty $, so that mean condition no longer chooses a greatest translate. The order assertion concerns separated potentials. Selection for arbitrary integrable densities and uniqueness of arbitrary critical potentials on the nonclosed product remain the questions of 33.1.1 and section 14.33.
